+| E-2026-08-14-01 | 2026-08-14 | Samarakoon, Siriwardhana, Porambage, Liyanage, Chang, J. Kim, J.H. Kim, Ylianttila — "5G-NIDD: A Comprehensive Network Intrusion Detection Dataset Generated over 5G Wireless Network" (Univ. Oulu, VTT, Univ. College Dublin, Univ. Colorado Colorado Springs, Texas A&M Univ.–Commerce, ETRI Korea; funded by Academy of Finland 6Genesis Flagship grant 318927 + a Korean IITP grant). Publishes a LABELED network-intrusion-detection dataset built on a REAL, fully functional 5G test network (5GTN, Univ. Oulu site, NSA option 3a — two Nokia Flexi Zone Indoor Pico base stations, MEC-hosted victim, Raspberry-Pi attacker nodes) rather than a simulated/virtual testbed like most prior IDS datasets (DARPA/KDD99/NSL-KDD/CICIDS2017/Bot-IoT, surveyed in the paper's own Table I); benign traffic generated by real mobile devices (live HTTP/HTTPS/SSH/SFTP). 8 attack sub-types across 2 classes — Port Scans (SYN scan, TCP connect scan, UDP scan via Nmap) + DoS/DDoS (ICMP flood, UDP flood, SYN flood via Hping3; HTTP flood via Goldeneye; Slowrate DoS via Slowloris/Torshammer) — captured over scheduled attack windows and converted to labeled flow records alongside benign traffic (9 classes total incl. benign), published for AI/ML-security research reuse. Benchmarks 5 ML models (Decision Tree, Random Forest, KNN, Naive Bayes, MLP): Random Forest best binary-classification accuracy 99.947%, 99.48% multiclass (9-class) accuracy at top-10 ANOVA-ranked features; Naive Bayes markedly weaker (41–96%). §VII flags future directions: distributed/federated detection, active defense (filtering/rate-control/re-routing) triggered off detection output, zero-day extension | Academic research (arXiv preprint, cs.CR; multi-institution, government-grant funded, not vendor/promotional) | arXiv:2212.01298v1, submitted 2 Dec 2022; file "2212.01298v1.pdf", Download folder | A | R14 (+R12) | watch | A — primary academic/technical source: first-hand dataset generation + benchmarked ML-IDS evaluation on a REAL 5G testbed, government-grant funded (Academy of Finland 6Genesis Flagship + Korean IITP), no vendor/promotional slant. Oulu 5GTN, NSA-mode) network-layer IDS study with ZERO rail/GSM-R/FRMCS/CCS-TSI content; no claim here is about the FRMCS bearer, the DB incident, or any regulatory point already on file. RELEVANCE: a concrete, reusable TECHNIQUE-CLASS resource — a labeled attack/benign dataset + benchmarked ML models for network-layer intrusion detection over 5G — kin to the Process-Mining anomaly-detection thesis already on file (E-2026-07-12-04, tied to R12) and directly on-point for ADR-012 action item 5 ("extend independent detection to security event detection / SIEM-IDS feed") and R14's CRA secure-by-design/detection posture: if a future workstream builds or evaluates a SIEM/IDS layer for the FRMCS 5G-SA core/RAN, this dataset + benchmark is a candidate input. +| E-2026-08-15-01 | 2026-08-15 | IEC 61508-2 "Functional safety of electrical/electronic/programmable electronic safety-related systems — Part 2: Requirements for E/E/PE safety-related systems" — **FDIS CIRCULATION DRAFT, doc ref 65A/254/FDIS/c2, dated "IEC: 1999"** (IEC SC 65A "System aspects", TC 65; foreword still carries unfilled `65A/xxx/FDIS` / `65A/xxx/RVD` placeholders → an unpublished pre-publication draft of what became **Edition 1 (2000)**). 69 pp, complete text. THE GENERIC PARENT of the rail EN 5012x series. Paraphrased content: the E/E/PES safety lifecycle in the realisation phase (§7.1–7.9: safety-requirements spec, validation planning, design & development, integration, O&M, validation, modification, verification), functional safety assessment (§8), Annex A (normative — control of failures during operation: diagnostics per subsystem class, watchdog/program-sequence, data paths, sensors/actuators, systematic-failure control), Annex B (normative — avoidance of systematic failures per lifecycle phase), Annex C (normative — diagnostic coverage + SAFE FAILURE FRACTION calculation). LOAD-BEARING CLAUSES for this engagement: **§7.4.2.3** — where a system implements BOTH safety and non-safety functions, "all the hardware and software shall be treated as safety-related UNLESS it can be shown that the implementation of the safety and non-safety functions is sufficiently independent" (i.e. failure of any non-safety function does not cause a dangerous failure of the safety function); "wherever practicable, the safety-related functions SHOULD BE SEPARATED from the non-safety-related functions"; NOTE 1 defines sufficient independence as dependent-failure probability low vs the highest SIL involved; NOTE 2 cautions that co-implementing non-safety + safety functions increases complexity and lifecycle difficulty. **§7.4.2.4** — requirements are set by the HIGHEST-SIL safety function unless implementations of different SILs are shown sufficiently independent. **§7.4.2.5** — where independence is required, BOTH "the method of achieving independence" AND "the justification of the method" shall be documented during design. Also: architectural constraints Tables 2/3 (safe-failure-fraction × hardware-fault-tolerance → max claimable SIL; type A = well-defined failure modes/determinable fault behaviour/sufficient field data, type B = otherwise — SIL 4 needs SFF ≥90% with HFT 1, or ≥60% with HFT 2, and type B at SFF <60% with HFT 0 is "not allowed"); intro sets the claim FLOOR for a single E/E/PE safety-related system at 10⁻⁹ dangerous failures/hour (high-demand/continuous mode) and 10⁻⁵ PFD (low demand); §1.2 designates Parts 1–4 "basic safety publications"; the standard is explicitly framed as the generic framework FROM WHICH application-sector standards are derived | International standard — **PRE-PUBLICATION FDIS DRAFT of a now-SUPERSEDED edition** (IEC SC 65A) | file "iec61508-2.pdf", Download folder; internal doc ref 65A/254/FDIS/c2, "61508-2 IEC: 1999"; PDF metadata: created 2000-08-07, Word source "IEC 61508 Part2-4.0.doc" | A (draft · superseded edition — see caveats; NOT citable as current) | R11 (+ADR-004 FFI/composition; ADR-003 assumption; R9, R10) | watch | **A BY SOURCE, but DOUBLY DISPLACED — hence WATCH, and it grounds nothing citable.** Tier A because it is a primary IEC SC 65A standards document, complete text, first-hand — no intermediary. TWO DISQUALIFYING CAVEATS, both load-bearing: (1) **DRAFT** — this is an FDIS *circulation draft* (65A/254/FDIS/c2), not the published standard; the foreword's `65A/xxx/FDIS` placeholders prove it was never the final artefact. Per the register's own precedent for draft standards (E-2026-08-01-17, IEC 63452 CDV: "DRAFT/not-for-reference, grounds nothing"), a draft cannot carry a safety-case citation. (2) **SUPERSEDED EDITION** — this is the draft of **Edition 1 (2000)**; the current text is **IEC 61508-2:2010 (Edition 2.0)**, which materially reworked exactly the areas of interest (systematic capability, element/"proven in use" routes, the architectural-constraint route 1H/2H). Under the ONE-ROW-PER-SPEC / edition discipline (E-2026-08-01-11/-13), the anchor must be the CURRENT edition — so this row anchors nothing; it is the *lineage*, not the reference. (3) **WRONG LAYER for the CCS TSI** — IEC 61508 is the GENERIC cross-sector parent; the rail sector-specific derivatives (EN 50126-1/-2:2017, EN 50716:2023, EN 50129:2018+AC:2019, EN 50159:2010+A1:2020) are what the CCS TSI Table A 3 cites and what the safety case must satisfy. **THEREFORE IT DOES NOT CLOSE THE PAYWALLED EN 5012x RESIDUAL** — it is the parent framework, not the sector text, and a superseded draft of it at that. WHAT IT DOES DO (genuine, and the reason to log it): it puts the GENERIC-PARENT ROOT of ADR-004's central argument on file in free primary-lineage form. ADR-004 §3 ("Freedom from interference / independence") and its governing composition principle — *"otherwise the agent inherits SIL-4"* — are currently carried as an **ASSUMPTION** resting on UNLICENSED EN 50129 composition wording (ADR-004 action item 4 says so explicitly, and ADR-003's "not high-risk" finding leans on the same unlicensed wording). §7.4.2.3/.4/.5 here state that principle at the parent-standard level in almost the engagement's own terms: mixed safety/non-safety ⇒ EVERYTHING is safety-related unless sufficient independence is DEMONSTRATED; requirements follow the HIGHEST SIL unless independence is shown; and the method AND its justification must be DOCUMENTED. That last point is a direct, if non-citable, corroboration that ADR-004 action item 2 (produce the FFI/independence analysis) is the right deliverable, and §7.4.2.3's "wherever practicable, SEPARATE them" corroborates the Option-A physical-segregation preference over Option-B shared-platform partitioning. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R11 stays *Proposed — load-bearing*; ADR-004 stays *Proposed*; ADR-003's classification assumption stays an assumption; no traceability-matrix status change. The residual is NARROWED in provenance-quality terms only (the generic principle is no longer known solely through secondaries) — recorded as an addendum to ADR-004 action item 4, not as a status flip. **FOLLOW-UP (to make this citable):** obtain **IEC 61508-2:2010 Ed 2.0** (published, current) and, for anything the safety case actually rests on, the licensed **EN 50129:2018+AC:2019** clause text — the sector standard, which is the real target. NOT a security item → master log only. KIN: ADR-004 (action items 2 + 4 — the FFI/independence deliverable and the paywalled residual), ADR-003 (the composition-wording assumption), E-2026-08-01-17 (IEC 63452 CDV — the draft-standard tiering precedent this row follows), E-2026-08-01-10/-11/-13 (the edition-discipline rule), E-2026-07-06-11 (the EN 5012x paywall residual), E-2026-07-07-01/-02/-04 (the free CSM-RA quantitative anchor + the systematic-integrity correction this parallels), E-2026-07-02-03 (separation-kernel/mixed-criticality), R11/R9/R10 |
+| E-2026-08-15-02 | 2026-08-15 | ERA (European Union Agency for Railways) webinar deck — "**ERA activities on cybersecurity**", presented at the joint **ERA-ENISA webinar** "Cybersecurity in Railways" (title slide sub-head: "Cybersecurity for Rail: new opportunities / novel challenges"); 9 slides, no author named in-document. DATE NOT STATED IN-DOCUMENT — inferred **~Nov 2020** from PDF print metadata (Foxit PhantomPDF Printer, created/modified 13.11.2020); content corroborates that window (TS 50701 still a DRAFT → published 2021; "the NIS Directive" not NIS-2 → pre-2023). Paraphrased: **ERA's three-part cyber mandate** — (1) MONITOR all railway-cybersecurity activity, "identify and promote adoption of **security-by-design** features in future **radio communication and signalling** systems"; (2) COVER SAFETY REQUIREMENTS of the rail system incl. "the assessment of **safety consequences originated by security threats**", with an explicit SCOPE BOUNDARY — "security threats based on **physical access** to assets **outside of scope**; **digital inherent threats considered**" — and "Safety AND Security Management Systems"; (3) REFLECT the above in EU railway regulation, naming the instruments explicitly: **TSIs (TAF/TAP, OPE, CCS)** = "technical requirements to guarantee interoperability, e.g. **increase the cyber robustness of ERTMS**", and **CSMs** = "process oriented requirements to guide the acknowledgement of cybersecurity issues that might influence safety". Plus COOPERATION posture: close relationship with **ENISA + EC** ("support railway stakeholders on cybersecurity strategy development", "**consider incident reporting schemes**"), cross-fertilisation with **EASA/EMSA**, dialogue with National Authorities on "**potential gaps** in cybersecurity requirements" — naming both cyber agencies (**ANSSI, BSI**) and **Railway Safety Authorities (Traficom, EPSF)** — and support for **ER-ISAC**, "considering their deliverables in the evolution of the rail regulatory framework". ECOSYSTEM MAP (slides 4-6): ER-ISAC (~50 members, IM/RU-led FR/DE/BE/NL; contact@er-isac.eu); CENELEC **TC9X WG26** draft TS 50701; Shift2Rail **IP2 TD2.11** security-by-design (Shared Security Services, Protection Profiles); **UIC Cyber Security Solutions Platform** ("from a telecom angle"); **ENISA TRANSSEC** — Transport Resilience and Security Expert Group (Air/Rail/Water/Maritime); **DG MOVE Transport Cybersecurity Toolkit**; **ETSI TC CYBER** TR "Implementation of the NIS Directive". +| E-2026-08-15-03 | 2026-08-15 | ENISA (European Union Agency for Cybersecurity) webinar deck — "**ENISA activities for rail**" / "ENISA: Cybersecurity in the Rail Sector", **Dimitra Liveri + Marianthi Theocharidou, Secure Infrastructures & Services Unit**, dated **13.11.2020 IN-DOCUMENT**; 10 slides. The **ENISA half of the joint ERA-ENISA webinar** whose ERA half is E-2026-08-15-02. Presents the findings of the **ENISA STUDY 2019-2020** behind the **2020 report "Security measures in the Railway Transport Sector"** (the fuller report is NOT on file — see follow-up). Paraphrased: **ENISA's rail workstreams** — support Operators of Essential Services on **NIS Directive** implementation; **partner of the European Railway ISAC**; support standardisation/certification via **CEN-CENELEC TC9X/WG26** and **TS 50701**; "**collaborate closely with ERA**" on "**Cybersecurity for ERTMS**". Study structure: policy & regulatory context · stakeholders · essential services · railway systems · cybersecurity measures · cybersecurity in ERTMS. **PRIMARY SURVEY DATA — railway services by stakeholder class** (IM = infrastructure manager, RU = railway undertaking, IM/RU = combined): operating traffic on the network IM 71% / RU 71% / IM-RU 100%; maintaining infrastructure and/or trains IM 64% / RU 29% / IM-RU 75%; carrying goods and/or passengers RU 57% / IM 7% / IM-RU 88%; ensuring safety and security of passengers/goods IM 64% / RU 43% / IM-RU 100%. **THE HEADLINE FINDING — a security-measure MATURITY SPLIT across EU rail operators.** Areas of **HIGH** maturity: security risk analysis · security policy and security audit · **system segregation** · access control · physical & environmental security · **logging** · **incident reporting** · communication with authorities · business continuity · disaster-recovery management · crisis management (general). Areas of **LOW** maturity: ecosystem mapping and relations · IT-security maintenance (for IMs) · security audit* and **security indicators** · accreditation · **cryptography** · **DETECTION** · **logs correlation and analysis** · industrial control systems · **crisis management (for CYBER incidents)**. **Areas of improvement** (slide 9): digital/cyber culture and awareness · cybersecurity standards and requirements · "specificities of the sector: **distributed infrastructure, presence of LEGACY SYSTEMS**" · "**reconciling SAFETY AND CYBERSECURITY worlds (accreditation, culture, jurisdiction)**" · "costs: security vs competitiveness and operational efficiency" · "complexity of regulatory ecosystem: **differences in national transposition of the NIS Directive**". NB it is NOT fully independent of E-2026-07-20-01 (ENISA Transport Threat Landscape) at INSTITUTIONAL level — same agency — but it is a DIFFERENT study, method and dataset, so it does add evidentiary weight rather than duplicate it (contrast the E-2026-08-15-02 / E-2026-07-19-01 pair, which must be counted as one). **VALIDATES R12 — the sharpest external corroboration of the awareness-gap thesis on file.** ENISA's own survey found EU rail operators mature at **logging** and **incident reporting**, but LOW-maturity at **DETECTION**, **logs correlation and analysis**, and **security indicators**. That is the awareness gap measured, sector-wide, by the EU cyber agency: **operators record, but do not correlate, analyse, or detect** — data is captured and not turned into a decision-ready signal, which is precisely the capability R12's Risk Sentinel / Assurance agents exist to supply, and precisely the "continuous risk register + decision-ready alert BEFORE threshold" formulation. The same split repeats one row down — crisis management (general) HIGH, crisis management **for cyber incidents** LOW. **HONESTY BOUND on that read (do not overclaim):** ENISA is measuring SECURITY-measure maturity, NOT safety-monitoring maturity, so this is NOT a direct measurement of the 23-Jun silent-fault failure mode (no error message → failover never engaged, E-2026-06-27-03). The bridge between the two is an ARGUMENT the engagement already makes explicitly — ADR-012 decision item 5: "the same 'don't rely on a component to self-report' principle applies to a compromise as to a silent fault" — and this row corroborates the SECURITY leg of that bridge with agency data, leaving the safety leg where it was. Cite it as: the detection-capability deficit is sector-wide and agency-measured on the security side, kin to (not identical with) the safety-side silent-fault gap. **ALSO VALIDATES:** R14/ADR-012 — "reconciling safety and cybersecurity worlds (accreditation, culture, JURISDICTION)" is ADR-012's central tension named by the agency itself, and "differences in national transposition of the NIS Directive" is the fragmentation NIS-2 was meant to cure (relevant to the German NIS2UmsG in-force residual, ADR-012 action 6); LOW-maturity **cryptography** is the same weakness class as the 3-DES/AES-128 crypto-lifecycle thread (E-2026-07-19-01 hook 3) and the PQC lead; LOW-maturity **industrial control systems** + "presence of LEGACY SYSTEMS" corroborate R1/R2 (obsolescence + the coexistence estate) from the security side; HIGH-maturity **system segregation** is mild corroboration that the zones/conduits pattern (TS 50701 → SUBSET-147 → FS.61) is actually practised. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R12 stays **Proposed · ADR-002** — this strengthens the JUSTIFICATION for the Risk Sentinel, it does not decide it; R14 stays Open, ADR-002/ADR-012 stay Proposed. **Matrix: evidence-ref added to R12 only — STATUS COLUMN UNCHANGED.** **FOLLOW-UP (this deck is a summary, not the source):** obtain the ENISA 2020 report **"Security measures in the Railway Transport Sector"** — it carries the method, sample and full measure-by-measure findings behind these bands, and would upgrade this from a summary slide to a citable dataset. RST). **(h) PKI/X.509 OPEN in 2020** — whether X.509 becomes the standard for onboard/trackside digital identity, and PKI cross-certification for ERTMS interoperability, both answered "needs to be discussed in a specific Working Group before being endorsed as a technical solution"; UNISIG named as a recognised participant. **(i) NO THREAT ANALYSIS YET (2020)** — "A detailed threat analysis for the railway sector has not been conducted yet"; known incidents to date "target the IT railway systems (e.g. Ransomware, DDoS, Phishing, Malware, Data Theft)". **(j) MISC:** metro/tram/light rail as essential services = Member-State discretion; EUCC scheme "applies to ICT products", further analysis needed for other sectors; the 4th Railway Package "does not elaborate on cybersecurity"; no study quantifying cybersecurity cost per railway system exists (2020); ENISA SOC/CSIRT good-practice guide due Dec 2020 (not rail-specific); ER-ISAC "doing quite well considering its young age", ENISA ISAC-in-a-box cited; ENISA engaging UNIFE; a follow-on ENISA-ERA technical conference announced for 16-17 March [2021]. **(k) URL CAPTURED:** the ENISA rail report is at https://www.enisa.europa.eu/publications/railway-Cybersecurity. The register already holds what subsequently landed in that destination: FRMCS SRS v1.0.0 §15 (E-2026-07-12-02) makes minimum crypto mandatory but leaves **monitoring, incident response and risk assessment OUT of scope**, and §17.2 system monitoring is EMPTY. So the chain is now closed end-to-end from primaries: **the sector standard deliberately excluded radio security → ERA pointed at the FRMCS spec as its proper home → the FRMCS spec, when delivered, omitted the monitoring layer.** That is not an inference about intent; it is a documented hand-off whose receiving end is on file, and it materially sharpens the R12/R14 "bring your own observability" gap (E-2026-07-12-04). **REFINES E-2026-08-15-03 CAUSALLY (c):** the low detection / log-correlation maturity is attributed by ENISA not to absent tooling but to **lack of expertise and resources**, and it is **worse for OT systems, "managed more generally by IMs"** — i.e. most acute exactly in the domain (OT) and the actor class (infrastructure manager, DB InfraGO's class) this engagement addresses. Combined with (a)'s explicit "for the OT (safety related) ones, there is a gap", the EU cyber agency is on record that the safety-related OT side is the under-served half — the §4a no-owner thread and R12's justification both strengthen. **NARROWS THE EN 50129 PAYWALL RESIDUAL SLIGHTLY (d):** confirms from a free primary agency source that **EN 50129:2018 contains a clause 6.4 on IT security** and that ERA's position is TS 50701 serves as its guidance — a small, free datum against the ADR-004 action-item-4 residual (does NOT substitute for the licensed clause text; the depth question the asker raised was left unanswered). **FORECAST → OUTCOME (g)(h), all resolved on file:** TS 50701 mid-2021 → published 2021 (E-2026-07-26-15); "first TSI could be CCS TSI 2022" → CCS TSI Reg (EU) 2023/1695 (E-2026-07-25-04); "a new Subset from UNISIG" → SUBSET-146 E2E security, CCS-TSI-mandated (E-2026-08-01-15); PKI/X.509 "to be discussed" → SUBSET-146 delivered TLS 1.3 + X.509 PKI (E-2026-08-01-15, E-2026-08-03-08). This row is therefore a **dated waypoint that documents the open questions of 2020 and lets the register show they were answered** — cite it for provenance of the trajectory, never for current status. **(f) is useful for the §4a ownership question in the negative:** the competent-authority split named here (ENISA = NIS; ERA = Safety & Interoperability) shows that as of 2020 NEITHER agency owned operational rail-telecom/interconnect security — consistent with the standing no-rail-owner finding. **(i) SUPERSEDED** — "no detailed rail threat analysis yet" (2020) is overtaken by E-2026-07-20-01 (ENISA Transport Threat Landscape, window to 10/2022) and the IT-only incident profile is overtaken by the OT-relevant material since logged (Poland radio-stop E-2026-07-29-05, GTPDOOR E-2026-08-02-02): do not cite (i) as current. **(k) CLOSES the E-2026-08-15-03 follow-up** — the canonical URL for the ENISA rail report is now recorded (fetch still pending; enisa.europa.eu not yet tested against the sandbox network policy). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R12 stays Proposed · ADR-002; R14 stays Open, ADR-012 Proposed; ADR-004 unchanged (the cl. 6.4 datum narrows but does not discharge action item 4). **No matrix or ADR edit made** — see the response note offering the R12/R14 evidence-ref additions and an ADR-004 cl. 6.4 footnote as follow-ups. +| E-2026-08-15-06 | 2026-08-15 | ERA — "**Questions and Answers — ERA-ENISA Webinar: Managing Cybersecurity Risks in Railways, 25 November 2021**", Q&A Document on ERA controlled letterhead (120 Rue Marc Lefrancq, Valenciennes); 2 pp, 12 Q&A pairs answered by ERA + ENISA; PDF created 28.01.2022 (author "MARTIN Cyriel"). **A LATER, DISTINCT event** from the 13.11.2020 webinar (E-2026-08-15-02/-03/-04) and the 03/2021 conference (E-2026-08-15-05) — this one accompanies the ENISA report **"Railway Cybersecurity — Good Practices in Cyber Risk Management"** (Nov 2021). Paraphrased: **(a) TS 50701 IS NOT MANDATORY — ERA, unambiguously.** Asked "Will ERA consider TS 50701 as mandatory standard, even if it is a technical specification only?", ERA answers "**No.** … the TS 50701 is considered a very valuable milestone in railway cybersecurity standards but **cannot be as is considered as a mandatory Standard in the EU railway regulatory framework**." **(b) EN 5012x ALREADY CARRIES CYBER** — "Is there an impact on EN 5012X due cybersecurity?" → "**Yes. The CENELEC series about railway applications already consider the cybersecurity risk in the EN 50126.**" **(c) THE REPORT + ITS CROSS-FRAMEWORK ANNEX** — the risk-treatment options mapped across **NIS Directive / ISO 27002 / NIST Cybersecurity Framework / CLC TS 50701** are "at the annex of the report" (URL given twice: enisa.europa.eu/publications/railway-cybersecurity-good-practices-in-cyber-risk-management). **(d) RISK SCENARIO 1 — CCS COMPROMISE TO PROCURE AN ACCIDENT:** asked whether this is a real risk versus simpler physical/terrorist alternatives, ENISA answers "**It was pointed to us as a PRIMARY CONCERN by the organisations participating in the study.** The likelihood of such a scenario should be considered during the risk assessment, and **based on the absence of previous incidents, it can be considered as LOW.** This does not mean other attacks, such as physical attacks of lower complexity should not be considered." **(e) NO RAIL SECTORAL CERTIFICATION ASSESSMENT (as of 11/2021)** — EU Cybersecurity Act (CSA) schemes are **voluntary**, market take-up requires industry/consumer acceptance; ENISA released a *methodology for sectoral cybersecurity assessments* as a preparatory step, but "**to the best of our knowledge, such an assessment has not been conducted yet for the Railway sector**". **(f) ER-ISAC DELIVERABLE IN PREPARATION** — asked for templates/examples for executing a security risk assessment: "Currently not, but **ER-ISAC is preparing a document 'Zoning and Conduits for Railways' based on EN 50701** that could be used as a guideline for performing the security risk assessment." **(g) HUMAN & ORGANISATIONAL FACTORS — ACKNOWLEDGED GAP:** asked for cyber risk-assessment tools covering human/organisational factors mappable to the ERA safety-culture model → "**Unfortunately, we are not aware of such cyber risk assessment tools.**" **(h) SCOPE + NIS-2 WIDENING** — the report covers "both the IT and the OT systems of Railway Undertakings and Infrastructure Managers"; "the proposal for a new NIS Directive covers more entities, **which may include the supply chain**". **(i) CROSS-MODAL** — dependencies/interfaces to service providers, third parties and other transport modes "could be considered within the risk assessment process"; **ENISA "planning a cyber threat landscape for all transport subsectors in 2022"**. **(j) IMPACT EXEMPLAR** — asked whether ransomware/DDoS/data theft can impact traffic operations: passenger-information-system availability/integrity attacks cited, plus **Maersk** (given in-doc as "July 2017"; the NotPetya outbreak was 27.06.2017 with impact running into July) — vessels unable to access or depart ports for weeks, entire computer estate destroyed, >US$300m. **(k) INFORMATION SHARING** — a common register of real situations/issues is "precisely an activity carried out by" ER-ISAC. This is the cyber-side counterpart of the three-layer rule established for the SAFETY standards (E-2026-08-01-11/-13, carried in ADR-004's standards-basis note: the EN 5012x are "**not a legal mandate** but a three-layer route" — TSI citation, non-exclusive safe harbour into CSM-RA, and OJ-harmonised presumption — and the assurance argument must **never** use "mandatory compliance" language). The same caution now has a primary agency source on the cybersecurity side. **NO CORRECTION NEEDED to ADR-012**, which already frames TS 50701 under "*Sector interfaces*" rather than as a mandate — this row CONFIRMS that framing was right, and it sharpens the pending TS 50701 → IEC 63452 basis migration (E-2026-08-01-17/-20): whatever the basis standard, it is a route to conformity, not an obligation in itself. **SECOND FREE DATUM AGAINST THE PAYWALL RESIDUAL (b):** EN 50126 already considers cybersecurity risk — pairing with the EN 50129:2018 clause 6.4 (IT security) datum from E-2026-08-15-04. Two free, agency-sourced facts about paywalled clause content now on file; both narrow, neither discharges, ADR-004 action item 4. **(d) IS THE MOST CITABLE RISK POSITION HERE, AND NEEDS ITS CAVEAT CARRIED:** rail organisations participating in ENISA's study named CCS-compromise-to-cause-an-accident as a **primary concern**, while ENISA assesses its likelihood **low** — expressly on the basis of "the absence of previous incidents". **ANALYST FLAG (opinion, not the source's):** absence-of-incidents is a weak likelihood basis for a low-frequency/high-consequence hazard, and it is the same inferential move the 23-Jun outage punished on the safety side (a failure mode that had never fired was treated as adequately covered until it did). Cite (d) as *"operators' primary concern; agency-assessed low likelihood on an absence-of-incidents basis"* — never as "ENISA says CCS attack risk is low" unqualified. **CONSISTENT WITH THE §4a NO-RAIL-ANALOGUE PATTERN (e):** no sectoral cybersecurity certification assessment had been conducted for rail as of 11/2021, and CSA schemes are voluntary — a fourth instance alongside MDSCert (devices), NESAS (equipment) and FS.11/19/20 (interconnect ops), all lacking rail counterparts (E-2026-08-01-08). **(g) IS A DIRECT R10/ADR-004 HIT:** ERA/ENISA know of **no** cyber risk-assessment tooling for human and organisational factors mappable to the ERA safety-culture model — which is precisely the terrain ADR-004's **automation-bias** obligation occupies (the advisory-system-plus-over-trusting-human failure path) and which R10's oversight design must cover. The agencies naming this an open gap strengthens the case that the engagement must build it rather than adopt it. **FORECAST → OUTCOME (i):** "planning a cyber threat landscape for all transport subsectors in 2022" → delivered as the ENISA Transport Threat Landscape already on file (E-2026-07-20-01, window to 10/2022) — this row is that report's stated provenance. **NEW FOLLOW-UP TARGETS:** (1) the ENISA report **"Railway Cybersecurity — Good Practices in Cyber Risk Management"** (Nov 2021, URL captured) — its **annex maps risk-treatment options across NIS / ISO 27002 / NIST CSF / TS 50701**, directly reusable for ADR-012; distinct from the 2020 "Security measures" report still outstanding from E-2026-08-15-03; (2) the ER-ISAC **"Zoning and Conduits for Railways"** guideline (f) — would extend the zones/conduits chain already on file (IEC 62443 → TS 50701 → FS.61 core → SUBSET-147 on-board) with an operator-authored risk-assessment method, and is an ER-ISAC *operational* deliverable, relevant to the §4a ownership question. NB the answer says "EN 50701" in 11/2021 when the artefact was then **TS** 50701 (the EN followed in 2023) — loose usage in the source, not a separate document. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open, ADR-012 stays Proposed (its TS 50701 framing confirmed, not changed); R11/ADR-004 unchanged (action item 4 narrowed, not discharged); R10 stays Proposed. **No matrix or ADR edit made** — see the response note offering an ADR-012 footnote on (a) and an ADR-004 note on (b)/(g). SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. TP-01-21-425-EN-N**. Authors: **Theocharidou Marianthi, Stanic Zoran (ENISA)** + De Mauroy Louise, Lebain Loïc, Haddad Jules (**Wavestone**, consultancy). Produced with ERA, ER-ISAC and **UNIFE's cybersecurity working group**; acknowledgements name ~25 practitioners across the EU sector — Trafikverket, Bane NOR, ProRail (×4), Infraestruturas de Portugal, ERA (Chatelet), RFI (Ciancabilla, Magnanini), **DB Netz (Cosic)**, ER-ISAC (De Visscher), Knorr-Bremse, CFL, UNIFE, SNCF Réseau, Adif, Alstom, Infrabel (Houbion), NS, Ministero Infrastrutture e Trasporti, Selectron. **This is the report behind E-2026-08-15-06's Q&A, and it closes that row's follow-up target #1.** It explicitly "builds upon the 2020 ENISA report on cybersecurity in the railway sector … which assessed the level of implementation of cybersecurity measures" — i.e. the study summarised in E-2026-08-15-03. STRUCTURE (complete text read): §2 cyber risk management (steps; sector approaches) · §3 railway assets & services taxonomy · §4 cyber-related threats (taxonomy + **7 risk scenarios**) · §5 cybersecurity measures · §6 conclusions · §7 bibliography · **Annex A** asset descriptions · **Annex B** threat descriptions · **Annex C** the full security-measures mapping (to p.55). Paraphrased highlights: **(a) THE SEVEN RISK SCENARIOS** — 1 compromising a signalling/automatic-train-control system **leading to a train accident**; 2 sabotage of traffic-supervising systems **leading to train traffic stop**; 3 ransomware → disruption of activity; 4 theft of clients' personal data (booking system); 5 leak of sensitive data via exposed database; 6 DDoS blocking ticket purchase; 7 disastrous event destroying the datacentre → IT-service disruption. **(b) SCENARIO 1 IN ENISA'S OWN WORDS** — "requires high motivation of the attacker and in-depth knowledge of railway systems and networks. It is considered a **low likelihood** scenario. It has been included as the **potential impact can be very high** and this is **one of the primary concerns of railway stakeholders**"; cross-referenced to the **Łódź, Poland 2008 tram hack**. Attack chain (effect-level): reconnaissance (trespass at depots/maintenance centres, malicious employee, or phishing) → build a device/software to command-and-control junctions and trains → control junctions/trains → inject false information → major disruption or train accident. Assets affected: automatic train control, interlocking systems, tracks, trains, passengers. **Its listed mitigations include "NIS - DF.1 Detection" and "NIS - DF.3 Logs correlation and analysis"** (plus physical/environmental security, HR security, cryptography, access rights, NIST PR.AT awareness/training, CLC/TS 50701 SR 1.2). **(c) SCENARIO 2** — targeted ICS malware against traffic supervision → urgent stop of train traffic; "**such an incident has not yet occurred in the railway sector**"; also applicable to freight docking. **(d) SECTOR PRACTICE (from the workshops)** — RUs/IMs "often choose a **two-step approach**": a general framework for **IT** risk treatment complemented by a more detailed industry-driven one for **OT**. Named: **ISA/IEC 62443 and CLC/TS 50701** as the main OT references; NISD national requirements, **ISO 27002** and the **NIST CSF** for IT; less commonly SANS Top 20, Forrester. Footnote 39: "the security measures of CLC/TS 50701 are **matching the measures described in IEC 62443-3-3:2013**". **(e) ANNEX C — the cross-framework mapping** the Q&A pointed to: NIS Directive measures (GV/PR/DF/RS domains) mapped against **ISO/IEC 27002 · NIST CSF · CLC/TS 50701** (the latter given as IEC 62443 SR-numbers, e.g. SR 1.1-1.12 identification/authentication, SR 7.1 DoS protection, SR 7.3/7.4 backup + recovery), built by a documented two-phase forward-and-reverse mapping so every NIS measure is covered. **(f) CONCLUSIONS — four findings, quoted in substance:** (i) **"there is a LACK OF A SINGLE CYBER RISK MANAGEMENT APPROACH for railway organisations to cover both IT and OT in a unified manner"** (the report's own pull-quote); (ii) **IT/OT differentiation "increasingly difficult"** — "it can be a complex process to identify which approach is better suited, whether a device can be considered IT or OT, or which security measures and which standard should be applied"; (iii) **SUPPLY-CHAIN REGULATORY GAP** — "key elements of the railway supply chain, both IT and OT, **do not fall under the same European regulatory framework**", with the supply industry advocating more EU certification schemes; (iv) **LEGACY** — "the **plethora of legacy systems** which add an additional degree of difficulty when managing cyber risk. **At present, it is not possible to provide relevant recommendations to address the cybersecurity of legacy systems in the railway sector**"; plus the need to keep risk assessments current, "that **risks are continuously monitored**", and to maintain an up-to-date sector threat landscape | Agency report (primary — formally published ENISA report with ISBN/DOI; Read together, from one agency's own published outputs: **the sector's stated primary concern is a scenario whose named mitigations are the sector's weakest capabilities.** That is the sharpest available external statement of the R12 awareness-gap thesis, and it is now sourced end-to-end from primaries rather than argued. **CARRY THE LIKELIHOOD CAVEAT (b):** ENISA rates Scenario 1 **low likelihood / very high impact**, and cites Łódź 2008 — which this register already distinguishes from the 2023 Poland radio-stop (E-2026-07-29-11). Cite as "low-likelihood, very-high-impact, and a stakeholder primary concern"; the ANALYST FLAG recorded at E-2026-08-15-06 stands — an absence-of-incidents likelihood basis is weak for low-frequency/high-consequence hazards. **(f)(iv) IS A DIRECT R1/R2 HIT AND SHOULD BE CARRIED INTO THE COEXISTENCE ARGUMENT:** ENISA states it is **not possible** to give relevant recommendations for securing rail **legacy** systems. R2 commits to GSM-R↔FRMCS coexistence "a decade+" (Accepted · ADR-001) — i.e. a decade of running a legacy 2G bearer for which the EU cyber agency, as of 2021, could offer no security recommendations. This does **not** unseat R2 (the transition decision stands and the obsolescence driver R1 is unchanged), but it names a security dimension of that decade that ADR-012 must own; kin the GSM-R hardening items already on file (E-2026-07-19-01 air-interface encryption; SIM-SEC-001/-003). **(f)(iii) FORECAST → OUTCOME:** the 2021 supply-chain regulatory gap — "key elements … do not fall under the same European regulatory framework" — is substantially the gap the **CRA (Reg (EU) 2024/2847)** subsequently closed for products with digital elements, which is ADR-012's own legal basis and its PR13 mitigation. This row is therefore useful provenance for *why* the CRA matters to rail, from the sector's own pre-CRA problem statement. **(f)(i)/(ii) UNDERWRITE ADR-012's CORE PRINCIPLE:** the absence of a unified IT+OT risk approach, and the difficulty of even classifying a device as IT or OT, is the sector-level form of the "one gate for change" argument; the IT/OT classification problem is also the security-side cousin of ADR-004's vital/non-vital boundary question (R11) — both turn on where a defensible line is drawn and who must justify it. **(d)/(e) ARE DIRECTLY REUSABLE:** Annex C's NIS ↔ ISO 27002 ↔ NIST CSF ↔ TS 50701(=IEC 62443-3-3 SRs) mapping is a ready control-crosswalk for ADR-012 action items 1-2 (PDE inventory + procurement clause), and footnote 39 confirms on a primary the TS 50701 ← IEC 62443 derivation previously held only from a secondary review (E-2026-07-29-11). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** it is a *good-practices* report — descriptive, non-binding — and every position it touches is already open or proposed: R14 stays **Open** (needs ADR — ADR-012 Proposed), R12 stays Proposed · ADR-002, R2/R1 stay Accepted · ADR-001, R8 stays Recommended, R11 stays Proposed. **No matrix or ADR edit made** — see the response note; the strongest candidates offered there are an R12 evidence-ref and an ADR-012 note recording the Annex C crosswalk + the legacy-recommendations gap. **RESIDUAL:** the **2020** ENISA report ("Security measures in the Railway Transport Sector") is still NOT on file in full — only its summary deck (E-2026-08-15-03); this 2021 report builds on it but does not replace it. +| E-2026-08-15-08 | 2026-08-15 | **ENISA — "METHODOLOGY FOR SECTORAL CYBERSECURITY ASSESSMENTS" (SCSA Methodology), sub-titled "EU Cybersecurity Certification Framework", SEPTEMBER 2021**; published ENISA report, **142 pp**, © ENISA 2021. Authored by a 15-member expert group (Andrukiewicz, Atallah, Bartels, Dörr, Marinos, Michota, Mongay Batalla, Nguyen, Payen, Polemi, **Preneel**, Quemard, Schaumüller-Bichl, Stütz, Ward). **This is the instrument named in E-2026-08-15-06(e)** — the methodology ENISA cited when stating that no sectoral cybersecurity assessment "has been conducted yet for the Railway sector". Legal notice is explicit: it "represents the views and interpretations of ENISA" and "**does not endorse a regulatory obligation**" under Reg (EU) 2019/881. Paraphrased scope: a **preparatory method for drafting SECTORAL cybersecurity CERTIFICATION SCHEMES** under the EU Cybersecurity Act (CSA), addressing ICT services in individual market sectors, to be run **with sectoral stakeholders before** a candidate scheme is defined. Core principle: establish a sound understanding of the sectoral ICT services/system first — a sectoral risk assessment identifying stakeholder objectives, primary assets and related risks, extended by a "**deep dive**" into the intended use of relevant subsystems/products/services, and by **cyberthreat intelligence (CTI)** on potential attackers, their motivation and capabilities, so security and assurance requirements can be assigned to ICT subsystems/products/services **based on risk**. Structure: §4 sectoral systems in the CSA context; §5 consistent definition of risk/security/assurance — incl. **CSA meta-risk classes** (a common approach to scaling risk), **using ATTACK POTENTIAL to select security and assurance requirements**, risk-based **common security levels (CSL)**, the **common assurance reference concept**, and §5.7 "**triggers for reactions to unexpected events**"; §6 implementation as four workflows — **A** context establishment & business-layer assessment, **B** primary-asset-layer assessment, **C** assessment of supporting assets, **D** supporting-asset **gap analysis**; §7 mapping to security-problem definition / assurance levels / security levels; §8 definition and application of **common controls** (+ a sample list); §9 **cyberthreat intelligence** — what a threat is, **types of attackers**, characterization of attackers, **estimating attacker potential from CTI**, and steps to implement CTI-based attack-potential assessments. §1.4 "State of the work": the methodology "relies as far as possible on standards and proven concepts", some parts "can be seen as innovations", and — the load-bearing detail — "**the practicality of the SCSA Methodology and its applicability in the area of 5G was evaluated in a dedicated pilot project**", with improvements folded into this version; future steps include enhancing the sample control list and consolidating the §9 attacker-potential methods "after their first practical implementation". READ: front matter, executive summary, full ToC/structure, §1.4, §9 headings; the 142 pp NOT read line-by-line | Agency methodology report (primary — published ENISA report under the CSA certification framework; expert-group authored; expressly non-binding) | file "ENISA Report - SCSA Methodology.pdf", Download folder; md5 9c4062ee…f586e; © ENISA 2021, September 2021 | A (published EU-agency report, primary; THREE FLAGS: **expressly NON-BINDING** — ENISA's own legal notice disclaims regulatory obligation, and CSA certification schemes are **voluntary** (E-2026-08-15-06(e)); **ZERO RAIL CONTENT** — "railway" appears **0 times** in 142 pp, sectors exemplified are health/energy/finance/transport-generic; **partially read** — structure and key sections, not the full 142 pp) | R14/ADR-012 (+ R8/PR13 procurement & certification; R12 CTI/attacker characterisation; ADR-010 method reuse) | watch | **A by source, WATCH by relevance — this is a METHOD, not a finding, and it has never been pointed at rail.** Tier A: a published ENISA report, primary, expert-group authored. But it is domain-generic (0 railway mentions) and expressly non-binding, so it grounds nothing about rail and moves nothing. **ITS REAL VALUE IS AS THE CONCRETE SHAPE OF A KNOWN GAP.** E-2026-08-15-06(e) recorded ENISA saying, in 11/2021, that no sectoral cybersecurity assessment had been conducted for the railway sector. This row supplies the missing half of that statement: **the method exists, is published, is 142 pp of worked-out procedure, and had already been piloted — just not on rail.** The gap is therefore not "no method exists" but "**the method was never applied to this sector**", which is a materially different and more actionable finding. It is the fifth instance of the register's **§4a no-rail-analogue pattern**, alongside MDSCert (devices), NESAS (equipment), GSMA FS.11/19/20 (interconnect operations) and the absent rail CSIRT model (E-2026-08-01-08, E-2026-08-15-05) — a mature EU cybersecurity instrument with no rail counterpart or application. **THE 5G PILOT IS THE SHARPEST HOOK (§1.4).** The methodology's practicality was evaluated "**in the area of 5G**" in a dedicated pilot. FRMCS is 5G SA — so the instrument has been exercised on the very technology family FRMCS adopts, in a non-rail framing. That makes a rail/FRMCS SCSA an unusually low-friction proposition: the sectoral method is proven, the technology substrate is already covered by the pilot, and what is missing is the rail sectoral application with its stakeholders. **CANDIDATE REUSE (method, not evidence)** — several constructs map onto engagement work already scoped: **attack potential** and §9 **attacker characterisation from CTI** parallel the adversary-emulation profiles in evidence-log-security.md (Sandworm G0034, LightBasin, APT28 — all currently analyst-assembled without a formal potential/capability scale); **workflow D supporting-asset gap analysis** and the **common security level (CSL)** construct are the shape of ADR-012's PDE inventory + procurement-clause work (action items 1-2); §5.7 **triggers for reactions to unexpected events** is kin to the detection/alerting thread (R12); the §8 common-controls approach parallels the Annex C crosswalk of E-2026-08-15-07. Record these as **available method**, not as adopted method. **CAUTION ON WEIGHT:** CSA certification is **voluntary** and this document binds nobody — for the engagement the **binding** product-security instrument is the **CRA (Reg (EU) 2024/2847)** with its mandatory conformity assessment (ADR-012 legal basis), and the CRA post-dates this report entirely. Do not let an SCSA framing displace CRA obligations; at most SCSA is a preparatory/voluntary layer above them. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open, ADR-012 Proposed, R8 Recommended; no matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UP / OPEN QUESTION worth putting to the client:** has any rail sectoral cybersecurity assessment been initiated since 11/2021 (ERA, ENISA, ER-ISAC, UNIFE, or ERJU System Pillar)? If not, "commission a rail/FRMCS SCSA" is a concrete, named, EU-sanctioned action the engagement could recommend — and the 5G pilot is the precedent to cite. NOT read line-by-line: if the method is actually adopted, §§5-9 need a full read first. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. +| E-2026-08-15-09 | 2026-08-15 | **European Commission, DG CONNECT** — "Latest EU cybersecurity legislative initiatives – NIS 2 and CRA", **Boryana Hristova-Ilieva, Legal officer, Unit H2 "Cybersecurity and digital privacy policy"**; 20 slides. Date not stated in-doc; PDF created **29.11.2022**, and the filename convention ("01Policy-0…") matches the sibling deck "03SRIS-1 - IEC - PT 63452.pdf" on disk, already logged from the **2nd ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 01.12.2022** (E-2026-08-01-20) — so almost certainly that conference's opening policy slot. **CRITICAL STATE FLAG: the CRA is presented here as a PROPOSAL** ("Main elements of **the proposal**"; "Tentative timeline — **Proposal (15 Sept. 2022)**"), i.e. this is the **pre-adoption** briefing, two years before Reg (EU) 2024/2847 was adopted. Paraphrased: **NIS-2** — timeline 2013→2022 ending at "POLITICAL AGREEMENT on NIS2"; three pillars (Member State capabilities: national authorities/strategies, **CVD frameworks**, crisis management · risk management & reporting: size threshold, **accountability for TOP MANAGEMENT for non-compliance**, mandatory risk-management measures and incident notification · cooperation: Cooperation Group, CSIRTs network, **CyCLONe**, European vulnerability database, peer reviews, biennial ENISA report); **Annex I sectors include "Transport (air, RAIL, water, road)"** alongside energy, banking, health, drinking/waste water, digital infrastructure, ICT service management, public administration, space; Annex II adds postal, waste, chemicals, food, manufacturing, digital providers, research. **SUPPLY CHAIN** — supply-chain security is one of the required measures; Member States must address ICT supply-chain security in national strategies; and "**the Cooperation Group is explicitly empowered with carrying out coordinated security risk assessments of specific critical ICT services, systems or products supply chains (based on the example of 5G)**". **CRA (as proposed)** — rules for placing hardware/software on the market, built on the **New Legislative Framework**; obligations on manufacturers, distributors, importers; essential requirements "across the life cycle (**5 years**)"; harmonised standards; **conformity assessment differentiated by risk**; market surveillance. **Scope**: products with digital elements incl. hardware (laptops, mobile phones, **network equipment**, CPUs), software, and remote data-processing solutions. **NOT covered**: non-commercial/open source outside commercial activity; **services, in particular cloud/SaaS — "covered by NIS2"**. **Outright exclusions**: "certain products sufficiently regulated on cybersecurity (**cars, medical devices, in vitro, certified aeronautical equipment**)" — **RAIL IS NOT IN THAT EXCLUSION LIST**. **Manufacturer obligations**: risk assessment → Annex I §1 product essential requirements + Annex I §2 vulnerability-handling + technical file → conformity assessment, **CE marking**, EU DoC → continued vulnerability-handling compliance across "5 years or product lifetime, whichever is shorter"; **report to ENISA within 24 hours** (exploited vulnerabilities; incidents affecting product security). **Product essential requirements** (proposal): appropriate security level; delivered without known vulnerability; then risk-based — security by default, protection from unauthorised access, confidentiality/integrity of data-commands-programs, data minimisation, **availability of essential functions**, minimise negative impact on other devices, limit attack surfaces, reduce incident impact, "**record and monitor security relevant events**", enable adequate security updates. **Vulnerability handling**: identify/document dependencies incl. **SBOM**; address without delay; test; publicly disclose fixed vulnerabilities; **CVD policy**; share information; secure update mechanisms; patches "without delay, free of charge and with advisory messages". **Conformity-assessment split (proposal)**: ~**90% default = self-assessment**; ~10% critical — "Class I" (standard application or third-party) and "Class II" (third-party), plus a "highly critical" tier for future mandatory EU certification. **Class criteria named: functionality (e.g. critical software); intended use (e.g. INDUSTRIAL CONTROL / NIS2); extent of impact; and for the highest tier "USED BY NIS2 ENTITIES" + "resilience of supply chain"** — all "to be amended/specified via delegated acts". **Sanctions**: MSA powers to require remediation, prohibit/restrict/withdraw/recall, and fines **up to €15 000 000 or 2.5% of worldwide turnover**. But it presents the **CRA as a PROPOSAL**, and the register already holds the **adopted** law verified twice against EUR-Lex primary text (E-2026-07-01-06, re-validated verbatim E-2026-07-10-02). **NOTHING HERE MAY RESTATE A CURRENT CRA OBLIGATION** — where deck and adopted law differ, the adopted law governs, and ADR-012's legal basis is unchanged. **KNOWN PROPOSAL-VS-ADOPTED DELTAS visible from what is already on file:** (1) **reporting** — the deck says simply "report to **ENISA** within 24 hours"; the adopted Art 14 is a **two-track** regime already recorded at E-2026-07-10-02 (vulnerability track 24 h / 72 h / final report **14 days** after a corrective measure is available, Art 14(2)(c); severe-**incident** track 24 h / 72 h / **1 month**, Art 14(4)) — the deck's single 24 h line is an over-simplification of the adopted text and must not be used to size the expedited path (ADR-012 action 3); (2) **support period** — "5 years or product lifetime, whichever is shorter" is the proposal's formulation, and ADR-012's support-period work must be sized against the adopted Article, not this slide; (3) **product classes** — the proposal's "default / Class I / Class II / highly critical" nomenclature was **reworked on adoption**, so the split is directional only. **CONSISTENT and unchanged:** the **€15M / 2.5% worldwide turnover** ceiling matches the adopted Art 64 figure already on file, and **NIS-2 Annex I "Transport (air, RAIL, water, road)"** confirms the rail-as-essential-entity premise ADR-012 rests on. **THE TWO GENUINELY ADDITIVE ITEMS (why log it at all):** **(i) RAIL IS ABSENT FROM THE CRA EXCLUSION LIST.** The deck names cars, medical devices, in vitro and **certified aeronautical equipment** as outright exclusions — products "sufficiently regulated on cybersecurity" by sectoral law. **Rail/CCS is not among them.** Directionally this corroborates ADR-012's founding premise that FRMCS products with digital elements sit squarely under the CRA rather than being carved out to rail sectoral law the way aviation is — a striking asymmetry between two safety-critical transport modes, and a useful framing for the client. **VERIFY before citing:** confirm against the adopted Reg (EU) 2024/2847 exclusions that no rail carve-out was added between proposal and adoption (the existing verified rows do not explicitly record the exclusion list). **(ii) "RECORD AND MONITOR SECURITY RELEVANT EVENTS" IS A CRA PRODUCT ESSENTIAL REQUIREMENT.** If that survived into the adopted Annex I, it means the **product regulation mandates the very monitoring capability the FRMCS specification left out** — FRMCS SRS §15 puts monitoring/incident-response out of scope and §17.2 system monitoring is EMPTY (E-2026-07-12-02), and ERA had delegated radio security into that spec (E-2026-08-15-04). A binding product requirement to record and monitor security-relevant events would cut directly across that gap, and would give R12's observability argument a **regulatory** lever rather than only an architectural one. **This is a LEAD, not a finding — it must be verified against the adopted Annex I text before any weight is placed on it.** **(iii) minor but useful:** the class criteria "**used by NIS2 entities**" and "intended use (e.g. **industrial control**/NIS2)" indicate the direction of travel for FRMCS core/RAN/MCX products supplied to an essential entity like DB InfraGO — likely pushed above default self-assessment; again directional, since the classes changed on adoption (ADR-012 action 1). **(iv)** the Cooperation Group's power to run **coordinated supply-chain risk assessments "based on the example of 5G"** is the 5G-Toolbox precedent applied to supply chains — kin to R8/PR13 vendor concentration and to the SCSA/5G-pilot thread (E-2026-08-15-08). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open, ADR-012 stays Proposed (its legal basis already rests on the verified adopted law, not on this); R8 Recommended; R12 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** verify (i) the CRA exclusion list and (ii) the "record and monitor security relevant events" essential requirement against the adopted Reg (EU) 2024/2847 Annex I — both are potentially load-bearing and both are currently proposal-state only. +| E-2026-08-15-10 | 2026-08-15 | **ANSSI + EPSF (French national authorities)** — "Cybersecurity and Railways safety: **Situation in France**", **Sadio BÂ** (ANSSI — French National Cybersecurity Agency, **Sectorial Coordinator for Transports**) and **Denis GARNIER, MSc** (**EPSF — the French NSA**, Head of Technical Expertise Unit, Regulatory/European/International Affairs Directorate); 9 slides; **"December, 1st in Lille" STATED IN-DOCUMENT** — which **confirms the 2nd ERA-ENISA Conference date (01.12.2022)** that E-2026-08-15-09 could only infer. PDF created 28.11.2022. Paraphrased: **(a) THE TWO BODIES COMPARED** — ANSSI: created 2009, workforce **573** (2021), under the **Prime Minister** (via SGDSN), mandate to foster a coordinated, ambitious, pro-active national cybersecurity response. **EPSF: created 2006, workforce 106, under the Transportation Ministry — and, in its own words on the slide, "NO LEGAL COMPETENCE FOR CYBERSECURITY MATTERS."** **(b) COLLABORATION TIMELINE** — 2017 first ANSSI intervention at an EPSF safety conference · 2018 letter of intent EPSF↔ANSSI · 2018 start of work (with **SNCF as both IM and RU**, and ERA) on a position paper on railway cybersecurity · **2021 publication of that position paper** · 2022 ANSSI entering the EPSF Management Board ("coming"). **(c) THE 2021 POSITION PAPER'S SEVEN RECOMMENDATIONS** — R1 step up cooperation between authorities toward "a shared and applicable position on **the link between rail safety and cybersecurity**"; R2 perform a **European benchmark** on that link; R3 promote information sharing and coordinate rail-industry action; R4 **integrate the cybersecurity dimension from the start of a project**; R5 "**implement a reasoned holding in safety condition while minimizing the impact on safety demonstrations**"; R6 identify the repercussions cybersecurity could have on **interoperability** "in a sector where the number of players and the amount of cross-border traffic is growing"; R7 increase the **robustness of the information systems essential for operating the railway system** against cyber threat. **(d) STATE OF PLAY 2022** — ANSSI and EPSF both sit on the **ATO train steering committee**; "collaboration on railways incidents **to identify incidents linked to cybersecurity (NOT YET FINALIZED)**"; EPSF workforce training — 2-day sessions, **22 people trained** (of 106), focused on railway aspects and **TS 50701**; regular **ERA/ANSSI/EPSF/SNCF** meetings; regular exchanges with **German and Finnish** authorities. **(e) REGULATORY QUESTIONS AND CHALLENGES**, which the authors label explicitly "**raised in France but NOT SPECIFIC to France**" — transposition of **NIS-2**: "will NSA and the national railways sector be involved and how?"; the coming obligation to declare cyber incidents to **ANSSI and ENISA**; "**limited cybersecurity requirement in the coming CCS TSI**"; various levels of awareness, with "**small RUs and local/regional transportation authorities may be more in risk**"; "announcement of **SMS integrating cybersecurity aspects. How shall ERA and NSAs deal with them? Time needed to acquire competencies or to upskill rail experts**"; "**increasing cost for certifications**"; impacts of the coming **Data Act, Cyber Resilience Act, Cybersecurity Act**; and "**future of TS 50701**". READ: complete text (9 slides) | National-authority conference presentation (primary institutional — the French NSA and the French national cybersecurity agency jointly, named officials) | file "01Policy-1 - EPSF-ANSSI - FR approach on Railway cybersecurity.pdf", Download folder; md5 22988ffd…07ae; 2nd ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 01.12.2022 (date confirmed in-document) | A (primary — two national competent authorities presenting their own mandates, collaboration and published position paper; TWO FLAGS: **slide-deck form**, telegraphic and non-normative; **2022 vintage** — NIS-2 has since been transposed, the CCS TSI has since issued as Reg (EU) 2023/1695, TS 50701 has since become EN 50701:2023, and the CRA has since been adopted) | R14/ADR-012 (+ **R11/ADR-004 supervisory competence**; R7 interoperability; R12; R10 competence/oversight) | validate | **A — and genuinely INDEPENDENT corroboration, which is what makes it valuable.** Every other cybersecurity source logged today came from ERA or ENISA (and several from a single event). This is a **different institutional vantage entirely** — two *national* competent authorities, in a Member State, describing their own legal mandates and practice. Where it agrees with the EU-agency material, that agreement carries real evidentiary weight rather than being the same body restating itself. **THE FINDING THAT MATTERS MOST — (a): THE NATIONAL SAFETY AUTHORITY HAS NO LEGAL COMPETENCE FOR CYBERSECURITY.** EPSF states it on its own slide. This is a **supervisory gap at national level**, and it bites directly on the engagement's central coupling: ADR-012's core principle is that **a security update to live safety-critical code IS a safety-relevant change** — classed, gated and assessed under ADR-011/CSM-RA, preserving Rückwirkungsfreiheit at the ADR-004 boundary. But the authority that supervises the safety case (the NSA) has, at least in France, **no legal competence over the security dimension of that same change**, while the authority that has cyber competence (ANSSI, 573 staff vs EPSF's 106) has no rail-safety mandate. The mitigation on display is *institutional goodwill* — a letter of intent, a joint position paper, board membership, joint meetings — not allocated legal competence. **This is the national-level instance of the register's standing §4a "no owner" pattern**, and it is the sharpest form yet: not a missing standard or an unapplied method, but a missing *legal competence* at exactly the safety-security seam ADR-012 relies on. It should be put to the client as a question about **Germany**: does the EBA hold cyber competence for rail, or does the same seam open between EBA and BSI? (E-2026-08-15-02 records ERA's dialogue with "cybersecurity agencies (e.g. ANSSI, **BSI**)" and "railway safety authorities (e.g. Traficom, **EPSF**)" as two distinct groups — consistent with the split being structural across Member States, not a French peculiarity.) **(c) R5 IS ADR-012's TENSION, NAMED BY A NATIONAL POSITION PAPER:** "implement a reasoned holding in safety condition while minimizing the impact on safety demonstrations" is precisely the CRA-security-updates-versus-authorisation-stability trade that ADR-012 identifies as load-bearing and resolves via one gated change process (ADR-011). An independent national-authority recommendation converging on the same problem statement strengthens that ADR's framing considerably. **(c) R6 + (e) "limited cybersecurity requirement in the coming CCS TSI"** are a matched pair worth carrying: a national authority flagging, pre-publication, that the CCS TSI then in preparation — which issued as **Reg (EU) 2023/1695** — carried only limited cyber requirements. That is a second, independent voice on the same gap ERA's own delegation chain produced (radio security pushed to the FRMCS spec, which omitted monitoring — E-2026-08-15-04 + E-2026-07-12-02), and it is the interoperability-side framing of it (R7). **(d) CORROBORATES THE COMPETENCE/RESOURCE THESIS INDEPENDENTLY:** ENISA attributed low detection maturity to "lack of expertise and resources" (E-2026-08-15-06); here a national NSA reports training **22 of its 106 staff** in two-day sessions and flags "time needed to acquire competencies or to upskill rail experts" as an open challenge. Two institutions, two vantages, same conclusion — the constraint is people, not tooling. **(d) ALSO — INCIDENT ATTRIBUTION WAS NOT A SOLVED PROCESS:** "collaboration on railways incidents to identify incidents linked to cybersecurity" was explicitly **not yet finalized** in 2022. Read against the 23-Jun 2026 outage, where a cyber cause was publicly *ruled out* early (E-2026-06-24-09) while the true cause took days to surface (E-2026-06-27-01/-03), this is a useful caution about how confidently and how quickly "not cyber" can be established — an R12/PR5 detection-and-attribution point, and one to raise gently rather than as a criticism of any specific finding. **(e) MINOR BUT REUSABLE:** small RUs and regional authorities flagged as the weaker tier (relevant to any sector-wide FRMCS rollout, R13); rising certification cost (R8/PR13, ADR-012 conformity burden); SMS-integrating-cybersecurity as an open ERA/NSA question (ADR-012 action 4). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open, ADR-012 Proposed, ADR-004/R11 Proposed, R7 Accepted; no matrix change, no ADR change. **STRONGEST ADR CANDIDATE OF THE DAY, offered not made:** an ADR-012 context note recording the NSA-cyber-competence gap and the German (EBA/BSI) question it raises — say the word and I will draft it. **FOLLOW-UP:** obtain the **2021 EPSF/ANSSI position paper** itself (referenced but not on file; produced with SNCF and ERA) — a national-authority primary on the safety↔cyber link, and the source behind R1-R7. EBA also "gathers information from railway infrastructure and traffic operations" via **regulatory reporting** and **Requests For Information / supervisory tasks**. **(c) "WHERE IT SECURITY JOINS RAILWAY SAFETY" — the German NSA's own list:** **ETCS · ATO · FRMCS · Infrastructure Digitisation (involving IP transport of traffic control data)** — followed by the rhetorical "That's it? …". **(d) GERMAN KRITIS SCOPE FOR RAIL** (sector Transport & Traffic → service Passenger & Freight Transport → section Railway Transport), system category / criterion / threshold: **passenger station** — station categorization → highest category; **freight station** — outgoing trains per year → **23 000**; **rail yard** — trains formed per year → **23 000**; **railway network AND INTERLOCKING SYSTEM** — rail network per **TEN-V → CORE NETWORK**; **traffic management and control system** — control system of the rail network per TEN-V → **CORE NETWORK**; **railway control centre** — scheduled passenger transport performance → **8 200 000 train-km** per network/sub-network, or freight → **730 000 000 tonne-km**. Plus a slide "Overview of new legal obligations according to BSI Act" (figure only, no extractable text). **(e) BSI ORGANISATION** (as of 15.12.2021) — divisions incl. **OC Operational Cyber Security** (Branch OC 1 **Detection**, OC 2 **CERT-Bund**, OC 3 IT-Security Situational Awareness), **WG 1 Cyber Security for Critical Infrastructures**, **SZ 3 Cyber Security in Mobile Infrastructures and Chip Technology**, SZ Standardization/Certification/Cyber Security of Telecommunication Networks. **(f) THE OPEN QUESTIONS THE AUTHORITIES THEMSELVES POSE** — "Is IT Security only relevant for 'Critical Infrastructure' parts of railways?"; "**Does the increased application of IT/OT introduce new safety-related aspects?**"; "**Can a ransomware attack at rolling stock manufacturer be safety-relevant?**". **EBA reads its AEG safety mandate as expressly encompassing IT security** ("Safety — *also involving IT Security*") and has backed that reading with a standing **Railway IT/Cyber Security Task Force since 2018**. **SELF-CORRECTION, recorded deliberately:** at E-2026-08-15-10 I suggested the NSA-cyber-competence gap might be "structural across Member States, not a French peculiarity". **That inference is WRONG for Germany and is withdrawn.** The correct finding is stronger and more useful: **national supervisory competence for rail cybersecurity DIVERGES materially between Member States** — a live interoperability and cross-border-assurance problem in its own right (and precisely what the French position paper's R2 "European benchmark" and R6 "repercussions on interoperability" recommendations were reaching for, E-2026-08-15-10). **THE MOST LOAD-BEARING ITEM FOR THIS ENGAGEMENT — (b): EBA ALREADY CLAIMS THE FUNCTIONS ADR-012 AND ADR-004 DEPEND ON.** "**Authorisation of IT-based railway systems and components**" and "railway operator permission approval/renewal" mean the German NSA asserts an authorisation gate over IT-based railway systems — which is exactly the gate ADR-012 item 2 routes CRA security updates through (a security update to live safety-critical code is a Class A/B change under ADR-011/CSM-RA) and exactly the perimeter ADR-004's boundary must satisfy. And "**licensing of IT security assessors**" is the security-side analogue of the ISA/AsBo/NoBo apparatus ADR-004 action item 5 depends on — i.e. **there is already a German licensing route for security assessors**, which materially de-risks the assurance path ADR-004 currently leaves as an open action. **This should be verified and then built into ADR-012/ADR-004 rather than designed around.** **(c) FRMCS IS NAMED BY THE GERMAN SAFETY REGULATOR** as one of four places "where IT security joins railway safety" (with ETCS, ATO and IP-transport infrastructure digitisation). That is a primary, in-jurisdiction, regulator-stated placement of FRMCS in the safety-security overlap — the exact premise of R14 and of ADR-012's coupling to ADR-004 — and it is the strongest single sentence on file for that premise. **(d) GROUNDS NIS-2/BSIG APPLICABILITY TO THE CLIENT CONCRETELY:** the **railway network and interlocking system**, and the **traffic management and control system**, are KRITIS where the network is **TEN-T Core Network** — which is DB InfraGO's core estate. So the operator-side obligations ADR-012 item 4 places in the SMS attach through a *named, threshold-defined* legal hook, not a general assumption. **Re-verify the thresholds against the current BSI-KritisV** (2022 figures; revised periodically) before any of them is quoted to the client. **(e) IS THE §4a COUNTER-EXAMPLE WORTH RECORDING:** the register's standing finding is that operational rail-telecom security has no owner. Germany shows that at *national KRITIS* level there **is** an operational owner — BSI, with a Detection branch and **CERT-Bund** — even though that does not reach the rail-telecom interconnect layer the §4a thread is actually about. Cite it as narrowing the §4a gap's scope (it is an interconnect/rail-specific gap, not an absence of any operational cyber authority), not as closing it. **(f) THE AUTHORITIES' OWN OPEN QUESTIONS ARE THE ENGAGEMENT'S QUESTIONS**, which is worth showing a client: whether IT security is relevant beyond KRITIS parts; whether increased IT/OT introduces **new safety-related aspects**; and whether **a ransomware attack at a rolling-stock manufacturer can be safety-relevant** — a supply-chain-to-safety question (R8/PR13) that the CRA subsequently began to answer. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays **Open** (needs ADR — ADR-012 Proposed), ADR-004/R11 Proposed, ADR-011 unchanged, R3/R4 unchanged; no matrix change, no ADR change. **STRONGEST ADR CANDIDATE ON THE BOARD — offered, not made:** an **ADR-012 context/decider note** recording (i) the EBA "safety also involving IT security" reading of the AEG, (ii) the 2018 EBA Cyber Task Force functions — especially **authorisation of IT-based systems** and **licensing of IT security assessors** — as the German supervisory route ADR-012 items 2/4 and ADR-004 item 5 should plug into rather than reinvent, and (iii) the FR/DE divergence as a cross-border assurance risk. NIS; "Policy Critical Sectors NL"; **I&W Cyber Strategy** (Ministry of Infrastructure & Water Management); **WBNI** (*Wet beveiliging netwerk- en informatiesystemen* — the Dutch NIS implementing act); **NL ISAC Spoor** (the Dutch rail ISAC); **Roadmap Vitaal Spoor**; **Cyber Security Governance Spoorsector**; and a *Ministeriële Regeling Beveiliging netwerk- en informatiesystemen IenW*. **(f) SECTOR GOVERNANCE OUTCOMES** ("Results of Cyber Governance and Spoor ISAC") — co-operation (Railforum, stakeholders) + laws and regulation feeding strategy and policy → a **Roadmap Cyber Security Framework**; 2) "**scoping is difficult and needs attention**"; 3) "**determine risks at various levels**"; 4) "**setting up sector wide mechanisms and methods takes time**"; 5) "**cooperation needs trust, cooperation builds trust**". A check confirms **"CER", "Critical Entities Resilience" and "2022/2557" have ZERO occurrences** across both evidence logs. The engagement's regulatory picture is built on **CRA (product) + NIS-2 (operator cyber)** — and CER is the **physical/organisational resilience** sibling adopted alongside NIS-2, addressing critical entities in transport. If it applies to rail infrastructure managers, it bears directly on **R3 (eliminate the central single point of failure)** and **R4 (fail-soft, no full standstill)** — i.e. on exactly the two requirements the 23-June outage promoted to load-bearing — from a *regulatory* direction the register has not examined at all. **This is a REGISTER GAP surfaced by an operator's slide, not a finding about CER's content**, which is unverified here: nothing about CER's scope, thresholds or rail applicability may be asserted from this deck. **FOLLOW-UP (highest-value on the board): pull Dir (EU) 2022/2557 (CER) from EUR-Lex, verify whether rail IMs/RUs are in scope, and if so open the question of whether ADR-012 should widen from CRA+NIS-2 to CRA+NIS-2+CER — potentially an ADR-scope change rather than a mere evidence row.** **(g) THE FIVE LESSONS ARE DIRECTLY REUSABLE** and, being operator-earned, carry a different weight from agency guidance: lesson **2 "scoping is difficult"** independently corroborates ENISA's conclusion that IT/OT classification is itself hard ("it can be a complex process to identify… whether a device can be considered IT or OT", E-2026-08-15-07) — and it is the operator-side echo of the ADR-004 boundary problem, where drawing and defending the line is the work; That is a usable reference shape for ADR-012 action item 4 (NIS-2 Art 21/23 into the operator SMS) and worth holding beside TS 50701/IEC 62443 as an implementation precedent from a peer operator. **(f) NL ISAC Spoor + a sector CSIRT plan + joint risk analysis** is a fourth national instance of sector-level cyber cooperation machinery (with ER-ISAC, FI's national network, and the FR authority cooperation) — R12 relevance: these are the venues an oversight layer's evidence chain would feed. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed), R12 Proposed, R3/R4 unchanged — the CER item is a *gap to investigate*, and nothing here changes a status until CER's rail applicability is verified against primary law.
