This deck supplies the mechanism: **aviation amended its Basic Regulation to address cyber risks FOR SAFETY, produced binding Part-IS (DR published, IR pending), applies it to ALL approved organisations, and backs it with a mature EUROCAE/RTCA standards suite (ED-202A/203A/204A/205A…) plus a standards coordination group (ECSCG, 2018) running a Rolling Development Plan that deliberately identifies gaps and avoids SDO overlaps.** Rail's comparable position, from rows already on file: **TS 50701 explicitly NOT mandatory** (ERA, E-2026-08-15-06), successor **IEC 63452 not stable before ~2028** (E-2026-08-01-17/-20), "**limited cybersecurity requirement in the coming CCS TSI**" (E-2026-08-15-10), and **no sectoral cybersecurity assessment ever conducted** (E-2026-08-15-08). So the CRA carve-out is not arbitrary: **aviation is excluded because it IS sufficiently regulated; rail is included because it is NOT.** That is a defensible, primary-grounded framing for the client, and it recasts the CRA from an external burden into the instrument filling a sector-specific regulatory vacuum. **THE EASA PRECEDENT IS THE MOST TRANSFERABLE ITEM, AND BEARS ON ADR-004/ADR-012.** Aviation resolved institutionally the exact coupling rail is still debating: it amended the **safety** regulation to cover **cyber risks for safety**, rather than leaving cyber to a parallel regime — and its AMC/GM impose ISMS, risk assessment, risk treatment, incident management, personnel requirements and continuous improvement on **both Competent Authorities and Organisations**. Compare rail: ERA proposed only an **informative** TS 50701 reference into the SMS (E-2026-08-15-04), EPSF asked how ERA and NSAs should deal with SMS-integrated cyber (E-2026-08-15-10), and Traficom recorded "ERA's SMS ~ no cyber (?)" (E-2026-08-15-12). **Aviation's answer to the safety-security seam is a named, adjacent-sector precedent worth putting in front of the client — and it is the same seam ADR-012's "one gate for change" principle and ADR-004's boundary are trying to hold.** Note the honest limit: an aviation *organisational* ISMS regulation is not the same problem as ADR-004's vital/non-vital partitioning of a learning component, so cite Part-IS as an institutional-design precedent, not a technical one. **(b) IS INDEPENDENT CONVERGENCE, NOT NEW FACT — and should be cited only that way.** Every item in the presenter's rail diagnosis is already on file from rail-domain primaries: non-harmonised standards · OT supply-chain maturity (ENISA's "for the OT (safety related) ones, there is a gap", E-2026-08-15-04) · legacy systems (ENISA: "not possible to provide relevant recommendations", E-2026-08-15-07) · distributed infrastructure · awareness/culture and "Safety vs. Security". Its value is that an agency from **another sector**, reading rail from outside, independently reaches the same diagnosis — corroboration of framing, not of fact. Because it is self-described secondary research, **do not cite it as a source for any rail claim**; cite the rail primaries and note the external concurrence. **(c) ADDS A FOURTH INDEPENDENT VOICE ON CAPACITY** — "support organisations to have appropriate **resources** in place" and "entities… have **different needs, capabilities and means**". With ENISA (expertise/resources, worse for OT), EPSF (22 of 106 trained) and Traficom (**~1 person** on railway cybersecurity), the resourcing constraint is now attested by four institutions across three sectors, and the "different capabilities" point matches the FI/FR concern that **small RUs and regional operators** are the weak tier — relevant to R13 (fleet/sector-wide feasibility) as much as to R14. **(c) ALSO NAMES THE CROSS-MODAL CRISIS CASE** — coordination with other transport stakeholders "in case of a large-scale cyber crisis" — kin to the EU Cyber Blueprint's rail-sectoral-mechanism gap (E-2026-07-29-02) and to ENISA's planned cross-modal threat landscape (E-2026-08-15-06(i) → E-2026-07-20-01). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed), R11/ADR-004 Proposed, R8 Recommended, R12 Proposed, R1/R2 Accepted. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **EASA Part-IS** as adopted (Delegated + Implementing Regulations) — the concrete text of the adjacent-sector precedent, and the best available model for integrating cyber into a safety regulator's remit; (2) **ECSCG's Rolling Development Plan** — a working example of SDO gap/overlap management that the rail landscape (ERA/CENELEC/IEC/UIC/UNISIG/GSMA/3GPP, per sdo-mapping) conspicuously lacks; (3) confirm the CRA aeronautical exclusion and rail non-exclusion against adopted Reg (EU) 2024/2847 — already an open follow-up from E-2026-08-15-09, now with a stronger reason to close it. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. KIN: **E-2026-08-15-09 (the CRA exclusion asymmetry this explains)**, E-2026-08-15-06 (TS 50701 non-mandatory — the rail contrast), E-2026-08-01-17/-20 (IEC 63452 timeline), E-2026-08-15-10 (limited cyber in the CCS TSI; FR), E-2026-08-15-12 (FI capacity), E-2026-08-15-08 (no rail sectoral assessment), E-2026-08-15-07 (ENISA legacy/OT findings), E-2026-07-29-02 (EU Cyber Blueprint — cross-modal crisis), sdo-mapping (the rail SDO landscape ECSCG's RDP would model), ADR-004/ADR-012, R14/R11/R8/R12/R13 |
+| E-2026-08-15-15 | 2026-08-15 | ⚠️ **TLP:AMBER — SEE HANDLING CONSTRAINT BELOW.** **SNCF Voyageurs, Rolling Stock Division / Engineering Department** — "**Sharing about cybersecurity on rolling stock projects**", **Quentin RIVETTE, Industrial Information Security Officer** (quentin.rivette@sncf.fr); 7 slides, internal doc name "…-RIVETTE-v1.00"; presented at the **ERA-ENISA Conference on Cybersecurity in Railways, 01 December 2022** (Lille; filename "02GoodPractises-3"). **The FIRST ROLLING-STOCK / VEHICLE-SIDE cybersecurity artefact in the register** — every prior row is network, infrastructure or governance. NB the slides are largely diagrammatic with sparse labelling, so what follows is headings and stated principles, not detail. Paraphrased: **(a) CYBERSECURITY AS AN INTEGRATED TOPIC** — "integrated safety and security", described as a pillar around "notably operational safety, safety of persons, or physical security / protection"; spans **IT & OT**; applied to both **new fleets** and **modification projects**. **(b) BASIS** — cybersecurity integrated into projects via an **internal process and/or public methodologies**, yielding **security requirements**. **(c) MAJOR PRINCIPLES & POINTS OF ATTENTION** — **Zones & Conduits** as the organising principle, with two named points of attention: **WIRELESS** and **MAINTENANCE ACCESS**. **(d) THE STATED CHALLENGE — "MAINTAIN IN SECURE CONDITIONS"**, decomposed as: **asset inventory & cartography** + **strategy** (**CyberCriticalAsset**, "objectives & rhythm", rules); supported by **"modular architecture ⇒ easier to patch"** and **"regularly pentest"**. **(e) OTHER STRATEGIC ORIENTATIONS** — "**link the security process with the AUTHORIZATION process**"; **TS 50701** (flagged "NEW"); and "**internalization of penetration testing competencies**". READ: complete extractable text (7 slides; predominantly graphics — text extraction yields titles and labels only) | Operator conference presentation (primary — a railway undertaking's rolling-stock engineering function describing its own practice, named officer) | file "02GoodPractises-3 - SNCF - Sharing about cybersecurity on rolling stock projects.pdf", Download folder; md5 ac84b0d1…39bf; ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 01.12.2022 | A (primary first-hand OPERATOR account of its own engineering practice — same basis as E-2026-08-15-13 (NS) and the DB Netz operator-account precedent E-2026-07-02-05/-14; FOUR FLAGS: **⚠️ TLP:AMBER**, see handling constraint; **self-reported** — stated practice, no external verification that it is implemented as described; **very sparse deck** — mostly diagrams, so the substance available is principles rather than method; **2022 vintage** — TS 50701 was "NEW" then and is now superseded by EN 50701:2023) | **R13/ADR-009 rolling stock** (+ R14/ADR-012 authorisation linkage; R11/ADR-004 patchability & separability; R2 modification projects; ADR-007 pentest; R6 modular architecture) | validate | **⚠️ HANDLING CONSTRAINT — READ BEFORE ANY REUSE. This source is marked TLP:AMBER** ("limited disclosure — recipients may share only on a need-to-know basis within their own organisation and its clients"), unlike the TLP:CLEAR Traficom deck (E-2026-08-15-12). Logging it here is within scope because **this repository is PRIVATE** (verified) and the register is an internal working record. **It must NOT flow into any public-facing output** — the LinkedIn drafts (`current/linkedin-post-*.md`), published papers, or any client deliverable intended for onward distribution — **without confirming the marking with SNCF or the conference organisers first.** Attribute-free, effect-level framing only if referenced externally; do not name SNCF practice publicly off this document. **A by source — and valuable as the register's FIRST ROLLING-STOCK cyber artefact.** R13 (fleet-scale FRMCS retrofit, 16-21k DE vehicles, external dependency · ADR-009) has to date been evidenced almost entirely on cost, approval burden, funding and chipset supply; this is the first evidence of what **cybersecurity engineering on the vehicle side** actually consists of at a major operator — and rolling stock is precisely where the FRMCS onboard segment (TOBA / OB_GTW, R6) will land. **THREE CONVERGENCES WORTH RECORDING.** **(1) "Link the security process with the AUTHORIZATION process" (e) is ADR-012's core principle, stated by an operator.** ADR-012 holds that a security change to live safety-carrying equipment is a safety-relevant change that must pass one gate; **EBA claims exactly that gate** on the regulator side ("authorisation of IT-based railway systems and components", E-2026-08-15-11); **EPSF/ANSSI's position-paper R5** framed the same trade ("reasoned holding in safety condition while minimizing the impact on safety demonstrations", E-2026-08-15-10); and here the **duty-holder** names linking security to authorisation as a strategic orientation. Regulator, national position paper and operator now converge on the single-gate design from three directions — the strongest support ADR-012's central principle has. **(2) "Modular architecture ⇒ easier to patch" (d) is the ARCHITECTURAL answer to the CRA-update-versus-authorisation tension**, and it matches a mandated spec precedent already on file: **UNISIG SUBSET-146 §3.1.1.6** deliberately separates the security layer from the safety layer so the security layer can be updated "without the need to re-approve any safety rating of the application" (E-2026-08-01-15), which ADR-004 already cites as the layering precedent for its own separability argument. Spec-level separation and operator-level modularity are the same move: **design so that the patchable part is not the certified part.** That is a reusable design principle for the FRMCS onboard architecture and for the ADR-004 boundary. **(3) Zones & Conduits on the vehicle** extends the zones/conduits chain the register already tracks end-to-end — IEC 62443 → TS 50701 → GSMA FS.61 (core interior) → **SUBSET-147** (on-board consist network, FRMCS zone separated from CCS zone, E-2026-08-01-14) — with confirmation that a major operator organises rolling-stock security the same way in practice. **THE TWO NAMED POINTS OF ATTENTION ARE DIRECTLY FRMCS-RELEVANT (c): WIRELESS and MAINTENANCE ACCESS.** These are the operator's own priority attack surfaces on rolling stock, and both map onto live threads: wireless = the bearer itself (SIM-SEC-001 radio-layer availability, SIM-SEC-003 GSM-R SIM/modem OTA hijack) and the FRMCS onboard radio; maintenance access = the supply-chain/remote-access vector that EBA/BSI raised as an open question ("can a ransomware attack at a rolling stock manufacturer be safety-relevant?", E-2026-08-15-11) and that the register has otherwise only in enterprise-IT form. **CAPACITY COUNTERPOINT (e):** "internalization of penetration testing competencies" is an operator responding to the sector-wide expertise shortage (ENISA · EPSF 22/106 · Traficom ~1 · EDA "appropriate resources") by **building capability in-house rather than buying it** — the first constructive answer on file to a constraint four institutions have now diagnosed. Worth surfacing to the client as an option alongside the ADR-007 test programme, of which "regularly pentest" and modular-architecture patch testing are natural components. **(d) ASSET INVENTORY & CARTOGRAPHY + CyberCriticalAsset** is operator practice matching **ADR-012 action item 1** (CRA PDE inventory, class by category) — a precedent for how a duty-holder actually scopes it, and a useful counter to NS's lesson that "scoping is difficult" (E-2026-08-15-13). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R13 stays **Open — external dependency · ADR-009**; R14 Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R11/ADR-004 Proposed; R2/R6 Accepted; ADR-007 unchanged. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UP:** SNCF's public methodologies reference (b) is unnamed — worth asking which public methodology the rolling-stock process builds on (TS 50701? IEC 62443-3-2 risk assessment?); and note that the still-unfetched **Masteris** article (blocked, awaiting network policy) is from **SNCF Voyageurs' own rolling-stock maintenance/engineering subsidiary** — the same corporate family and domain as this deck, so the two should be read together when it lands. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md (with the TLP:AMBER marking carried). KIN: **E-2026-08-15-13 (NS — the other operator good-practice account; "scoping is difficult" vs this deck's cartography answer)**, **E-2026-08-01-15 (SUBSET-146 §3.1.1.6 — the mandated separability precedent (2) matches)**, E-2026-08-01-14 (SUBSET-147 on-board zones), E-2026-08-15-11 (EBA authorisation gate + the rolling-stock-manufacturer ransomware question), E-2026-08-15-10 (EPSF/ANSSI R5), E-2026-07-26-15 (TS 50701), ADR-012 (core principle; action 1), ADR-011 (the change gate), ADR-004 (separability), ADR-007 (pentest/test programme), ADR-009/R13, R6/R2 |
+| E-2026-08-15-16 | 2026-08-15 | **ENISA — "NIS INVESTMENTS IN THE TRANSPORT SECTOR", Dr. Athanasios Drougkas (Cybersecurity Expert)**; 12 slides; **2nd ERA-ENISA Conference, 01.12.2022** (Lille). The transport-sector cut of the **2022 NIS Investments report** — ENISA's **3rd annual** edition, **>1000 OES/DSP across 27 EU MS** (2020: 251 OES/DSP, 5 MS · 2021: 947, 27 MS · **2022: 1080, 27 MS**), now providing historical datasets; sectorial deep dives (Energy & Health); "focus on operational aspects and OES capabilities"; and — provenance worth noting — the **survey questionnaire was aligned to collect data for the CRA Impact Assessment**. **RAIL SECTOR OES in the sample: 59.** **This closes the forecast logged at E-2026-08-15-06**, where ENISA answered a question about the cost of rail cybersecurity with "a study quantifying the costs… is not available. ENISA is currently conducting a study on overall cybersecurity investments in the EU." **METHOD NOTE: chart values below were read from page images rendered at 130 dpi**, because text extraction returned axis labels only. Paraphrased data: **(a) IS BUDGET AS % OF IT BUDGET (2021, n=1070)** — all sectors **average 7.2% / median 6.7%**, and **BOTH DECLINING**: average 8.8%→7.2% (**-1.6 ppt**), median 7.7%→6.7% (**-1.0 ppt**) year on year. By sector (avg/median): Digital Infrastructures 9.6/10.0 · Online Marketplace 8.9/8.8 · Cloud computing 8.9/8.5 · Financial market infrastructures 8.6/8.3 · Online Search Engine 8.2/8.0 · Banking 7.7/7.5 · Healthcare 6.9/6.7 · Drinking water 6.4/5.4 · **TRANSPORT 5.5/5.0** · Energy 5.3/5.0. **(b) IS FTEs (n=1078)** — all sectors IS-vs-IT FTE share average **15.3%** / median **12.0%**, also declining (17.0%→15.3%, -1.7 ppt; 13.0%→12.0%, -1.0 ppt). Absolute IS FTEs by sector (avg/median): Banking 47/15 · Healthcare 27/10 · **TRANSPORT 23/7** · Energy 18/5 · Online Marketplace 16/3 · Drinking water 11/3 · Cloud computing 10/2 · Digital infrastructures 7/1 · Online Search Engine 6/1 · Financial market infrastructures 4/2. **(c) CTI SPENDING, thousands of € (n=1065)** — Banking 945/300 · Online Marketplace 451/35 · Energy 446/70 · Healthcare 350/100 · **Transport 295/47** · Digital Infrastructures 250/8 · Cloud computing 238/15 · Drinking water 148/20 · Financial market infra 125/20 · Online Search Engine 50/5 — and, called out separately, **RAIL: 514 average / 80 median (€ thousands)**. All-sector median CTI spend €50 000; "large operators invest significantly more on CTI compared to smaller ones… internal SOC capabilities seem to be very closely correlated with CTI spending → **need to facilitate access to CTI for smaller operators**". **(d) FACTORS DRIVING IS BUDGETS** (stacked responses; Transport total 145) — **NIS Directive 43** · Threat landscape 30 · Geopolitical situation 28 · Regulatory environment excluding NIS 26 · plus smaller counts for suffered-a-breach, competitive landscape and COVID-19. **(e) SOC PROVISION — Transport** (n shown as counts/percentages): fully **internal/insourced SOC 44 (30%)** · **hybrid SOC 46 (32%)** · **fully outsourced 13 (9%)** · **"no SOC but we have some detection and incident response capabilities" 41 (28%)** · **"no detection and incident response capabilities at all" 1 (1%)**. All-sector headline: **37% of OES/DSP in the EU do not operate a dedicated SOC**. **(f) MEDIAN CTI SPEND BY SOC MODEL** — fully internal/insourced SOC **€350 000** · hybrid **€97 500** (annotated **-72%** vs internal) · fully outsourced **€15 000** · no SOC but some detection/IR **€11 000** · no detection/IR capability **€0**. **(g) SKILLS PRIORITISED FOR INTERNAL DEVELOPMENT** — Incident Response **73%** · Threat Intelligence **54%** · Risk Management **53%** · Service Management 38% · Technology Management 35% · **Manual Penetration Testing 30%** · Tier ½ SOC Analysis 26% · Data Science and Analysis 22% · **Threat Hunting 13%** · **Digital Forensics 4%**. **(h) COST OF A MAJOR INCIDENT, thousands of €** — Banking 475/300 · Energy 462/270 · **TRANSPORT 450/225** · Healthcare 423/300 · Drinking water 270/130 · Online Marketplace 150/100 · Digital infrastructures 143/90 · Cloud computing 104/65; all-sector median **€200 000, twice last year's**. Cost composition: **incident response incl. vulnerability fix 33%** · data recovery & business-continuity management 22% · loss of productivity 19% · loss of revenue 9% · legal costs/fines/lawsuits 7% · loss of property 6% · crisis communication 4%. **(i) THIRD-PARTY RISK MANAGEMENT** — **86% have implemented TRM, but only 47% have a dedicated TRM budget and only 24% a dedicated TRM role**; a chart compares All sectors / Transport / Rail on policies, budget and dedicated role (values not read). **(j) CYBER INSURANCE** — 30% of OES/DSP held cyber insurance in 2021, **down 13% on 2020**, with **only 5% of SMEs** subscribing; a rail pie shows 49%/51% (direction not verified — not read). READ: full text + pages 5, 6, 7, 8, 11 read as rendered images; pages 9-10 charts (TRM detail, insurance direction) NOT read | Agency conference presentation of published survey data (primary — ENISA expert presenting ENISA's own annual NIS Investments dataset) | file "02GoodPractises-4 - ENISA - NIS Investments in Transport sector.pdf", Download folder; md5 c54a9125…e309; 2nd ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 01.12.2022 | A (primary agency survey data, n>1000 across 27 MS, presented by its own author; FIVE FLAGS: **a deck OF a fuller report** — the full 2022 NIS Investments report is not on file; **data year 2021**, published 2022; **SELF-REPORTED survey data** — operators self-declare budgets, FTEs and incident costs, so definitional variance and self-report bias apply throughout; **rail subsample n=59** — rail-specific figures carry materially wider uncertainty than the all-sector ones and should be quoted as indicative; **chart values read from rendered images**, not from machine-extracted text) | **R14/ADR-012** (+ **R12 SOC/detection capability**; R8/PR13 third-party risk; R13 investment feasibility; R3/R4 incident cost) | validate | **A — and the register's FIRST HARD QUANTITATIVE DATA on rail/transport cybersecurity investment. It converts a qualitative thread into numbers.** **THE CAPACITY FINDING IS NOW MEASURED.** Four institutions had diagnosed a resourcing shortage qualitatively — ENISA ("lack of expertise and resources… even more pronounced for OT", E-2026-08-15-06), EPSF (22 of 106 staff trained, E-2026-08-15-10), Traficom (**~1 person** on railway cybersecurity, E-2026-08-15-12) and EDA ("support organisations to have appropriate resources", E-2026-08-15-14). Here is the measurement: **transport spends the SECOND-LOWEST share of IT budget on information security of any NIS sector — 5.5% average / 5.0% median — ahead of only Energy**, against Digital Infrastructures at nearly double (9.6/10.0); **the median transport OES employs 7 information-security FTEs**; and **both the budget share and the FTE share FELL year on year across all sectors**. The constraint the sector describes is real, sector-specific and *worsening* on this dataset. **THE DETECTION GAP IS ALSO NOW MEASURED — DIRECTLY FOR R12.** (e) **28% of transport OES have no SOC at all** (only "some detection and incident response capabilities"), and **1% report no detection or incident-response capability whatsoever**; (g) the skills **least** prioritised for internal development, of ten, are exactly the detection-depth ones — **Threat Hunting 13% and Digital Forensics 4%**, bottom two. Read against ENISA's 2020 maturity finding that **detection and log correlation were the low-maturity areas** (E-2026-08-15-03) and the 2021 attribution of that to expertise and resources (E-2026-08-15-06), this is the same gap seen from the **investment** side: not merely low current maturity, but **low investment intent in the deep detection skills**. That is the strongest quantitative support R12 has, and it sharpens the argument — an oversight layer cannot assume a mature detection substrate beneath it, because the sector is not building one. **(d) IS THE MOST CLIENT-USEFUL FINDING: REGULATION MOVES BUDGETS MORE THAN THREAT DOES.** For transport, the **NIS Directive is the single largest driver of information-security budget (43 responses)** — ahead of threat landscape (30) and the geopolitical situation (28). This quantifies NS's operator lesson "**implement legislation to boost existing initiatives**" (E-2026-08-15-13) and materially reframes ADR-012: the CRA/NIS-2 workload is not only a compliance burden, it is **the mechanism by which security budget actually gets released**. Worth putting to the client in exactly those terms. **(c) IS A RAIL-POSITIVE DATUM, honestly caveated:** **rail CTI spend (€514k avg / €80k median) runs well above transport as a whole (€295k/€47k)** — rail is investing in threat intelligence above its modal peers. With n=59 this is indicative, not firm, and it sits oddly beside transport's low overall IS budget share; the plausible reading, consistent with (f), is that rail's larger operators run internal SOCs (internal-SOC median CTI spend is **€350k** versus **€97.5k** hybrid and **€11k** for no-SOC) and pull the rail average up, while smaller RUs — the tier FI and FR both flagged as weakest (E-2026-08-15-12, -10) — sit at the bottom. ENISA's own recommendation, "**facilitate access to CTI for smaller operators**", points the same way. **(h) GIVES ADR-007 AND THE R3/R4 BUSINESS CASE A NUMBER** — a major incident costs transport **€450k average / €225k median**, third-highest of all sectors, with **33% of that being incident response including the vulnerability fix** and 22% data recovery/business continuity. **CARRY THE LIMIT:** these are *cyber* incident costs, and the 23-June outage was **not** cyber (E-2026-06-27-01/-03) — so this is an order-of-magnitude analogue for the cost of a major availability event, not a comparator for that incident, and must never be presented as one. **(i) THIRD-PARTY RISK IS POLICY WITHOUT RESOURCING** — 86% have TRM implemented, but only 47% fund it and only 24% staff it. That is precisely the supply-chain exposure PR13/R8 track and that the CRA's SBOM/support-period regime (ADR-012 actions 1-2) is meant to convert from paper into practice; it also matches EBA/BSI's open question about a ransomware attack at a rolling-stock manufacturer (E-2026-08-15-11). **PROVENANCE BONUS:** the questionnaire was aligned to feed the **CRA Impact Assessment**, so this dataset sits underneath ADR-012's own legal basis — useful when justifying why the CRA took the shape it did. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R12 Proposed — this strengthens its justification substantially but decides nothing; R8 Recommended; R13 Open; R3/R4 unchanged. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) the **full 2022 NIS Investments report** (and its later editions — this is an annual series, so 2023/2024/2025 editions should exist and would show whether the declining trend continued); (2) pages 9-10 of this deck (rail TRM breakdown; rail cyber-insurance direction) remain unread; (3) whether any edition breaks **rail** out on budget share and FTEs as it does here for CTI — that would give the single most useful number for this engagement. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. Paraphrased: **(a) THE FRAMING — "The end-user does all the work."** Three situations contrasted: (i) "we check and assess our equipments and systems for vulnerabilities · we list our compliancy gaps · **we go back to our suppliers for solutions**"; (ii) "our suppliers proactively come with new state-of-the-art products that solve our problems **(that they have brought into our systems)**"; (iii) "the market proposes solutions & new trends · we check the market to get what we need · we make POC, pilots & deployments". **(b) ECO-SYSTEM** — CSSP positioned among **UIC, ER-ISAC, ERA, ENISA** and "The Market", under the banner "**Sharing for Efficiency**". **(c) INPUTS & MAIN ACTIVITIES** — a **CSSP Threat Landscape Heatmap** structured as **ASSETS × ACTORS**; and **supplier slots** (solution presentations, product discussions, technical use cases). **(d) CATALOGUE CHAPTERS** — CSSP **Threat Landscape Report** (inputs, conclusions, recommendations, and its own process: "Build, Validate, Maintain & Communicate"); **workshops' inputs** (expert presentations, project presentations, **assessment results**); **guidelines** (members' best practices, **members' example of architecture**, "new railway standard"); and **solutions** (products used · products **being assessed** · future solutions). **(e) NAVIGATION** — entry by use cases, vulnerabilities and heatmap, leading to solutions, best practices and recommendations. **(f) NEXT ACTIVITIES (as at 12/2022)** — 1 deliver the Threat Landscape Report for 2022 · 2 complete the catalogue for the products gathered · 3 complete the Best Practices chapter · 4 improve the catalogue from user comments · **5 "Architecture & project Assessments (FRMCS)"** · 6 build a platform of expertise and develop processes; captioned "a pragmatic approach". **(g) ROADMAP** — **2020** start of the project · **2022** first version of the CSSP catalogue · **2023 "FRMCS security assessment; build our processes further"** · **2024** new version of the CSSP catalogue. E-2026-07-30-04, E-2026-08-01-24, E-2026-08-01-33; FOUR FLAGS: **⚠️ TLP:GREEN** — community-limited distribution, see handling note; **slide-deck form**, partly graphical; **describes an initiative, not its results** — the catalogue, the Threat Landscape Report and the FRMCS assessment are all OFF-FILE and unverified; **2022 vintage** with a roadmap whose 2023/2024 milestones are now two to four years past with unknown outcomes) | R14/ADR-012 (+ R8/PR13 supplier burden & SBOM; R12 threat landscape/heatmap & SOC practice; **R5/R7 via the planned FRMCS assessment**) | watch | **⚠️ HANDLING: TLP:GREEN** — limited disclosure, shareable within the community but **not for public release**. Less restrictive than the TLP:AMBER SNCF deck (E-2026-08-15-15), more restrictive than TLP:CLEAR (Traficom, E-2026-08-15-12). Logged here because this repository is **PRIVATE** (verified). Do not publish CSSP specifics — particularly the platform's internal structure and the supplier framing — in the LinkedIn drafts or client-facing deliverables without checking with UIC. **A by source, WATCH by substance: this describes a platform, not findings.** Its value is that it converts a name into a described initiative and surfaces one high-value lead. Two prior rows referenced the "UIC Cyber Security Solutions Platform" as a name only — ERA's 2020 ecosystem slide (E-2026-08-15-02) and the 1st conference agenda, where Houbion presented it (E-2026-08-15-05). This gives it structure, membership position, method and a roadmap. **THE LEAD THAT MATTERS — (f)5 AND (g): UIC PLANNED AN "FRMCS SECURITY ASSESSMENT" FOR 2023.** That is the single most directly relevant item in this conference batch for this engagement, and it is **entirely unverified**: whether it was carried out, what scope it took, and what it concluded are all unknown from this deck. If it exists, a UIC-level architecture and project security assessment of FRMCS is **first-order evidence for R14 and for the FRMCS security posture generally** — and it would bear on the gap this register has documented from the other side, where ERA delegated radio-device security into "UIC EIRENE or FRMCS specifications" (E-2026-08-15-04) and the delivered FRMCS SRS §15 then placed monitoring, incident response and risk assessment **out of scope** with §17.2 empty (E-2026-07-12-02). **A UIC FRMCS security assessment is precisely the artefact that would show whether that gap was subsequently addressed at the sector level. Pursuing it is the top follow-up from this batch** — via cssp.uic.org or the named UIC contacts, noting that CSSP appears to be a members' platform, so access may be gated. **(a) IS A POINTED OPERATOR-SIDE STATEMENT ON SUPPLIER BURDEN**, and it belongs with the R8/PR13 thread: the end-user assesses, finds the gaps, and returns to suppliers for solutions — while suppliers arrive with products solving "our problems (**that they have brought into our systems**)". That is the sector articulating, in 2022, the exact market failure the **CRA** was drafted to correct by putting secure-by-design, SBOM and vulnerability-handling duties on manufacturers (ADR-012 actions 1-2; PR13 proprietary opacity / vendor-fix dependency). Useful as the sector's own pre-CRA problem statement, alongside ENISA's 2021 finding that supply-chain elements "do not fall under the same European regulatory framework" (E-2026-08-15-07). **(c)/(d) ARE METHODOLOGICALLY KIN TO WORK ALREADY SCOPED HERE:** an **ASSETS × ACTORS threat heatmap** is structurally what evidence-log-security.md's vector/adversary matrix does (ATT&CK-ICS + FiGHT + MoTIF vectors against Sandworm/LightBasin/APT28 profiles); **SBOM and SOC implementation as named best practices** map onto ADR-012 actions 1-2 and 5; and a **"risk assurance process"** alongside risk analysis is the shape of the assurance argument ADR-004 needs. If the catalogue is obtainable, its guidelines chapter ("members' example of architecture") could be a peer-architecture reference the engagement currently lacks. **(b) PLACES CSSP IN THE §4a OWNERSHIP PICTURE** — it sits deliberately between UIC members, ER-ISAC, ERA, ENISA and the market, i.e. it is a **solution-sharing and assessment** function, distinct from ER-ISAC's threat-*sharing* role (E-2026-08-02-01) and from the absent operational-hardening owner. It narrows the §4a gap a little on the *solutions/assurance* axis without touching the operational-interconnect axis. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed), R8 Recommended, R12 Proposed, R5/R7 Accepted — nothing here is a result. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **the 2023 UIC FRMCS security assessment** — existence, scope, conclusions; (2) the **CSSP catalogue** and **CSSP Threat Landscape Report(s)** (2022 and later) via cssp.uic.org or Houbion/Mandoc; (3) confirm whether the 2024 catalogue version shipped, i.e. whether CSSP is still active. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md (TLP:GREEN carried). They are unrelated. Note pointedly that this deck's legislation slide (e) covers NIS-2, CRA and the CSA but **does not mention the CER Directive at all** — so it neither closes nor speaks to that gap, and the follow-up to pull Dir (EU) 2022/2557 stands unchanged. **A by source — and its value is concentrated in (d), (e) and (f), because (a) and (b) are restated ENISA material.** **(e) IS THE MOST USEFUL CONTENT AND IS NEW TO THIS REGISTER: the NIS-2 essential/important SPLIT applied to rail actors.** ADR-012 records that DB InfraGO is an **essential** entity in Transport/Annex I. This adds the other half: **manufacturers of rolling stock, signalling, safety and traffic-control equipment, computers and electronics are IMPORTANT entities** — i.e. **the FRMCS supply chain is itself inside the NIS-2 perimeter**, at the lighter-supervision tier. If that holds, then Nokia, Kontron, Siemens and the other FRMCS suppliers carry their own NIS-2 duties alongside the CRA manufacturer duties ADR-012 already places on them, which materially strengthens the procurement-clause argument (ADR-012 action 2) and the PR13 vendor-opacity mitigation: the operator is not the only regulated party in the chain. **VERIFY before relying on it** — this is a sector association's summary slide, not the legal text; confirm the essential/important allocation against **Annexes I and II of Dir (EU) 2022/2555**, where "manufacturing of computer, electronic and optical products" and similar categories sit in Annex II. **(b) PLACES TELECOM AT THE BASE OF THE DEPENDENCY STACK** — radio and wired/wireless transmission underneath signalling/command-control (ETCS, ATC, ATP, ATS, KMS) and operations planning. Even though the slide is derivative, that layering is the sector's own picture of what depends on what, and it is exactly the structure the 23-June outage demonstrated in practice: the bearer failed and everything above it stopped (R3/R4). It is also the clearest available statement of where FRMCS sits — the foundation layer, whose loss propagates upward — which is the architectural premise of R3/R4/R5/R6 and of ADR-001's bearer-independence design. **(d) IS A USEFUL OWNERSHIP MAP FOR THE §4a THREAD AND FOR sdo-mapping**: standardisation/regulatory (CER, EIM, UNIFE → working parties → ERA → TSI; ERJU System Pillar; EUG; OCORA/RCA/TOBA) is cleanly separated from policy (EC → ENISA) and from information sharing (UIC, ER-ISAC, CSSP). Read with the §4a finding, it shows the sector has **well-populated standardisation and sharing functions and no operational-security function** — the map itself has no box for who runs security operations, which is the gap stated by omission. Worth cross-checking against `current/project/sdo-mapping-frmcs-gsmr-5gsa.md`, since it names bodies (EIM, EUG) that the register may not yet track. **(f) THE ASSOCIATION'S OWN OPEN QUESTIONS TRACK THE ENGAGEMENT'S:** "relation to existing railway legislation" and "relation to standardisation, **including TSIs**" is the CRA/NIS-2-versus-CCS-TSI/CSM interface ADR-012 must resolve; and "**classification of products and components**" is precisely ADR-012 action item 1 (CRA PDE inventory, class by product category) and the DG CNECT class criteria recorded at E-2026-08-15-09 — the sector association naming it unresolved in 12/2022 is useful evidence that this is a genuine open industry problem, not an artefact of the engagement's own scoping. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed), R8 Recommended, R3/R4 unchanged, R5/R6 Accepted, R12 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **verify the NIS-2 essential/important allocation for rail manufacturers against Annexes I/II of Dir (EU) 2022/2555** — potentially significant for ADR-012 action 2 and PR13; (2) slide 5 "Risks in the Ecosystem" is unread — re-render if the risk taxonomy is wanted; (3) CER's own cyber outputs (www.cer.be) are not on file — a sector-association position on rail cyber would complement the operator (NS, SNCF) and authority (EPSF/EBA/Traficom) views already held; (4) **the CER *Directive* follow-up from E-2026-08-15-13 remains open and is unaffected by this row.** SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. PDF author metadata names **max.schubert@deutschebahn.com** plus **richard.poschinger@incyde.com** and **roger.metz@incyde.com** (INCYDE, consultancy) — i.e. **DB-led with consultancy support**; the PDF Title field carries an unrelated leftover ("Abstimmung zur Zulassung und Inbetriebnahme von bbIP"), a template artefact. Group webpage given: **https://ertms.be/workgroups/cyber_security**. Paraphrased: **(a) THE HEADLINE SLIDE — "TSI Subsets contain basic Security thoughts – However, Operators face the need to implement state-of-the-art Security", with four stated challenges:** 1. "**Current subsets focus on cross border interoperability (RBC-Euroradio) ONLY, while Security requires a HOLISTIC approach**"; 2. "**Commonly agreed cyber security standards require a HIGHER level of security than requested by current TSI subsets**"; 3. "**Current installations must be seen as 'LEGACY SYSTEMS' with the need for COMPENSATING MEASURES** – some operators now are defining cyber security measures, **which lead to SPECIFIC SOLUTIONS PER OPERATOR**"; 4. "future installations should be based on **commonly agreed** cyber security requirements specifications". **(b) ESCG'S FIVE GUIDANCE GOALS** — best-practice ERTMS implementation based on TSI 2016(2019) for common understanding and security level · **future ERTMS requirements to support the next TSI (recast)** and guide "standard" implementation for suppliers and infrastructure managers · **provision of a norm- and regulation-compliant risk analysis** · provision of security-measure definition documentation for efficient and unified implementation · **synchronisation with ERJU System Pillar, CER, EIM, ENISA, EULYNX, OCORA** and further European bodies. **(c) THE METHOD — "the Management is compliant to IEC 62443 and TS 50701"**, built on IEC 62443 · TS 50701 · **EN 50159** · ERTMS scope, with inputs from the **EULYNX/RCA Security Cluster**, the **OCORA Security Working Group** and **Shift2Rail X2Rail-3**, and governed by a **Project Security Management Plan / Security Guideline**. Phase flow: *ERTMS System under Consideration* → **Phase 2** analysis of protection requirements per partial system ("initial risk assessment") → **Phase 3 zoning and conduits** + **cybersecurity threat and risk analysis (TS 50701, IEC 62443-3-2)** → **Phase 4 System Measures (IEC 62443-3-3, -2-1)** → **Phase 5 Component Measures (IEC 62443-4-2, -4-1)**; plus "adoption of architecture/design". **(d) THE ZONE-AND-CONDUIT MODEL, subject to analysis — the concrete ERTMS asset set: ETCS-OB · Euroloop · Eurobalise · RIU · RBC · PKI · KMC.** **(e) RISK ANALYSIS BY APPLICATION PHASE** — separate threat-and-risk analyses for **existing installations (TSI CCS 2016/2019)** · **near-future installations (TSI CCS 2016/2019)** · **future installations (TSI CCS 2022 and up)**, producing measure-definition and application guidelines per system/component (RBC, interlocking, …), "respect of application status", a **base for migration plan**, and a **proposal for future TSI Subsets**. **(f) EUROPEAN COLLABORATION already started** — representative-body process synchronisation · **EUG exchange on KMS, KMC, PKI set-up** · synchronisation with **EULYNX/RCA and OCORA on security process and Shared Security Services** · **Shift2Rail X2Rail-3 on Shared Security Services**; and two scheduling statements: "**System Pillar Security Group will make use of the already performed analysis and definitions**" and "**the ESCG will finish its current task until 03/2023**". **(g) CONCLUSIONS** — *achieved*: common understanding of status and need for security; practical guidance on improving ERTMS security **for existing installations**; a practical method for future ERTMS/CCS security; an active team with on-board and trackside ERTMS security knowledge. *To be addressed*: **development of an ERTMS security MIGRATION ROADMAP** · **include the guideline in the TSI CCS application guide** · **transfer guideline requirements into TSI CCS subsets** · **possible expansion to ATO-OB and ATO-TS** · ESCG to serve as "extended work bench and mirror group" reviewing and delivering content to the System Pillar Security group. DB-authored with consultancy support) | file "04CybSecChallenges-2 - ERTMS UG - Cybersecurity guidelines in support of ERTMS.pdf", Download folder; md5 b0cadffe…b58e; 2nd ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 01.12.2022 | A (primary — EUG is the infrastructure managers' ERTMS body and ESCG is its own security working group, presenting its own analysis and method; **FOUR FLAGS**: **slide-deck form**, with the two architecture/zone diagrams unread beyond their labels; **2022 vintage** — ESCG's task was to close 03/2023, "TSI CCS 2022" became **Reg (EU) 2023/1695**, and the ERJU System Pillar has since progressed; **the DELIVERABLES ARE NOT ON FILE** — this describes guidelines, risk analyses and measure definitions without containing them; **consultancy co-authorship** (INCYDE) — a methods presentation, not marketing, so no promotional discount, but noted) | **R7 interoperability** (+ **R14/ADR-012**; **R2/R1 legacy & migration**; R11/ADR-004 via EN 50159 / 62443 layering; R5/R6 ETCS-ATO) | validate | **A — and the most technically on-point artefact of this entire conference batch for the engagement, because it speaks about ERTMS/CCS security from inside the users' group that runs it.** **(a) IS THE FINDING, AND IT IS THE MOST PRECISE STATEMENT ON FILE OF THE CCS-TSI SECURITY GAP.** The register already holds two independent assertions that the CCS TSI is thin on cybersecurity — EPSF's "**limited cybersecurity requirement in the coming CCS TSI**" (E-2026-08-15-10) and ERA's own 2019 intent to "increase the cyber robustness of ERTMS" via the TSIs (E-2026-07-19-01, E-2026-08-15-02). **This supplies the mechanism those statements lacked: the subsets' security scope is confined to CROSS-BORDER INTEROPERABILITY (RBC-Euroradio) ONLY, while "commonly agreed cyber security standards require a HIGHER level of security than requested by current TSI subsets."** That is the ERTMS users themselves saying the mandated baseline is below the state of the art — and it converts a general observation into a specific, citable scoping defect. It also contextualises SUBSET-146 (E-2026-08-01-15): a real, mandated E2E application-security spec exists, but per this deck the subsets' security remit remains interoperability-shaped rather than holistic. **(a)3 IS A DIRECT RISK TO R7, AND SHOULD BE READ AS ONE.** Because the mandated baseline is below what operators need, "some operators now are defining cyber security measures, **which lead to specific solutions per operator**". R7's whole purpose is preserving interoperability and regulatory conformance; here **security is becoming a divergence vector inside a system whose defining property is cross-border commonality** — each IM compensating differently for the same under-specification. R7 stays **Accepted** (the decision to conform to the CCS TSI is unaffected and remains correct), but this is a named, sector-sourced *risk to the requirement*, and it is exactly the risk ESCG exists to close by driving common requirements back into the subsets (b2, g3). Worth flagging to the client as an argument for engaging with ESCG/System Pillar output rather than developing bespoke measures. **(a)3 ALSO ANSWERS ENISA'S LEGACY DEAD-END WITH PRACTICE.** ENISA concluded in 2021 that "**it is not possible to provide relevant recommendations to address the cybersecurity of legacy systems in the railway sector**" and that industry involvement would be needed (E-2026-08-15-07). ESCG is that involvement: it explicitly treats current installations as **legacy systems requiring compensating measures**, runs **separate risk analyses per application phase** (existing / near-future / future, (e)), and produces a **base for a migration plan** and an **ERTMS security migration roadmap** (g). For **R2** — a decade-plus of GSM-R↔FRMCS coexistence, i.e. a decade of running legacy alongside new — this is the first evidence on file of a *worked sector method* for securing the legacy half of a migration, and it is directly transferable in shape to the bearer migration even though its subject is ERTMS. **(c)/(d) GIVE THE ZONES-AND-CONDUITS CHAIN ITS ERTMS LINK, AND A CONCRETE ASSET LIST.** The register tracks that chain end-to-end — IEC 62443 → TS 50701 → GSMA FS.61 (core interior) → SUBSET-147 (on-board consist) → SNCF rolling-stock practice (E-2026-08-15-15). ESCG adds the **ERTMS/CCS trackside-and-onboard link with named assets under analysis: ETCS-OB, Euroloop, Eurobalise, RIU, RBC, PKI, KMC** — and the **PKI/KMC inclusion** ties to the on-line key-management spec already on file (SUBSET-137, E-2026-08-01-18) and to the EUG's own KMS/KMC/PKI exchange (f2). The five-phase method (protection requirements → zoning/conduits → TRA per TS 50701 & IEC 62443-3-2 → system measures 62443-3-3/-2-1 → component measures 62443-4-2/-4-1) is a **usable reference process** for ADR-012's risk-analysis and measure-definition work, and it independently confirms the TS 50701 ← IEC 62443 derivation now held on a primary (E-2026-08-15-07 fn39). **EN 50159 APPEARS AS A NAMED INPUT (c)** — the safety-related communications standard ADR-004 relies on for its boundary argument — placing it inside the security method rather than beside it, which is mild support for the safety/security co-treatment ADR-012 and ADR-004 both assume. **(g) "POSSIBLE EXPANSION TO ATO-OB AND ATO-TS"** extends the thread to automatic train operation, where SUBSET-148 already delegates security to SUBSET-146 and designates ATO as SIL-0 over vital ETCS (E-2026-08-01-19) — the same non-vital-over-vital pattern ADR-004 uses, now with a security work programme aimed at it. **(f) IS A SCHEDULING/HANDOVER DATUM WORTH CHECKING:** ESCG was to finish "until 03/2023" and the **ERJU System Pillar Security Group** was to build on its analysis and definitions. Whether that handover happened, and what the System Pillar produced, is unknown here and is the natural follow-up. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** **R7 stays Accepted · ADR-001** (a risk to it is recorded, not a status change); R14 Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R2/R1 Accepted; R5/R6 Accepted; R11/ADR-004 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change — though of everything logged today this is the strongest candidate for an **ADR-012 context note** (the TSI-subsets scoping defect + the per-operator divergence risk) and for a line in **ADR-001/R7's risk framing**; offered, not made. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **the ESCG deliverables themselves** — the ERTMS security guideline, the risk analyses, the measure-definition documentation — via https://ertms.be/workgroups/cyber_security (likely members-gated); (2) **what the ERJU System Pillar Security Group produced** after taking over in 2023, and whether ESCG's requirements were in fact transferred into TSI CCS subsets or the application guide as (g) intended — this would show whether the gap at (a) has since been closed in the 2023/1695-and-later regime; (3) the **EULYNX/RCA Security Cluster** and **OCORA Security Working Group** outputs on Shared Security Services, neither on file. +| E-2026-08-15-20 | 2026-08-15 | **UNIFE (The European Rail Supply Industry Association)** — "**The European rail supply industry's position on cybersecurity in railway transport**", **Nicolas Furio, UNIFE Head of Technical Affairs**; 9 slides; **ERA & ENISA conference, Lille, 1st December 2022**. UNIFE self-description: representing the European rail supply industry in Brussels since 1992; **110+ member companies** (large and medium-sized, **1/3 SMEs**) active in design, manufacture, maintenance and refurbishment of rail systems/subsystems/equipment, plus **13 national rail industry associations**. Paraphrased: **(a) FRAMING** — cybersecurity is "one of the key priorities in the rail sector" and "key for the digital transformation"; drivers given as digitalisation, a fast-evolving threat landscape ("volume and complexity of cyber attacks is increasing quickly"), and a legislative framework aiming "notably at harmonising responses". **(b) COLLABORATION** — a fully cyber-resilient railway relies on "the capacity of the rail sector to **build trust** between its stakeholders" and on "**avoiding silos** at every level of cybersecurity detection, analysis and response". **(c) THE INDUSTRY'S STATED METHODS** — **IEC 62443**; "its railway-specific adaptation **CENELEC CLC/TS 50701:2021**… **currently being migrated to IEC 63452**"; plus sector-specific regulation, supply-chain requirements and contractual requirements. **(d) THE POSITION PAPER** — "UNIFE position paper on Cybersecurity in Railways, **September 2021**", "aimed at harmonising cybersecurity considering a **sectoral approach** across the European Union". **(e) SHORT-TERM HIGH-PRIORITY CHALLENGES** — *01 Standardisation*: TS 50701 as "a major milestone", "an example of a collaborative approach", and "**the most promising way to harmonise requirements in the short-term**", building on "the already well rolled-out IEC 62443"; *02 Legislative framework*: "**NIS2 Directive, Cyber Resilience Act proposal, Cybersecurity Act**", with the demands that a "**horizontal approach shall ensure that there is not an overlapping**" and that "considering the particularities of the industrial sector is key". **(f) "PARTICULARITIES OF THE RAIL SECTOR TO BE CONSIDERED"** — the sector's regulatory complexity, "including **Operational Technologies (OT) in rail systems, that primarily interacts with the physical world**"; "to ensure the interaction with the physical world, **the rail sector has already its own regulation**"; "**legacy systems coexist with new systems**"; "**complexity of the value chain and interactions**"; "**rail is a system of sub-systems**"; "**long life-cycle of the rail products (over 30 years)**"; "**long life-cycle of the manufacturing process (up to 7 years)**". **(g) THE HEADLINE — UNIFE'S POSITION ON THE CRA PROPOSAL.** UNIFE states that a coherent regulatory framework covering connected-product cybersecurity "is of utmost importance", but argues: "it is critical to consider the particularities of different sectors, especially industrial sectors, like for the rail sector, where a **VERTICAL approach** is key"; there are "**higher overlapping risks with existing rail regulations, and a huge financial impact on the European rail supply industry putting at risk its competitiveness**"; and "it is important to **avoid the setting-up of additional regulation in an already highly regulated industry** where cybersecurity provisions **can be integrated in the existing European rail regulation**" — concluding, as a boxed statement: **"The rail sector should be EXCLUDED from the scope of the CRA proposal."** A follow-on UNIFE position paper on the CRA proposal was announced as forthcoming. Further flags: **slide-deck form**; **2022 vintage** — this addresses the CRA **proposal**, and the CRA has since been **adopted** as Reg (EU) 2024/2847 | **R14/ADR-012 — potentially FOUNDATIONAL** (+ R8/PR13 supplier position; R2/R1 legacy coexistence & 30-year lifecycles; R13) | watch | **B by tier — but the single most consequential row of this conference batch, because it bears on ADR-012's foundation.** **THE FINDING: THE EUROPEAN RAIL SUPPLY INDUSTRY ACTIVELY LOBBIED FOR RAIL TO BE EXCLUDED FROM THE CRA.** ADR-012's entire legal basis is that the CRA applies — "every FRMCS product with digital element… **and the agentic-oversight layer**" is in scope, with manufacturer duties (SBOM, support period, CVD, conformity assessment) forming the procurement gate at action item 2 and the PR13 mitigation. **If that lobbying had succeeded, ADR-012's product-side foundation would be void.** **THIS COMPLETES A THREE-PART PICTURE ASSEMBLED FROM THE SAME DAY'S PROCEEDINGS:** (i) DG CNECT listed the CRA's outright exclusions — cars, medical devices, in vitro, **certified aeronautical equipment** — with **rail conspicuously absent** (E-2026-08-15-09); (ii) EDA showed *why* aviation qualifies as "sufficiently regulated on cybersecurity" — an amended Basic Regulation covering cyber-risks-for-safety, binding Part-IS across all approved organisations, a mature EUROCAE/RTCA suite, and ECSCG's standards gap-management (E-2026-08-15-14); (iii) **this row shows the rail supply industry asking for the same treatment on the same day.** The three together explain the asymmetry flagged at (i) as a *contested* outcome rather than an oversight. **THEREFORE — THE HIGHEST-PRIORITY VERIFICATION IN THE REGISTER, AND IT IS NO LONGER HOUSEKEEPING:** confirm against the **adopted Reg (EU) 2024/2847** whether any rail carve-out, partial exclusion, or sectoral deferral was introduced between the 2022 proposal and adoption. The register's verified CRA rows (E-2026-07-01-06; re-validated verbatim E-2026-07-10-02) establish the CRA's dates, fines and obligations but **do not explicitly record the exclusion list**, and E-2026-08-15-09 already carried "verify the CRA exclusion list" as an open item. **That item is now load-bearing: the answer determines whether ADR-012 stands as written.** The strong prior is that UNIFE did *not* succeed — the engagement's verified reading has consistently been that the CRA applies to FRMCS PDEs — but a prior is not a verification, and an ADR foundation should not rest on one. **THE INTERNAL CONTRADICTION IS ITSELF EVIDENCE, AND WORTH PUTTING TO THE CLIENT.** UNIFE's central justification is that rail is "**an already highly regulated industry where cybersecurity provisions can be integrated in the existing European rail regulation**". **That claim is contradicted by the sector's own participants, several speaking at the same conference on the same day:** the **ERTMS Users Group** stated that the CCS TSI subsets' security scope covers **cross-border interoperability (RBC-Euroradio) only** and that "commonly agreed cyber security standards require a **higher** level of security than requested by current TSI subsets" (E-2026-08-15-19); **ERA** stated flatly that **TS 50701 "cannot be as is considered as a mandatory Standard"** (E-2026-08-15-06); the **French NSA** flagged "**limited cybersecurity requirement in the coming CCS TSI**" (E-2026-08-15-10); and **ENISA** recorded that **no sectoral cybersecurity assessment had ever been conducted for rail** (E-2026-08-15-08) and that legacy-system security recommendations were **not possible** to give (E-2026-08-15-07). The existing rail regulation into which UNIFE proposes to fold cybersecurity provisions is, on this evidence, **non-mandatory, narrowly scoped and incomplete** — which is precisely the condition that distinguishes rail from the aviation regime the CRA does exclude. **Cite this contrast carefully and fairly**: UNIFE's underlying concerns about regulatory overlap and cost are legitimate and shared (EPSF also flagged "increasing cost for certifications", E-2026-08-15-10; NS flagged that sector-wide mechanisms take time, E-2026-08-15-13); it is the *"already highly regulated"* premise specifically that the record does not support. **(f) DELIVERS A GENUINELY VALUABLE, SUPPLIER-SOURCED DATUM FOR ADR-012 — AND IT CUTS BOTH WAYS.** "**Long life-cycle of the rail products (over 30 years)**" and "long life-cycle of the manufacturing process (up to 7 years)" quantify, from the party that would have to honour it, the mismatch ADR-012 already records in its *Harder* consequences: CRA support-period commitments versus long-life rail assets, where an end-of-life element "may lack CRA-conformant vendor support, re-raising PR13/PR14". The CRA proposal's support period was framed as **5 years or the product lifetime, whichever is shorter** (E-2026-08-15-09); against **30+ year** rail product lives that is a structural gap, not a detail. **This strengthens ADR-012's own analysis while being offered in support of the opposite conclusion** — UNIFE uses it to argue for exclusion; the engagement should use it to argue that support-period terms must be contractually extended at procurement (ADR-012 action 2), because the statutory floor will not cover the asset life. Also note "legacy systems coexist with new systems" (R2) and "rail is a system of sub-systems" from the supply side. **(c)/(e) CORROBORATE TWO TRACKS ALREADY ON FILE:** TS 50701 "currently being migrated to **IEC 63452**" independently confirms the successor path (E-2026-08-01-17/-20), and UNIFE's promotion of TS 50701 as "the most promising way to harmonise requirements in the short-term" is **exactly the position that was challenged from the floor at the 25.11.2021 webinar** — where a questioner asked ENISA's opinion of the UNIFE paper's short-term TS 50701 certification proposal and its "added value… in comparison with using 62443 like the basis", and separately objected that "**UNIFE Paper is scoped to Signalling but headlined to 'Railway'**" (both recorded at E-2026-08-15-06). Those two criticisms now attach to a named document, and neither is answered in this deck. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION — but flags one as unverified:** R14 stays **Open**, ADR-012 stays **Proposed** and its CRA basis stands **pending the verification above**; R8 Recommended; R2/R1/R13 unchanged. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **VERIFY the adopted CRA exclusion list and any rail treatment in Reg (EU) 2024/2847 — foundational for ADR-012**; (2) **UNIFE's promised position paper on the CRA proposal** (announced here as forthcoming) and the underlying **UNIFE position paper on Cybersecurity in Railways, September 2021** — neither on file, and the 2021 paper is the document the ERA-ENISA floor criticised; (3) whether the "scoped to signalling but headlined railway" criticism holds against the 2021 paper's actual scope. That last framing is conceptually adjacent to ADR-004's **automation-bias** obligation and R10's human-oversight design — the human interposed as the final barrier, whose reliability is itself the risk. It is cyber-framed rather than AI-framed, so cite it as an *adjacent* development, not as evidence about advisory-AI over-trust. **(2) LEGACY RETROFIT SECURITY NOW HAS A NAMED SESSION — directly R13/R2.** "**Improving Cybersecurity on Legacy Trains through Retrofit**", co-presented by **NS** with a vendor, plus "Testing trains for security weaknesses" and rail-sector **inspections**. ENISA had concluded in 2021 that "it is not possible to provide relevant recommendations to address the cybersecurity of legacy systems" (E-2026-08-15-07); ESCG then supplied a method for ERTMS (E-2026-08-15-19); this is the **rolling-stock** limb, and it is the closest thing yet to an answer for R13's fleet-retrofit problem viewed from the security side, alongside SNCF's rolling-stock practice (E-2026-08-15-15). **(3) THE ASSOCIATIONS CONVERGED.** In 12/2022 UNIFE argued alone that "the rail sector should be excluded from the scope of the CRA proposal" (E-2026-08-15-20). By 10/2024 the session is "**UNIFE, CER and EIM joint effort on cybersecurity**" — supply industry, undertakings/IMs and infrastructure managers presenting a *joint* position. Whether that joint effort retains the exclusion demand is unknown from an agenda and is worth establishing, since the CRA was adopted as Reg (EU) 2024/2847 in the same month as this conference. **(4) NIS-2 HAS MOVED FROM PROPOSAL TO IMPLEMENTATION** — two full sessions, including a **national transposition** case (Belgium, via the Centre for Cyber security Belgium — a **fourth** national model after FR/DE/FI) and industry-impact views from **Skoda** and **Hitachi**. This is the maturation of the thread that in 12/2022 was still "in voorbereiding" (E-2026-08-15-13). **ALSO NOTABLE:** a rail sector **CISO Forum** exists, reported by **Infrabel** — the same operator that presented "A CyberSOC for Railway" at the 3rd conference (E-2026-08-15-21), suggesting Infrabel is the leading practitioner on the detection-ownership question (R12/§4a); "**Applying PenTest to ERTMS**" from an academic team is directly kin to ADR-007 and the SIM-SEC backlog; **Supply Chain Management** as its own slot matches R8/PR13 and the NIS-2 important-entity finding (E-2026-08-15-18); the **European Cybersecurity Competence Centre** and the **Digital Europe Programme** enter as funding/capability instruments new to this register; and **CYRUS** returns with an update, continuing the capacity thread. **NOTABLY ABSENT: no FRMCS-specific session in 2024** — the 3rd conference had Houbion's "Cybersecurity risk-assessment of FRMCS" (E-2026-08-15-21) and the 4th has none visible, so the FRMCS security thread does not appear to have been carried forward at this venue. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION** — nothing in an agenda can: R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed, R13 Open, R10/R11 Proposed, R2 Accepted. No matrix change; the only edit made is the factual ordinal correction described above. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) the **UNIFE/CER/EIM joint position** — does it still seek CRA exclusion, post-adoption? (kin the foundational CRA verification at E-2026-08-15-20); (2) the **legacy-train retrofit** material (Razor Secure / NS) for R13; (3) **Infrabel CISO Forum** and CyberSOC material for R12; (4) the **EU-Rail System Pillar cybersecurity** output, now presented three years running. READ: complete text (2 pp) | Conference agenda (primary institutional — joint ERA/ENISA event programme, ENISA-authored) | file "5th era enisa conference agenda - v5.pdf", Download folder; md5 df539938…be83; conference 01-02.12.2025, Tallinn | A by source — **but an AGENDA carries almost no evidentiary content; see action** | **R14/ADR-012** (+ **R3/R4 wartime & hybrid-threat resilience**; R12 CyberSOC; R13 rolling stock; R11/ADR-004 safety-critical-to-cloud) | watch | **A by source, WATCH by weight — an agenda proves scheduling, not content; nothing here is citable as a finding.** But it is the **most current** artefact in this batch and it moves two register questions materially. **✅ THE SERIES IS NOW FULLY MAPPED, five editions: 1st 16-17.03.2021 (online) · 2nd 01.12.2022 (Lille) · 3rd 08-09.11.2023 (Athens) · 4th 02-03.10.2024 (Lille) · 5th 01-02.12.2025 (Tallinn).** The ordinal correction applied to E-2026-08-01-20 (see E-2026-08-15-22) holds. **① STRONG BEHAVIOURAL EVIDENCE THAT THE CRA EXCLUSION BID FAILED — the foundational question at E-2026-08-15-20.** In 12/2022 UNIFE argued that "the rail sector should be **excluded** from the scope of the CRA proposal". Three years later the sector devotes an entire **policy session to CRA COMPLIANCE**: "practical insights and FAQ from corporate practice", "**practical approach to CRA Compliance in Railway Systems**", and — decisively in framing — "**Applying the CRA in rail: a sector-wide approach**", **jointly presented by the rail supply industry (Knorr-Bremse) and by CER & EIM (SNCF)**. A sector does not build a sector-wide application approach, corporate-practice FAQs and compliance methodologies for a regulation it has been exempted from. **This raises confidence substantially that ADR-012's premise — the CRA applies to FRMCS products with digital elements — is correct.** **IT DOES NOT DISCHARGE THE VERIFICATION**, because an agenda is not a legal text and cannot exclude a partial carve-out, a deferral, or sector-specific treatment within the adopted Regulation; the check against **Reg (EU) 2024/2847** stands as the top legal follow-up. But it downgrades the risk from "ADR-012 may rest on a false premise" to "ADR-012's premise is very likely sound, pending textual confirmation". Note also that the same three associations that mounted the 2022 exclusion argument (UNIFE) and its 2024 successor joint effort (UNIFE/CER/EIM, E-2026-08-15-22) are here **representing the sector in applying** the CRA — a complete arc from resistance to implementation across three conferences. **② UKRAINIAN RAILWAYS ON "CYBERSECURITY IN RAILWAYS UNDER WARTIME CONDITIONS" IS EXTRAORDINARY R3/R4 MATERIAL — and it is the single most valuable deck to obtain from this edition.** The engagement's R3 (eliminate the central single point of failure) and R4 (guarantee fail-soft; no full standstill) are, on the register's own account, load-bearing-open and must be **proven by test** (ADR-007). Ukrainian Railways has operated a national rail network under sustained kinetic and cyber attack — i.e. it holds *empirical* evidence about degraded-mode operation, resilience and continuity that no European PoC can manufacture. Paired with the Finnish ministry's "**era of hybrid threats**" framing and the Estonian NCSC's regional threat landscape, this Tallinn edition is markedly more **threat- and resilience-oriented** than its predecessors, which were regulation- and standards-oriented — a shift in the sector's centre of gravity that is itself worth reporting to the client. **③ THE CAPACITY THREAD SHOWS REAL MOVEMENT.** At the 2nd conference Traficom reported staffing railway cybersecurity at "**~1**" person (E-2026-08-15-12); here **Ville Lahti of Traficom — the named contact on that very deck — presents "Finland's 1st UNIVERSITY-LEVEL online course on railway cybersecurity"**. Alongside Infrabel's awareness programme framed around "**11,000 people**" and CYRUS now offering a "**free** solution for flexible, role-based cybersecurity training", the shortage that four institutions diagnosed and ENISA quantified (E-2026-08-15-16: median 7 IS FTEs, transport second-lowest spend) is being answered with **pipeline-building instruments** rather than recruitment alone. For R13 and for any staffing assumption in the oversight design (R10/R12), these are concrete and current. **④ INFRABEL IS NOW THE SECTOR'S REFERENCE CYBERSOC PRACTITIONER — a three-conference arc**: "A CyberSOC for Railway, why and how" (3rd, E-2026-08-15-21) → "CISO Forum updates" (4th, E-2026-08-15-22) → "**The journey of a CyberSOC for an Infrastructure Manager**" (5th, here). Against ENISA's finding that **28% of transport OES have no SOC** and that detection skills are the least-funded (E-2026-08-15-16), and against the standing §4a detection-ownership gap, Infrabel's material is the most directly applicable operational precedent identified anywhere in this register for R12. **⑤ TWO ITEMS TOUCH ADR-004 DIRECTLY:** "**Securing connection between safety-critical systems and the cloud**" (UITP Cybersecurity Committee) is the security face of the vital/non-vital and cloud-hosting question ADR-004 and the Cloud4Rail thread work through (E-2026-07-02-27, E-2026-07-02-03); and "**Zero Trust in Railway**" (Estonian Railways) is an operator instantiating the Zero-Trust/ITN direction ETSI framed as the design answer to the interconnect-trust gap (E-2026-08-02-14) — the first *operator* Zero-Trust item on file. **⑥ NEW BODIES AND ENFORCEMENT SIGNALS:** a **"Cybersecurity Rail Sector Group"** now exists with a named Secretary (moderating the policy session) — new to this register and worth identifying; the Dutch **Transport Inspectorate (ILT)** presents "**increasing the pressure** for cybersecurity in Dutch rail", i.e. supervision is moving from guidance to enforcement; **RailNetEurope** brings cybersecurity-by-design into the international **Common Interface**; and Sweden (Stockholm PTA, Swedish Transport Authority) joins FR/DE/FI/BE/NL/EE/UA as national participants. **NOTABLY ABSENT AGAIN: no FRMCS-specific session** — as at the 4th conference. The FRMCS security thread visible at the 3rd (Houbion's risk assessment) has not resurfaced at this venue in two subsequent editions, which sharpens rather than softens the case for obtaining that 2023 deck. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION** — nothing in an agenda can: R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed, its CRA premise now better supported but still textually unverified); R3/R4 unchanged; R12/R10/R11 Proposed; R13 Open. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **the Ukrainian Railways wartime-conditions deck** — unique empirical resilience evidence for R3/R4/ADR-007; (2) the **CRA sector-wide approach** material (Knorr-Bremse / SNCF for CER & EIM) — the sector's actual CRA implementation position, and the practical complement to the legal verification; (3) **Infrabel's CyberSOC journey**; (4) identify the **Cybersecurity Rail Sector Group**; (5) the UITP safety-critical-to-cloud material for ADR-004; (6) whether a **6th** edition is scheduled for 2026.
