+| E-2026-08-15-24 | 2026-08-15 | **KULDAR LEIS, Estonian Minister of Infrastructure** — opening speech, **5th ERA-ENISA Conference on Cybersecurity in Railways, Tallinn, 01.12.2025**; 5 pp speech text (PDF created 11.12.2025; speaker identified from the agenda, E-2026-08-15-23). Paraphrased: **(a) FRAMING** — Estonia as "a digital state" on "**NATO's eastern flank**", host of **NATO's Cooperative Cyber Defence Centre of Excellence**, building "the largest infrastructure project in the Baltic region"; the two questions posed as keeping railways functioning as "the backbone of our everyday economy" and ensuring "this backbone does not break under the pressure of **hybrid attacks, cyber threats and sabotage**". **(b) THREAT CLAIMS — see the tiering caveat, these are political assertions:** "attacks against railways are no longer a theoretical scenario… part of everyday reality"; "**over the last two years, Russian hackers have carried out THOUSANDS of cyberattacks on the railway infrastructure of the European Union**" (no source given); four illustrations offered as "different facets of one and the same hybrid campaign" — the **Czech** railway company's "signalling systems… under attack, disrupting the ticketing system"; near **Bremen**, "a railway **cable shaft was set on fire**", causing a full day's disruption Hamburg-Bremen; in **Spain**, "**cable theft** and a technical failure" disrupting high-speed services for two days and stranding "more than 10 000 people" Madrid-Seville; and in **Estonia**, "an attack targeted the **ticketing system** of the passenger rail company". **(c) SATELLITE NAVIGATION AND COMMUNICATIONS — the most technically consequential passage:** "In the Baltic region… the **disruption of satellite navigation and communications**"; Estonia and neighbours have seen "a **sharp increase in GPS jamming**, both in aviation and in maritime transport", with "at times **up to 85% of flights to and from Estonia** experienc[ing] navigation disturbances at some point"; "these disruptions are **increasingly reaching land** as well"; "**precise location information, the correct time and reliable communications are just as critical in RAIL TRAFFIC MANAGEMENT**, in logistics chains and in military planning as they are in aviation"; and the design principle — "**if satellite navigation or communications fail, the rest of the system – including the railway – must be designed so that it does not collapse like a row of dominoes**". **(d) ESTONIAN RAILWAYS PROGRAMME** — electrifying and digitalising the entire core network and raising speeds; "thoroughly upgrading our control, signalling and communications systems"; "a large part of this work means **replacing legacy technology – phasing out Soviet-era systems** and introducing modern, software-based control and signalling solutions"; with the caution that "the more we rely on software and networked solutions, the more important it becomes that **cybersecurity is built in from the outset, rather than bolted on as an afterthought**", and that "**cyber and physical security must go hand in hand**". **(e) RAIL BALTICA** — built with Latvia and Lithuania; "a new main axis for passenger and freight transport", "our economic corridor to Europe", and "from the perspective of the EU and NATO, **one of the key corridors for allied movements on the eastern flank**"; the European Commission's "new development plan for high-speed rail" confirms it as a strategic core route; **target readiness 2030**; and — the operative sentence — "if we are building a railway that must be able, **in a crisis, to carry NATO supplies**, then it has to be designed to withstand cyberattacks, satellite navigation disturbances and acts of sabotage. **Cybersecurity requirements in Rail Baltica have therefore been written into the DESIGN GUIDELINES, the ARCHITECTURE of the technical systems and the CONDITIONS OF THE TENDERS. The same principle must also apply to future maintenance and operation contracts.**" **(f) TWO ELEMENTS BEYOND REGULATION** — *situational awareness*: "we cannot afford to have anyone working in an information vacuum. We must **share information about attacks, incidents and emerging trends BEFORE they escalate into a crisis**"; "hybrid influencing activities do not respect national or sectoral borders, which means our defence must also be **interconnected rather than fragmented**". *People, skills and exercising*: "we may have the best technology in the world, but if, at the moment of an attack, our team is **exhausted, understaffed, or has never practised a similar scenario in a realistic exercise**, that may become our weakest link"; calling for "**more joint exercises**", more cooperation between authorities, companies and cybersecurity units, and "more practical training specifically focused on **industrial control systems and the particularities of rail**". Closes by quoting **Ursula von der Leyen**, in Tallinn: "Critical infrastructure is the new frontier of warfare. And Europe will be prepared." READ: complete text (5 pp) | Ministerial speech (government primary — a serving national minister, at a joint EU-agency conference) | file "session 1-1 - ee infrastructure minister - speech on rail cybsec.pdf", Download folder; md5 1255fc53…1b52; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025 | **SPLIT TIER — A for Estonian policy and programme; the THREAT CLAIMS are political assertion.** **A** where the minister speaks authoritatively on his own government's position: the Rail Baltica cybersecurity requirements (e), the Estonian Railways modernisation programme (d), and Estonia's policy posture (f) — a serving minister is the primary source on these. **NOT A** for (b): "thousands of cyberattacks", the attribution to "**Russian hackers**", and the "one and the same hybrid campaign" framing are **unsourced political assertions in a political speech**, offered without corroboration, and must be treated as **claims, not verified incident data** — nearer C/D in evidentiary weight and **not citable as fact**. Further flags: **speech genre** — rhetorical, persuasive, written for effect; **speaker not named in-document** (identified via the agenda); **2025-12 vintage** (current, ~8 months old) | **R3/R4 (GNSS/timing dependency & anti-cascade)** (+ **R14/ADR-012 Rail Baltica procurement precedent**; R12 situational awareness; R13/R2 legacy replacement; R10/ADR-007 exercises & staffing) | watch | **Split-tiered, WATCH — because its headline claims need verification and nothing here moves a status; but three passages are genuinely valuable.** **⚠️ HANDLE (b) CAREFULLY — THE RHETORIC OUTRUNS THE EXAMPLES, AND THE REGISTER SHOULD SAY SO.** The minister frames four incidents as facets of a single hybrid campaign. On his own descriptions: **Bremen was ARSON** (a cable shaft set on fire) and **Spain was CABLE THEFT plus a technical failure** — both **physical**, not cyber; **Estonia was a TICKETING system** — business IT; and the **Czech** description is internally odd ("signalling systems… under attack, disrupting the ticketing system"), conflating two layers. **None of the four is a demonstrated cyber compromise of safety-critical OT producing a safety consequence.** That is precisely consistent with the register's better-sourced material: Traficom's OSINT database recorded ≥19 European rail cyber incidents in 2022 with "**no direct impacts on safety**", mostly ransomware and DDoS (E-2026-08-15-12); ENISA's scenario 2 (ICS malware sabotaging traffic supervision) "**has not yet occurred in the railway sector**" (E-2026-08-15-07); and ERA/ENISA's 2020 position was that known incidents "target the IT railway systems" (E-2026-08-15-04). **So the speech is useful evidence of POLITICAL FRAMING and of physical-sabotage pressure on rail, and poor evidence of cyber-attack-on-signalling.** Use it for the former; do not let "thousands of cyberattacks" enter client-facing material as fact. The "thousands" figure in particular should be sourced or dropped. **① THE GENUINE FIND — GNSS/TIME DENIAL IS A REGISTER GAP, AND (c) OPENS IT.** A check confirms every existing GNSS mention in this register concerns **GNSS as a surveying and acceptance TOOL** (the D3iP/OPAL georeferenced positioning work replacing measuring-wheel methods for CCS element acceptance) — **GNSS as a jammable DEPENDENCY is nowhere on file.** Yet the minister's point is precise and correct in principle: "**precise location information, the correct TIME and reliable communications**" are critical to rail traffic management. Timing matters more than the speech says: the register already holds **UTC/time-service requirements inside the mandated security specs** — SUBSET-146's UTC time service (E-2026-08-01-15) and SUBSET-147 §9.3.5 TTLS time-service security (E-2026-08-01-14) — and **time synchronisation is a named residual in the SIL4-Cloud work** (E-2026-07-02-03). **A 5G-SA bearer is itself GNSS-timing-dependent for network synchronisation**, so FRMCS inherits this exposure at the transport layer as well as the application layer. **And the minister's design principle — "must be designed so that it does not collapse like a row of dominoes" — is R4's fail-soft requirement stated in a sentence, applied to a dependency R4 does not currently enumerate.** **FOLLOW-UP (new, and it belongs on the ranked list): does the FRMCS/ERTMS architecture have a stated GNSS-denial posture — holdover timing, alternative sync, positioning fallback — and is GNSS jamming/spoofing in the threat model at all?** This is a candidate SIM-SEC vector (kin SIM-SEC-001 radio-layer availability) and a candidate addition to R4's fallback set. **② RAIL BALTICA IS A CONCRETE ADR-012 ACTION-2 PRECEDENT — greenfield, and unusually complete.** Cybersecurity requirements written into **design guidelines + technical architecture + TENDER CONDITIONS**, with the explicit extension that "the same principle must also apply to future **maintenance and operation contracts**". ADR-012 action item 2 proposes exactly this — manufacturer CRA evidence (SBOM, support-period commitment, CVD contact) as a **bid condition** — and this is a live EU project doing it, on a 2030 delivery horizon. It is also the answer to the through-life problem UNIFE raised from the other side (30+ year product lives versus a 5-year support floor, E-2026-08-15-20): **carry the requirement into the O&M contract, not just the supply contract.** Worth citing to the client as a peer precedent; note it is a **greenfield** build, so it does not answer the harder brownfield/retrofit case (R13, R2). **③ THE MILITARY-MOBILITY FRAMING IS NEW TO THE REGISTER AND RAISES THE CRITICALITY BAR.** Rail Baltica as "one of the key corridors for **allied movements** on the eastern flank" and a line that "must be able, in a crisis, to carry **NATO supplies**" makes rail explicitly **dual-use logistics infrastructure**. That changes the threat model's adversary assumptions (state military planning, not only criminal or intelligence actors — kin the Sandworm profile) and the availability requirement's justification: R3/R4 are argued in this register from safety and economic continuity; military mobility is a third, independent argument for the same design properties, and one that will carry weight with EU/national funders. **④ CORROBORATIONS worth noting, at ministerial level:** (f)'s "share information… **before they escalate into a crisis**" is R12's formulation almost verbatim (a decision-ready alert *before* the threshold) and its "interconnected rather than fragmented" defence matches the §4a ownership thread; "**exhausted, understaffed**, or has never practised" puts the capacity constraint — already attested by ENISA, EPSF, Traficom, EDA and quantified at E-2026-08-15-16 — into a **government minister's** mouth, which is useful for a client conversation about funding oversight staffing; and the call for "**more joint exercises**" and ICS-specific rail training is direct support for the ADR-007 test/exercise programme, alongside Traficom's zero-budget national tabletop (E-2026-08-15-12). (d)'s "**cybersecurity built in from the outset, rather than bolted on**" and "cyber and physical security must go hand in hand" are the security-by-design and safety/security-coupling positions ADR-012 already takes. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R3/R4 stay as they are (a *new dependency* is flagged for R4's fallback analysis, not a status change); R14 Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R12/R10 Proposed; R13 Open; R2 Accepted. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **GNSS-denial posture for FRMCS/ERTMS — timing holdover, sync alternatives, positioning fallback; add to R4's fallback set and the SIM-SEC backlog if unaddressed**; (2) source or drop the "thousands of cyberattacks" claim — check ENISA Transport Threat Landscape editions and national CERT reporting; (3) the four cited incidents (Czech signalling/ticketing, Bremen arson, Spain Madrid-Seville, Estonian ticketing) each need a primary source before any use — the **Spain Madrid-Seville** and **Bremen** events are physical-sabotage data points worth having properly; (4) **Rail Baltica's actual cybersecurity tender conditions and design guidelines** — the most directly reusable procurement artefact identified for ADR-012 action 2. +| E-2026-08-15-25 | 2026-08-15 | **GERT AUVÄÄRT, Director of the Estonian National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC-EE)** — "**Regional Threat Landscape in Critical Infrastructure**", **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025** (Regional Focus session); 8 slides, bullet-only, no figures; PDF created 10.12.2025. **The national technical authority's assessment, delivered at the same conference and on the same day as the ministerial opening speech (E-2026-08-15-24) — and materially more measured.** Paraphrased: **(a) NCSC-EE's ROLE in critical-infrastructure cyber defence** — sectoral exchange and dissemination of threat information; cyber-defence courses, trainings and information days for providers of **vital and essential services**; and "**security testing of critical infrastructure; consultations**". **(b) INCIDENTS RELATED TO THE ESTONIAN RAILWAY SECTOR** — "Estonian Railways and **Elron websites** were popular targets of **DDoS** attacks"; "**attacks on service providers**"; the "**Ridango ticket system**"; and "**physical accidents damaging the system**". **(c) INCIDENTS IN THE TRANSPORT SECTOR** — *DDoS*: **Tallinn Airport, Tallinn Port, Estonian Railways, Elron, Pilet.ee**; *service-provider attacks*: **Ridango**, **Collins Aerospace**; and a third category, "**configuration errors, equipment failures, accidents**". **(d) MAIN CONCLUSIONS — the substantive slide:** transport is a "**preferred target for CYBERCRIMINALS**"; the pattern is "**ransomware, data breaches, and service disruptions**"; "**DDoS on ticketing systems, phishing, and service-availability threats**"; "**MOST ATTACKS TARGET INSTITUTIONAL WEBSITES AND OFFICE IT SYSTEMS**"; and — stated as the bottom line — "**OVERALL CYBER-RISK LEVEL: MODERATE**". **(e) HOW RESILIENCE IS BUILT** — a "**National SOC monitoring 24/7**"; "**CERT-EE helps with incident response WHERE THEY CAN**"; awareness raising and prevention (secure passwords, MFA, antivirus, software updating); international cooperation, naming **Cyber Europe**; and trainings for essential service providers. md5 ee7a4e00…3745; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025. **No TLP marking observed** | A (primary national technical authority — the NCSC/CERT function itself, which is the competent body for this assessment; **FOUR FLAGS**: **very sparse deck** — bullets only, **no incident counts, no trend data, no time window stated**, so "moderate" is an unquantified qualitative rating; **national scope** — Estonia and its region, not an EU-wide picture; **slide-deck form**; **2025-12 vintage**, i.e. current) | R14/ADR-012 (threat picture) (+ **R3/R4 availability/DDoS**; **R8/PR13 service-provider attacks**; R12 national SOC/CERT) | validate | **A — and its principal value is CALIBRATION: it is the sourced, technical counterpart to the ministerial speech logged an hour earlier in the same programme, and it gives a distinctly more measured picture.** E-2026-08-15-24 recorded the Estonian Infrastructure Minister asserting "**thousands of cyberattacks**" by "**Russian hackers**" on EU railway infrastructure, framed as "one and the same **hybrid campaign**" and "part of everyday reality"; that row flagged those claims as unsourced political assertion and not citable as fact. **Here the Director of Estonia's own national cyber security centre, at the same conference, concludes: transport is a "preferred target for CYBERCRIMINALS"; "MOST ATTACKS TARGET INSTITUTIONAL WEBSITES AND OFFICE IT SYSTEMS"; and "OVERALL CYBER-RISK LEVEL: MODERATE."** On attribution (criminal rather than state), on target class (websites and office IT rather than operational systems) and on severity (moderate), **the technical authority's assessment is materially different from the political framing** — and where they differ, the register should follow the NCSC. **BE FAIR TO BOTH, THOUGH — the scopes are not identical.** The minister addressed *hybrid* threat across Europe, explicitly including **physical sabotage** (Bremen arson, Spanish cable theft) and **GNSS jamming**, neither of which this deck addresses at all; NCSC-EE is assessing **cyber** incidents in Estonia and its region. So this does not refute the minister's physical-sabotage or satellite-navigation points — **it refutes nothing outside the cyber domain, and within it, it moderates.** The honest formulation for client use: *on cyber specifically, Estonia's national technical authority rates the risk moderate, with criminal actors and IT/web targets predominating; the elevated framing concerns the broader hybrid picture, including physical and GNSS effects, which this assessment does not cover.* **THIS IS NOW THE THIRD INDEPENDENT NATIONAL/AGENCY CONFIRMATION OF THE IT-NOT-OT THREAT PROFILE**, and the most recent: Traficom's OSINT database found ≥19 European rail cyber incidents in 2022 with "**no direct impacts on safety**", mostly ransomware and DDoS (E-2026-08-15-12); ENISA/ERA stated in 2020 that known incidents "target the IT railway systems" (E-2026-08-15-04) and in 2021 that the ICS-malware traffic-supervision scenario "**has not yet occurred**" (E-2026-08-15-07); and NCSC-EE now says the same for 2025. **That is a consistent, four-year, multi-source finding, and it should be stated plainly in client material: the observed European rail cyber-incident record is availability- and business-IT-shaped, not safety-actuating.** Its corollary matters just as much for this engagement — **it is precisely why the safety-critical scenarios remain low-likelihood/high-impact rather than demonstrated**, and why R3/R4 rest on the *23-June non-cyber* failure rather than on any attack. **(c) IS THE MOST OPERATIONALLY INTERESTING SLIDE — SERVICE-PROVIDER ATTACKS ARE A SUPPLY-CHAIN DATUM (R8/PR13).** NCSC-EE separates "service provider attacks" from direct attacks and names two: **Ridango** — a ticketing service provider whose compromise propagated into the rail operators' ticketing (and which is evidently the incident the minister described as "an attack targeted the ticketing system of the passenger rail company") — and **Collins Aerospace**, an aviation systems supplier. **That is the third-party-dependency failure mode in concrete form**, and it lands on the thread already carrying UNIFE's 30-year support-period problem, the NIS-2 "important entity" status of rail manufacturers (E-2026-08-15-18), ENISA's finding that 86% of OES have TRM policies but only 47% fund and 24% staff them (E-2026-08-15-16), and EBA/BSI's open question about a ransomware attack at a rolling-stock manufacturer (E-2026-08-15-11). **Note the pattern in (b)/(c): the operator was harmed through a supplier, not through its own perimeter** — which is exactly the case ADR-012's procurement gate (action 2) and PR13 exist to address. **THE DDoS CONCENTRATION IS AN R3/R4 NOTE, NOT A SAFETY ONE:** every named rail victim is a **website or ticketing** asset (Estonian Railways, Elron, Pilet.ee), alongside airport and port. This is availability harm to passenger-facing services — commercially and reputationally serious, operationally disruptive, but not movement-authority or signalling. Keep the distinction sharp when the client asks "have railways been attacked?" **(e) ADDS TWO THINGS TO THE §4a AND CAPACITY THREADS.** First, Estonia runs a **national SOC with 24/7 monitoring** — a second national-level operational owner after Germany's BSI/CERT-Bund (E-2026-08-15-11), further narrowing the §4a gap at *national* level while leaving the rail-telecom/interconnect layer still unowned. Second, and candidly, "**CERT-EE helps with incident response WHERE THEY CAN**" — a national CERT publicly hedging its own capacity, which belongs with the capacity thread now attested by ENISA, EPSF, Traficom ("~1 person"), EDA, the ENISA investment data and the minister's own "exhausted, understaffed". **Cyber Europe** is named as the international exercise vehicle — relevant to ADR-007's exercise programme and kin to the EU Cyber Blueprint's crisis-management layer (E-2026-07-29-02) and Traficom's national tabletop (E-2026-08-15-12). Also note (a): NCSC-EE performs "**security testing of critical infrastructure**" — a national authority offering testing as a service, which is a possible model for the ADR-007 test surface where operators lack in-house capability. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R3/R4 unchanged — this concerns availability of passenger-facing IT, not the bearer or vital layer; R8 Recommended; R12 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) the **Ridango** compromise — a rail ticketing service-provider incident affecting multiple operators is a clean, citable supply-chain case study for R8/PR13 if a primary account exists; (2) NCSC-EE's underlying **annual cyber assessment** (this deck is a summary — the Estonian annual report would supply the counts, window and trend this lacks); (3) **Cyber Europe** scope and whether rail participates — relevant to ADR-007. +| E-2026-08-15-26 | 2026-08-15 | **TÕNU TAMMER, CIO of ESTONIAN RAILWAYS** — "**Implementing Zero Trust @ Estonian Railways**", **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025**; 23 slides (statement-style — most pages carry a single line plus an illustration; PDF created 10.12.2025). **Speaker credentials given in-deck and materially relevant:** 2 years CIO of Estonian Railways, **5 years Director of CERT-EE** (the national CERT), 9 years in Home Affairs (diplomat, CSO, project manager, CQO); 2024 Knight of the Order of the White Star 4th Class for promoting cybersecurity. Paraphrased: **(a) WHAT ZERO TRUST IS** — "not a product but [a] 15 years old concept / mindset / strategic approach"; the analogy "requiring a **badge for every door, not just the building entrance** — even if you're inside, you must prove your identity and intent at every step"; and three principles: **Verify Explicitly** ("always authenticate and authorize based on all available data points – user identity, device health, location, service, and anomalies… enforced at **every access attempt, not just at login**"); **Least Privilege Access** ("only the minimum access rights required… limits the potential damage from compromised accounts or devices"); **Assume Breach** ("operate under the mindset that a breach **may have already occurred**. Segment access, monitor continuously, and **minimize the BLAST RADIUS** of any incident"). **(b) WHY — "the IT world we know"** — "**NIS2D and GDPR: cost of prevention becomes cheaper than risk of impact / fine**"; "we're under attack… all the time"; **legacy**; **lack of MFA**; "**huge stash of different technology**"; users outside the office. **(c) WHY — "the OT world we (DON'T) know"**, presented as four oppositions: "airgapped and isolated **vs** moved to cloud and connected"; "perimeter we trust **vs** zero trust and identity driven access"; "pure OT environment **vs** hybrid OT + IT + cloud"; and "**one system to rule for 40 YEARS vs mix of legacy/modern OT with modern IT / cloud**". **(d) KEY TAKEAWAYS** — "**microsegmentation has never been easier**"; "**MFA access on all applications incl. LEGACY**"; "**access based on device**"; "**access based on external validation (4 EYES PRINCIPLE)**"; "**partners get what they need not all routes**"; and the conclusion "**Zero Trust works in the context of rail**". Closing slide: "What did you patch today?" READ: complete extractable text; page-density check and a rendered sample confirm the low-text pages are single-statement slides with illustrations, not unread substance | Operator conference presentation (primary — a railway undertaking's CIO describing his own organisation's implementation) | file "session 2-2 - estonian-railways - zero trust rail.pdf", Download folder; md5 1ed11c73…0d084; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025. No TLP marking observed | A (primary first-hand OPERATOR account of its own programme — the basis on which this register A-weights operator self-accounts, cf. E-2026-08-15-13 (NS), -15 (SNCF), E-2026-07-02-05/-14 (DB Netz); **FOUR FLAGS**: **self-reported** — stated practice and a stated conclusion, with no external verification and no metrics offered; **statement-style deck** — assertions without architecture, evidence or scope boundaries; **IT/ENTERPRISE-WEIGHTED, NOT VITAL OT** — see the load-bearing caveat below; **2025-12**, i.e. current) | R14/ADR-012 (+ **R8/PR13 partner access**; R2/R13 legacy & 40-year lifecycles; R12 continuous monitoring; R11/ADR-004 segmentation/blast-radius — **with the caveat**) | validate | **A — the register's FIRST OPERATOR ZERO-TRUST IMPLEMENTATION, and it comes with an unusually credible speaker: a serving rail CIO who previously spent five years directing the national CERT.** That biography matters for weighting — this is not a vendor pitch or a consultant's framework, it is a practitioner who has seen the national incident picture and then had to defend a railway. **⚠️ THE LOAD-BEARING CAVEAT, WHICH MUST TRAVEL WITH EVERY CITATION: this is ENTERPRISE/IT Zero Trust, NOT Zero Trust in the vital layer.** Every takeaway at (d) — microsegmentation, MFA including on legacy applications, device-based access, four-eyes external validation, partner route restriction — is an **IT and IT/OT-boundary** control set. The OT slide (c) is written as *oppositions and open questions*, and the speaker's own heading is "the OT world **we (don't) know**" — an honest admission that OT is the less-charted half. **Nothing here demonstrates Zero Trust applied to interlocking, ETCS, or any SIL-4 vital function**, which is precisely the layer ADR-004's boundary governs. So: cite "**Zero Trust works in the context of rail**" as an operator's conclusion about **enterprise and hybrid IT/OT estates**, and do **not** let it imply the vital layer. **THIS ADVANCES THE ZERO-TRUST THREAD FROM VISION TO PRACTICE.** The register holds ETSI White Paper 62 framing **Zero Trust and an "Intelligent Trusted Network" overlay as the design answer** to the interconnect-trust gap — but that is explicitly a non-normative 6G-forward *vision* (E-2026-08-02-14), and GSMA FS.61 supplies **micro-segmentation inside the virtualised core** as deployment guidance (E-2026-08-01-07). This is the first artefact showing an **operator actually doing it on a railway** and reporting that it holds. Together they now form a usable chain: vision (ETSI) → core-network guidance (GSMA) → **operator practice (here)** — though the chain still terminates above the vital layer. **"ASSUME BREACH… MINIMIZE THE BLAST RADIUS" IS THE SAME CONTAINMENT LOGIC AS THE ZONES-AND-CONDUITS WORK**, arrived at from the identity side rather than the network side. The register's zones/conduits chain runs IEC 62443 → TS 50701 → GSMA FS.61 → SUBSET-147 on-board (E-2026-08-01-14) → EUG/ESCG's ERTMS zone model (E-2026-08-15-19) → SNCF rolling stock (E-2026-08-15-15). Zero Trust is the **identity-and-verification complement** to that segmentation-and-boundary approach; both are blast-radius arguments, and ADR-004's freedom-from-interference case is a third instance of the same family — which is worth stating explicitly when the ADR is next revised. **(d) "PARTNERS GET WHAT THEY NEED NOT ALL ROUTES" IS THE CONTROL FOR THE THREAT LOGGED ONE SESSION EARLIER.** E-2026-08-15-25 recorded NCSC-EE naming **service-provider attacks — Ridango (ticketing) and Collins Aerospace** — as a distinct incident class in Estonian transport, i.e. **the operator harmed through a supplier rather than through its own perimeter**. Here, at the same conference, the same country's rail CIO names partner-route minimisation as a takeaway. **Threat and control, same event, same estate** — a clean, citable pairing for R8/PR13 and for ADR-012's procurement/third-party work, and a concrete complement to ENISA's finding that 86% of OES have third-party risk policies but only 47% fund and 24% staff them (E-2026-08-15-16). **(b) IS THE ECONOMIC MECHANISM BEHIND A FINDING THE REGISTER ALREADY HAS AS A STATISTIC.** ENISA's investment data showed the **NIS Directive is transport's single largest driver of information-security budget**, ahead of threat landscape and geopolitics (E-2026-08-15-16). Here the operator states *why*, in one line: "**NIS2D and GDPR: cost of prevention becomes cheaper than risk of impact / fine**". That is the causal link between regulation and spend, from the person who has to make the business case — directly useful when presenting ADR-012's CRA/NIS-2 workload to a client as a budget-unlock rather than a burden, and consistent with NS's operator lesson that legislation "boost[s] existing initiatives" (E-2026-08-15-13). **(c)'s "ONE SYSTEM TO RULE FOR 40 YEARS" IS THE LIFECYCLE TENSION STATED AS SECURITY ARCHITECTURE**, and it independently exceeds UNIFE's "over 30 years" product-life figure (E-2026-08-15-20). Read with R2 (a decade-plus of GSM-R↔FRMCS coexistence) and R13 (fleet retrofit), it is the same problem the register tracks commercially and legally, now framed as an *architectural* one: the security model must span a mixed estate of legacy and modern OT plus modern IT and cloud, for decades — which is exactly why "airgapped and isolated" is no longer available as an assumption. **"MFA ACCESS ON ALL APPLICATIONS INCL. LEGACY" IS A SMALL BUT REAL ANSWER TO A DEAD-END.** ENISA concluded in 2021 that "it is **not possible** to provide relevant recommendations to address the cybersecurity of legacy systems in the railway sector" (E-2026-08-15-07). ESCG then supplied a method for ERTMS (E-2026-08-15-19), the 4th conference showed legacy-train retrofit (E-2026-08-15-22), and this adds the **enterprise-IT limb**: identity controls can be retrofitted over legacy applications. Three partial answers to a stated dead-end, none of them covering vital signalling. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R8 Recommended; R2 Accepted; R13 Open; R11/ADR-004 Proposed — and note explicitly that **nothing here changes the ADR-004 position**, since the vital layer is untouched by this account. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) whether Estonian Railways' Zero Trust programme reaches the **OT/CCS** estate at all, and if so with what safety-case treatment — that is the question this deck raises and does not answer, and it is the one that would matter for ADR-004; (2) the **four-eyes access-validation** mechanism — a concrete implementation of dual authorisation that ADR-011's change-control and ADR-004's human-in-command designs could reference; (3) whether "microsegmentation has never been easier" reflects a specific platform choice worth knowing. +| E-2026-08-15-27 | 2026-08-15 | **JANNE HAUTA, Ministry of Transport and Communications, FINLAND** — "**Cybersecurity strategy of Finland and railways**" (agenda title: "Preparedness and Trust: Finland's Approach to Railway Cybersecurity in an Era of Hybrid Threats"), **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025**; 12 slides. Paraphrased: **(a) THE NIINISTÖ REPORT** — "Report on Preparedness and Readiness of the EU" by **Special Adviser Sauli Niinistö** (Finland's ex-president) to Commission President von der Leyen: all relevant **military and civilian** crisis-response actors must be "fully ready and capable to respond effectively and seamlessly"; a higher level of preparedness is needed across the board. Framing points: "**preparedness is an attitude**"; it "may be misunderstood as a separate policy area"; "**preparing for the risk is not escalatory**"; and "**preparedness requires a high level of TRUST. Not only internally, but with external partners**". Scope: preparing for worst-case scenarios — "pandemic, war of aggression, climate change. **Hybrid, cybersecurity, sabotage**"; "**shifting to comprehensive preparedness: ALL-HAZARD, WHOLE-OF-GOVERNMENT, WHOLE-OF-SOCIETY approach ⇒ under all circumstances**"; citizens at the core, with "**private sector as operators of critical infrastructure and services**"; and "**safer together: threats don't stop at the borders**". **(b) FINNISH COOPERATION STRUCTURE** — national cybersecurity cooperation and preparedness "coordinated by the **Office of the National Cyber Security Director**"; and an **ISAC network of over 300 organisations**, with activities and information sharing "**voluntary based**". **(c) DIGIRAIL — the Finnish national ERTMS programme.** Owned by the **Ministry of Transport and Communications**, but organised as an "**alliance construction**" between the infrastructure manager **FTIA (Finnish Transport Infrastructure Agency)** and the traffic-control operator **Fintraffic Rail Ltd**, with "all main stakeholders involved from day one". Security approach: "**Cybersecurity by Design**", with "railway cybersecurity **standardization as a framework (TS 50701 / draft IEC 63453 [sic — evidently IEC 63452])**"; "active contribution to the **EUG cyber and standardisation working groups**"; and "close co-operation with **NSA** (railway safety, cyber, and resilience)". **SCOPE — the load-bearing detail:** "Digirail is **not merely a railway signalling or interlocking project based on hardware**: impacts extend across the entire railway system" — **rolling stock**; "**Telecommunications (the PRE-FRMCS SOLUTION BASED ON COMMERCIAL RADIO NETWORKS)**"; **traffic management (software)**; and **harmonised operational practices**. Concluding that "digitalization means new type of cybersecurity solutions and preparedness", that Finland is developing "innovative and cyber-secure solutions that can serve as a **model for the broader evolution of Europe's railways**", and that participating companies are "jointly contributing to the creation of unique solutions for the railway market". **(d) MILITARY MOBILITY AND CYBERSECURITY — a dedicated slide:** "EU put more emphasize to military mobility: target to develop the EU's **resilience and defence readiness**"; "**railway cybersecurity is an essential ENABLER of military mobility**"; the open question "**how TSI's should react to cyber threats of digitalized railway infrastructure and ensure OPERATIONAL CAPABILITY IN ALL SITUATIONS**"; "it's **not limited to operational systems**"; "protecting information and **mission-critical data**"; "preparing for hybrid threats and hostile cyber activities". **(e) SUMMARY** — preparedness is attitude (requires trust); cooperation as bedrock; prepare for worst-case; "**railways need new type mindset because digitalisation (HARDWARE TO SOFTWARE)**"; Finland as a model; threats don't stop at borders. No TLP marking observed | A (primary — a serving national ministry describing its own programme, strategy and cooperation structures; **FOUR FLAGS**: **slide-deck form**; **policy/strategy genre** — parts are aspirational ("innovative… model for the broader evolution of Europe's railways") rather than evidential; **two slides are unread figures**; **an in-deck typo**, "IEC 63453" for **IEC 63452**, worth noting because it is the standard the register tracks; **2025-12**, current) | **R4 (pre-FRMCS on commercial radio)** (+ R2 migration; R5; R14/ADR-012 cyber-by-design; **R7 the TSI question**; R3 preparedness; R12 ISAC) | validate | **A — and it materially SHARPENS the register's most important open Finnish datum.** E-2026-08-15-12 recorded Traficom stating "Digirail: **commercial 5G network**, ETCS hybrid level 2-3, ATO GoA2", which upgraded the B-tier "Finnish model" of public-5G slicing (E-2026-06-24-08) to primary — and that row was careful to note **"Digirail ≠ FRMCS"**, because the slide said commercial 5G and ETCS/ATO without mentioning FRMCS. **The Ministry now closes that ambiguity in its own words: Digirail's telecommunications limb is "the PRE-FRMCS SOLUTION BASED ON COMMERCIAL RADIO NETWORKS."** So it is neither FRMCS nor unrelated to it — it is an explicitly **transitional, pre-FRMCS bearer running on commercial networks**, ministry-confirmed. **THAT IS DIRECTLY ON R4's THESIS AND ON A MANDATED TRANSITION PATH ALREADY ON FILE:** UIC's **FRMCS-T transition guideline** contemplates exactly this — FRMCS service over 4G/LTE and **public MNO** transport, with §15 cybersecurity and the observation that public-MNO transport expands the attack surface (E-2026-08-01-24). Digirail now reads as a **live national instance of the FRMCS-T pattern**, alongside 5G-RACOM's field-proven multipath fallback (E-2026-07-02-10/-21) and the DE national-roaming thread (E-2026-07-28-01). For **R4** — "guarantee fail-soft; multi-bearer fallback; public-5G/satellite as a *designed* path", status **Open, primary PoC objective** — this is the strongest evidence yet that a Member State is building rail operations on a commercial bearer as deliberate architecture rather than expedient. **It does NOT discharge R4**, which requires proof by test in this engagement's own PoC (ADR-007); a national programme's design intent is not a demonstrated failover. But it moves the question from "is public-bearer rail credible?" to "**on what terms, and with what proven fallback?**" — and it identifies Finland as the peer to study. **(d) IS THE SECOND MILITARY-MOBILITY FRAMING FROM THIS CONFERENCE, AND THE PAIRING MATTERS.** The Estonian minister framed Rail Baltica as "one of the key corridors for **allied movements** on the eastern flank", a line that "must be able, in a crisis, to carry **NATO supplies**" (E-2026-08-15-24). Finland's ministry now states independently that "**railway cybersecurity is an essential enabler of military mobility**". **Two Member States, same conference, same framing — this is no longer one minister's rhetoric but a shared regional policy position**, and it gives R3/R4 a third justification alongside safety and economic continuity: **defence readiness**. That is likely to matter for funding arguments and for how a client frames availability requirements. **(d) ALSO POSES A DIRECT QUESTION TO THE TSI REGIME THAT THE REGISTER CAN NOW ANSWER PARTLY:** "how TSI's should react to cyber threats of digitalized railway infrastructure and **ensure operational capability in all situations**". The register already holds *why* that question is live — EUG/ESCG established that the CCS TSI subsets' security scope is confined to **cross-border interoperability (RBC-Euroradio) only**, below commonly agreed standards (E-2026-08-15-19), with EPSF and ERA concurring on the thinness (E-2026-08-15-10, -06). **Finland is now attaching a military-mobility rationale to widening that scope** — a new and politically weighty argument for closing the gap, and one bearing on **R7**. **(c)'s GOVERNANCE MODEL IS WORTH NOTING FOR THE CLIENT:** a ministry-owned programme delivered as an **alliance between the infrastructure manager and the traffic-control operator**, with all stakeholders from day one, cybersecurity-by-design anchored on **TS 50701 / IEC 63452**, active participation in **EUG** working groups, and close NSA cooperation on safety, cyber **and resilience** together. Compare the FR/DE/FI/BE supervisory divergence already logged (E-2026-08-15-10/-11/-12/-22): Finland's unified-agency structure appears to extend into unified *programme* governance. **(a) SURFACES A DOCUMENT THE REGISTER DOES NOT HOLD** — the **Niinistö report on EU Preparedness and Readiness**, an EU-level doctrine document commissioned by the Commission President, whose "**all-hazard, whole-of-government, whole-of-society**" and "preparedness is an attitude / preparing for the risk is not escalatory" framing sits directly above the resilience requirements R3/R4 encode, and whose "private sector as operators of critical infrastructure" line is the duty-holder framing NIS-2 and CRA operationalise. **Worth obtaining** — it would give the engagement's resilience case an EU-doctrine anchor it currently lacks. **(b) ADDS A SCALE DATUM TO THE INFORMATION-SHARING THREAD:** a Finnish ISAC network of **over 300 organisations**, voluntary — larger than ER-ISAC's ~50 members (E-2026-08-02-01), and a national counterpart to the sector-level sharing bodies. **(e)'s "HARDWARE TO SOFTWARE" IS THE SAME TRANSITION THIS ENGAGEMENT IS ABOUT**, stated as a mindset problem rather than a technical one — kin to the Estonian CIO's "one system to rule for 40 years vs mix of legacy/modern OT with modern IT/cloud" (E-2026-08-15-26) and to R2/R13's lifecycle tensions. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** **R4 stays Open — primary PoC objective** (evidence base strengthened, nothing proven by test); R2/R5 Accepted; R7 Accepted (the TSI-scope risk recorded at E-2026-08-15-19 now carries an additional rationale for closure, not a status change); R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R3 unchanged; R12 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **Digirail's pre-FRMCS commercial-radio architecture** — the terms, the fallback design, and how it satisfies mission-critical QoS; this is now the clearest peer instance of the R4/FRMCS-T question and the most useful national programme for the engagement to study; (2) the **Niinistö report** on EU preparedness; (3) whether Digirail's "cybersecurity by design" produced published artefacts via the **EUG** working groups; (4) the Finnish ISAC network's rail participation. +| E-2026-08-15-28 | 2026-08-15 | **reuschlaw (German law firm) — "CRA: Practical Insights and FAQ from Corporate Practice", Christoph Callewaert, Attorney-at-Law (Germany), Senior Associate Digital Business**; **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025** (Policy session); 20 slides, of which the final three are firm marketing ("what we do", "what makes us different", office contacts in Berlin and Saarbrücken). Paraphrased: **(a) SCOPE** — *material*: products with digital elements — software products; **hardware products with remote data processing capabilities**; software or hardware components placed on the market separately; "**exceptions for certain regulated products**" (**not enumerated in the deck**). *Territorial*: paid or unpaid making available on the Union market in the course of a commercial activity → "**market location principle**". **(b) FIVE ESSENTIAL MANUFACTURER OBLIGATIONS** — conformity of products with the requirements of **Annex I**; assessment of cybersecurity risks; **provision of (free) security updates**; **reporting obligations for vulnerabilities**; technical documentation plus information and instructions for the user. **(c) TIMELINE** — publication in the Official Journal **20.11.2024**; **entry into force 10.12.2024**; requirements for **conformity assessment bodies 11.06.2026**; **reporting obligations for manufacturers 11.09.2026**; **full applicability of the CRA 11.12.2027**. **(d) THE FAQ — the substance, all of it the presenter's professional reading:** **① product series vs individual product** — "the CRA applies to the **individual product**"; the relevant moment is when the *specific product* was placed on the market, not the series. **② projects vs products, with a rail worked example** — "contracts typically cover entire projects (e.g. **the production of multiple coaches**), with the project starting before December 2027… some coaches being completed before December 2027, and others afterwards. Under the CRA the product is the individual product with digital elements → in this case, **each individual coach**. **If a coach is placed on the market after December 2027, it falls under the CRA; if placed before, it does not.** This makes clear **contractual management** essential." **③ legacy products** — products placed on the market before **11.12.2027** "do not fall under the Regulation… **even if they are made available again at a later point in time**", with the **"exception: subsequent SUBSTANTIAL MODIFICATION of the product"**. **④ placing on the market** = the first making available on the Union market; making available = any supply for distribution, consumption or use in the course of a commercial activity. **⑤ tailor-made products** — "**tailor-made products with digital elements are also subject to the CRA**", but with "**two key exemptions**": deviation from the **secure default configuration**, and deviation from the **obligation to provide free security updates** — each "**requires an individual contractual agreement**". **⑥ spare parts** — the CRA "does not apply to spare parts intended to replace **identical** components… manufactured according to the **same specifications**"; a practical approach is needed for **obsolescence**, where a replacement of the same specifications may still count as identical "**provided the replacement does not affect the product's cybersecurity**"; **exception — substantial modification**: if the change affects conformity with the essential cybersecurity requirements or alters the original intended purpose, the spare part **does** fall under the CRA. **⑦ third-party components** — "the key is **contractual control across the supply chain**", with typical clauses listed as: assurances of cybersecurity compliance (**Annex I** essential requirements); obligations to support risk management (**documentation, testing, metrics**); **binding processes for security updates and vulnerability notifications**; **mandatory SBOM** (complete software bill of materials); and obligations to **cooperate with authorities** in case of inquiries. No TLP marking observed | **B** — professional/promotional: this is **expert SECONDARY commentary on the Regulation, not the Regulation**, delivered by a firm whose final three slides solicit business. Follows the register's precedent for industry CRA readings (E-2026-07-01-05, VDB CRA-Leitfaden, tiered **B** and then verified against primary law by E-2026-07-01-06). **CONFIDENCE-RAISER worth recording:** the timeline at (c) — **reporting obligations 11.09.2026** and **full applicability 11.12.2027** — **matches the register's EUR-Lex-verified dates exactly** (E-2026-07-01-06, re-validated verbatim E-2026-07-10-02, and carried in ADR-012's legal basis). Independent agreement on the checkable facts raises confidence in the interpretive material — **but does not substitute for it**: every FAQ answer below is the presenter's reading and **must be verified against Reg (EU) 2024/2847 before being relied on**. Further flags: **slide-deck**; **no article citations** given for any FAQ answer; **German-law firm perspective**; **2025-12**, current | **R14/ADR-012** (+ **R13 fleet programmes**; **R2/R1 legacy, retrofit & spares**; R8/PR13 supply chain) | validate | **B by tier, but the most PRACTICALLY USEFUL CRA material in the register — it converts ADR-012's legal basis into operational boundaries, and three of its answers land directly on live requirements.** **① ON THE FOUNDATIONAL QUESTION (E-2026-08-15-20): further strong indication that rail is NOT excluded, though still not textual proof.** The scope slide notes "exceptions for certain regulated products" **without enumerating them** — so it does not close the exclusion-list check. But a German product-compliance firm presenting *practical CRA compliance* to a **rail** audience, working its examples through **railway coaches**, is not consistent with rail being exempt. Read with the sector-wide CRA-application session at the same conference (E-2026-08-15-23), the risk that ADR-012's product-side foundation is void is now **low**. **The EUR-Lex verification remains the closing step** and should still be done. **② THE DECEMBER-2027 BOUNDARY RUNS THROUGH THE MIDDLE OF FLEET PROGRAMMES — a genuinely new constraint for R13.** Because the CRA attaches to the **individual product** at the moment *it* is placed on the market (①), a multi-year delivery contract straddling 11.12.2027 produces **some units in scope and some not, within one programme** — the presenter's own example is coaches. R13 concerns FRMCS retrofit across **16-21k German vehicles plus ~40k mobile / ~3.5k stationary GSM-R devices by 2035** (ADR-009), i.e. exactly the kind of long-running, unit-by-unit programme this rule bisects. **Consequence for the engagement: CRA applicability must be tracked per vehicle/unit against its placing-on-market date, and the contracts must say so** — the presenter's "clear contractual management is essential" is the right conclusion and is currently nowhere in ADR-009 or ADR-012. **③ "SUBSTANTIAL MODIFICATION" IS THE HINGE FOR THE ENTIRE RETROFIT PROGRAMME — and it creates a TWO-TEST problem the register has not previously identified.** Under ③, pre-2027 products stay out of scope *even when re-supplied later* — **unless substantially modified**. A GSM-R→FRMCS retrofit is, on its face, a candidate substantial modification of an existing vehicle: it changes the communication bearer, adds digital elements, and plausibly affects cybersecurity conformity. **If retrofits count, the retrofitted fleet is pulled INTO CRA scope** — which would be a material, unbudgeted expansion of ADR-012's perimeter and directly relevant to R2's decade-plus coexistence and R13's retrofit economics. **And note the collision:** the same retrofit already faces the **CSM-RA Art 4(2) "significant change"** test on the safety side (E-2026-06-28-06, ADR-011). So **one physical modification may trigger two independent change-significance assessments under different legal regimes with different criteria and different assessors** — safety (CSM-RA / AsBo) and product security (CRA / substantial modification). ADR-012's "one gate for change" principle and ADR-011's change classes were designed for the safety limb; **this is the security-product limb, and it is not yet in either ADR.** Strongest new ADR-012 material of the day. **④ TAILOR-MADE PRODUCTS ARE IN SCOPE — WITH TWO CONTRACTUAL LEVERS THAT MATTER TO THE 30-YEAR PROBLEM.** Rail systems are overwhelmingly bespoke, and ⑤ confirms tailor-made products are covered — but permits contractual deviation from the **secure default configuration** and, more significantly, from the **obligation to provide free security updates**. That second lever speaks directly to UNIFE's central complaint that rail product lives exceed 30 years against a support period framed as five-years-or-lifetime (E-2026-08-15-20), and to ADR-012's own *Harder* consequence about long-life assets lacking conformant vendor support. **The lever cuts both ways and the engagement should be alert to it: a supplier may seek to contract OUT of free updates for bespoke rail products, which is precisely the outcome ADR-012's procurement gate exists to prevent.** Recommend that ADR-012 action 2 explicitly address whether such deviations are acceptable and on what compensating terms. **⑤ SPARE PARTS AND OBSOLESCENCE (⑥) TOUCH R1/PR14.** Identical-specification spares are out of scope *provided the replacement does not affect cybersecurity* — but obsolescence forces non-identical substitution, and the register already tracks GSM-R obsolescence with dwindling spares and a shrinking supplier base (R1; the 1991-node case at ADR-012's consequences; PR13/PR14). **So the obsolescence path itself can drag legacy equipment into CRA scope** at exactly the moment the vendor base is least able to support it. Worth flagging to the client. **⑥ (⑦) IS ADR-012 ACTION ITEM 2, DRAFTED.** ADR-012 already requires "manufacturer CRA evidence (SBOM, security-update + support-period commitment, CVD contact) as a bid condition". The clause list here is fuller and professionally framed — **Annex I compliance assurances · risk-management support (documentation, testing, metrics) · binding security-update and vulnerability-notification processes · mandatory complete SBOM · authority-cooperation obligations** — and it is directly reusable as the skeleton of the procurement clause, subject to legal review. It also complements the 5G-core-specific control list already attached to that action item (E-2026-07-24-01, E-2026-07-26-14). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays **Open** (ADR-012 Proposed — its CRA dates are corroborated and its premise better supported, but nothing here changes a status); R13 Open · ADR-009 unchanged; R2/R1 Accepted; R8 Recommended. No matrix change, no ADR change — **though this is the strongest candidate on the board for ADR-012 additions**: the individual-product/December-2027 boundary, the substantial-modification-versus-CSM-RA two-test problem, the tailor-made free-updates deviation, and the drafted supply-chain clause list. Offered, not made. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **verify "substantial modification" against Reg (EU) 2024/2847** and determine whether a GSM-R→FRMCS retrofit qualifies — this is now the single most consequential open CRA question for R13/R2, above the exclusion-list check; (2) **verify the exclusion list** (still open from E-2026-08-15-09/-20); (3) confirm the tailor-made exemptions and their limits; (4) the **11.06.2026 conformity-assessment-body milestone** is new to the register — check it against the adopted text and add to ADR-012's timeline if correct. +| E-2026-08-15-29 | 2026-08-15 | **tmc (Track Machines Connected) — "Enhancing Railway Resilience through Secure Software Development: Practical Approach to Cyber Resilience Act Compliance"**; presenter per the agenda **M. Wachert-Rabl** (named in-deck as **CISO**); **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025**; 18 slides, marked **PUBLIC**. Company self-description: founded **2019**, **117 employees**, sites in Austria/India/USA, "the IT branch of world market leader **Plasser & Theurer**"; product **tmOS** for track-machine automation, track geometry and point clouds, addressing "lifecycle management, obsolescence and cybersecurity"; portfolio spans assistance systems for track machines, connected systems for fleet operation, and digital systems for track surveying. Claims compliance provision for **NIS-2, CRA, EU Radio Equipment Directive, EU Data Act, GDPR, EU AI Act**, and products "on track for compliance with **IEC 62443, TS 50701**"; ISO 27001 certified. Paraphrased: **(a) A THREE-STEP PATH TO CRA COMPLIANCE.** **1. Product classification** ("identify and cluster products"). **2. Security processes** — *A. risk assessment*: "**SW splitting in security zones**", risk assessment via **threat modelling**; *A(2). CRA product requirements*: "reset function, user management, encryption, **logging**, **DoS resilience**, automatic updates, etc."; *B. design, development & production*: "**security-by-default (e.g. V-Cycle and IEC 62443-4-1)**, security zones", "limit attack surface, increase availability", and testing comprising "**vulnerability scans, fuzz testing, overloads, pen-testing**"; *C. vulnerability handling*: "identify & document SW components & vulnerabilities (**via SBOM**)", "standardized process for updates", "**public disclosure of fixed vulnerabilities and incident reporting**". **3. Documentation** — *A. technical documentation*: intended purpose, essential functionalities, **system architecture**, drawings/overview, functionality & security test reports & vulnerability handling, **single point of contact**, "**support end & lifecycle (min. 5 years)**", manual for installing updates & patches, "**SBOM (in a stand-alone document)**", cybersecurity risk and measures, threat modelling, and "**different client architectures must be considered**"; *B. information & instructions to the user*: "an excerpt of the technical documentation must be made available to the customers — less comprehensive & less confidential"; *C. conformity assessment*: "**I. self assessment: for most products, a self-assessment confirms CRA compliancy**"; "**II. third-party assessment: high security risk products must be assessed by a 3rd party**"; "**III. declaration of conformity = CE marking**". **(b) NAMED OPEN-SOURCE CRA TOOLING** — *risk assessment (**STRIDE** methodology)*: Microsoft Threat Modelling Tool, **OWASP Threat Dragon**, Draw.io; *vulnerability management*: SBOM creation via **CycloneDX (OWASP)**, tracking/analysis of CVSS within software components via **Dependency-Track (OWASP)**; *process documentation*: Docmost, BookStack, LibreOffice. **(c) SBOM SPECIFICS** — the CRA definition quoted ("a formal record containing details and supply chain relationships of components included in the software elements of a product with digital elements"), and three operational rules: "**at least the FIRST LEVEL must be documented (direct dependencies)**"; "provided in a **common machine-readable format (SPDX, CycloneDX etc.)**"; "**updated during the product lifecycle or for 5 years**". A CycloneDX JSON component example is shown with **cpe** (vulnerability matching), **purl**, **omniborId** (GitOID source integrity), **swhid** (Software Heritage) and **swid** identifiers. **(d) CI/CD INTEGRATION** — SBOM generated inside the CI pipeline (coding → code repository → build → **SBOM** → unit test → **static code scan** → artifact repository → deploy to test → acceptance test → deploy to staging → deploy to production), with **vulnerability management spanning the entire DevOps cycle**; Dependency-Track shown as SBOM production → analysis → vulnerability streams → investigation → continuous monitoring → vulnerability response. **(e) THE CANDID CLOSING QUESTIONS** — "vulnerabilities must actively be tracked and alerted throughout the whole DevOps cycle"; "**How can manufacturers guarantee vulnerability management over the whole product lifecycle?**"; "**100% accurate SBOMs are extremely challenging — what SBOM quality is sufficient?**" READ: complete extractable text (18 slides; several Dependency-Track screenshots are images, not read) | Vendor conference presentation (supplier describing its own CRA-compliance implementation) | file "session 3-2 - tmc - pratical approach to cra compliance in railway systems.pdf", Download folder; md5 ab224c7e…c116; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025; slides marked **PUBLIC** | **B — vendor-promotional.** Unambiguous on the register's definition ("vendor-primary — true but promotional"): a supplier deck opening with company statistics and portfolio slides, asserting its own compliance across six EU instruments, and closing "Power to the railways". The technical content is specific and credible, but it is **also a capability pitch**, and its compliance claims are **self-declared and unaudited here**. Further flags: **track-machine domain** — tmc's products are track maintenance/surveying software, **not signalling, CCS or FRMCS**, so the criticality context differs from the vital layer; **interpretive claims about the CRA are the vendor's reading**, not law (contrast the EUR-Lex-verified rows); **2025-12**, current | **R14/ADR-012** (+ **R8/PR13 SBOM & supply chain**; ADR-011 update processes; ADR-007 testing; R11/ADR-004 security zones) | validate | **B by tier — but the most OPERATIONALLY CONCRETE CRA material in the register, and the natural complement to the lawyer's view logged alongside it.** E-2026-08-15-28 (reuschlaw, also B) gives the **legal boundaries** — individual-product scope, the December-2027 line, substantial modification, tailor-made exemptions. **This gives the ENGINEERING IMPLEMENTATION** — how a manufacturer actually discharges Annex I in a software organisation. Read as a pair, they turn ADR-012's legal basis into something a procurement team could specify against. **① IT OPERATIONALISES ADR-012 ACTION ITEMS 1 AND 2 WITH UNUSUAL SPECIFICITY.** Action 1 is "CRA PDE inventory — list every product with digital elements; class by CRA product category" — which is exactly step 1, "product classification (identify and cluster products)". Action 2 requires "manufacturer CRA evidence (SBOM, security-update + support-period commitment, CVD contact) as a bid condition" — and (a)3A supplies the **precise documentation set a compliant manufacturer should already hold**: system architecture, security test reports, **single point of contact** (the CVD contact), **support end & lifecycle**, update/patch installation manual, **SBOM as a stand-alone document**, threat modelling. **A procurement gate can simply ask for that list.** **② THE SBOM RULES AT (c) ARE THE MOST USEFUL SINGLE ITEM, BECAUSE ADR-012 CURRENTLY SAYS "SBOM" WITHOUT SPECIFYING IT.** Three concrete parameters appear here and are new to this register: **depth** — "at least the first level (direct dependencies)"; **format** — machine-readable, **SPDX or CycloneDX**; **duration** — "updated during the product lifecycle **or for 5 years**". Note that **CycloneDX, SPDX and Dependency-Track have zero prior occurrences in this log** — the register has required SBOMs since ADR-012 was drafted without naming a format or a depth rule. **Recommend ADR-012 action 2 adopt format and depth explicitly**, subject to verifying the depth rule against Annex I of Reg (EU) 2024/2847, since "first level only" is a materially weaker commitment than a transitive SBOM and a supplier will read it in its own favour. **③ (e) IS THE MOST HONEST SLIDE AND THE MOST USEFUL TO THE ENGAGEMENT — a vendor naming two unsolved problems that are precisely ADR-012's exposure.** "**How can manufacturers guarantee vulnerability management over the whole product lifecycle?**" is the 30-plus-year rail asset life against a five-year support floor — the same gap UNIFE raised from the supply side (E-2026-08-15-20), that reuschlaw showed can be *contracted around* for tailor-made products (E-2026-08-15-28 ⑤), and that ADR-012 already lists under *Harder*. **A supplier volunteering that it cannot guarantee this is stronger evidence of the gap than any amount of operator complaint.** And "**100% accurate SBOMs are extremely challenging — what SBOM quality is sufficient?**" identifies a live acceptance-criterion problem: **a procurement clause that demands an SBOM without defining sufficiency is unenforceable in practice.** That is a concrete drafting risk for ADR-012 action 2 and should be resolved before the clause is issued. **④ THE SELF-ASSESSMENT DEFAULT MATTERS FOR PROCUREMENT POSTURE.** "For most products, a **self-assessment** confirms CRA compliancy; high security risk products must be assessed by a **3rd party**" is consistent with the ~90%/10% split DG CNECT presented in the proposal (E-2026-08-15-09) — but it means **the default assurance a supplier offers will be its own declaration plus CE marking**, not independent assessment. For FRMCS elements supplied to a NIS-2 essential entity, the engagement should decide **contractually** whether self-assessment is acceptable or whether third-party assessment is required regardless of category — a question ADR-012 action 2 does not currently address. **⑤ THE TESTING AND ZONING VOCABULARY LINES UP WITH WORK ALREADY SCOPED:** "vulnerability scans, **fuzz testing**, overloads, **pen-testing**" is an ADR-007 test-surface list from the manufacturer side, complementing SNCF's in-housed pen-testing (E-2026-08-15-15) and the academic ERTMS pen-testing at the 4th conference (E-2026-08-15-22); "**SW splitting in security zones**" and **IEC 62443-4-1** secure-development extend the zones/conduits chain into the *software process* layer; and "automatic updates" plus a "standardized process for updates" is the change-stream ADR-011 must govern. **⑥ ONE FLAG WORTH RAISING SEPARATELY: the EU RADIO EQUIPMENT DIRECTIVE appears in tmc's compliance list and is ABSENT from this register** (zero occurrences of "Radio Equipment Directive"). For an engagement whose subject is a **radio** system, the RED — and specifically its delegated act on cybersecurity for radio equipment — is a plausible additional product-law obligation on FRMCS terminals and radio modules alongside the CRA. **Worth checking whether RED applies to FRMCS cab radios/modems and how it interacts with the CRA** — a possible third instrument beside CRA and NIS-2, and a candidate ADR-012 scope question. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R8 Recommended; ADR-011/ADR-007/ADR-004 unchanged. No matrix change, no ADR change — **but ADR-012 action 2 has three concrete additions waiting here** (SBOM format/depth/duration; self-assessment-versus-third-party posture; SBOM sufficiency criterion), offered not made. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **verify the SBOM depth rule ("first level / direct dependencies")** against Annex I of Reg (EU) 2024/2847 before adopting it in a clause; (2) **the EU Radio Equipment Directive's cybersecurity delegated act — does it reach FRMCS radio equipment?** (new, and it may be a register gap on the scale of the CER Directive); (3) whether the register should name **CycloneDX/SPDX** as accepted SBOM formats and **Dependency-Track** as a reference tool in ADR-012.
