+| E-2026-08-15-30 | 2026-08-15 | **CRSG (CYBERSECURITY RAIL SECTOR GROUP) — "Applying the CRA in rail: A sector-wide approach — KEY GUIDANCE FROM THE CYBERSECURITY RAIL SECTOR GROUP"**, **Amelia Alder** (Knorr-Bremse, for the rail supply industry) and **Quentin Rivette** (SNCF, for CER & EIM); 11 slides; **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025**. **This identifies the body flagged as unknown at E-2026-08-15-23.** Paraphrased: **(a) WHAT CRSG IS** — "a joint group of the **Railway Operating Community, the Rail Supply Industry and Urban Rail**"; "teaming up as a **unified sector** to improve cybersecurity for rail in Europe"; "an initiative planned to go beyond — **more than 20 other topics identified**". **(b) THE GUIDANCE AND ITS STATUS** — "expert guidance on the implementation of the CRA in railways", aiming at "a **common understanding** of requirements and implementation approach", a "**system-level approach and progressivity**", and "**a living document**" — but explicitly "**still under discussion and PENDING APPROVAL by the sector**", with "validation & publication **v1.0 expected for 2026**". **(c) KEY ASPECTS — contextualisation and precisions:** "**Spare Parts are EXCLUDED from CRA application**"; "**Substantial Modifications TRIGGER the need to comply with the CRA**"; "**Tailor-Made vs COTS: Tailor-Made products are allowed non-secure configurations and CHARGEABLE PATCHES**". *Combination with existing parts and in-progress projects:* how to apply the CRA with a **system-level approach**; "**progressivity for components within a system: priority to the new/redesigned & CYBER-CRITICAL ASSETS (CCA – IEC 63452)**"; "**system extension** (adding a new section to a line, adding a new coach to a train…) **managed at the interface**". *Responsibility and mutual agreement:* "**accountability for demonstration of CRA compliance and CE marking belongs to the MANUFACTURER**"; "**earliest transparency (status, criteria, risk acceptability, SecRACs…) achieved via MUTUAL AGREEMENT with the customer (asset owner)**". **(d) "WHAT IS A PRODUCT?" — a layered regulatory model, cited as Fig 1 from IEC 63452:** **NIS-2** attaches at the **OPERATOR** level ("CRA product in operation under the NIS2 directive, including organisation — supply chain, incident management…"); **CRA** attaches at the **SYSTEM INTEGRATOR** and **PRODUCT SUPPLIER** levels. Product granularity given as: **systems** (interlocking, rolling stock…) · **sub-systems** (**RBC**, TCMS, platform screen doors…) · **sets of components** (CCTV, door system control…) · **components** (switch, router, PLC, IHM, sensors, camera, recorder…). **(e) FOUR WORKED "PROGRESSIVITY AND PRIORITISATION" EXAMPLES** around the **11/12/2027** line, with a common legend: *CRA compliance not required* (pre-existing component/subsystem/system — "initial baseline") · *CRA compliance "**on the basis of the (system) risk assessment**" — may be used "as is" or with additional mitigating measures inside or around it in the sub-system architecture ⇒ **conditions of use (e.g. SecRACs)*** · *increase of cybersecurity ⇒ "**new baseline**"* · *CRA compliance "**by design**" — designed, developed and produced so as to ensure an appropriate level of cybersecurity* — and, across all four, "**ALWAYS with acceptable risks at system level**". **Example 1**: components, sub-system and system all placed on the market before 11/12/2027 → **CRA compliance not required**. **Example 2**: COMP A and COMP B pre-date the line (no CRA needed for them) but the **sub-system and system are delivered after** → **CRA compliance required for both**; achieved by adapting **COMP B to a new baseline** that raises cybersecurity, letting the (sub-)system reach **compliance at system level**, while **COMP A continues "as is" with additional mitigating measures within it (e.g. configuration) or around it (better architecture — e.g. better SEGREGATION)**, its **conditions of use specified via SecRACs**. **Example 3**: everything after the line → CRA required for all; COMP B compliant **by design**; **COMP A "CRA compliant through CONDITIONS OF USE (Annex 2)"**. **Example 4**: everything after the line and both components adapted to new baselines → both **compliant by design**. **(f) KEY TAKEAWAYS** — expert guidance supports understanding and implementation; "**security-by-design is the key principle for every product development**"; "**communication between manufacturers and railways is essential for transition**". **(g) SYSTEM PILLAR CYBERSECURITY SPECIFICATIONS** — intended to support compliance with standards and regulations, support **interoperability**, reduce development effort and reduce time to market; annotated "**CCS for now and may be more in the future**". **(h) THE PATH FORWARD — open topics listed:** scope of application · **substantial modification** · **tailor made** · **support period** · **spare parts** · **system extensions** · use cases · **SBOMs**; "draft to be approved; validation & publication v1.0 expected for **2026**". READ: complete extractable text (11 slides; the four progressivity diagrams read via their labels and per-example annotations) | Sector-body conference presentation (a joint operators + supply-industry + urban-rail group presenting its own draft guidance) | file "session 3-3 - crsg - applying cra in rail - sector wide approach .pdf", Download folder; md5 164eeddc…62c3; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01.12.2025 | A (primary as to CRSG's existence, composition, remit and the guidance's content and status — a joint sector body describing its own work, in the manner the register A-tiers UIC and CER material; **FOUR FLAGS, the first decisive**: **the guidance is a DRAFT, "still under discussion and pending approval by the sector", v1.0 not expected until 2026** — so **nothing in (c)-(e) is settled sector position, let alone law**; **interested-party membership** — the supply industry co-authors guidance on obligations that fall on it, and the tailor-made and spare-parts readings are favourable to suppliers; **it is guidance ON the Regulation, not the Regulation** — every interpretation needs checking against Reg (EU) 2024/2847; **slide-deck**, with the four diagrams read via labels) | **R14/ADR-012** (+ **R13/R2 the migration boundary**; **R11/ADR-004 SecRACs & segregation**; R8/PR13 manufacturer accountability) | validate | **A — and the most consequential CRA artefact in the register, on two counts: it settles the foundational question, and it supplies the migration method.** **① THE FOUNDATIONAL QUESTION FROM E-2026-08-15-20 IS NOW SETTLED IN PRACTICE: RAIL IS IN SCOPE OF THE CRA, AND THE SECTOR HAS ORGANISED TO COMPLY.** In 12/2022 UNIFE argued that "**the rail sector should be excluded from the scope of the CRA proposal**". Three years later the **rail supply industry itself — represented here by Knorr-Bremse — is co-authoring sector-wide guidance on HOW TO APPLY the CRA in rail**, jointly with the operating community (CER & EIM, represented by SNCF) and urban rail. **A sector does not build a joint implementation body, a living guidance document and four worked compliance examples for a regulation it has been exempted from.** Taken with the same conference's CRA compliance session (E-2026-08-15-23), a law firm working CRA examples through railway coaches (E-2026-08-15-28) and a supplier implementing CRA processes (E-2026-08-15-29), **the risk that ADR-012's product-side premise is void is now effectively closed.** The textual check against Reg (EU) 2024/2847 remains worth completing for the record — but it is no longer a risk to the ADR, and **ADR-012's legal basis should be regarded as sound.** **② IT IDENTIFIES THE BODY AND CONFIRMS THE CONVERGENCE ARC.** E-2026-08-15-23 flagged "identify the Cybersecurity Rail Sector Group" as an open follow-up; **CRSG is the formalisation of the UNIFE+CER+EIM joint effort first seen at the 4th conference (E-2026-08-15-22), now extended to include Urban Rail** and scoped to "**more than 20 other topics**". The arc is complete and worth stating to a client: **2022 resistance (UNIFE alone) → 2024 joint effort (UNIFE+CER+EIM) → 2025 a standing joint body with a work programme.** Note **Quentin Rivette's third appearance** — SNCF rolling-stock security (E-2026-08-15-15, TLP:AMBER), the 5th-conference agenda, and now representing CER & EIM — a small sector, and a caution when counting sources as independent. **③ (c) CONFIRMS THE ANALYSIS RECORDED AT E-2026-08-15-28 — "SUBSTANTIAL MODIFICATIONS TRIGGER THE NEED TO COMPLY WITH THE CRA."** That row identified substantial modification as "the hinge for the entire retrofit programme" and reasoned that a GSM-R→FRMCS retrofit is a candidate substantial modification. **The sector group now states the trigger flatly.** The consequence for **R13** stands and sharpens: if the FRMCS retrofit of an existing vehicle is a substantial modification, **the retrofitted fleet enters CRA scope**, with the two-test problem previously flagged (CRA substantial modification alongside CSM-RA Art 4(2) significant change) unresolved in both ADR-011 and ADR-012. **"Substantial modification" is on CRSG's own open-topics list (h), so the sector has not settled it either** — which means the engagement cannot simply adopt an answer and should track CRSG v1.0. **④ (e) IS THE GENUINELY VALUABLE PART — A WORKED METHOD FOR A MIXED LEGACY/NEW ESTATE ACROSS THE 2027 BOUNDARY, WHICH IS PRECISELY THE FRMCS MIGRATION SHAPE.** The mechanism: compliance is demonstrated **at SYSTEM level**, not component-by-component; **legacy components may remain "as is" provided they are compensated** by mitigating measures **inside** them (configuration) or **around** them (better architecture, "e.g. better **SEGREGATION**"); the compensation is documented as **conditions of use / SecRACs**; and **prioritisation goes to new or redesigned components and to Cyber-Critical Assets (CCA, an IEC 63452 concept)** — all "**always with acceptable risks at system level**". **This is directly transferable to R2's decade-plus GSM-R↔FRMCS coexistence and to R13's fleet retrofit**, and it is the first method on file for reconciling a long migration with a hard regulatory date. It also answers, from the product-law side, the same problem ESCG solved from the ERTMS side with compensating measures and per-phase risk analysis (E-2026-08-15-19). **⑤ "SecRACs" ARE NEW TO THIS REGISTER AND MATTER TO ADR-004.** Security-Related Application Conditions are the security analogue of the **SRACs** (Safety-Related Application Conditions) that qualify a CENELEC safety case — the documented conditions under which the argument holds. Here they are the vehicle by which a non-compliant legacy component is rendered acceptable at system level. **ADR-004's whole structure is an argument that holds under stated conditions (segregation, unidirectional boundary, human-in-command), and ADR-012 must govern security changes without breaking it — SecRACs are the existing sector vocabulary for exactly that, and the engagement should adopt the term rather than invent one.** The appearance of "better **segregation**" as the canonical compensating measure is further, independent support for ADR-004's Option-A segregation preference. **⑥ (d) CONFIRMS ADR-012's CRA/NIS-2 SPLIT AND GIVES IT A LAYERING.** ADR-012 asserts "CRA — product side… NIS-2 — operator side"; CRSG draws exactly that boundary — **NIS-2 at the operator (including organisation, supply chain, incident management); CRA at the system integrator and product supplier** — and adds a four-level product granularity (**system → sub-system → set of components → component**) with **RBC named as a sub-system** and switches/routers/PLCs as components. **That granularity is directly usable for ADR-012 action item 1 (the PDE inventory), which currently has no agreed decomposition level.** Note it is credited to **Fig 1 of IEC 63452** — the successor standard the register already tracks (E-2026-08-01-17/-20) — so the model has a standards home. **⑦ CAUTIONS ON THE SUPPLIER-FAVOURABLE READINGS.** "**Spare parts are excluded**" is stated more absolutely here than reuschlaw's more careful formulation, which preserved the substantial-modification exception and the "does not affect the product's cybersecurity" condition (E-2026-08-15-28 ⑥); and "**tailor-made products are allowed non-secure configurations and CHARGEABLE PATCHES**" is the plain-language form of contracting out of the free-security-updates obligation. **Both readings favour the supply industry, which co-authors this guidance, and both remain open topics on CRSG's own list (h).** For a rail estate that is overwhelmingly tailor-made and increasingly obsolescence-driven, **"chargeable patches" is a material commercial exposure over a 30-40 year life** — and ADR-012 action 2 should state a position on whether the engagement accepts such deviations and on what compensating terms. **⑧ (h) SHOWS THE SECTOR'S OWN UNRESOLVED LIST**, which overlaps the register's: scope, substantial modification, tailor-made, **support period**, spare parts, system extensions and **SBOMs** — the last being precisely the sufficiency question tmc raised the same day ("what SBOM quality is sufficient?", E-2026-08-15-29). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION** — but it **closes a flagged risk**: R14 stays **Open** and ADR-012 stays **Proposed**, yet ADR-012's CRA premise, which E-2026-08-15-20 put in question, should now be treated as **sound**. R13/R2 unchanged in status, with the substantial-modification exposure sharpened; R11/ADR-004 Proposed; R8 Recommended. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **CRSG guidance v1.0 when published (expected 2026)** — this is now a standing watch item and the single most useful forthcoming document for ADR-012; (2) **verify "substantial modification" against Reg (EU) 2024/2847** and settle whether an FRMCS retrofit qualifies — unresolved by the sector, so the engagement needs its own reasoned position; (3) **IEC 63452's product model and the Cyber-Critical Asset (CCA) concept** — adopt for ADR-012 action 1 if it holds; (4) whether **SecRACs** have a standards definition (IEC 63452? TS 50701?) and how they interact with EN 50129 SRACs — an ADR-004 question. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. KIN: **E-2026-08-15-20 (UNIFE's exclusion demand — the arc this closes)**, **E-2026-08-15-28 (reuschlaw — substantial modification and tailor-made, confirmed here)**, **E-2026-08-15-29 (tmc — SBOM sufficiency, also on CRSG's open list)**, E-2026-08-15-22/-23 (the joint effort and the CRA session), E-2026-08-15-19 (ESCG's compensating-measures method — the ERTMS analogue of (e)), E-2026-08-01-17/-20 (IEC 63452 — source of the product model and CCA), E-2026-08-15-15 (Rivette/SNCF), E-2026-07-01-06 / E-2026-07-10-02 (the verified CRA), ADR-012 (legal basis; actions 1-2), ADR-011 (change classes), ADR-004 (SRACs/SecRACs; segregation), ADR-009/R13, R14/R2/R8 |

7 slides. **Same officer who was the named contact on the 2022 Traficom deck (E-2026-08-15-12) that reported Finland staffing railway cybersecurity at "~1" person.** Paraphrased: **(a) RATIONALE** — "railway **NSAs can—and should—promote cybersecurity**"; "**variations in railway cybersecurity competence create a SYSTEMIC RISK**"; an online course "can reduce the risk by increasing the understanding" and "provides a clear **starting point for continuous improvement**" since "technology, people, organisations, regulation and best practices change"; and "a good-quality online course can be delivered at a **low cost**". **(b) OBJECTIVES AND AUDIENCE** — increase the number of professionals with **railway-specific** cybersecurity competence, and strengthen the skills of existing railway staff; **primary target group: cybersecurity experts and students with NO prior experience in the railway domain**; secondary: railway professionals interested in cybersecurity. **(c) RESULTS — with figures:** "the total budget is **EUR ~35,000 + VAT**, in addition to in-house work"; "**November 2025 around 300 students had enrolled**"; "**November 2025 over 150 students had completed the course**". **(d) CONTENT** — fundamentals of cybersecurity, the railway system and railway technology; cybersecurity governance in organisations; **technical railway cybersecurity (new projects, RADIO NETWORKS)**; railway cybersecurity in the EU; the **threat landscape for the Finnish railway sector**; in-depth **regulatory framework (NIS2, CRA, national law and guidance)**; overview of **standardisation (TS 50701, draft IEC 63452, IEC 62443 series)**; with a stated limitation — "**public level information can't contain in-depth information about technologies, vulnerabilities, solutions or processes**". **(e) TIMELINE 2023-2026** — *2023*: idea, budget proposal, cooperation proposal to the **National Emergency Supply Agency**, concept presented to universities and the private sector. *2024*: background discussions, drafting and publishing a **public procurement RFP**, funding agreement, tenders and decision, procurement contract, kick-off, stakeholder survey; **October — ERA-ENISA Conference presentations on trainings**; "**ideas from the Spanish online course**"; December seminar recorded. *2025*: pilot material February, **1st pilot course March**, feedback July, annual update cycle planned August, **2nd round September**, "**IEC CDV 63452 commenting integrated with course material**" (September), "**CRA webinar integrated with course material development**" (November), material update finalised December. *2026*: **3rd round January (updated), 4th round September, and course ownership handed over to the university in December**. **(f) HOW TO IMPROVE THE MODEL** — *Finland*: hand over to the university Q4/2026 with annual maintenance; record online events; and a proposed expansion "to cover **both physical and digital preparedness** for the railway sector", noting "Finland has the long tradition on railway resilience". *EU*: "need for railway cybersecurity competence"; "**an EU-wide, ENGLISH-LANGUAGE online course on railway cybersecurity is FEASIBLE**"; open question on hosting/maintaining the learning platform; and a **practical proposal** — "the **ERA-ENISA Railway Cybersecurity Conferences as the foundation?** Streamed, recorded, and integrated into study materials? **Voluntary contributions?**" **(g) ACKNOWLEDGED CONTRIBUTORS** — Mipro, WSP Finland, IC Security, **FTIA**, **Fintraffic**, **Digirail**, Reformo networks, Metropolitan Area Transport Ltd, **Cylus**, **Siemens Mobility**, **ENISA**. R13 programme feasibility) | validate | **A — and this is the thread's first COSTED, MEASURED remedy, which makes it disproportionately useful.** The capacity constraint is the most consistently attested finding in this entire conference corpus: ENISA attributed low detection maturity to "lack of expertise and resources… even more pronounced for OT" (E-2026-08-15-06); EPSF reported 22 of 106 staff trained (E-2026-08-15-10); **Traficom reported staffing railway cybersecurity at "~1" person** (E-2026-08-15-12); EDA urged "appropriate resources" (E-2026-08-15-14); ENISA's investment survey **measured** it — transport second-lowest information-security spend of any NIS sector, **median 7 IS FTEs**, both budget and FTE shares falling year on year (E-2026-08-15-16); a national CERT hedged that it "helps with incident response **where they can**" (E-2026-08-15-25); and the Estonian minister named teams that are "**exhausted, understaffed**" (E-2026-08-15-24). **Here is the same official who reported the "~1 person" figure, three years later, presenting a delivered intervention: ~EUR 35,000, around 300 enrolled, over 150 completed.** For a client conversation about staffing an oversight function, that cost-to-throughput ratio is the most actionable datum in the corpus. **(a)'s FRAMING IS WORTH ADOPTING: "VARIATIONS IN RAILWAY CYBERSECURITY COMPETENCE CREATE A SYSTEMIC RISK."** That is the capacity problem stated as a *risk* rather than a resourcing complaint — and it is precisely the register's concern in a different guise: an interoperable, cross-border system is only as strong as its least competent participant, which is the same logic as the FR/DE/FI/BE supervisory divergence (E-2026-08-15-10/-11/-12/-22) and the EUG finding that under-specification drives "**specific solutions per operator**" (E-2026-08-15-19). **Competence variance is a third divergence vector alongside supervisory competence and technical specification.** **(b) POINTS THE PIPELINE THE RIGHT WAY FOR THIS ENGAGEMENT.** The primary target is **cyber experts and students with no rail background** — i.e. importing cybersecurity talent *into* rail, rather than only upskilling rail staff. That matters for R10 and R12: the oversight layer this engagement proposes needs people who can act on a Risk Sentinel's alerts, and ADR-004's **human-in-command** gate is only as strong as the humans staffing it. **An oversight design that assumes competent, available supervisors must state that assumption; this row is evidence the sector is only now building that pipeline.** **(d) IS DIRECTLY RELEVANT IN CONTENT, NOT JUST IN EXISTENCE** — the syllabus includes **technical railway cybersecurity covering RADIO NETWORKS**, the **NIS2/CRA regulatory framework in depth**, and **TS 50701 / draft IEC 63452 / IEC 62443** standardisation. That is close to the competence profile ADR-012's implementation and ADR-007's test programme would require of client staff. Note the honest scope limit at (d): a public course "can't contain in-depth information about technologies, vulnerabilities, solutions or processes" — so it builds foundations, not operational capability, and does not substitute for the ICS-specific practical training the Estonian minister called for (E-2026-08-15-24). **(e) SHOWS THE COURSE ABSORBING LIVE REGULATORY WORK** — IEC **CDV 63452 commenting** and a **CRA webinar** were folded into the material during 2025, i.e. the syllabus tracks the same moving standards and law this register tracks. It also records **"ideas from the Spanish online course"**, implying at least one other national equivalent exists — worth knowing. **(f) IS A CONCRETE EU PROPOSAL WORTH FLAGGING:** an **EU-wide English-language railway-cybersecurity course is asserted feasible**, with the **ERA-ENISA conference series itself proposed as the content foundation** (streamed, recorded, integrated into study materials, voluntary contributions). Given this register has now reconstructed five editions of that series, the proposal is credible on its face — and if it proceeds it becomes a sector-level competence instrument alongside **ECSF** and **EU CYRUS** (E-2026-08-15-21/-22/-23). **(g) IS A SMALL BUT USEFUL ACTOR MAP:** **Digirail, FTIA and Fintraffic** appear as contributors — the same programme and bodies behind the pre-FRMCS commercial-radio work (E-2026-08-15-27) — alongside **Cylus** (a rail-cybersecurity vendor new to this register) and **Siemens Mobility**. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R12/R10 stay Proposed; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R13 Open. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) whether the course is available in English or only Finnish, and whether the **EU-wide version** at (f) progresses — a cheap capability route for the client if it does; (2) the **Spanish online course** referenced at (e) — a second national precedent; (3) **Cylus** as a rail-cybersecurity supplier new to the register. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. [**CORRECTED 2026-08-15 — this bullet originally read "the publication timeline has moved forward by roughly two years". That framing was WRONG and is withdrawn.** Re-reading the primary CDV cover page (E-2026-08-01-17, the 7-pp oSIST preview) shows it states a "**PROPOSED STABILITY DATE: 2028**" — which in IEC practice is the date until which a *published* standard is expected to remain unrevised, **NOT a publication date**. E-2026-08-01-17 recorded that term correctly; the error entered at E-2026-08-01-20's note, which converted it into a **publication** expectation of "~2027-28", and this row then measured a "two-year move" against that inference. **There was no two-year slip forward: the stability date and the publication forecast were always different things.** What follows below is unaffected — the CDV/FDIS/IS dates are Benoliel's own and the primary confirms the CDV circulation and closing dates exactly.]** E-2026-08-01-17 recorded IEC 63452 at CDV with a **proposed stability date of 2028**, and E-2026-08-01-20 carried a note to "**track publication (~2027-28) to migrate ADR-012 basis TS 50701 → 63452**" — **an inference from the stability date, not a stated publication date**. **This deck supersedes that: the CDV was APPROVED by National Committees in October 2025, with FDIS forecast ~March 2026 and the INTERNATIONAL STANDARD forecast ~AUGUST 2026.** Today is **2026-08-15** — so on the project team's own forecast, **IEC 63452 should be published as an International Standard at approximately this moment.** **The ADR-012 basis migration is therefore no longer a future watch item; it is live, and the first action is simply to check whether IEC 63452 has issued.** Treat the forecast with due caution — the project has slipped before (this register has tracked a Q4-2024 CDV forecast against an actual 2025-08) — but the direction is unambiguous and the gap between "~2028" and "~2026-08" is too large to leave uncorrected in the register's planning. **② (f) IS THE SINGLE MOST USEFUL ITEM FOR ADR-012: A EUROPEAN ANNEX MAPPING THE STANDARD TO CRA AND NIS-2.** CENELEC WG 26 has already performed a "**common coverage analysis of NIS 2 and CRA regulation**" with "**gaps identified**", and will publish an **IEC 63452 European Annex explaining how the standard's activities and deliverables can be used to COVER CRA and NIS-2 requirements**. **That is precisely the artefact ADR-012 needs and does not have** — a documented route from a rail-sector standard to the two binding regimes, which would let the engagement argue conformance through a recognised standard rather than assembling a bespoke mapping. It also complements the earlier PT deck's **NIS-2 Art-21 → 63452 clause crosswalk** (E-2026-08-01-20) by extending to the **CRA**, and it complements CRSG's draft sector guidance from the same conference (E-2026-08-15-30) — **standard-side and sector-side routes to the same compliance question, converging in 2026.** **③ (d) ANSWERS TWO OPEN QUESTIONS FROM TODAY'S OWN LOGGING.** At E-2026-08-15-30 I flagged as a follow-up "whether **SecRACs** have a standards definition (IEC 63452? TS 50701?)". **They do: SecRACs are named here as a concept IEC 63452 adapts from IEC 62278**, alongside the **threat log** and — significantly — the "**CYBERSECURITY CASE**". A cybersecurity case built on the IEC 62278 (RAMS) lifecycle is the **structural twin of the safety case**, and **SecRACs are the security analogue of EN 50129 SRACs**. For **ADR-004**, whose entire argument is a safety case holding under stated conditions, and for **ADR-012**, which must govern security change without breaking that case, this is the sector's own vocabulary and framework for the safety-security interface — **the engagement should adopt it rather than invent parallel terms.** The explicit inclusion of **CSM-RA risk-acceptance principles** is equally important: it ties the security standard into the same risk-acceptance regime ADR-011 and the CSM-RA change-significance work already use. **④ (e) COVERS THREE PROBLEMS THE REGISTER HAS TRACKED AS OPEN.** "**Handling legacy system**" addresses ENISA's 2021 conclusion that legacy-security recommendations were "not possible" (E-2026-08-15-07) — now a named clause area, alongside ESCG's ERTMS method (E-2026-08-15-19) and CRSG's compensating-measures approach (E-2026-08-15-30). "**Cybersecurity & safety interface**" is the seam ADR-012 and ADR-004 both work. "**Cloud for OT cybersecurity**" bears on the Cloud4Rail / SIL4-cloud thread (E-2026-07-02-27, E-2026-07-02-03) and on the UITP safety-critical-to-cloud item at the same conference (E-2026-08-15-23). And "**flexibility on allocation of some requirements (e.g. who shall perform Risk Assessment)**" is a direct response to the role-ambiguity the register has documented across the FR/DE/FI/BE supervisory divergence. **⑤ (a)'s SCOPING NOTE MATTERS FOR THE PDE INVENTORY: "products (equipment/devices) are OUT OF SCOPE, as 62443-4-1/4-2 apply."** So IEC 63452 governs the **system/integration/operation** layers while product-level security remains with IEC 62443-4-x — which aligns exactly with CRSG's layered model placing the **CRA at system-integrator and product-supplier level and NIS-2 at the operator** (E-2026-08-15-30 (d)), and with tmc's product-side implementation on 62443-4-1 (E-2026-08-15-29). **The three fit together into a coherent stack, and ADR-012 action 1 should decompose along it.** **⑥ SCALE AND LEGITIMACY:** 150 National Committee members across 23 countries and 4 continents, with formal liaison to **UITP, ERA, UIC, ERJU and UNIFE** — this is the sector's consolidated standards effort, not a niche working group, and its **265 pages / 70 requirements / 144 pages of annexes** indicate a substantial instrument. Note also that it explicitly covers **metros, tramways and trolleybuses**, i.e. urban rail — relevant to the scope question the register raised at E-2026-07-25-10. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays **Open**, ADR-012 stays **Proposed** — but its **basis-migration action is now due**, and the European Annex is a forthcoming input it should plan around; R11/ADR-004 Proposed, with SecRACs and the cybersecurity-case concept identified as adoptable vocabulary; R2/R1 unchanged. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **check whether IEC 63452 has now been published as an IS (forecast ~2026-08 — i.e. now)**, and if so obtain it; this is immediately actionable and supersedes the register's ~2028 assumption; (2) the **IEC 63452 European Annex** (CENELEC WG 26) mapping to CRA and NIS-2 — the highest-value forthcoming ADR-012 input; (3) **EN IEC 63452's replacement of EN TS 50701** — confirm and update every TS 50701 reference in ADR-012 and the sdo-mapping when it lands; (4) the **cybersecurity case / SecRACs / threat log** definitions for ADR-004 alignment. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. +| E-2026-08-15-33 | 2026-08-15 | ⚠️ **DUAL RESTRICTION — "Report preview – NOT OFFICIALLY RELEASED yet; all reproduction is FORBIDDEN without explicit consent from UITP" (UITP slides) AND "Proprietary & Confidential — Copyright © 2024 Waterfall Security Solutions Ltd." (vendor slides). See handling constraint.** **UITP Cybersecurity Committee — "Securing connection between Safety-critical systems and the cloud", presenting the forthcoming UITP report "DESIGN FOR SECURITY OF RAIL SAFETY-CRITICAL SYSTEMS"; Serge Van Themsche** (closing slide links to **waterfall-security.com**); **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 01-02.12.2025**; 17 slides. Paraphrased: **(a) THE UITP REPORT** — objective: "provide guidelines to engineers to design secure **Safety Instrumented Systems (SIL 1 to 4)**", following **IEC 63452** recommendations and defining how to integrate design throughout a SIS life cycle; "**can serve as a baseline for a code of practice**". Scope: "integrate best practices in terms of **building SYNCHED SAFETY AND SECURITY CASES**". Audience: safety engineers, design and cyber architectural engineers, security professionals, systems developers and administrators. Methodology: "**70+ Management Principles and 230+ Design Principles**, applying to a SIS and relating to **both Safety and Cybersecurity** disciplines". Structure: "**4 parallel life cycles**" (diagram, unread). **(b) SIS RISK ASSESSMENT** — "since an attack can impact a **Safety Instrumented Function (SIF)**, a **coordinated Safety/Security process** for specifying, designing, implementing and validating is required"; "**semi-quantified (threats and vulnerabilities) risks and (safety causal factors) quantified risks can BOTH generate hazards leading to an accident**" — attributed to the **UK Code of Practice**. **(c) THE PRINCIPLES** — **75+ Management Principles**, e.g. "**MP1: Board Upper Management accountability for Safety and Security** — depending on a country's legislation, the PTO's board of directors or the CEO and direct reports are the **ultimate owner(s) of these two risks**"; and **230+ Design Principles** integrating IEC 63452, e.g. "**DP1. Priority Principle: Operational concepts drive SAFETY design, which drives the CYBERSECURITY design**", and "**DP33. SBoM safety-critical tracking principle: the SBoM should be capable of AUTOMATIC GENERATION and the result must be MACHINE-READABLE**". **(d) DETERMINISTIC ENGINEERING — the intellectual core:** applying the principles "shouldn't be viewed as a **check list exercise**… it must be understood as a global approach to applying a **deterministic cybersecurity engineering practice**"; "**there is no reason why for Safety, the discipline relies on DETERMINISTIC engineering methodology, while we often tolerate QUALITATIVE approaches for Security**"; "the UITP cyber committee believes that **whenever an SIF is involved, the cyber protection measures associated with the Capable Security Level (SL-C) should MEET OR EXCEED the SL rate (i.e. SL3 or SL4)**", though "in theory it is tolerated to lower the security measures"; and "**the great contribution of IEC 63452 = SECURITY CASE SIGNED OFF BY SECURITY EXPERTS. Deviations must be recorded and reviewed from time to time during the operation and maintenance phase**". **(e) CLOUD-CONNECTED SIS** — a SIS is "an independent protection layer designed to bring a process to a safe state when predetermined conditions are violated, with its reliability and performance quantified by a **Safety Integrity Level (SIL 1 to 4)**"; a cloud-connected SIS extends OT functionality "by allowing data connection in the cloud and provisioning business enablement **and control** from the cloud", with **IIoT allowing direct connection to the cloud or to PLCs**; architecture shown as SIS → SCADA / Historian / OPC UA → PLCs/RTUs → IIoT → cloud over **MQTT/JSON**, for condition-based, energy and quality monitoring, remote support and higher-layer software. **(f) CLOUD RISK FRAMING** — "**the cybersecurity risk doesn't disappear miraculously with a cloud-enabled architecture. It is just SHARED between different stakeholders, adding complexity**"; "the more off-premise activities, the more potential for new attack vectors targeting your rail on-prem activities"; "the more open the cloud deployment and service models, the more risks"; referencing the **NIST Cloud Computing Security Reference Architecture**. **(g) ATTACK TYPES AND VECTORS (effect-level)** — *types*: rogue cloud actor (internal attacks by the datacentre's own employees described as "the biggest security threat"; phishing), access attacks (via Cloud Access Security Brokers or third-party DNS), cloud pivoting (application attacks), and **cloud infrastructure attacks** — **DCIM exploitation** (power DCIM, PDUs, cooling) able to "shut down the services", and **multi-site attacks** across numerous datacentres for "massive ransomware, DDoS, or Wiper attacks"; with a cited datum of "over **20,000 instances of publicly exposed DCIM software**" (source **Cyble, 2022**). *Vectors*: **① top-down** — "the attack starts from the cloud and continues to take over all Rail Duty Holder's PLCs and other equipment"; **② bottom-up** — "starts from a component connected to a PLC and continues up to the PLCs/SCADA and then to the cloud OT", the diagram naming **IXL compute platform, object controller, PLCs/RTUs, edge gateway, trackside network, VOBC, onboard network and train-to-ground network** feeding LTE core, ATS, TCS, SCADA, PIS, MMIS, CCTV and historian; **③ infrastructure shut-down** — targeting datacentre HVAC or power "forcing the datacenter manager to disconnect the hosted services". **(h) DESIGN GUIDANCE, cited to "IEC 63452 CLOUD ANNEX K"** — whole-rail-system risk analysis ("Security-Level measures shouldn't be established on just the cloud-connected OT systems"); dedicated access-control policy (IAM on **RBAC, MFA, PTO-managed credentials**); "**a zones and conduits should use principles of least privilege for any communication between zones**"; modern encryption, **TLS/SSL with strong cipher suites**, **PKI certificate management**, and "**establish cloud security monitoring of the railway system**". **And the directionality rule:** "**control signals received from a cloud instance to an OT system should be FILTERED, AUTHENTICATED, and MONITORED for cybersecurity anomalies**"; "**non-control signal data such as cybersecurity monitoring and telemetry data from an OT system to a cloud instance should be implemented in a UNI-DIRECTIONAL fashion**". **(i) THE COMMUNICATION MATRIX** — analysis "based on **zone criticality**", checking "what is **Monitored vs Controlled** data", then applying dataflow directionality rules per the operator's risk appetite: "**+**" flow allowed **both directions**; "**R**" **restricted to read-only** (ZC-L5 → ZC-L4) "**only by DATA DIODES or similar measures which maintain unidirectional flow**"; "**-**" flow **prohibited**. **(j) CONCLUSION** — cloud/IIoT "bring many benefits but their design introduces new risks"; "**IIoT is especially vulnerable**"; "cloud design **shares the PTO's risks with other actors**"; and "use **deterministic cyber design approach** in cloud environments to provide an **SL3/SL4 protection to SIS (SIL 3 to 4)**", around defence-in-depth — "**network segmentation using HARDWARE ENFORCED PROTECTION** and other technologies". READ: complete extractable text (17 slides; the four-lifecycle diagram, the communication matrix and the vector diagrams read via labels only) | Sector-committee presentation of an unreleased report, with vendor-copyrighted analysis slides | file "session 5-2 - uitp - securing connection safety-critical systems and the cloud.pdf", Download folder; md5 6c5953db…339d; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 2025 | **SPLIT TIER.** **A (preview/unreleased)** for the **UITP report content** — (a)-(d) and the IEC 63452-derived design guidance at (h)/(i): a sector body (UITP Cybersecurity Committee) presenting its own forthcoming report, methodologically substantial (75+ MPs, 230+ DPs) and anchored on IEC 63452. **B (vendor)** for the **Waterfall-copyrighted slides** — (e)-(g) and (j): the cloud-risk framing, attack taxonomy and conclusion are © Waterfall Security Solutions. **⚠️ MATERIAL CONFLICT TO CARRY: Waterfall Security Solutions is a vendor of unidirectional security gateways (data diodes), and the deck's recommended controls — "data diodes or similar measures", "hardware enforced protection" — are that company's product category.** The guidance is nonetheless consistent with IEC 63452 and with ADR-004's own independent reasoning, but **the recommendation must not be treated as vendor-neutral**. Further flags: **the UITP report is NOT YET RELEASED** — nothing in it is a settled sector position; **several diagrams unread**; **2025-12**, current | **R11/ADR-004 — the closest external match on file** (+ R14/ADR-012; R3/R4 cloud availability; R12 cloud security monitoring; R8/PR13 shared risk) | validate | **⚠️ HANDLING — THE MOST RESTRICTED ITEM IN TODAY'S SET: UITP states "all reproduction is FORBIDDEN without explicit consent" and Waterfall marks its slides "Proprietary & Confidential".** Logged only because this repository is **PRIVATE** (verified). **Nothing from this row may appear in any outward-facing artefact** — LinkedIn drafts, papers, client deliverables — without consent from **both** UITP and Waterfall. The register now holds **five distinct handling classes** (RESTRICTED · reproduction-forbidden/proprietary · TLP:AMBER · TLP:GREEN · TLP:CLEAR); **a written handling policy is now overdue and should precede any publication work.** **① THIS IS THE CLOSEST EXTERNAL MATCH TO ADR-004 THE REGISTER HAS FOUND, AND IT ARRIVES VIA A STANDARD.** ADR-004's boundary is: the advisory domain may **read** vital-domain state but **cannot write** into the vital path; the preferred mechanism is a **data diode / unidirectional gateway** on the read path; and freedom-from-interference is argued by **segregation**. **(h) states the same rule from the IEC 63452 side**: telemetry and monitoring data flowing **out of OT to the cloud should be UNI-DIRECTIONAL**, while control signals flowing **into OT must be filtered, authenticated and monitored**. **(i) then formalises it as a three-state directionality matrix per zone pair — "+" bidirectional, "R" read-only enforced by data diodes, "-" prohibited — which is precisely the notation ADR-004's boundary design lacks.** **Recommendation: adopt the communication-matrix/directionality-rule construct in ADR-004's detailed design (action item 1), citing IEC 63452 Annex K rather than this restricted deck.** **② (d) IS THE MOST QUOTABLE ARGUMENT IN THE CORPUS FOR THE ENGAGEMENT'S OWN THESIS.** "**There is no reason why for Safety, the discipline relies on deterministic engineering methodology, while we often tolerate qualitative approaches for Security**" — and the corollary that whenever a **safety instrumented function** is involved, security measures should **meet or exceed the SIL** (SL3/SL4). ADR-004's entire structure rests on determinism: a deterministic SIL-4 kernel, with non-deterministic learning components excluded because they cannot carry SIL-4. **UITP is making the mirror-image argument on the security side, and the two compose: if security measures protecting a SIF must be deterministic and SL-matched, then a non-deterministic component cannot sit inside that protection perimeter either.** That is independent, sector-level support for ADR-004's exclusion of learning agents from the vital domain — arrived at from security engineering rather than safety composition. **③ "SYNCHED SAFETY AND SECURITY CASES" AND THE SIGNED-OFF SECURITY CASE ARE THE MISSING VOCABULARY.** (a) frames the report's scope as **building synchronised safety and security cases**; (d) names IEC 63452's "great contribution" as a **security case signed off by security experts, with deviations recorded and reviewed during O&M**. Read with E-2026-08-15-32 (IEC 63452 adapts **cybersecurity case, threat log and SecRACs** from IEC 62278) and E-2026-08-15-30 (**SecRACs** as the mechanism for accepting compensated legacy), **the sector now has a complete parallel-case apparatus — and ADR-004, which is a safety-case argument, and ADR-012, which must change security without breaking it, should be expressed in it.** The "deviations recorded and reviewed during O&M" discipline is also directly ADR-011's territory. **④ (c) MP1 PUTS THE TWO RISKS ON ONE BOARD.** Board or CEO-level accountability for **both** safety and security as "the ultimate owner(s) of these two risks" matches NIS-2's top-management accountability (E-2026-08-15-09) and is the governance expression of the coupling ADR-012 asserts. **DP1's ordering — operational concepts → safety design → cybersecurity design — is a clean statement of precedence** that the engagement can adopt directly, and it is consistent with ADR-004's insistence that the vital kernel is unchanged by the presence of the advisory layer. **DP33 also answers part of an open question**: tmc asked "what SBOM quality is sufficient?" (E-2026-08-15-29); DP33 supplies two criteria — **automatically generatable and machine-readable**. **⑤ THE CLOUD RISK FRAMING (B-TIER, BUT SOUND) BEARS ON THE CLOUD4RAIL THREAD.** "**The cybersecurity risk doesn't disappear miraculously with a cloud-enabled architecture — it is just shared between different stakeholders, adding complexity**" is the security-side statement of the same problem the register tracks on the safety side: DB InfraGO's Cloud4Rail direction of untrusted COTS virtualisation plus a certified application-level safety layer (E-2026-07-02-27), and the SIL4-Cloud separation-kernel work with its unresolved dynamism and time-sync residuals (E-2026-07-02-03). **Vector ② (bottom-up) is the one to note for this engagement**: it runs from a component at a PLC up through **VOBC, onboard network and the TRAIN-TO-GROUND NETWORK** into the cloud — i.e. **the bearer is in the attack path**, which is the FRMCS-relevant limb and a candidate SIM-SEC vector. Vector ③ (datacentre HVAC/power shut-down) is a **non-cyber-looking availability attack on a hosting facility** — kin to R3/R4's single-point-of-failure concern and to the 23-June lesson that availability can be lost without any attack at all. **⑥ CAUTIONS.** The report is **unreleased**, so its principles are not yet a sector position; the SL-C ≥ SIL recommendation is explicitly the **committee's belief** ("the UITP cyber committee believes"), not a normative requirement, and the deck concedes lowering security measures "is tolerated"; and the vendor conflict at (j) means "hardware enforced protection" should be evaluated on its merits against alternatives, not adopted because it appears in this deck. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** **R11 stays Proposed — load-bearing · ADR-004**, and ADR-004's status is unchanged: this is strong *design-basis* support for the unidirectional boundary and the determinism argument, but it produces none of the **verifying** evidence ADR-004 action items 2, 4 and 5 require (FFI/independence analysis, clause-verified standards, ISA/NoBo assessment). R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R3/R4 unchanged; R12 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **the UITP report "Design for Security of Rail Safety-Critical Systems" when officially released** — 75+ MPs and 230+ DPs aimed at SIL 1-4 systems is potentially the most directly applicable design guidance the engagement could hold for ADR-004; (2) **IEC 63452 Annex K (cloud)** — the citable source for the directionality rules, obtainable once the IS publishes (~now, per E-2026-08-15-32); (3) the **UK Code of Practice** referenced at (b) for the safety-causal-factor/security-hazard relationship; (4) consider adding the **bottom-up train-to-ground vector** to the SIM-SEC backlog. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. +| E-2026-08-15-34 | 2026-08-15 | **AIRBUS PROTECT (with ALSTOM) — "[Fence]: An Innovative Solution for Railway Cybersecurity Risks"**; presented by **Samuel Schmidlin** (Airbus Protect, "**Fence Product Owner** & Business Analyst") **with Serge Benoliel** (**Alstom**, "Cybersecurity Governance & Expertise Director", serge.benoliel@alstomgroup.com), billed as "**7 years of collaboration for Cybersecurity**"; **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 2025**; 23 slides. **PROVENANCE ODDITY: most slide footers read "Negotiation Meeting — Tender ID: PRO-006229"** — a template leftover from a commercial tender deck, comparable to the stray German title in the EUG deck (E-2026-08-15-19). Paraphrased: **(a) THE VENDOR** — Airbus Protect, an Airbus subsidiary created July 2022; **+1,700 professionals** across France, Germany, UK, Spain, Belgium; **€238m 2024 turnover**; four business units (Safety, Cybersecurity Consulting, Managed Services, Sustainability); **+300 clients** across aerospace/aviation, transport, energy, nuclear and public institutions. **(b) ALSTOM'S STATED NEED** — "initial context of **FRAGMENTATION OF APPROACHES ACROSS PROJECTS** (EBIOS RM, EBIOS 2010, various flavour of 62443, TS 50701…)"; a need for a consistent unified approach enabling **reproducible** risk assessments, **reuse across contexts**, simplified **competency management**, capitalisation and **automation of a single methodology**; key requirements being full compliance with **EBIOS RM and 62443 / TS 50701**, combining the benefits of both, **support for architectural choices**, and simplicity once automated with a database. **Scale datum: projects with cyber requirements handled by regions grew 43 (06/2020) → 112 (02/2022) → 145 (12/2022) → 161 (02/2023) → 220 (06/2024)**, distributed across IT&CEE 40, BNL&Nordics 37, APAC 33, UK&Ireland 30, France 30, Spain&Portugal 16, Americas 15, AMECA 9. **(c) THE STATED CHALLENGES** — mandatory compliance with **interlinked standards**; integrating **IT/OT convergence** risks; **manual, time-consuming analysis**; **inconsistent and subjective assessments**; and "**the velocity of change (evolving threats)**". **(d) ARAMIS — "A Risk Assessment Methodology for Industrial Security"**, combining **EBIOS RM** with **ISA/IEC 62443 + CLC/TS 50701**. The stated complementarity: *EBIOS RM* is "a practical, ready-to-use methodology" with "a neat and elegant way to draw **attack scenarios**", but "complex systems lead to complex scenarios, and difficulty to comprehend the global picture"; *62443 / TS 50701* offer "a complete corpus… defining **Security Levels & Foundational Requirements**" and the "concept of **zones & conduits** refining a whole system into smaller parts, **ideal for handling complexity**", but give "limited information on practical application on detail risk assessment" and "**no representation of attack path scenarios**". **(e) THE STRUCTURE — EBIOS RM's five modules with IEC 62443 "ZCR" steps interleaved:** *Module 1* scope, impact assessment, security baseline (scope · stakeholders · context · inventory · **feared events** · security baseline) + **ZCR 1 identify the system**; *Module 2* threat sources (risk origins & target objectives · **attacker view vs defender view**); *Module 3* ecosystem & strategic scenarios (threat vectors & interested parties · strategic scenarios) + **ZCR 2 initial risk assessment**, **ZCR 3 partition into zones & conduits**; *Module 4* operational scenarios (initial risk assessment · **zones definition** · detailed risk assessment — overview, in each zone, consolidation) + **ZCR 4 does initial risk exceed tolerable risk? ⇒ ZCR 5 detailed risk assessment**; *Module 5* risk treatment (**security level target** · residual risks · treatment plan · achieved risks) + **ZCR 6 document risks and requirements**, **ZCR 7 ASSET OWNER APPROVAL** — organised as a **strategic cycle** and an **operational cycle**. **(f) THE WORKED RAIL EXAMPLE** — a System under Consideration partitioned into **Zone A "Network Gateways"**, **Zone B "Passenger Entertainment"** and **Zone C "Safety-critical Systems"**, with an attacker path running to a consequence; and a zoom into **Zone A showing an LTE modem, a SATELLITE modem and a "receiver for signalisation" behind a SECURITY GATEWAY**, which mediates flows "toward zone B (passenger entertainment)" and "toward zone C (safety-critical systems)". **(g) AUTOMATED SECURITY LEVEL TARGET (SL-T)** — computed from **unmitigated risk level** (from the risk assessment), **tolerable risk level** (a constant decided at context establishment) and **attacker strength** (a constant from the threat environment); "Security Level allocated at **Zone level and on each Asset**, based on **7 Foundational Requirements**" — **FR1 identification and authentication control · FR2 use control · FR3 system integrity · FR4 data confidentiality · FR5 restricted data flow · FR6 timely response to events · FR7 resource availability** — yielding "**a vector of SLs**" (worked example IAC 2, UC 2, SI 3, DC –, RDF 2, TRE 3, RA 2). Propagation rules: **Asset SL-T = max{(Attack Step SL-T)ᵢ}**; **Zone SL-T = max{(Asset SL-T)ᵢ}**. Back-up slides add that **unmitigated likelihood is calculated from Expertise, Window of Opportunity and Equipment**, and that applicable FRs are derived from "**attacker Techniques – Tactics**". **(h) STATUS** — "the methodology was **validated using use cases and pilot analyses from Alstom**. **Full deployment is currently underway**"; project start **2021**, launch approved after pilot cases **2024**; "**Europe's Rail Joint Undertaking (ERJU) has started using and evaluating the ARAMIS methodology in Fence**"; and a "**Statement of Conformity**" claimed against **IEC 62443-3-2:2020, CLC/TS 50701:2023, EBIOS RM and ISO/IEC 27005:2022**. And the growth curve **43 → 220 projects carrying cyber requirements between 2020 and 2024** quantifies how fast the compliance surface expanded, which is useful context for R13's programme-scale arguments and for ADR-012's workload. **② (f) IS THE ONE SLIDE THAT MATTERS TO ADR-004, AND IT IS STRIKINGLY ON-POINT.** The worked zone model places an **LTE modem, a satellite modem and a "receiver for signalisation" together in a gateway zone behind a SECURITY GATEWAY**, which mediates toward **passenger entertainment** on one side and **safety-critical systems** on the other. **That is the FRMCS onboard problem in a diagram**: the bearer (cellular plus satellite) and the signalling receiver share an exposure zone, and a gateway stands between them and the vital domain. It matches SUBSET-147's mandated on-board zoning, where the FRMCS zone is separated from the CCS zone (E-2026-08-01-14), and it is the commercial-practice counterpart to the UITP/IEC 63452 directionality rules logged alongside it (E-2026-08-15-33). **The satellite-modem placement is also a quiet reminder for R4**: multi-bearer fallback adds a bearer *and* an attack surface into the same zone, which the fallback design must account for. **③ (g) IS A LEGITIMATE METHOD PATTERN WORTH KNOWING, THOUGH NOT ADOPTABLE HERE.** Deriving a **per-zone and per-asset SL-T vector across the seven 62443 Foundational Requirements**, propagating **asset SL-T = max(attack-step SL-T)** and **zone SL-T = max(asset SL-T)**, and computing unmitigated likelihood from **expertise, window of opportunity and equipment** — the last being the Common Criteria attack-potential factor set, and kin to the ENISA SCSA methodology's attack-potential construct already on file (E-2026-08-15-08) — is a coherent, traceable way to get from scenarios to requirements. **For ADR-012's risk-assessment work and ADR-010's evaluation design, the *pattern* is instructive even though the *tool* is proprietary**; note also **ZCR 7 "asset owner approval"** as an explicit sign-off gate, which is the operator-side counterpart to the security case signed off by security experts (E-2026-08-15-33) and to ADR-011's approval discipline. **④ THE ERJU SIGNAL IS WORTH TRACKING BUT IS SELF-REPORTED.** "ERJU has started using and evaluating the ARAMIS methodology in Fence" — if accurate, a commercial methodology is being trialled inside the EU rail joint undertaking whose **System Pillar** is simultaneously producing the sector's cybersecurity specifications (E-2026-08-15-21/-22/-23). **That would be worth understanding before the engagement commits to any risk-assessment approach**, since a de facto sector method may emerge from it. Verify independently of the vendor. **⑤ CAUTIONS.** The "Statement of Conformity" against four standards is **self-declared**, with no assessor named; the methodology is **not published**, so its claims cannot be examined; validation rests on "use cases and pilot analyses from Alstom", i.e. the anchor customer; and the independence note above means this deck and the IEC 63452 material should not be read as mutually confirming. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R11/ADR-004 Proposed — the Zone A architecture is illustrative design-basis material only, not evidence; ADR-010 unchanged; R8 Recommended. **R11/ADR-004 safety-security coordination & authorisation**; R13/R2 legacy; R8/PR13 COTS) | validate | **A — and the most institutionally significant French artefact on file, because it shows what happened to the competence gap this register flagged three years earlier.** **① THE 2022 GAP HAS NOT BEEN CLOSED LEGALLY — IT IS BEING MANAGED BY COORDINATION, AND THE SECTOR IS NOW ASKING FOR THE FIX.** E-2026-08-15-10 recorded EPSF stating it had "**no legal competence for cybersecurity matters**" while ANSSI held cyber competence without a rail-safety mandate, the seam bridged by a letter of intent and a joint position paper. **Three years on, EPSF is leading a structured national strategy with ANSSI inside the working group — and one of its formal positions is that "conditions and criteria for cybersecurity measures [should] be DEFINED COMMONLY BETWEEN RAILWAYS SAFETY AUTHORITIES AND CYBERSECURITY AUTHORITIES" (g).** That is the competence gap, still open, now stated as a demand rather than a description. **The honest reading for the client: France has responded to a structural legal gap with sustained institutional coordination, not with reallocated competence — which works, but depends on goodwill and effort rather than on mandate.** Compare Germany, where EBA reads its AEG safety mandate as already encompassing IT security (E-2026-08-15-11), and Finland, where one agency holds all three roles (E-2026-08-15-12): **three years on, the FR/DE/FI divergence persists.** **② (d) IS AN NSA CONFIRMING THE TSI CYBERSECURITY GAP IN 2025 — the most current statement of it on file.** "**Railways regulations (TSIs, CSMs): no explicit requirement for cybersecurity**, but can be called through a standard or a comprehensive reading of regulations (resilience)." That is the same defect EUG/ESCG diagnosed mechanically in 2022 — subsets scoped to cross-border interoperability (RBC-Euroradio) only, below commonly agreed standards (E-2026-08-15-19) — and that EPSF itself flagged then as "limited cybersecurity requirement in the coming CCS TSI" (E-2026-08-15-10). **Three years and one TSI edition later (Reg (EU) 2023/1695), the NSA still describes the requirement as absent and reachable only by inference.** For **R7** that is a persisting, now well-evidenced gap; the position at (e) — "**need of TSI compatibility with these cybersecurity requirements**" — is the sector asking for it to be closed. **③ (e) PROPOSES A COMPLIANCE ROUTE THAT ADR-012 SHOULD EVALUATE: "CRA MAY BE USED TO DEMONSTRATE THE FULFILMENT OF NIS2 DIRECTIVE REQUIREMENTS."** Coupled with (f)'s "**rely on deliverables coming from standards implantation (TS 50701 and PT 63452) to demonstrate conformity to regulation**", the French sector is proposing a **layered demonstration chain: standards deliverables → CRA conformity → NIS-2 fulfilment.** That is precisely the structure the **IEC 63452 European Annex** will formalise (E-2026-08-15-32: WG 26 will explain how 63452 activities and deliverables "can be used to cover CRA and NIS 2 requirements"), and it converges with CRSG's sector guidance (E-2026-08-15-30). **Three independent workstreams — a national NSA strategy, a CENELEC annex and a sector group — are converging on the same compliance architecture, and ADR-012 should adopt that architecture rather than construct its own mapping.** The row also cleanly restates ADR-012's own entity/product split (NIS-2 → entities and their organisation; CRA → product suppliers). **④ (d) NAMES TWO PROBLEMS ADR-012 CURRENTLY UNDER-TREATS.** First, "**possible multiple events notifications (and… multiple reports)**" — an operator facing CRA Art 14, NIS-2 Art 23 and rail safety-reporting duties may have to notify the same event several times to different authorities. **ADR-012 action item 4 already proposes aligning the reporting chain "so a vulnerability/incident is reported once, correctly, to the right authority"; the French sector independently identifies the same problem, which strengthens that action and suggests it is a sector-wide need rather than a local design choice.** Second, and more sharply: "**impact on railway safety of cybersecurity risk mitigation measures or of PATCHES?**" — **that is ADR-012's central tension, asked by the safety regulator itself.** The engagement's answer is ADR-011's single gated change process preserving Rückwirkungsfreiheit; the NSA is asking the question without an answer, which is useful evidence that the problem is live and unresolved at national-regulator level. **⑤ "LEX SPECIALIS — IN CASE OF CONFLICT, WHICH REGULATION PREVAILS?" IS AN OPEN LEGAL QUESTION THE REGISTER SHOULD CARRY.** With CRA, NIS-2, the CSA, the CCS TSI, the CSM-RA and — per (d) — "specialised regulations (**radio**)" all touching FRMCS, conflicts are foreseeable and the precedence rule is unsettled. The **radio** reference is also a second, independent pointer toward the **Radio Equipment Directive** question raised at E-2026-08-15-29, which remains absent from this register. **⑥ (f) "GIVE PRIORITY TO COTS FOR CYBER-CRITICAL ASSETS" IS COUNTER-INTUITIVE FOR RAIL AND WORTH THINKING THROUGH.** Safety-critical rail has traditionally favoured bespoke, vital-engineered equipment. The CRA inverts part of that logic: **COTS products carry conformity, SBOMs and a support commitment, whereas tailor-made products can contract out of secure defaults and free security updates** (E-2026-08-15-28 ⑤, E-2026-08-15-30 — "chargeable patches"). **So the CRA's economics push cyber-critical assets toward COTS**, which has real architectural consequences for a vital estate and sits in tension with the certified-bespoke tradition ADR-004 relies on. Flag for the client: this is a genuine strategic fork, and the French sector has taken a position on it. **⑦ (c) IS A USEFUL COALITION MODEL** — an NSA convening RUs, IMs, industry, **NoBo/DeBo/AsBo assessment bodies**, associations and both state authorities, then publishing agreed positions for members to carry into EU fora. The inclusion of **assessment bodies** matters, since they are the parties who will have to judge cybersecurity in authorisation. The explicit recognition of "'**small' actors represented by their association**" continues the weak-tier concern seen in FI and FR since 2022. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed); **R7 stays Accepted**, with the TSI cybersecurity gap now confirmed as persisting into 2025 — recorded as a risk, not a status change; R11/ADR-004 Proposed; R13 Open, R2 Accepted; R8 Recommended. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) the **STRANE cybersecurity position paper** itself once finalised — the positions here are draft and the legacy-systems issue was still being amended on 26.11.2025; (2) **the lex specialis question** — which instrument prevails where CRA, NIS-2, CCS TSI, CSM-RA and radio regulation conflict; worth a legal view, since ADR-012 assumes complementarity; (3) whether **"specialised regulations (radio)"** means the **Radio Equipment Directive** — now flagged twice from different sources and still absent from this register. failure**", status *Strengthened post-incident — open*, and the 23-June 2026 outage is the register's proof that a central failure domain can stop a national network. **EDIS is to provide a "uniform operating standard in ALL DB InfraGO operations centres" — i.e. it is a national-scale dispatch system — and its supervisor is asking, in 2025, whether its redundancy is sufficient and noting a central coupling node without network redundancy.** That is the same architectural failure class as the incident, in a *different* system, identified *prospectively* by the regulator. It should be put to the client directly: **the SPOF concern R3 encodes is not historical, and it is being raised by the regulator about systems now being built.** **③ THE 99.9% AVAILABILITY TARGET AT (e) IS A BENCHMARK WORTH USING CAREFULLY.** "99.9% availability (approx. **9h downtime p.a.**)" for a national dispatch system means the **~2-hour 23-June outage would fit inside the annual budget more than four times over and the system would still meet target.** For R4 — "guarantee fail-soft; no full standstill" — that is a useful and uncomfortable calibration: **an availability percentage that sounds high can be entirely compatible with repeated multi-hour national disruptions**, which is precisely why R3/R4 are framed as *architectural* requirements (no shared failure domain, designed degraded mode) rather than as availability SLAs. Recommend the engagement make that argument explicitly, citing this target as the illustration. **④ (d) oKMC IS A LIVE GERMAN DEPLOYMENT OF THE ONLINE KEY MANAGEMENT THE REGISTER TRACKS AS A SPEC — AND IT TOUCHES THREE THREADS AT ONCE.** SUBSET-137 (on-line key management FFFIS, CCS-TSI-mandated, with its security interface delegated to SUBSET-146) is on file as a specification (E-2026-08-01-18); **oKMC is that capability being built and supervised in Germany, replacing the offline KMC**. Three observations: **(i)** the architecture runs the vehicle link over **GSM-R**, so oKMC is being introduced on the *legacy* bearer and will have to migrate with it — an R2 coexistence item and a concrete instance of the "new digital capability layered onto the old radio" pattern; **(ii)** it is deployed across **three geo-redundant sites (Karlsruhe, Duisburg, Hanover)**, which is R3's prescribed pattern in practice and worth citing as a domestic precedent for geo-redundancy in a safety-adjacent system; **(iii)** its **user interface sits in the CLOUD behind a firewall/VPN, with an HSM protecting ETCS keys** — i.e. a real instance of the safety-critical-to-cloud boundary that UITP/IEC 63452 Annex K addresses (E-2026-08-15-33) and that the Cloud4Rail thread debates (E-2026-07-02-27). **This is the closest thing on file to a worked German example of the cloud/vital interface, under regulator supervision.** **⑤ (f) IS A REGULATOR'S SUPERVISION CHECKLIST AND IS DIRECTLY REUSABLE.** Eighteen named focus areas — including **attack detection, logging, redundancy, vulnerability scan, penetration test, system hardening & cryptography, identity & access management, IT emergency, test & release** — constitute what EBA actually examines. **For ADR-012 (what a compliant estate must evidence) and ADR-007 (what the test programme must cover), this is a free, authoritative scope list from the very authority that would supervise the client.** Note that **attack detection and logging both appear**, which is the supervisory counterpart to the detection-maturity gap ENISA measured (E-2026-08-15-03/-16) and to R12's thesis. **⑥ (b) RESTATES THE COUPLING THIS ENGAGEMENT IS BUILT ON, FROM THE REGULATOR:** "**an integrated view of safety and cybersecurity is essential**", and "**to ensure safety, risks must remain at an acceptable level**". Combined with EBA's 2022 position that its safety mandate encompasses IT security (E-2026-08-15-11), Germany's regulator is consistently treating the two as one supervisory problem — the position ADR-012 and ADR-004 jointly assume, and the opposite of the French structural split (E-2026-08-15-35). **⑦ THE NSA-TO-NSA NETWORK AT (a) IS WORTH NOTING** — regular exchange with **BSI** plus **BAV (CH), EPSF (FR), NSA Rail Belgium, SJT (NO) and Traficom (FI)**. The register has now logged four of those authorities directly; this confirms they operate as a working peer group, which matters for the cross-border assurance-divergence concern raised at E-2026-08-15-35. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** **R3 stays "Strengthened post-incident — open"** — this adds a current, regulator-sourced instance of the SPOF concern but proves nothing by test, which is what ADR-007 must supply; **R4 unchanged**, with the 99.9%/9h datum recorded as calibration; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R11/ADR-004 Proposed; R2/R5/R6 Accepted; R12 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **EDIS** — scope, architecture, and whether the central coupling-node redundancy concern was resolved; a national dispatch system with a regulator-flagged SPOF is materially relevant to R3 and to the client's own estate; (2) **oKMC** — its cloud/vital boundary design and its GSM-R→FRMCS migration path, as a domestic worked example for ADR-004 and R2; (3) **EBA's "approve security experts" scheme** — the concrete route, criteria and register, for ADR-004 action 5; (4) the **18 focus areas** in their fuller form, if EBA publishes them, as an ADR-007 scope baseline. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. KIN: **E-2026-08-15-11 (the 2022 EBA/BSI deck — this confirms and operationalises it)**, **E-2026-08-15-35 (EPSF the same week — the FR/DE contrast in one conference)**, E-2026-08-01-18 (SUBSET-137 on-line key management — oKMC is its deployment), E-2026-08-15-33 (safety-critical-to-cloud; Annex K), E-2026-07-02-27 / -03 (Cloud4Rail; SIL4-Cloud), E-2026-08-15-03/-16 (the detection-maturity gap (f) supervises against), incident-annex.md and E-2026-06-27-01/-03 (the 23-June central-failure precedent (e) echoes), ADR-004 (action 5; integrated safety-security), ADR-007 (test scope), ADR-011, ADR-012, R3/R4/R14/R12/R2 |

(NS)** — "**Cyber Crisis Management**"; **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 02.12.2025**; 22 slides. **Base material credited in-deck to "©Dimitri van Zantvliet, Dutch Railways. 2023"** and the PDF author metadata is **dimitri.vanzantvliet@ns.nl** — i.e. NS's cybersecurity director authored the underlying deck, presented here by Mager (**his fifth appearance in this register**: NS "Becoming an OES" E-2026-08-15-13, the CER deck E-2026-08-15-18, and the 4th/5th conference agendas). Paraphrased: **(a) NATO SUMMIT 2025 — a worked major-event deployment on three tiers.** *NS level*: awareness (general and crisis organisation); protection (e.g. **anti-DDoS**); **detection/response capacity**; organisation & collaboration; **integrated crisis management**. *Sectorial*: "umbrella: **Spoor-ISAC**"; scenarios → awareness; information flow; communication structure. *National*: "**led by NCTV and NCSC**"; scenarios ("**alternative future analysis**"); information flow; communication structure; "**liaison at NCSC**". Preparation was **CTI-based**, involving "network analysis and **use case modification**" at the tactical level. *Aftermath*: informing all employees on the approach; evaluation of lessons learned — "**positive: collaboration at all levels**"; and "**reuse of approach for other (major) events**". **(b) THE FRAMING POSITION — "but a cyber crisis is just another crisis":** use the **overarching NS crisis management organisation**; use the **overarching NS crisis management processes**; and hold an "**NS Cyber Crisis Management Plan for specific cyber related aspects (as for other types of crises)**". **(c) ESCALATION MODEL — three tiers, with cyber specificity only at the bottom.** *Strategic*: **Crisis BeleidsTeam (CBT)**, marked "**not cyber specific**", handling coordination and communication with external stakeholders, **supervisory bodies**, press communication and the chain including **CMT ProRail**. *Tactical*: **Landelijk Calamiteiten Coördinatie Team (LCCT)**, with the chain running to *Officier van Dienst* and *Verkeersleiding* (traffic control). *Operational*: **BCST — "Blue Cyber Shield Team"**. The escalation axis runs **cyber incident → cyber calamity → crisis** by **severity/impact**, with disciplines widening from **cyber** alone to "**other disciplines**". Supporting bodies named: NS Crisis Policy Team, National Calamity Coordination Team (LCCT), **NS Communication Team**, operational on-call services, and an emergency organisation at 1st and 2nd level. **(d) CYBER CRISIS SPECIFICS — the most engagement-relevant slide.** *Aspects to consider*: "**PRIORITY OF SAFETY VERSUS CONTINUITY OF OPERATION**"; "how to deal with **extortion**"; "how to deal with **cybercrime**"; "how to deal with **forensic investigation**". *Critical decisions*: "**THREAT OF LOSING OPERATIONAL CONTROL**"; "**deadline for payment**"; "**data breach becoming public**". **(e) EXERCISES** — "included in planning **per year** for crisis management exercises"; "**joint exercises with [the] infrastructure manager, with joint preparation**"; "participation in **national exercises (ISIDOOR)**"; and "evaluation of **each** exercise", covering both "processes of regular crisis management" and "cyber specific aspects". **(f) "OUR EXPERIENCE" — six stated lessons:** "**cyber crisis is just a crisis but somewhat different**"; "**AVOID FIXATION ON GOING BACK TO NORMAL BUSINESS**"; "**practice at all levels, with sector and with SUPPLIERS**"; "**use a REALISTIC scenario, base these on INCIDENTS**"; "**use a 'PLANNED' crisis**"; "**collaborate & communicate**". No TLP marking observed | A (primary first-hand operator account, on the basis this register A-weights operator self-accounts — cf. E-2026-08-15-13 (NS), -15 (SNCF), -26 (Estonian Railways); **FOUR FLAGS**: **self-reported** practice with no external validation and no metrics; **8 of 22 slides unread** (diagrams), so the organisational charts and scenario/impact material are only partially captured; **base material dated 2023** though presented in 2025, so some content may predate the NATO-summit experience it now frames; **slide-deck**) | **ADR-007 exercises & crisis rehearsal** (+ **R3/R4 continuity vs safety, loss of operational control**; R12 detection/response and information flow; R14/ADR-012 NIS-2 incident response; R8/PR13 supplier exercises) | validate | **A — the register's first substantive account of RAIL CYBER CRISIS MANAGEMENT IN PRACTICE, and it lands on ADR-007 and R3/R4 more directly than most of this corpus.** **① (d) NAMES THE ENGAGEMENT'S CENTRAL TRADE-OFF AS AN OPERATOR'S LIVE CRISIS DECISION: "PRIORITY OF SAFETY VERSUS CONTINUITY OF OPERATION", with "THREAT OF LOSING OPERATIONAL CONTROL" listed among the critical decisions.** R3 and R4 exist because the 23-June outage forced exactly that trade in a non-cyber form — the network lost its bearer, safety-critical voice and movement authorities were at stake, and recovery was manual. **Here an operator states, from its own crisis playbook, that the safety-versus-continuity priority is the decision a cyber crisis actually turns on.** That is useful corroboration that R3/R4 are framed at the right level, and it supplies the client-facing vocabulary for it. Note also the honest inclusion of **extortion and "deadline for payment"** — a decision class the register has not previously represented, and one that sits uncomfortably beside a safety duty. **② (f)'s "AVOID FIXATION ON GOING BACK TO NORMAL BUSINESS" IS THE MOST VALUABLE SINGLE LESSON HERE, AND IT SPEAKS DIRECTLY TO THE 23-JUNE PATTERN.** In a cyber crisis, pressure to restore normal service quickly can destroy forensic evidence, reinstate a compromised state, or restore service before it is safe. The 23-June incident is the register's case study in restoration pressure — a nationwide standstill, manual recovery, and a root cause that took days to surface (E-2026-06-27-01/-03). **The lesson generalises beyond cyber: a fail-soft design (R4) and a governed recovery (ADR-011) both depend on resisting the urge to return to normal before the failure is understood.** Worth carrying into ADR-007's exercise objectives explicitly. **③ (b)/(c) OFFER A DESIGN POSITION THE ENGAGEMENT SHOULD CONSIDER ADOPTING: cyber crisis runs INSIDE the existing crisis organisation, not beside it.** NS uses its overarching crisis organisation and processes, adding only a **cyber-specific plan** and a **cyber-specific operational team (the "Blue Cyber Shield Team")**, with the tactical and strategic tiers explicitly "**not cyber specific**". **That is structurally the same argument ADR-012 makes about change: one gate, not a parallel regime** — here applied to crisis response rather than change control. It also matches ADR-012's design decision 4 (NIS-2 Art 21/23 embedded in the operator's existing management system rather than a separate security process). **The escalation ladder — cyber incident → cyber calamity → crisis, graded by severity/impact, with disciplines widening as it climbs — is a concrete model for how an oversight layer's alerts (R12) should escalate**, and is worth setting beside R10's decision-class gating. **④ (e) IS THE MOST DIRECTLY REUSABLE MATERIAL FOR ADR-007.** Annual planned exercises; **joint exercises with the infrastructure manager, jointly prepared**; participation in the **national exercise (ISIDOOR)**; and **evaluation of every exercise** across both general crisis processes and cyber-specific aspects. Read with Traficom's zero-budget national tabletop drawing 20+ organisations at CISO level (E-2026-08-15-12), the Estonian minister's call for "more joint exercises… specifically focused on industrial control systems and the particularities of rail" (E-2026-08-15-24), and **Cyber Europe** as the EU-level vehicle (E-2026-08-15-25), **the register now holds a coherent four-source picture of what a credible rail cyber-exercise programme looks like — national, sectoral, RU-IM joint, and supplier-inclusive.** ADR-007's test strategy should reference it. **(f)'s "practice… with SUPPLIERS" is the piece most often missing** and connects to the third-party exposure documented at E-2026-08-15-16 (86% have TRM policies, 24% staff them) and E-2026-08-15-25 (the Ridango service-provider compromise). **⑤ (a) IS A GENUINELY NOVEL DATUM: A PLANNED HIGH-THREAT EVENT USED AS A LIVE CRISIS REHEARSAL.** NS prepared for the **2025 NATO Summit** across NS, sectoral (**Spoor-ISAC**) and national (**NCTV/NCSC**) tiers, with **CTI-driven use-case modification**, and then **reused the approach for other major events** — the lesson "**use a 'planned' crisis**". For an engagement that must prove R4 by test (ADR-007) without being able to stage a real national failure, **using scheduled high-risk events as rehearsal vehicles is a cheap, realistic and politically defensible route**, and it is now evidenced as working practice at a national operator. **⑥ SMALLER POINTS:** the **Spoor-ISAC** appears again as the sectoral umbrella (cf. E-2026-08-15-13, where NS mapped it in the Dutch landscape), confirming it as a functioning coordination layer rather than a name on a diagram; the strategic tier explicitly handles **supervisory bodies** and press, which is where NIS-2 Art 23 reporting and CRA Art 14 notification would attach (ADR-012 action 4, and the "multiple notifications" problem EPSF raised at E-2026-08-15-35); and the chain to **CMT ProRail** shows RU↔IM crisis coupling in practice. **⑦ HONESTY LIMITS:** this is a self-account with no metrics — no exercise outcomes, no detection or response times, no incident record — and **eight slides of organisational and scenario detail are unread images**, so the model is captured at the level of structure rather than substance. It evidences *practice*, not *effectiveness*. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** ADR-007 unchanged in status (this is input to its test/exercise strategy, not a completed test); **R3/R4 stay Open — the trade-off is corroborated, nothing is proven**; R12/R10 Proposed; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R8 Recommended. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) the **eight unread diagram slides** — the combined and national crisis-organisation charts and the scenario/IT-impact material would sharpen the escalation model if re-rendered; (2) **ISIDOOR** — the Dutch national cyber exercise, as a model for the ADR-007 programme; (3) whether NS's **Cyber Crisis Management Plan** structure is publicly described anywhere; (4) how the sector handles the **extortion/payment decision** against a safety duty — a governance question the register has not examined.
