+| E-2026-08-15-38 | 2026-08-15 | **PONTUS BLOMQVIST (Swedish Transport Administration / Trafikverket; Member, EIM Cybersecurity WG) and ANDERS JONSSON (Stockholm Public Transport Authority / SL; consultant; Member, ENISA AHWG EUCS; Observer, ENISA AHWG on the Cybersecurity Market)** — "**Proactive Collaboration within the transport sector is needed for managing resilience and operational efficiency under NIS2 AND CER DIRECTIVES**"; **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 02.12.2025**; 13 slides, marked "Version 1.0" and "**K1**" (an unexplained marking, possibly a Swedish information class — treat cautiously). **THE FIRST SUBSTANTIVE CER-DIRECTIVE MATERIAL IN THIS REGISTER** — see the gap note. Paraphrased: **(a) EU CYBERSECURITY STRATEGY GOALS** as framed by the presenters — strengthen resilience (NIS2 & CER, "ensure all Member States reach a common high level"); build collective defence and information-sharing; **secure digital technologies and supply chains** ("security by design", the **CRA**, and "**reduce Europe's dependence on non-EU technologies** and ensure trustworthy supply chains"); develop skills and ecosystem (**ECCC**); promote a global, open, secure internet. **(b) THE CENTRAL CLAIM** — "when we began working with the directives, we quickly recognized that **rail-bound public transport, particularly in LARGE CITIES, plays an even MORE CRITICAL ROLE for [the] functioning of other essential sectors than traditional railway services**". Stockholm scale given as **2.5 million boarding passengers per working day (2025)** — metro 439 cars (39%), bus 2,396 vehicles (38%), commuter train 129 cars (15%), tram/local train 120 cars (8%), boat 25 vessels + 50 chartered (<1%); 1.5M Saturdays, 1.2M Sundays; with a 2050 plan raising work trips from 56% (2015) to 64% and business trips from 43% to 57%. **(c) AREAS OF SECTOR-SPECIFIC COLLABORATION** (slide repeated) — cybersecurity **training (IT/OT)** and participation in European programmes; "**develop and share a COMMON METHOD FOR NIS2 — how to identify and assess CRITICAL FUNCTIONS, IT & OT services**"; "**analyze incident and report hierarchy — WHEN IS THE INCIDENT CRITICAL ENOUGH TO REPORT?**"; "**data-driven dynamic maintenance reduces costs. But it can also increase RESILIENCE**"; "**what can we learn from each other about AI, share experience within our sector**"; and "**collaboration to secure the supply chain within our transport sector — CRA**". **(d) A JOINT WORKSHOP announced** — "cooperation with local NSA and **financed together with ENISA**", **5 February 2026, 09.00-16.30, Trafikförvaltningen, Stockholm**, with an open invitation via "your local NSA/ENISA". **(e) THE CORE SLIDE — NIS2 AND CER SIGNIFICANCE CRITERIA SIDE BY SIDE, WITH A LAYERED FUNCTION MODEL.** *NIS2 significant incident*: "an incident shall be considered to be significant if it has caused or can cause **severe operational disruption of the services (public transport) or financial loss** for the entity concerned; [or] it has affected or can affect other natural or legal persons by causing **considerable material or non-material damage**" → **notification within 24 h · incident report within 72 h · full report within 30 days**. *CER significant incident / disruptive effect*: "the following should be considered — the **number of users** relying on the essential service; the extent to which **other critical sectors depend** on the essential service; the **duration** of the disruption; and the **geographical area** affected, considering whether the area is **geographically isolated**" → **incident report 24 h · full report in 30 days**. *And a three-tier function model keyed to time-to-effect*: **primary functions & IT/OT services** ("to be able to carry out your mission"), exemplified by **traffic control** → "**direct effects on the traffic — minutes/hours**"; **secondary functions/services** → "**indirect effect on the traffic — days/weeks**"; **other systems/services to facilitate operations** → "**low effect on the traffic**". **(f) A QUANTIFIED REPORTING THRESHOLD — explicitly a "(Discussed proposal)".** Framing: "SL manage 2.5 million boarding passengers per day and every ticket is valid for 75 min at all type of traffic modes! **Our conclusion is that we can only act on our PLANNED REGULARITY!**" The proposed **§6**: "a significant incident that has caused serious operational disruption to the service offered is an incident where the unavailability or reduced functionality of one or more sector critical systems has meant that **(a) the sector's operations can only be provided to a limited extent, corresponding to LESS THAN 95 PERCENT OF PLANNED DEPARTURES during a traffic day (24 h) PER TRAFFIC MODE**, [or] **(b) sector operations have had to use ALTERNATIVE TRAFFIC MODES OR METHODS to offer the service for MORE THAN SIX HOURS**" (traffic mode = metro, bus, tram, local train, boat, train…). **(g) A CRA CATEGORY TABLE** — "CRA is necessary to secure transparency and security in the supply chain": *common consumer products* (low; smart home devices, apps) → self-assessment to standards, CE; *medium risk products* (medium; **operating systems, firewalls, VPN**) → self-assessment, CE; *high-risk products* (high; **SCADA, network control, cloud security**) → **third-party assessment, EUCC, EUCS**; ***Annex IV* extra critical products** (critical; **smartcards, control systems, crypto HSM/KMS**) → **third-party assessment, EUCC, EUCS**. **(h) AN UNSOURCED QUOTED CLAIM** — "'**90% of the data packages are routed back to China from their electric vehicles**'", presented in quotation marks with **no attribution**. **(i)** A closing reference to "**Inside the Lion Cage**" as "a good example on how we should collaborate to raise resilience in our sector" (unexplained in-deck). md5 7f4fca26…e291; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 02.12.2025 | A (primary — two named officials of public authorities describing their own sector collaboration and their own proposed method; **FIVE FLAGS**: **the §6 threshold at (f) is explicitly a "(Discussed proposal)"**, not adopted law or an agreed sector position; **the NIS2/CER criteria at (e) and the CRA table at (g) are the presenters' READING**, not quoted legal text, and must be verified against Dir (EU) 2022/2555, Dir (EU) 2022/2557 and Reg (EU) 2024/2847 respectively; **(h) is an unsourced assertion** and is **not citable**; **URBAN/metropolitan public transport is the primary subject**, adjacent to but not identical with the mainline-rail scope of this engagement; **"K1" marking unexplained** — treat handling cautiously pending clarification) | **R14/ADR-012 — CER + NIS2 reporting and CRA categories** (+ **R3/R4 criticality tiering & incident thresholds**; R12 incident detection/reporting; R8/PR13 supply chain) | validate | **A — and it CLOSES, or at least substantially opens, the register gap flagged at E-2026-08-15-13.** That row recorded that "**CER**", "**Critical Entities Resilience**" and "**2022/2557**" had **zero occurrences** across both evidence logs, and that pulling Dir (EU) 2022/2557 was the highest-value regulatory follow-up because CER — the physical/organisational-resilience sibling of NIS-2 — could bear on **R3 and R4** from a direction never examined. **This is the first substantive CER material on file, and it confirms the concern was well founded: CER carries its own significance test and its own 24 h / 30-day reporting duty, distinct from NIS-2's.** It does **not** discharge the follow-up — this is a practitioner reading, not the legal text — but it now has a shape, and **obtaining Dir (EU) 2022/2557 moves from "unexamined" to "specifically needed".** **① (e) IS THE MOST USEFUL SINGLE SLIDE AND IT LANDS ON ADR-012 ACTION 4.** EPSF flagged "**possible multiple events notifications (and… multiple reports)**" as an unresolved burden (E-2026-08-15-35), and ADR-012 action 4 proposes aligning the reporting chain so an incident is "reported once, correctly, to the right authority". **Here are two of the regimes side by side with different tests and different clocks**: NIS-2 turns on *severe operational disruption or financial loss to the entity, and material/non-material damage to others*, at **24 h / 72 h / 30 days**; CER turns on *number of users, dependence of other critical sectors, duration, and geographic isolation*, at **24 h / 30 days**. **The tests are not co-extensive** — CER's cross-sector-dependency and geographic-isolation criteria have no NIS-2 counterpart — **so a single incident can qualify under one and not the other, and the "report once" ambition in ADR-012 action 4 is harder than it looks.** Add the CRA Art 14 vulnerability/incident tracks (E-2026-07-10-02) and rail safety reporting, and the operator faces **four** regimes. Recommend ADR-012 action 4 be re-scoped to *map* the regimes and their triggers before assuming they can be collapsed. **② (e)'s THREE-TIER FUNCTION MODEL IS A DIRECTLY REUSABLE CRITICALITY METHOD, KEYED TO TIME-TO-EFFECT.** Primary functions (traffic control) → **direct effect in minutes/hours**; secondary → **indirect effect in days/weeks**; other → low effect. **That is a cleaner basis for ADR-012 action 1 (the PDE inventory and criticality classification) than a purely asset-type taxonomy**, because it ranks by *how fast loss propagates to service* — which is exactly the property R3 and R4 care about. It also complements the **Cyber Critical Asset (CCA)** concept from IEC 63452 that CRSG and EPSF both prioritise (E-2026-08-15-30, -35): CCA says *which* assets matter, this says *how fast* their loss bites. **③ (f) IS THE MOST CONCRETE THING IN THE CORPUS ON "WHEN IS AN INCIDENT REPORTABLE" — AND IT IS WORTH TESTING AGAINST 23 JUNE.** The proposed threshold — **<95% of planned departures over a 24 h traffic day per traffic mode**, or **>6 hours on alternative modes** — converts a legal abstraction into something an operations centre can measure. Their reasoning is instructive: "we can only act on our **planned regularity**", i.e. the only reliable real-time signal is departures against plan. **Applied to the 23-June DB outage (~2 hours, nationwide, services halted), limb (a) would almost certainly have been breached** — a useful sanity check that the threshold is set at a meaningful level, and a candidate model if the client needs a reportability trigger. **Carry the caveat that it is a discussed proposal, not adopted**, and that it is calibrated to a dense metropolitan network with modal substitution available — mainline rail has fewer alternatives, so the (b) limb may not transfer. **④ (g) GIVES THE ADOPTED-CRA CATEGORY STRUCTURE WITH RAIL-RELEVANT EXAMPLES — AND ONE MAPPING MATTERS IMMEDIATELY.** Unlike the proposal-state "default / Class I / Class II / highly critical" split recorded from DG CNECT (E-2026-08-15-09), this presents four tiers with **Annex IV "extra critical products"** at the top requiring **third-party assessment (EUCC/EUCS)**, and it names **"crypto HSM/KMS"** and "**control systems**" in that tier, with **SCADA, network control and cloud security** as high-risk. **Set against E-2026-08-15-36, where EBA describes oKMC using an HSM to encrypt and store ETCS keys, the German online key-management centre would fall in or near the top CRA tier and require third-party conformity assessment rather than self-declaration.** That is a concrete, checkable consequence for ADR-012 action 1 and for procurement — and it sharpens the point made at E-2026-08-15-29 that a supplier's default offer will be self-assessment plus CE marking. **Verify the tiering and the Annex IV contents against Reg (EU) 2024/2847 before relying on it.** **⑤ (b) IS A SCOPE ARGUMENT THE ENGAGEMENT SHOULD NOTE RATHER THAN ADOPT.** The claim that **urban rail is more critical to other essential sectors than mainline rail** is plausible for metropolitan economies and is the reasoning behind treating metro/tram as critical entities — it also explains why **UITP** and urban rail now sit inside CRSG (E-2026-08-15-30) and why IEC 63452's scope explicitly covers metros, trams and trolleybuses (E-2026-08-15-32). For this engagement it is **adjacent context**, not directly applicable: FRMCS and the GSM-R transition are mainline/ERTMS concerns. Useful when explaining why the sector's cyber governance keeps widening beyond mainline rail. **⑥ (c) CONTAINS A QUIET AI ITEM WORTH FLAGGING** — "**what can we learn from each other about AI, share experience within our sector**" — the first appearance in this conference corpus of AI as a *sector collaboration topic* rather than a threat vector. Given ADR-002's agentic oversight layer and ADR-003's AI-Act classification, sector-level AI experience-sharing is a venue the engagement may want to track. **⑦ REJECT (h).** "90% of the data packages are routed back to China from their electric vehicles" is quoted without attribution, without a subject ("their"), and without a source. **It must not be repeated in any engagement artefact.** It sits under a legitimate policy heading (reducing dependence on non-EU technology, a real EU strategy goal and kin to the R8/PR13 vendor-concentration thread) — but the claim itself is unsupported here and would damage credibility if carried. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed — though action 4 should be re-scoped, see ①); R3/R4 unchanged; R12 Proposed; R8 Recommended. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **Dir (EU) 2022/2557 (CER) itself — now specifically needed**: does it apply to rail infrastructure managers, and what are its resilience obligations beyond incident reporting? This is the gap from E-2026-08-15-13, now sharpened rather than closed; (2) **map the four reporting regimes** (NIS-2, CER, CRA Art 14, rail safety) with their triggers and clocks — the input ADR-012 action 4 needs; (3) verify the **CRA Annex IV** tiering and whether **HSM/KMS** products sit there, given oKMC; (4) the **5 February 2026 Stockholm workshop** (ENISA co-financed) — recent, and its outputs may exist; (5) identify "**Inside the Lion Cage**". +| E-2026-08-15-39 | 2026-08-15 | ⚠️ **MARKED "Restricted \| © Infrabel 2025" ON EVERY SLIDE — see handling.** **CÉDRIC CECOTTI, "Chief Cyber-Resilience Architect" (titled in-deck "from 2025 to 2045"), INFRABEL** — "**A «Railway» CyberSOC (Episode 2)**"; **5th ENISA-ERA Conference, Tallinn, 2025**; 33 slides. **His third appearance in the series** (3rd conference "A CyberSOC for Railway, why and how"; 4th "CISO Forum updates"; this). Paraphrased: **(a) MATURITY TIMELINE** — **2019 Infrabel designated OSE**; *before 2020* cyber-security management "with due care and diligence"; *NIS1, 2020-2024* adding critical-services protection measures, a **dedicated cyber-security team**, risk analyses, annual internal audits and an external audit in 2022; *NIS2, 2025-2027* adding a **focus on NIS2 certification**, "**a division to manage Cyber Resilience**", and a **training programme for all employees** — with scope widening from **critical services** to **all Infrabel**. **(b) FIVE NIS2 OBJECTIVES** — securing the entire **IT/OT/Railway** perimeter · culture, awareness & training · maturity of controls → certification · **CyberSOC and crisis management** · security-by-design. **(c) TARGETS AND APPROACH** — explicit **MTTD** ("average time to recognize a threat requiring further analysis and response") and **MTTR** ("average time to respond and ultimately resolve the incident"); a risk-based strategy in which the **CyberSOC spans Detect–Respond–Recover**, alongside monitoring, audits and **pentests**. **(d) THE PERIMETER** — "24/7 monitoring and incident response", "end-to-end defense in depth" across **tracks, signalling, catenary, traffic management, power, tunnels, warehouse, end-users**, on-prem/hybrid/cloud, SaaS and IaaS/PaaS; OT items named include **IXL (interlocking), ERTMS, SCADA, cameras, hypervisors, access and intrusion detection, sensors**. **(e) DELIVERY MODEL** — "Infrabel has chosen to set up a CyberSOC with a **MANAGED SERVICES CONTRACT** in which the partner supports Infrabel"; the partner is named in the take-aways as **THALES**. Services are mapped to **GOVERN / IDENTIFY / PROTECT / DETECT / RESPOND / RECOVER**, including asset management, **network segmentation**, patch management, **threat hunting**, "collecte des logs → Big Data → CyberSOC", vulnerability management, **CSIRT + 3rd lines with suppliers**, **incident-response playbooks**, "**crisis management + NIS2 notification**", **DRP + testing**, backup/restore + tests and **root-cause analysis**. **(f) USE CASES** — defined as "a security monitoring scenario aimed at identifying cyber threats", spanning strategic, tactical and operational levels "from the highest level (cybercriminals' modus operandi) to the lowest level (security events, logs)", each linked to **playbooks** and to operational impacts, with every detection creating an incident. Fifteen "**prevention**" use cases (compliance to NIS2, **IEC 62443**, asset inventory, identity and access, system hardening, removable media, external hardware, network access, **network segmentation**, firewall rules, endpoint configuration, secure remote access, internet connections, DevSecOps) and fifteen "**detection**" use cases (unknown/rogue asset, vulnerability attacks, system changes, network scan, unknown remote access, malware/ransomware, phishing/social engineering, **supply-chain attacks**, **(D)DoS**, **man-in-the-middle**, data breach/loss, compromised credentials, unusual privileged-account behaviour, **physical attacks**). **(g) ASSETS BY NUMBERS** — cloud: +50 log sources, 2+1 datacentres; **80,000 identities**, 10,000 emails, 700 applications; 8,000 PCs, 5,000 smartphones; OT/ICS: **10,000 cameras**, 5 tunnels, 5,000 buildings/shelters; 3,000 servers, 1,000 databases; **10,000 network devices, 100,000 IP addresses**; and **railway infrastructure: 3,600 km of track, 700 BTS GSM-R, 32 RBC ETCS L2, 800 level crossings, 10,000 signals**. **(h) DATA BY NUMBERS** — "**+700 billion logs indexed (yearly)**", "**+640 data ingestion pipelines**", "**over 900 TB of data**", +70 Kibana spaces and +200 unique Kibana users in the last month; and the note that "**cybersecurity represents +60% of logs data**". Flow: assets → logs & events → **unified data lake** → detection → incident response → **crisis management**, enriched by CMDB, threats, vulnerabilities, risks & mitigation, risk register, use cases, strategy, policies and NIS2. **(i) SECURITY ARCHITECTURE** — "all interconnections and data flows are secured by design", showing **Zones «Railway»** and **Zone «Infrabel»** with a **log collector → gateway → firewall → PAM → VPN → Kafka/ELK → CyberSOC** path, plus DMZ, third-party probes and third-party solutions each traversing FW/PAM/VPN. **(j) RESULTS — USE-CASE COVERAGE, headed "We didn't achieve 100% coverage…":** cloud **100%** · identities/emails/apps **90%** · IT end-user devices **100%** · **OT/ICS devices 25%** ("extension of logs collection to OT systems…") · IT infrastructure & data **90%** · network infrastructure **90%** · **RAILWAY INFRASTRUCTURE 10%** ("**need to be restarted from the beginning with our suppliers…**") · **TOTAL = 60%**. Roll-out slide: "we are here → 60%… we still have a lot of work to do with our suppliers and teams to deploy **OT and Railway use cases**" (Q2 2025). **(k) INCIDENTS** — "**715 incidents in Q3 2025**", "**36% incidents escalated in Q3 2025**", with trend charts for escalated/resolved-by-SOC and **MTTD in minutes**, and an outcome trend split **BTP / TP / FP** (chart values not readable). **(l) IVMS** — an "Integrated Vulnerability Management Service": multi-source vulnerability aggregation, contextualised risk analysis, automated prioritisation and remediation guidance, continuous monitoring and reporting. **(m) TAKE-AWAYS** — collaboration between Infrabel and **Thales**; **improved visibility** on systems and architectures; **improved root-cause analysis**; raising awareness/knowledge through incident feedback; "**not easy journey, but the path is more important**". **(n) LESSONS LEARNED on building an OT and Railway CyberSOC** — "**raise knowledge before starting → project team**"; "**maturity in risk assessment → use cases on OT/Railway**"; "**do not overlook the difficulty of collecting QUALITY logs**"; "**don't deploy systems and only if this is really necessary**"; "**your suppliers are keys, but…**". READ: complete extractable text (33 slides; the defence-in-depth map, data-flow, architecture and incident-trend charts read via labels — **numeric values on the incident/MTTD charts are not readable**) | Operator conference presentation (primary — an infrastructure manager's chief cyber-resilience architect reporting his own programme, with operational metrics) | file "session 7-1 - infrabel - a « railway » cybersoc.pdf", Download folder; md5 1b22903b…926d; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 2025 | A (primary first-hand operator account **carrying real operational numbers** — asset counts, log volumes, coverage percentages and incident counts — which is rare in this corpus; **FIVE FLAGS**: **⚠️ "Restricted \| © Infrabel 2025"** on every slide, see handling; **self-reported and unaudited**; **Thales is the managed-services partner**, so the framing is partner-inflected even though the figures are Infrabel's; **chart values unreadable**, so MTTD/MTTR are defined but not disclosed numerically; **Belgian IM scale**, materially smaller than DB InfraGO, so figures are indicative not transferable) | **R12 — the strongest empirical grounding on file** (+ **R8/PR13 supplier dependency**; R14/ADR-012 NIS2 & CyberSOC; R11/ADR-004 zone/log-egress architecture; R3/R4 visibility & RCA; ADR-007 pentests) | validate | **⚠️ HANDLING: "Restricted | © Infrabel 2025".** Logged because this repository is **PRIVATE** (verified). **Do not reproduce Infrabel's figures or architecture in outward-facing material without consent.** Sixth handling class in today's set — the policy is overdue. **THIS IS THE MOST IMPORTANT R12 ARTEFACT IN THE REGISTER, AND ITS CENTRAL NUMBER SHOULD GO TO THE CLIENT.** **(j) MEASURES THE DETECTION GAP FROM THE INSIDE OF A REAL, RUNNING, 24/7 RAIL CYBERSOC: cloud 100%, IT end-user devices 100%, network and IT infrastructure 90% — but OT/ICS 25% and RAILWAY INFRASTRUCTURE 10%. Total 60%.** An infrastructure manager ingesting **700 billion logs a year across 900 TB through 640 pipelines** has achieved **one tenth** of its intended monitoring coverage **on the railway estate itself**. **The detection gap is therefore not a maturity abstraction or a survey artefact — it is an operator's own measured shortfall, and it is worst exactly where the safety-relevant systems are.** This converts a four-year, multi-source qualitative finding into a single hard figure: ENISA measured detection and log correlation as the low-maturity areas in 2020 (E-2026-08-15-03); attributed it to expertise and resources, "**even more pronounced for OT systems, managed more generally by IMs**", in 2021 (E-2026-08-15-06); reported "for the OT (safety related) ones, **there is a gap**" (E-2026-08-15-04); and its investment survey found detection skills the least funded of ten, with 28% of transport OES having no SOC at all (E-2026-08-15-16). **Infrabel is one of the operators that *does* have a SOC, and it is at 10% on railway infrastructure.** **② THE STATED CAUSE IS SUPPLIER DEPENDENCY, AND IT IS THE SAME MECHANISM THE REGISTER HAS TRACKED ELSEWHERE.** The railway line reads "**need to be restarted from the beginning with our suppliers…**", the roll-out slide says the remaining work is "with our **suppliers** and teams to deploy OT and Railway use cases", and the closing lesson is "**your suppliers are keys, but…**" — the ellipsis doing considerable work. **An IM cannot monitor equipment whose logs, interfaces and cooperation it does not control.** That is R8/PR13 (vendor opacity and fix-dependency) expressed as an observability failure, and it is the operational counterpart to the CRA's SBOM and vulnerability-handling duties (ADR-012 actions 1-2), to the NIS-2 "important entity" status of rail manufacturers (E-2026-08-15-18) and to the third-party-risk funding gap ENISA measured (86% policy / 47% budget / 24% role, E-2026-08-15-16). **Procurement is the lever: monitorability — log formats, feeds and access — must be a contractual requirement, not a hoped-for cooperation.** Recommend adding it explicitly to ADR-012 action 2 alongside SBOM and support-period terms. **③ (g) PUTS GSM-R AND ETCS INSIDE A CYBERSOC SCOPE — DIRECTLY RELEVANT TO THIS ENGAGEMENT.** The monitored railway estate includes **700 GSM-R base stations, 32 ETCS L2 RBCs, 10,000 signals and 800 level crossings**. **This is the first evidence on file of an IM bringing its radio bearer and ERTMS estate into cyber monitoring at all** — and it is at 10% coverage. For the FRMCS transition the implication is direct and actionable: **the bearer's monitorability should be designed in from the start rather than retrofitted against suppliers later.** That is precisely the gap the register documented from the specification side, where FRMCS SRS §15 places monitoring out of scope and **§17.2 system monitoring is EMPTY** (E-2026-07-12-02), after ERA had delegated radio-device security into the FRMCS specs (E-2026-08-15-04). **Infrabel's 10% is what that specification gap looks like in an operations centre.** **④ (i) IS AN OUT-OF-BAND MONITORING ARCHITECTURE AND IT ALIGNS WITH ADR-004.** Logs leave **Zones «Railway»** through a **log collector → gateway → firewall → PAM → VPN** into Kafka/ELK in **Zone «Infrabel»**, where the CyberSOC consumes them; third-party probes and vendor solutions traverse the same controlled path. **That is telemetry egress from the vital estate to an analysis domain across a mediated boundary** — the same shape as ADR-004's unidirectional read path, and as the IEC 63452 Annex K rule that monitoring data from OT to a cloud instance "should be implemented in a uni-directional fashion" (E-2026-08-15-33). **A working operator instance of the boundary ADR-004 proposes, with PAM as the access-control mechanism — worth citing as design-basis precedent.** **⑤ (n) IS THE MOST USEFUL SET OF LESSONS FOR ANYONE BUILDING THIS.** "**Do not overlook the difficulty of collecting QUALITY logs**" is the practical form of the observability problem — quantity is not the constraint (700 billion logs) but *quality and coverage* are. "**Raise knowledge before starting**" and "**maturity in risk assessment → use cases on OT/Railway**" say the method must precede the tooling; note that Infrabel's use cases are derived from risk assessment, which connects to the ARAMIS/SL-T and 63452 risk-method threads (E-2026-08-15-34, -32). "**Don't deploy systems and only if this is really necessary**" is a restraint principle worth carrying into any oversight-layer design — every added system is added attack surface and added telemetry burden. **⑥ (k) AND (c) GIVE R12 ITS OPERATIONAL VOCABULARY:** **715 incidents in Q3 2025 with 36% escalated**, tracked against **MTTD/MTTR**, with outcomes classified **benign-true-positive / true-positive / false-positive**. **That is the quantitative shape an oversight layer's alerting must fit** — and the 36% escalation rate is a useful anchor for the automation-bias and alert-fatigue concerns in ADR-004/R10: roughly two thirds of incidents are resolved without escalation, so the human-in-command gate sees a filtered stream whose filtering is itself a trust dependency. **⑦ (a) AND (e) ARE GOVERNANCE DATA POINTS:** Infrabel created "**a division to manage Cyber Resilience**" under NIS2, and delivers the SOC through a **managed-services contract** rather than fully in-house — a different answer from SNCF's "internalization of penetration testing competencies" (E-2026-08-15-15) and worth setting beside it when advising on build-versus-buy for the capacity constraint. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** **R12 stays Proposed · ADR-002** — this is the strongest justification yet for the Risk Sentinel/Assurance layer, and it sharpens the design constraint (an oversight layer cannot assume a mature detection substrate; on the railway estate there may be almost none), but it decides nothing; R8 Recommended; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R11/ADR-004 Proposed; R3/R4 unchanged. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **"Episode 1"** — the 3rd-conference deck "A CyberSOC for Railway, why and how" (E-2026-08-15-21), which would give the design rationale behind these results; (2) whether the **railway-infrastructure coverage** has improved since Q2/Q3 2025 and what unblocked it with suppliers — the single most useful follow-up for R12; (3) **monitorability as a procurement clause** — draft for ADR-012 action 2; (4) the **CISO Forum** Infrabel reported at the 4th conference (E-2026-08-15-22), as a sector venue for exactly this problem. +| E-2026-08-15-40 | 2026-08-15 | ⚠️ **TLP:GREEN.** **DAVID VONK, Senior Inspector NIS-directive, ILT (Inspectie Leefomgeving en Transport / Human Environment & Transport Inspectorate), Directorate Oversight and Investigation, DEPARTMENT CYBERSECURITY OVERSIGHT** — "**Increasing the Pressure for Cybersecurity in Dutch Rail Sector: Combined approach to decrease vulnerability of LEGACY SYSTEMS**"; **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 2025**; 8 slides (sparse, bullet-only; one footer carries a stray "27 februari 2017" template date beside "10 december 2025"). **The register's FIRST ENFORCEMENT-SIDE artefact** — a supervisor describing how it actually compels compliance. Paraphrased: **(a) THE PROBLEM, stated bluntly — "INSECURE TRAINS":** "**No security before 2015**"; "**2015-2025 mostly insecure**"; "**Not NIS(2) compliant**"; and on type/vehicle approval, retrofit is "**expensive to retrofit**" and "**complex to retrofit**". **(b) THE APPROACH — "combined forces":** *use of the **SERA-directive*** — "**Requirements Capture + NIS(2)**"; *use of **existing developments*** — "**ERTMS implementation**"; and *communication* — obligations, **consequences**, "**Acceptable Means of Compliance (e.g. PASSIVE NETWORK MONITORING)**", and "possibilities for **collaboration between RUs**". **(c) RESULTS AND FUTURE** — "**one RU changed designs of trains**"; "**now working with another RU**"; future: continue with RUs, "**add infrastructure (same legal construct)**", and extend to "**tram, metro, bus**". **(d) EXTRA — ACCEPTABLE MEANS OF COMPLIANCE, in two families:** *monitoring* — "**IDS implementation DURING RETROFIT**" and "**PIS while other measures unavailable**" (the abbreviation is **not expanded in-deck and is ambiguous** — possibly Passenger Information System used as a proxy signal, possibly a passive-monitoring shorthand; **do not resolve it by guess**); and *physical security* — "**technical panels**", "**doors**", "**toilets**". **(e) EXTRA — AN APPEAL TO SHARE KNOWLEDGE**, three items: "**manufacturers figuring out monitoring rules/use cases**"; "**preventive measures & compartmentalisation**"; and "**cyber incidents — lessons, patterns**". READ: complete text (8 slides) | National-inspectorate conference presentation (primary — a serving senior inspector of the competent supervisory body describing his own enforcement approach) | file "session 7-2 - ilt - increasing the pressure for cybersecurity in dutch rail.pdf", Download folder; md5 8ae1bd63…6696; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 2025 | A (primary — the Dutch transport inspectorate's cybersecurity-oversight department, named senior inspector, on its own supervisory practice and outcomes; **FOUR FLAGS**: **⚠️ TLP:GREEN** — community-limited, see handling; **very sparse deck**, bullet-only, so the legal mechanics behind (b) are asserted rather than explained; **self-reported outcomes** ("one RU changed designs") with no detail or verification; **"PIS" unexplained**; Dutch national scope) | **R13 fleet retrofit** (+ **R2 legacy coexistence**; **R12 monitoring as a compliance route & manufacturer use cases**; R14/ADR-012 NIS-2 enforcement; R8/PR13 manufacturers; R11/ADR-004 compartmentalisation) | validate | **⚠️ HANDLING: TLP:GREEN** — shareable within the community, **not for public release**; logged because this repository is **PRIVATE**. Now the **seventh** distinct handling class in today's set (RESTRICTED · reproduction-forbidden/proprietary · Restricted©Infrabel · TLP:AMBER · TLP:GREEN ×2 · TLP:CLEAR) — **a written handling policy should precede any publication work.** **A — and it opens a perspective the register has been missing entirely: how a supervisor turns obligation into change.** Every prior national artefact described *mandates and competences* (FR/DE/FI/BE); this describes **enforcement technique and its results**. **① (a) IS A REGULATOR SAYING THE QUIET PART PLAINLY, AND IT LANDS ON R13.** "**No security before 2015 · 2015-2025 mostly insecure · not NIS(2) compliant**", with retrofit "**expensive**" and "**complex**". R13 is "make fleet-scale FRMCS retrofit feasible & funded", status **Open — external dependency · ADR-009**, and the register already holds that **approval burden is ~30% of retrofit cost** (E-2026-06-24-13) and that there is **no EU/national fit-obligation** today. **This adds the security dimension: the existing fleet is not merely un-upgraded, it is — in the supervisor's own words — mostly insecure and non-compliant, and the type/vehicle-approval regime is what makes fixing it expensive.** That is the same approval-burden lever R13 and ADR-009 already track (D3iP, OPAL, Umbaucluster), now pointing at cyber. **② (b) IS THE TRANSFERABLE INSIGHT AND IT SHOULD GO INTO THE R13 THINKING: BUNDLE THE CYBER RETROFIT INTO THE CCS RETROFIT ALREADY HAPPENING.** ILT's second lever is "**use of existing developments — ERTMS implementation**": when a vehicle is already opened up and re-approved for ERTMS fitment, the marginal cost and approval burden of adding cyber measures is far lower than a standalone cyber retrofit. **For an engagement whose central programme is a fleet-scale FRMCS/CCS retrofit across 16-21k German vehicles, this is directly applicable — the FRMCS fitment campaign is the cheapest available window for cyber retrofit, and treating them as one workstream rather than two is a concrete recommendation.** It also aligns with the Umbaucluster/Serienzulassung batching logic already in ADR-009. The first lever — using the **SERA directive's requirements-capture** route combined with NIS-2 to reach vehicles — is a legal technique worth understanding, since it is how ILT reached rolling stock at all; **the deck asserts it without explaining it, so the mechanism needs verification before it is relied on.** **③ (b)/(d) INTRODUCE "ACCEPTABLE MEANS OF COMPLIANCE" AS A REGULATORY DEVICE — AND THE AMC IS MONITORING.** Where full retrofit is infeasible, ILT accepts **passive network monitoring**, **IDS implementation during retrofit**, and physical-security compensations. **This is the same compensating-measures logic that CRSG applies to CRA scope (legacy kept "as is" if compensated, documented as SecRACs — E-2026-08-15-30) and that ESCG applies to ERTMS legacy (E-2026-08-15-19), now applied by a supervisor to rolling stock.** Three independent instances of one pattern: **where you cannot fix the component, instrument it and constrain it, and document the conditions.** That is also, notably, **an argument for observability as a regulatory answer, not merely an engineering one** — which strengthens R12's position considerably: monitoring is not just how you detect, it is how you become compliant when you cannot remediate. **④ (d)'s PHYSICAL-SECURITY AMC IS CHEAP, CONCRETE AND EASILY OVERLOOKED — "technical panels, doors, TOILETS".** On trains the technical cabinets are frequently in or adjacent to toilet and vestibule areas; controlling physical access to them is a low-cost compensating control against the on-board access vector. It pairs with SNCF's named rolling-stock attack surfaces — **wireless and MAINTENANCE ACCESS** (E-2026-08-15-15) — and with the Airbus/Alstom zone model where the gateway zone sits between bearer and vital systems (E-2026-08-15-34). **⑤ (e)1 IS THE EXACT PROBLEM INFRABEL REPORTED THE SAME DAY, NOW VOICED BY A REGULATOR.** ILT appeals for "**manufacturers figuring out monitoring rules/use cases**"; Infrabel reported **10% use-case coverage on railway infrastructure** with the note "**need to be restarted from the beginning with our suppliers**" and the lesson "your suppliers are keys, but…" (E-2026-08-15-39). **Operator and supervisor, at the same conference, identifying the same blocker: manufacturers have not defined monitoring use cases for their own equipment.** That is a strong, two-sided evidential base for making **monitorability a procurement requirement** (ADR-012 action 2) rather than a hoped-for cooperation — and it is the operational face of the FRMCS specification gap where §17.2 system monitoring is empty (E-2026-07-12-02). **⑥ (c) IS MODEST BUT REAL — ENFORCEMENT PRODUCED A DESIGN CHANGE.** "One RU changed designs of trains" is the only outcome claimed, and it is unverified — but it is the first evidence in this corpus that **supervisory pressure has actually altered rolling-stock design**, as against strategies, frameworks and working groups. The stated intention to "**add infrastructure (same legal construct)**" and to extend to **tram, metro and bus** indicates the technique is being generalised, and matches the urban-rail widening seen elsewhere (E-2026-08-15-38, -32). **⑦ THE SUPERVISORY POSTURE ITSELF IS THE CONTEXT SIGNAL:** a department titled **Cybersecurity Oversight** inside a transport inspectorate, with a **Senior Inspector NIS-directive**, presenting under the banner "**increasing the pressure**". Read with the Dutch NIS-2 transposition picture (E-2026-08-15-13, -22) and the German supervisory apparatus (E-2026-08-15-11, -36), **the direction of travel across Member States is from guidance to enforcement** — which is the environment ADR-012's conformance work has to land in. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** **R13 stays Open — external dependency · ADR-009**; R2 Accepted; R12 Proposed — though ③ materially strengthens its justification; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R8 Recommended; R11/ADR-004 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **the SERA-directive requirements-capture route** — how ILT legally reaches vehicle cybersecurity through it, since if it generalises it is a supervisory lever other Member States could use; (2) **ILT's published Acceptable Means of Compliance**, if any exist in writing — an AMC list for legacy rolling stock would be directly reusable for R13/ADR-009; (3) what "**PIS**" denotes; (4) whether the "one RU changed designs" case is documented anywhere. +| E-2026-08-15-41 | 2026-08-15 | **TIMON ESSLINGER, CODEWERK GmbH (Siemensallee 75, Karlsruhe)** — "**Cybersecurity for Rolling Stock**"; **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, 02.12.2025**; 18 slides. Paraphrased: **(a) THREE CRA USE CASES FOR ROLLING STOCK** — "**greenfield development of components**" (start from scratch) · "**upgrading existing components**" (enhance existing systems) · "**railway retrofitting**" (adapt legacy trains). **(b) GREENFIELD — "the security lifecycle IN REALITY"**, presented as a wry four-stage degradation: "**sales promised SL3**" → "**how security understood requirements**" → "**security after budgeting**" → "**from a pentester's perspective**". *Key learnings*: "**verify that your hardware is designed for IEC 62443-4-2 SL3**" and "**execute security tests in a very early phase**". **(c) UPGRADING EXISTING COMPONENTS** — *key learnings*: "**prioritised strategy based on an IEC 62443-3-2 risk assessment**" and "**focus on evaluation criteria of IEC TS 62443-6-2**". **(d) THE TECHNICAL CORE — "product development according IEC 62443-4-2 and IEC TS 62443-6-2":** **IEC 62443-4-2** answers "**what shall be implemented?**" and requires of network protocols **confidentiality, integrity and authenticity**; but the **insecure OT protocols in use are named as CAN, MVB, WTB, TRDP and PROFINET**, prompting the question "**is it possible to achieve 4-2 conformity with these protocols?**"; **IEC TS 62443-6-2** answers "**how shall it be tested?**" and supplies the resolution — "**insecure fieldbus protocols CAN BE USED IF COMPENSATING COUNTERMEASURES ARE IMPLEMENTED**". **(e) RAILWAY RETROFITTING** — opens with the objection it must answer: "**why invest in cybersecurity for older trains? They've been operating safely for decades!**"; diagnoses the legacy baseline as a "**FLAT NETWORK ARCHITECTURE WITH DIRECT CONNECTION TO THE OPERATOR**"; and prescribes "**segment the network**", "**add a security gateway**", "**add an intrusion detection system**". **(f) MEASURES IN RETROFITTING PROJECTS**, split two ways: *technical* — **network segmentation, security gateway** — "**greatly improves security, but must be CLOSELY COORDINATED**"; *organizational* — **physical access control, intrusion detection systems** — "**less intrusive changes, but require NEW PROCESSES**". (NB the placement of **IDS under "organizational"** is anomalous — IDS is a technical control; likely a layout artefact, so do not read significance into the classification.) READ: complete text (18 slides; the four-stage "reality" sequence is conveyed by captions over an image) | Vendor/consultancy conference presentation (an engineering and penetration-testing firm) | file "session 7-3 - codewerk - cybersecurity for rolling stocks.pdf", Download folder; md5 f34675e1…2bb; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 02.12.2025. No TLP marking observed | **B — vendor/consultancy.** Codewerk is a commercial engineering and pentest firm; the deck closes with company address and contact and is, in effect, a capability demonstration ("from a pentester's perspective"). **But it is technically specific and standards-grounded rather than marketing-led**, and its claims are checkable against IEC 62443. Flags: **self-selected examples**, no data or case detail; **slide-deck**; the IDS classification anomaly at (f); 2025-12, current | **R13 rolling-stock retrofit** (+ **R2 legacy**; R11/ADR-004 segmentation & gateway; R12 IDS; R14/ADR-012 CRA/62443 route) | validate | **B by tier, and the value is concentrated in (d) and (e) — two specific technical points that sharpen threads the register already runs.** **① (d) NAMES THE INSECURE TRAIN PROTOCOLS AND GIVES THE STANDARDS-SANCTIONED WAY AROUND THEM.** The on-board buses in service — **CAN, MVB (Multifunction Vehicle Bus), WTB (Wire Train Bus), TRDP (Train Real-time Data Protocol) and PROFINET** — offer none of the confidentiality, integrity or authenticity that **IEC 62443-4-2** requires of network protocols, so 4-2 conformity cannot be achieved through the protocols themselves. **IEC TS 62443-6-2's resolution is that insecure fieldbus protocols may still be used where COMPENSATING COUNTERMEASURES are implemented.** The register already carried this problem in general terms — a 2024 MDPI review flagged the "legacy MVB/CAN integration gap" (E-2026-07-29-11) — **but this names the full protocol set and, more usefully, supplies the standards route by which a non-conformant bus can remain in service.** That matters for **R13/R2** because it means **the on-board legacy bus estate does not have to be replaced to reach conformity**, which is the difference between a feasible and an infeasible retrofit programme. **② AND IT IS THE FOURTH INDEPENDENT INSTANCE OF ONE PATTERN — "compensate and document".** CRSG applies it to CRA scope, keeping legacy components "as is" where compensated, recorded as **SecRACs** (E-2026-08-15-30); ESCG applies it to ERTMS legacy with per-phase risk analysis (E-2026-08-15-19); **ILT applies it as a supervisor, accepting passive monitoring and IDS as Acceptable Means of Compliance** (E-2026-08-15-40); and here **IEC TS 62443-6-2 applies it to insecure fieldbus protocols**. **Four different regimes — product regulation, sector standard, national supervision and industrial-security standard — converging on the same mechanism: where the component cannot be made conformant, constrain and instrument it, and document the conditions.** That is now a well-evidenced sector principle and should be stated as such in ADR-012, and echoed in ADR-004's treatment of conditions under which an argument holds. **③ (e)'s "FLAT NETWORK ARCHITECTURE WITH DIRECT CONNECTION TO THE OPERATOR" IS THE LEGACY BASELINE THE REGISTER HAS LACKED IN PLAIN TERMS.** It explains why on-board segmentation matters and why the prescribed remedies — **segment, add a security gateway, add an IDS** — recur across every rolling-stock artefact on file: SNCF's zones-and-conduits practice with wireless and maintenance access as the named surfaces (E-2026-08-15-15), the Airbus/Alstom worked zone model with LTE and satellite modems behind a security gateway (E-2026-08-15-34), SUBSET-147's mandated on-board zoning separating the FRMCS zone from CCS (E-2026-08-01-14), and ILT's AMC list (E-2026-08-15-40). **For the FRMCS onboard segment the implication is the same one this register keeps reaching: the bearer must land behind a gateway in a segmented consist network, not on a flat bus with a path to the operator.** **④ (b) IS A VENDOR MAKING A POINT THAT SUPPORTS A PROCUREMENT POSITION.** The four-stage decline from "**sales promised SL3**" to "from a **pentester's** perspective" is self-serving — a pentest firm arguing that claimed security levels do not survive testing — **but it is the same argument the register has reached from the regulatory side**: tmc noted that "for most products a **self-assessment** confirms CRA compliancy" (E-2026-08-15-29), and the Swedish deck placed **control systems and crypto HSM/KMS in CRA Annex IV requiring third-party assessment** (E-2026-08-15-38). **A supplier's declared Security Level is a claim, not a verified property** — which is precisely why ADR-012 action 2 should state whether the engagement accepts self-declaration or requires independent assessment, and why "**execute security tests in a very early phase**" is sound advice for ADR-007's test surfaces. **⑤ (a)'s THREE USE CASES ARE A USEFUL TAXONOMY** and map cleanly onto CRSG's CRA progressivity model — greenfield ≈ "compliant by design", upgrading ≈ "new baseline", retrofitting ≈ "used as is with mitigating measures and conditions of use" (E-2026-08-15-30). **The engagement's FRMCS programme spans all three simultaneously**, which is worth saying plainly: new FRMCS components are greenfield, the CCS estate they attach to is being upgraded, and the existing fleet is retrofit — three different compliance postures inside one programme, on top of the December-2027 individual-product boundary (E-2026-08-15-28). **⑥ (f)'s HONEST TRADE-OFF IS WORTH CARRYING:** segmentation and gateways "greatly improve security **but must be closely coordinated**" (i.e. they touch the vehicle's functional architecture and its approval), whereas physical access control and monitoring are "**less intrusive… but require new processes**" (i.e. they shift cost from engineering to operations). **That is the retrofit economics question R13 turns on, stated compactly.** **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R13 stays Open — external dependency · ADR-009; R2 Accepted; R11/ADR-004 and R12 Proposed; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **IEC TS 62443-6-2** — the compensating-countermeasures evaluation criteria for insecure fieldbus protocols; if it is obtainable it is directly useful to R13 and to any FRMCS on-board security case, and it is not on file; (2) whether **TRDP/MVB/WTB** security is addressed anywhere in the UNISIG/SUBSET-147 consist-network work already logged, or left entirely to compensating measures; (3) IEC 62443-3-2 as the risk-assessment method referenced here and by ARAMIS (E-2026-08-15-34) — a recurring anchor worth holding directly. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. +| E-2026-08-15-42 | 2026-08-15 | **HARALD REISINGER, Chief Information Officer / Chief Financial Officer, RAILNETEUROPE (RNE), Vienna** — "**From Data Exchange to Digital Tracks — IT**"; **5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 2025**; 18 slides. **Membership**: "nearly all European IMs and ABs are RNE members", the exceptions being "IMs/ABs from **Ireland, Northern Ireland, and parts of the Balkans**". Paraphrased: **(a) CURRENT-STATE DIAGNOSIS** — *planning*: "**isolated with a national focus, lack of strategic planning**", "**cooperation 'islands'**", "tedious planning meetings with limited scalability"; *allocation*: "**varying levels of maturity and digitalization**", "**request country by country for a single train run**", "tedious coordination for changes"; *traffic management*: a "**Common European System (tis.rne.eu)**" alongside "national systems and rules". **(b) TARGET STATE — "Europe with Digital Capacity Management"** — harmonised international traffic flows and "universal European processes"; "**single-place solution for international requests**" with traceable change management; and traffic management "**including all information related to train operation in ONE SYSTEM**", "**reducing national rules and procedures**". **(c) REGULATORY DRIVERS** — the **Telematics TSI** and the **Capacity Regulation**; the implementation strategy spans **Digital Train Information** (data-driven exchange per the Telematics TSI), **Digital Capacity Management** (annual request → late request → rolling planning → short-term request → after allocation; capacity strategy/model/supply; temporary capacity restrictions) and **Digital Infrastructure Information (RINF)**, exchanged among IMs, RUs, service facilities, wagon keepers and multimodal actors. **(d) COMMON INTERFACE (CI) SECURITY — three slides, the substantive part.** *Transport*: "establishes secure **SSL/TLS** communication channels between Common Interface implementations"; "**certificate-based authentication ensures only legitimate CIs can connect to the network**"; "**mutual TLS authentication** verifies identity of both communicating parties"; and — the load-bearing statement — "**all certificates are issued and managed by RNE to ensure trust across the rail network**", with the feature list adding "**protects across intermediaries**". *Message layer, explicitly "according to ERA"*: "**the CI signs outbound messages according to the provisions of ERA TECHNICAL DOCUMENT 104**"; signing uses "a private key derived from the CI's certificate"; messages are encrypted "for confidentiality, ensuring only intended recipients can access"; the "standardized process enables interoperability between different railway CI implementations"; and "**RNE acts as CERTIFICATE AUTHORITY for CI**". *Stated security benefits*: **integrity** (prevents message tampering), **authenticity** (verifies message source), **confidentiality**, and **non-repudiation** ("sender cannot deny sending"). Architecture shown as CI ↔ XML/SOAP over HTTPS ↔ CI, each holding certificate, public and private key, against a CA. **(e) RNE'S INFORMATION SECURITY SYSTEM — four pillars**: **ISO 27001 conformity** ("the ISMS shall be fully aligned with the standard"); **risk orientation** (documents developed "according to the risk matrix and risk appetite of RNE"); **economical sustainability** ("long-term feasible solutions by allowing cost-effective choices and limited administration/documentation need for JO staff"); and **cyber resilience** ("best possible cyber resilience to be reached for the long term commitment and the NC role"). **(f) RISK TREATMENT PROCESS** — risk identification (**asset classification, threat sources, BIA**) → risk analysis and quantification (**likelihood × impact on 4×4-level scales**) → risk evaluation (prioritisation on a risk matrix) → risk treatment (**avoid / reduce / share / accept**), with "**monitoring: continuous tracking, quarterly reports**". **(g) THE ISO 27001 SCALES, R = L × I** — *likelihood*: 0 impossible · 1 every 5 years/extremely rare · 2 once a year/rare · 3 monthly/likely · 4 daily/very likely; ***impact, expressed ENTIRELY IN EUROS***: 0 **< €10k** · 1 **€10k–€250k** · 2 **€250k–€1m** · 3 **€1m–€4m** · 4 **> €4m**. **(h) WHY INFORMATION SECURITY MATTERS FOR RNE** — strengthens trust among IMs and EU institutions; supports legal and regulatory compliance; "**protects continuity of critical European rail coordination**"; "enables secure and resilient operation of digital tools across the EU"; improves consistency and quality of processes; increases credibility toward RUs and applicants. READ: complete extractable text (18 slides; membership maps and the project-portfolio slide are images, unread) | Sector-association conference presentation (primary — the pan-European IM/allocation-body association's CIO on its own systems and security architecture) | file "session 7-4 - rne - from data exchange to digital tracks.pdf", Download folder; md5 c309dc93…4cfc; 5th ERA-ENISA Conference, Tallinn, 2025. No TLP marking observed | A (primary — RNE describing RNE's own platform, membership and ISMS, by its CIO; **THREE FLAGS**: **self-reported** with no assurance evidence (ISO 27001 conformity is stated as an aim — "shall be fully aligned" — not as a certification held); **slide-deck**, several slides unread images; **information-layer scope** — the CI and TIS are planning, allocation and train-information systems, **not** the operational control or bearer layer, so relevance to FRMCS is indirect) | R7 interoperability (+ **R3/R4 European concentration point**; R14/ADR-012 ISMS & PKI; R12 monitoring) | validate | **A, moderate value — the information layer rather than the operational one, but it contributes two things the register should hold.** **① IT IDENTIFIES A PAN-EUROPEAN CONCENTRATION POINT: RNE IS THE CERTIFICATE AUTHORITY FOR THE COMMON INTERFACE, AND NEARLY ALL EUROPEAN IMs AND ALLOCATION BODIES USE IT.** "All certificates are issued and managed by RNE to ensure trust across the rail network", and "RNE acts as Certificate Authority for CI". **That is a single trust anchor underneath the cross-border capacity-request, timetabling and train-information exchange of almost every European infrastructure manager.** For **R3** — "eliminate the central single point of failure" — this is a different *kind* of central point from the one the 23-June outage exposed (that was a national bearer/core; this is a European trust and data-exchange service), but it is a concentration nonetheless, and the register should record it as such. Loss or compromise of the CI trust chain would not stop trains directly — the CI is an information system, not a control system — **but per RNE's own framing it "protects continuity of critical European rail coordination"**, so degradation would impair cross-border path allocation and train information. **Worth noting alongside the other shared-dependency findings and worth asking about: what is RNE's CA continuity and compromise-recovery posture?** **② ERA TECHNICAL DOCUMENT 104 IS NEW TO THIS REGISTER** (zero prior occurrences), and it is the ERA instrument governing **CI message signing**. Together with the mutual-TLS and PKI construction at (d) it means the **information layer already has a mandated, ERA-referenced application-security scheme** — which is a useful contrast to draw: **the message-exchange layer has ERA-specified signing and a working PKI, while the operational layer's cybersecurity requirements in the TSIs are, per the ERTMS Users Group and two national NSAs, absent or scoped only to cross-border interoperability** (E-2026-08-15-19, -10, -35). **The sector has better-specified security for timetable messages than for the systems that move trains.** That is a sharp, defensible observation for client material, and it is grounded entirely in primary sources already on file. **③ (g) IS THE MOST QUIETLY REVEALING SLIDE: RNE'S ISO 27001 IMPACT SCALE IS DENOMINATED PURELY IN EUROS**, topping out at "> €4 million", with **no safety, service-continuity or societal dimension at any tier**. For an ISMS covering systems that coordinate European rail capacity, impact is measured only as financial loss. **This is the safety/security and IT/OT divide showing up inside a risk matrix** — and it contrasts directly with the rail-safety regime the register already holds, where CSM-RA harmonised design targets are consequence-based (10⁻⁹/h catastrophic, 10⁻⁷/h critical, E-2026-07-07-02) and severity is fatality-anchored. **It is a concrete instance of the problem ENISA named as "reconciling safety and cybersecurity worlds" (E-2026-08-15-07) and that UITP's determinism argument attacks from the other side (E-2026-08-15-33).** Not a criticism of RNE — an information-exchange body may reasonably scope impact financially — but **a caution the engagement should carry: an ISO 27001 ISMS imported wholesale into a safety-relevant context will measure the wrong thing unless its impact scale is extended.** Directly relevant to ADR-012, which must couple security management to a safety case rather than run alongside it. **④ (a) IS THE FRAGMENTATION THEME AGAIN, IN A FOURTH DOMAIN.** "Isolated with a national focus", "cooperation islands", "varying levels of maturity and digitalization", "request country by country for a single train run" — the register has now documented European rail fragmentation in **supervisory competence** (FR/DE/FI/BE), **technical specification** (TSI subsets scoped to RBC-Euroradio, per-operator security solutions), **supplier practice** (Alstom's EBIOS/62443/TS 50701 mix) and now **capacity-management IT**. That consistency is worth stating: **fragmentation is not a cybersecurity problem the sector happens to have; it is the sector's structural condition, and cybersecurity inherits it.** **⑤ (f) IS A CONVENTIONAL BUT USABLE ISMS RISK PROCESS** — asset classification, threat sources and BIA feeding a 4×4 likelihood-impact matrix with avoid/reduce/share/accept treatment and **quarterly monitoring reports**. Unremarkable in itself, but it is the fourth risk-method on file (with ARAMIS/EBIOS-RM+62443 at E-2026-08-15-34, IEC 62443-3-2 at E-2026-08-15-41, and the ENISA SCSA method at E-2026-08-15-08) — **another instance of method proliferation, and a reminder that ADR-012 should choose and justify one rather than inherit whichever arrives with a supplier.** **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R7 stays Accepted; R3/R4 unchanged — the CA concentration is recorded as an observation, not a status change; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R12 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **ERA Technical Document 104** — the message-signing provisions, as a primary and as the contrast case for the operational-layer gap; (2) **RNE's CA continuity and compromise posture** — a European single trust anchor deserves the same scrutiny the register applies to other central points; (3) whether the **Telematics TSI** (TAF/TAP) carries cybersecurity requirements the CCS TSI lacks — ERA said in 2020 that IT security would be included in TAF/TAP, OPE and CCS (E-2026-08-15-02), and this deck suggests TAF/TAP-side security is comparatively developed. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. KIN: **E-2026-08-15-19 / -10 / -35 (the CCS-TSI cybersecurity gap this contrasts with)**, E-2026-08-15-02 (ERA's 2020 intent to put IT security into TAF/TAP, OPE and CCS), E-2026-08-15-36 (EDIS — TAF/TAP-TSI implementation, and a national counterpart concentration concern), E-2026-08-01-18 / E-2026-08-01-15 (SUBSET-137 KMC and SUBSET-146 TLS+X.509 — the operational-layer PKI analogue), E-2026-07-07-02 (CSM-RA consequence-based targets — the contrast to (g)), E-2026-08-15-07 / -33 (reconciling safety and security; the determinism argument), E-2026-08-15-34 / -41 / -08 (the other risk methods on file), ADR-012, R7/R3/R4/R14 |
+| E-2026-08-15-43 | 2026-08-15 | **EMMANUELE MASTRODONATO, ERA** — "**The link between Safety Culture & Cyber-security**"; **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, 02-03/10/2024, LILLE** (stated in-document; PDF created 09.10.2024); 24 slides. **The FIRST 4th-conference DECK on file** — the register previously held only that conference's agenda (E-2026-08-15-22) and the IEC PT 63452 deck (E-2026-08-01-20). Presented in the "Safety Culture and Human & Organisational factors" workshop. Paraphrased: **(a) RAILWAY SAFETY CULTURE** — the **European Safety Culture Model 2.0**, built from **Cultural Enablers** ("levers through which an organisational culture develops"), **Behaviour Patterns** ("shared ways of thinking and acting which convey the organisational culture") and **Railway Safety Fundamentals** ("core principles which must be reflected by behaviour patterns to achieve sustainable safety performance"); delivered through a **Safety Culture Programme** "to devise dissemination and evaluation instruments… within the Single European Railway Area", whose key principle is that "**any deliverable is tested with partner organisations**". **(b) ORGANISATIONAL JUST CULTURE (OJC)** — developed through "dedication and consistency", "**open and respectful dialogue (staff report safety issues)**" and "analyse and act upon to improve", to achieve "appropriate, sustainable, safe operational performance"; supported by a **free online basic module** and the **ERA Just Culture Flow** cycle (search for inputs → initialise understanding and care → explore operational reasoning → explore system influences → decide and implement → disseminate outcomes and monitor). **(c) SMS AND THE RU-IM INTERFACE** — the SMS is "the organisation, arrangements and procedures established by an IM or an RU to ensure the safe management of its operations", with **RUs and IMs each responsible for their own part**; "**RUs [and] IMs shall co-operate together to manage the INTERFACE RISKS**", demonstrating through their SMS the ability to identify, assess and control "risks which arise from its own activities **and risks caused by others**"; and the **NSA "has still the ability to check effectively the co-operation arrangements put in place by RUs [and] IMs during its supervision activities"**. On the risk-based approach: "**new/amended rules shall be traced back to the original identified risk**", but "**difficult to relate numerous old rules to risks. There is no single solution**". **(d) SIGNIFICANT CHANGE — Reg (EU) 402/2013** — its criteria for assessing the significance of a safety-related change require "adequate documentation to justify the decision"; "**the question on the significance of a safety-related change does NOT release the railway company from its obligation to perform a proper risk assessment**" demonstrating that safety "is maintained or even improved"; and "**when the change is significant, in addition to the RA, an INDEPENDENT ASSESSMENT BODY must be appointed, to verify independently the correct application of the RA — Annex I Reg. 402-2013**". **(e) CYBERTHREAT IN RAILWAY — the slide that matters most here:** "**'Cyber' risks CANNOT BE DISSOCIATED from railway risks**"; "**Boundary between railway safety and cybersecurity TO BE DETERMINED…**"; "**essential to clarify the functioning at the INTERFACE between the 'worlds' SMS – IMS**"; "the increasing degree of connectivity of the infrastructure and rolling stock (i.e. **DAC – FDFTO**) is creating a **significant increase of their attack surface**"; and "**programmable devices [are] exposed to cyberthreats when 'NOT CONNECTED' — besides communication, we need to protect design, production, maintenance (standards, certifications…)**". **(f) WHAT TO HIGHLIGHT** — the respective scopes of the railway and cybersecurity regulatory frameworks; "**ongoing works aiming to build a SHARED FRAMEWORK between safety and cybersecurity**"; operational subjects justifying "the need for a clear operating framework in the area of '**overall' safety**"; and recommendations from the discussions. **(g) REGULATORY FRAMEWORKS — "terms & competences are different":** *railway safety* = "overall functioning of the system, subsystems (INF, RST, CCS, OPE…)", where "the risks are above all, technical and environmental"; *operating safety* = the ability of components (brakes, etc.) to meet their functions; *cybersecurity* = protecting networks, computers and data against attacks, damage and unauthorised access; and "**IT 'security' includes cybersecurity & physical safety**". Standards named: **IEC 62443**, **CLC/TS 50701**, and **IEC 63452** — described in the future tense as a standard that "**aims to create** a strong, clear, and implementable framework… across rolling stock, fixed installations, management systems and additional services". **(h) IMPLEMENT AND SHARE** — the **ENISA & ER-ISAC report "Building cyber secure Railway Infrastructure" (2022)**, on "sectorial implementation of the NIS Directive"; and the **ENISA Transport Threat Landscape** mapping cyber incidents 2021-2022 across aviation, maritime, railway and road. **(i) THE LINK, in five dimensions, each mapped to named Safety Culture Model elements:** *risk management perspective* → **F1.1 risk awareness, F1.3 questioning attitude**; *organisational awareness and training* → E2.3 organisational systems, **E3.2 competence management**, F2.2 system complexity; *resilience and incident response* → **F1.2 resilience, F2.3 reporting, F3.2 analysis, F3.3 learning from others (cross-sector mitigation)**; *regulatory compliance* → E2.1 roles and responsibilities, E2.2 organisational design, E2.3 organisational systems, E1.3 regulatory relationships — noting an "**area of potential conflict, transparency vs privacy**"; *cultural norms and organisational values* → E1.1 teamwork and collaboration, E1.2 interpersonal values, **E4.1 leading by example, F4.3 decision making**. **(j) RESOURCES** — ERA guides on safety culture, TSI OPE, **human and organisational factors (HOF)**, CSM on SMS, and single safety certificate applications. No TLP marking observed | A (primary EU-agency artefact on ERA's own safety-culture, SMS and CSM frameworks; **THREE FLAGS**: **slide-deck**; **October 2024 vintage — partially superseded by the 5th conference, see the supersession note**; the "link" material at (i) is **conceptual and exhortative** rather than normative — it maps affinities between two disciplines, it does not specify requirements) | **R10 human oversight & safety culture** (+ **R11/ADR-004 the safety-security boundary**; R14/ADR-012 SMS-IMS interface & regulatory scopes; **ADR-011 significant change & AsBo**; R12 reporting culture) | validate | **A — and it is the register's first 4th-conference deck, which makes it the natural baseline against which to measure what the 5th conference superseded.** **⚠️ SUPERSESSION NOTE — WHAT IS OBSOLETE BY THE 5TH CONFERENCE (Tallinn, 12/2025):** **(1) (e)'s "boundary between railway safety and cybersecurity TO BE DETERMINED" is now PARTIALLY OVERTAKEN.** In 10/2024 ERA recorded the boundary as undetermined and the shared framework as "ongoing works" (f). By 12/2025 that framework exists in draft and has a vocabulary: **IEC 63452 adapts the cybersecurity case, threat log and SecRACs from IEC 62278 and carries a "cybersecurity & safety interface" clause area** (E-2026-08-15-32); **UITP's forthcoming report builds "synched safety and security cases" over 75+ management and 230+ design principles for SIL 1-4 systems** (E-2026-08-15-33); and **CRSG applies compensation-and-conditions at system level via SecRACs** (E-2026-08-15-30). **The boundary is no longer "to be determined" so much as "specified in draft and awaiting publication" — cite the 2025 material for the framework and this row only for ERA's 2024 problem statement.** **(2) (g)'s description of IEC 63452 in the FUTURE TENSE ("aims to create") is obsolete** — the CDV was approved by National Committees in 10/2025 with the International Standard forecast ~2026-08 (E-2026-08-15-32). **(3) NOT superseded and still current:** the **European Safety Culture Model 2.0**, **Organisational Just Culture** and the **Just Culture Flow** (a), (b); the **SMS and RU-IM interface-risk duties** (c); and the **Reg (EU) 402/2013 significant-change and independent-assessment-body machinery** (d) — none of which the 5th conference touched. **THE MOST VALUABLE FINDING FOR THIS ENGAGEMENT IS (i): ERA'S SAFETY CULTURE MODEL ALREADY CONTAINS THE COMPONENT ADR-004's AUTOMATION-BIAS OBLIGATION NEEDS.** ADR-004 requires that the human in the loop be "**a genuine decision-maker, not a rubber stamp**", and its assurance argument names automation bias as "the residual path by which an *advisory* system can still contribute to harm if a human over-trusts its output"; R10's oversight design must actively counter it. **ERA's model names "F1.3 QUESTIONING ATTITUDE" and "F1.1 risk awareness" as Railway Safety Fundamentals, and "F4.3 decision making" and "E4.1 leading by example" as behaviour patterns.** **That means the engagement can anchor its automation-bias requirement in an EXISTING ERA framework — with named components, published guidance and an evaluation programme — rather than inventing a bespoke construct.** This is a materially better position than ADR-004 currently assumes, and it is the direct answer to the gap ERA and ENISA themselves confessed in 11/2021, when asked for human-and-organisational-factors cyber risk-assessment tools mappable to the ERA safety-culture model: "**unfortunately, we are not aware of such… tools**" (E-2026-08-15-06). **Here, three years later, ERA is doing exactly that mapping** — and by the 4th conference it had become a full workshop (E-2026-08-15-22). Recommend ADR-004 action item 3 and R10's design cite the **ERA Safety Culture Model 2.0 component identifiers** directly. **(b) ORGANISATIONAL JUST CULTURE IS THE SUBSTRATE R12 DEPENDS ON.** R12 is about closing the awareness gap; its mechanism is an evidence chain and decision-ready alerts. **None of that works if staff do not report.** OJC — "open and respectful dialogue (**staff report safety issues**)", analysed and acted upon — is the cultural precondition for the reporting on which any Risk Sentinel depends, and ERA supplies a **free online module** and a defined flow. Worth naming in ADR-002/R12 as a dependency rather than an assumption. **(c) AND (d) STRENGTHEN ADR-011's POSITION FROM THE AGENCY SIDE.** The **RU-IM interface-risk duty** — each party responsible for its own SMS but jointly for the interface, with the **NSA supervising the co-operation arrangements** — is the governance frame within which a shared bearer like FRMCS sits, and it is the safety-side counterpart to the shared-responsibility problem the cyber material keeps raising. And (d) restates the **CSM-RA significance test with the independent-assessment-body trigger**, which the register already holds verified (E-2026-06-28-06) — **but note the collision now documented: the same modification may face BOTH this safety significance test AND the CRA's "substantial modification" test** (E-2026-08-15-28, -30), which neither ADR-011 nor ADR-012 currently addresses. This row is the safety half of that pair, stated by ERA. **(e)'s TWO SUBSTANTIVE WARNINGS REMAIN CURRENT AND ARE WORTH CARRYING:** **DAC and FDFTO** (digital automatic coupling; full digital freight train operations) are named as connectivity increasing the attack surface — **neither is on file elsewhere in this register**, and DAC in particular is a Europe-wide freight-fleet programme with obvious bearer and on-board-network implications; and "**programmable devices exposed to cyberthreats when NOT CONNECTED**" is the supply-chain/offline exposure point — design, production and maintenance need protecting, not just communication — which anticipates the CRA's manufacturer duties and matches the register's supplier-side findings. **(h) NAMES A REPORT NOT ON FILE**: the **ENISA & ER-ISAC "Building cyber secure Railway Infrastructure" (2022)**, on sectorial NIS implementation — a joint agency/ISAC output the register has never held, and a reasonable follow-up given ER-ISAC's recurring role. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R10/R11 stay Proposed; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; ADR-011 unchanged; R12 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change — **though ADR-004 action 3 and R10 have a concrete, citable anchor here that they currently lack.** **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) the **ERA Safety Culture Model 2.0** component definitions and the **HOF guide** — to cite F1.3/F4.3/E4.1 precisely in ADR-004/R10; (2) the **ENISA/ER-ISAC 2022 report** at (h); (3) **DAC and FDFTO** — connectivity programmes named as attack-surface drivers and absent from this register. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. +| E-2026-08-15-44 | 2026-08-15 | **Dr. EYLEM THRON, Principal Human Factors Consultant, MIMA** (a human-centred design agency) — "**Human Factors — Operators as the last line of defence**"; **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 02-03.10.2024** (PDF created 07.10.2024); 25 slides. Presenter credentials given in-deck: 15 years in rail, aerospace and defence as HF consultant/manager, HCI designer, HF integration lead, **Notified Body Lead Assessor**, accessibility consultant; PhD, MSc, BEng, **C.ErgHF**. **Cites her own peer-reviewed paper twice: Thron, E., Faily, S., Dogan, H. and Freer, M. (2024), "Human factors and cyber-security risks on the railway — the critical role played by signalling operations", *Information & Computer Security*, 32(2), pp.236-263.** Paraphrased: **(a) THE CENTRAL THESIS** — "**HUMAN ACTIONS ARE THE VEHICLE BY WHICH SECURITY RISKS BECOME SAFETY HAZARDS**", and "**operators often do not directly cause cyber-related risks but find themselves as the LAST LINE OF DEFENCE**". **(b) THE AUTOMATION SLIDE — the direct hit on ADR-004:** "a new way of working for all operators… changing skilling of the workforce"; "**increased automation and less manual checks lead to SKILL FADE and REDUCED SITUATIONAL AWARENESS**"; "'**OVERTRUST**' **of the system rather than personal experience / knowledge**"; "day to day pressures to complete jobs on time may lead to **not noticing cyber-security related issues**"; and "**where decisions are time sensitive and workload is high, the risk of human error escalates, especially in DEGRADED OR EMERGENCY MODES**" — all attributed to the 2024 paper. **(c) RAILWAYS AS A SOCIO-TECHNICAL SYSTEM** — actors and their interfaces: **drivers** (ETCS, CBTC), **passengers** (CIS, PIS, mobile phones), **maintainers** (laptops), **signallers** (control-room interfaces) and **threat actors** (various devices). A layered HOF factor model (adapted from Wilson & Sharples, 2015) runs societal/cultural pressures → legal and regulatory rules → organisational and management behaviour → team and group behaviour → individual behaviour → individual differences → physical device, across cognitive, physical, organisational and environmental axes, naming factors including **security awareness, decision making, judgement, memory, vigilance, perception of responsibility, risky beliefs, attention to detail, security culture, safety culture, shift and work pattern, training, goal setting, constraints on system design, economic and political pressures**. **(d) THE TUNNEL SCENARIO — a degraded-mode human-factors analysis.** Posed as a question ("due to cyber attack a train/metro stops in a tunnel — how might people react, and how might those reactions affect safety?"), with consequences: *passengers* — "fear, confusion, anxiety, frustration, panic, injuries, **lose patience and attempt to find their own way out of trains**"; *operators* — "increase in workload, **operators have to manage SECONDARY HAZARDS in addition to their existing role**"; *railway staff* — "now dealing with a safety hazard as they attempt to manage the situation and **safely return passengers to stations WITHOUT FULL SYSTEM FUNCTIONALITY**". **(e) WHAT HF ADDS THAT ENGINEERING DOES NOT** — "while system developers and operators often prioritize technical solutions such as passwords, firewalls, and adherence to security standards, we look at **usability issues, time pressures, organizational ambiguity, inadequate training, and lack of risk awareness**". **(f) CRISIS MANAGEMENT / POST-INCIDENT** — HF identifies safety hazards arising from human factors in emergencies; "unknown risks mean **changing role of the station staff** (e.g. an attack/system failure may mean **panic and over-crowding of stations**… secondary risks e.g. **stampede**)"; "**lack of opportunity for passengers to contact staff / emergency services during adversity**"; and "anxiety, reduced morale, loss of confidence, confusion, lack of information". **(g) DESIGN AND PROCESS** — task analysis and scenario testing of operator tasks (**job design**); "increased workload may mean limitation of cognitive reactions and more mistakes" (**interface design**); evaluating "**whether the front-line staff have the RIGHT PRIVILEGES**"; and, against the observation that "engineers often design for process/scenario", advocacy for "**expectation-led design**". **(h) HF APPLIED TO THREAT MODELLING** — "Human Factors can help to **identify possible thinking of THREAT ACTORS, e.g. through PERSONAS** — evaluation of skills and characteristics", providing "input about an attacker's motivation, attitudes, attributes, skill set… through **persona development**" and identifying risks "through **visual modelling**". **(i) CULTURAL CHANGE** — "railways are evolving and becoming more connected (**new systems / technology, AI, etc.**), which will make them vulnerable in ways that [they have] not been before — this will require a **cultural change and re-education of staff**"; "**organisational factors must be part of the solution**". **(j) TARGETED TRAINING — three operator capabilities**: help operators **identify** a risk (whether an attack or a risk the operators/process might generate); help them **prevent the situation escalating**; help them **mitigate through reversing the situation**. "Operators (e.g. signallers) are trained individuals with an eye for detail; thus they can be **an asset to increase railway resilience when all fails**" — framed as two areas of **digital resilience**: "**resilience to risks operators might generate**" and "**risks operators might be able to prevent**". **(k) FINAL NOTES** — "the role of HF has been **OVERLOOKED to date in cybersecurity**"; HF methods "provide data and evidence based on real people, so **engineers need to consider the human element in their design**"; HF can "**detect, predict, quantify, prevent, reduce and mitigate** against vulnerabilities"; delivered through system-requirements input, system design, cultural change, re-education and targeted training. **(l) AN UNSOURCED FIGURE** — human error and non-malicious intent "due to increased digitisation / social engineering and human vulnerabilities (**over 80%**)", given without attribution in-deck.
