No TLP marking observed | **B — consultancy presentation** in form (agency introduction, "design that connects us", closing contact details), consistent with the register's tiering of other consultancy decks (E-2026-08-15-28 reuschlaw, -29 tmc, -34 Airbus, -41 Codewerk). **BUT WITH AN IMPORTANT QUALIFICATION: its substance rests on PEER-REVIEWED WORK by the presenter — Thron, Faily, Dogan & Freer (2024), *Information & Computer Security* 32(2):236-263 — and the HOF model is adapted from published academic work (Wilson & Sharples, 2015). The underlying paper is an A-grade academic source and should be obtained; cite THAT, not this deck.** Further flags: **the "over 80%" human-error figure at (l) is unsourced in-deck and not citable**; **slide-deck**; **10/2024 vintage** | **R10 human oversight** (+ **R11/ADR-004 automation bias — the closest external match on file**; **R4 degraded-mode human factors**; R12 operator detection; R13 training) | validate | **B by tier, but this is the single best external support ADR-004's AUTOMATION-BIAS obligation has, and it should be cited through the underlying paper.** **① (b) STATES ADR-004's RESIDUAL RISK IN AN HF PROFESSIONAL'S TERMS.** ADR-004's assurance argument names automation bias as "**the residual path by which an ADVISORY system can still contribute to harm if a human over-trusts its output**", and makes R10's oversight design responsible for countering it — "the boundary holds only if the human is a genuine decision-maker, not a rubber stamp". **Thron names the same mechanism and adds its causes: "'OVERTRUST' of the system rather than personal experience/knowledge", preceded by "increased automation and less manual checks lead to SKILL FADE and REDUCED SITUATIONAL AWARENESS", and worsened "especially in DEGRADED OR EMERGENCY MODES".** That last clause matters more than it looks: **ADR-004 relies on the human as the interposed decision-maker precisely when things go wrong — and this says human reliability is *lowest* exactly then.** The engagement's human-in-command gate is therefore weakest at the moment it is most load-bearing, which is a real design constraint and should be stated explicitly in ADR-004 rather than left implicit. **② (a) IS THE CLEANEST ONE-LINE STATEMENT OF THE SAFETY-SECURITY COUPLING THE REGISTER HAS FOUND: "human actions are the vehicle by which security risks become safety hazards."** ADR-012 argues that security and safety couple; ERA said the boundary was "to be determined" (E-2026-08-15-43); UITP argued determinism must extend to security (E-2026-08-15-33). **This supplies the causal path between them — and it is a human path, not a technical one.** Worth quoting in client material (via the paper). **③ (d) AND (f) GIVE R4 A HUMAN-FACTORS DIMENSION IT CURRENTLY LACKS.** R4 requires fail-soft — "safety-critical voice + movement authorities stay alive locally" — and ADR-007 must prove degraded mode by test. **This describes what degraded mode does to people: operators managing SECONDARY HAZARDS on top of their normal role, staff returning passengers to stations WITHOUT FULL SYSTEM FUNCTIONALITY, passengers self-detraining, station over-crowding and stampede risk.** The 23-June outage is precisely this scenario — a ~2-hour nationwide standstill with passengers stranded (incident-annex.md) — and the register has analysed it technically and organisationally but **never in human-factors terms**. **Recommend that ADR-007's degraded-mode test objectives include the human limb, and that R4's "fail-soft" be understood to include whether people can cope, not only whether systems stay alive.** **④ (j) REFRAMES THE OPERATOR FROM LIABILITY TO ASSET, WHICH IS USEFUL FOR R10.** The two-sided formulation — "**resilience to risks operators might generate**" and "**risks operators might be able to prevent**" — captures exactly the ambivalence in ADR-004: the human is both the automation-bias failure path *and* the last barrier. Thron's answer is targeted training on three capabilities (identify, prevent escalation, **mitigate by reversing the situation**), and the observation that signallers are "trained individuals with an eye for detail… an asset to increase railway resilience **when all fails**". **That is a constructive complement to ERA's Safety Culture Model components (F1.3 questioning attitude, F4.3 decision making — E-2026-08-15-43), and together they give R10 a design vocabulary and ADR-004 action 3 a concrete method.** **⑤ (g)'s "DO THE FRONT-LINE STAFF HAVE THE RIGHT PRIVILEGES?" IS A DESIGN QUESTION THE REGISTER HAS ONLY MET FROM THE SECURITY SIDE** (least privilege, RBAC, four-eyes — E-2026-08-15-26, -33). **Asked as a human-factors question it inverts: too little privilege in a degraded mode prevents the last line of defence from acting.** That tension — least privilege versus operational agency in emergencies — is unresolved in ADR-004 and worth naming. **⑥ (h) IS A METHOD THE REGISTER CAN USE:** HF **persona development** for threat actors — motivation, attitudes, attributes, skill set. The security log's adversary profiles (Sandworm, LightBasin, APT28) are currently assembled from threat-intel reporting without a formal capability or motivation model; **HF persona methods and the ENISA SCSA attack-potential construct (E-2026-08-15-08) are two routes to the same gap.** **⑦ (i) NAMES AI EXPLICITLY** as part of the connectivity that "will make [railways] vulnerable in ways that [they have] not been before", requiring cultural change and re-education — a small but relevant marker for ADR-002/ADR-003, and consistent with the Swedish sector's AI experience-sharing item (E-2026-08-15-38). **⑧ CAUTIONS.** This is a consultancy pitch in form, and its central claims should be sourced to the **2024 peer-reviewed paper** rather than to the deck; the "**over 80%**" human-error figure is unattributed in-deck and must not be repeated; and the material is **advocacy for a discipline** ("the role of HF has been overlooked") as well as analysis — true, in the register's own view, but not neutral. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R10 and R11/ADR-004 stay Proposed — this is strong design-basis support for the automation-bias obligation, not the verifying evidence ADR-004 actions 2/3/5 require; R4 Open; R12 Proposed; R13 Open. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **obtain Thron, Faily, Dogan & Freer (2024), *Information & Computer Security* 32(2):236-263** — a peer-reviewed, rail-specific, signalling-focused treatment of human factors in cyber risk, and the citable A-grade source behind this deck; **the most directly useful academic paper identified for ADR-004/R10**; (2) the **ERA HOF guide** referenced in the companion ERA deck (E-2026-08-15-43), to pair the model with ERA's own framework; (3) whether the **privilege-versus-agency** tension at (g) has a published treatment. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. +| E-2026-08-15-45 | 2026-08-15 | **YASMINE BENGHENAME, PhD student, Université de Technologie de Compiègne (yasmine.benghename@hds.utc.fr — Heudiasyc lab)** — "**Applying Penetration Testing Techniques to Strengthen ERTMS Communication Security**"; **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 02-03.10.2024** (PDF created 08.10.2024); 20 slides, workshop format with audience quizzes. Paraphrased: **(a) A TWO-DECADE INCIDENT TIMELINE** — US Sobig 2003 · Japan antivirus 2005 · **Poland hack 2008** · NW third-party 2011 · China reservation 2014 · South Korea & Germany disruptions 2015 · Ukraine **BlackEnergy** 2016 and UK four incidents · Sweden ticket-booking apps 2017 · Denmark DDoS 2018 · US OmniTRAX 2020 · US & Iran system disruption 2021 · Italy, Belarus, Romania, Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia 2022 · **Poland cmd "STOP" 2023**. **(b) THE TWO POLISH EVENTS, distinguished** — *2008*: "**four trams derailed; emergency stops injured passengers; a dozen people hurt**"; *2023*: "**no injuries; no damage; sabotage operation affected radio systems**". Cause analysis of 2023 given as a combination: "**use of OUTDATED RADIO TECHNOLOGY**" — "the Polish railway network used older radio technology that was **susceptible to interference and manipulation**"; "**availability of the equipment** — radio transmitters and receivers… widely available"; "**lack of sufficient security measures**"; and "**potential insider knowledge**". **(c) RESEARCH OBJECTIVES** — analyse existing security gaps in railway systems; **assess vulnerabilities in the ERTMS/ETCS communication system**; validate the effectiveness of cybersecurity solutions; evaluate the risk of cyberattacks on railway operations; and, as the final objective, "**develop a comprehensive cybersecurity RISK MANAGEMENT FRAMEWORK that integrates BOTH SAFETY AND SECURITY**". **(d) A FIVE-STAGE PENETRATION-TESTING METHODOLOGY** — **PLAN** (define goals, gather information, passive reconnaissance) → **SCAN** (identify potential vulnerabilities, dig deeper into misconfigurations and weaknesses) → **GAIN & MAINTAIN ACCESS** (exploitation, privilege escalation) → **ANALYZE & REPORT** → **REMEDIATION** (fix, then verify the fixes). **(e) THE CORE — SEVEN NAMED WEAKNESSES OF THE EUROBALISE TRANSMISSION SYSTEM (BTS)**, whose components are given as EuroBalise, antenna unit, **BTM** (balise transmission module), **EVC** and **LEU** (lineside electronic unit): **1. use of a CODING ALGORITHM INSTEAD OF ENCRYPTION · 2. absence of AUTHENTICATION and identification · 3. no formal user ACCESS RIGHTS assignment · 4. absence of ACCESS ACTIVITY TRACKING · 5. lack of TIMESTAMPING for data · 6. vulnerability to PHYSICAL ACCESS · 7. unsecured area installation**. **(f) CONSEQUENCES ANALYSED (effect-level, no method given)** — from "unauthorised access to communications": "**illegal extraction of information embedded in [the] telegram**", "**unauthorized telegram modification without being detected**", "**disruption of telegram transmission**", and "**impersonation of legitimate sources to send FAKE TELEGRAMS**". The intended report structure is listed as vulnerability ID, name/description, **severity (CVSS)**, impact, likelihood, risk level, root cause, exploitation method, affected systems/assets and **proof of concept**. **(g) REMEDIATION, split short-term and strategic** — *short-term*: install **anti-jamming systems**; implement **intrusion detection/prevention systems**; **strengthen telegram validation processes**; **update coding and decoding algorithms**. *Strategic*: adopt a **security-by-design** approach; **implement advanced cryptography**; **enhance message validation with security controls**; **develop a cybersecurity response plan**. Plus **continuous security audits and testing**. **(h) FUTURE WORK — A LAB TESTBED**: "ongoing lab project: launching new lab platform… **upgrade the current railway platform in our lab by integrating cutting-edge communication technologies**", with the goal to "**validate the effectiveness of our cybersecurity solutions and recommendations in a controlled, EMULATED environment**". **(i)** A closing quiz identifies the main cybersecurity challenge of **autonomous trains** as "**increased attack surface due to digital communication**". No TLP marking observed | A (academic/primary first-hand, on the basis the register uses for student research — cf. E-2026-07-12-04, the Federico II master's thesis; **FOUR WEIGHT CAVEATS, all load-bearing**: **PhD work IN PROGRESS**, with no peer-review status stated and **no results, data or proof-of-concept presented** — the deck sets out a methodology and an enumeration, not findings; **the seven BTS weaknesses at (e) appear to be an analysis of KNOWN DESIGN PROPERTIES of the Eurobalise rather than new discoveries**, and the deck does not claim otherwise; **workshop format** with audience quizzes, so the register should not over-read its assertions; **2024 vintage**) | **R5/R6 ETCS & balise transmission** (+ **R1/R2 outdated radio as an attack enabler**; ADR-007 pentest methodology & testbed; R14/ADR-012; R11/ADR-004 safety-security integration) | validate | **A with caveats — and its most useful contribution is (b), which gives R1 a SECURITY argument it has so far lacked.** **① "USE OF OUTDATED RADIO TECHNOLOGY" AS THE ENABLING CAUSE OF THE 2023 POLISH ATTACK REFRAMES THE OBSOLESCENCE CASE.** R1 is "remove obsolescence risk", and the register has argued it almost entirely on **supportability** grounds — dwindling spares, a shrinking supplier base, declining expertise, a 2035 national switch-off (E-2026-06-24-08, -11, -13). **This adds the security limb: the legacy radio is not merely hard to support, it is the vulnerability itself** — "susceptible to interference and manipulation", exploitable with "widely available" equipment. The register already holds the 2023 Poland radio-stop confirmed at A-tier by the Polish NSA's own annual report (E-2026-07-29-05) and carries it as **SIM-SEC-001**; this supplies the causal framing. **For the FRMCS transition argument that is a materially stronger position: migration off GSM-R is a security remediation, not only an obsolescence one.** Worth putting to the client in those terms. **② IT CONFIRMS THE DISTINCTION THE REGISTER ALREADY DREW BETWEEN THE TWO POLISH EVENTS, and adds detail to the 2008 one.** E-2026-07-29-11 flagged that "Łódź-2008 = tram hack (distinct from 2023 radio-stop)"; this separates them explicitly and records the 2008 consequences — **four trams derailed, emergency stops, "a dozen people hurt"** — against 2023's "no injuries, no damage". **That contrast is worth keeping: the only European rail cyber event on file with PHYSICAL CASUALTIES is a 2008 TRAM system, not a mainline signalling compromise** — consistent with the four-year, multi-source finding that the observed incident record is availability- and IT-shaped rather than safety-actuating (E-2026-08-15-12, -25, -07). **③ (e) IS THE SUBSTANTIVE TECHNICAL CONTENT AND IT RAISES A QUESTION THE REGISTER SHOULD PURSUE.** The Eurobalise transmission system is characterised as using **coding for error detection rather than encryption**, with **no authentication, no access-rights assignment, no access logging and no timestamping**, plus physical exposure. Those are, as the deck implies, **properties of the Eurobalise design** — a passive, unidirectional transponder read at speed — rather than defects introduced by implementation. **The open question is whether the mandated ERTMS security specifications reach them at all.** The register holds **SUBSET-146** as the ERTMS end-to-end application security spec (TLS 1.3 + X.509 PKI, E-2026-08-01-15) and **SUBSET-137** for on-line key management (E-2026-08-01-18) — **both of which secure the RBC/radio path, not a passive balise telegram.** If that is right, the balise layer sits outside the mandated cryptographic protections, and (f)'s "impersonation of legitimate sources to send fake telegrams" would be constrained by physical access and by ETCS's own consistency checks rather than by cryptography. **This should be verified rather than assumed — it is a specific, checkable question about a mandated CCS constituent, and it bears on R5/R6 and on any FRMCS-era security case.** **④ (g)'s REMEDIATION LIST INTERSECTS WORK ALREADY ON FILE**: "**anti-jamming systems**" connects to the GNSS/RF-denial gap opened at E-2026-08-15-24 and to SIM-SEC-001's radio-layer availability vector; "**intrusion detection/prevention**" is the same answer ILT accepts as an Acceptable Means of Compliance for legacy rolling stock (E-2026-08-15-40) and that Infrabel is deploying at 10% coverage on railway infrastructure (E-2026-08-15-39); and "**update coding and decoding algorithms**" sits in the crypto-lifecycle thread running from 3-DES replacement (E-2026-07-19-01) through AES-128's stated inadequacy over the FRMCS lifespan to PQC. **⑤ (d) AND (h) ARE ADR-007 MATERIAL.** A five-stage pentest methodology applied to ERTMS, and an **emulated lab platform being upgraded with "cutting-edge communication technologies" to validate cybersecurity solutions**, are directly relevant to the engagement's test programme and to the SIM-SEC backlog — **an academic ERTMS security testbed is a potential collaboration or reference point for ADR-007**, alongside SNCF's in-housed pentesting (E-2026-08-15-15) and Codewerk's testing practice (E-2026-08-15-41). **⑥ (c)'s FINAL OBJECTIVE — "a comprehensive risk management framework that INTEGRATES BOTH SAFETY AND SECURITY" — is the same goal the sector pursued through IEC 63452, UITP's synched cases and CRSG's SecRACs** (E-2026-08-15-32, -33, -30). Worth noting that an independent doctoral programme converged on the same problem statement in 2024. **⑦ HANDLE THE INCIDENT TIMELINE AT (a) WITH CARE** — it is a compiled list without sources or severities, of the kind the register has previously found defective in secondary compilations (E-2026-07-29-03, Cyber Forte, tiered D for exactly this reason). **Use it as a pointer, not as a citable chronology**; the individual events need their own primaries. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R5/R6 stay Accepted; R1/R2 Accepted — the security framing strengthens R1's justification without changing its status; ADR-007 unchanged; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **does any mandated ERTMS specification provide cryptographic protection for BALISE telegrams, or is the balise layer secured only by physical means and ETCS consistency checks?** — a specific, checkable question raised by (e); (2) any **published output** from this doctoral work (the deck is work-in-progress; a paper would be the citable source); (3) the **UTC lab platform** as a possible ADR-007 reference or collaboration. SECURITY item → also indexed in evidence-log-security.md. +| E-2026-08-15-46 | 2026-08-15 | **JOSEF DOPPELBAUER, EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR, ERA** — "**Cyber threats and challenges to the railway sector**"; **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 02-03.10.2024** (PDF created 04.10.2024); 13 slides. **The most senior ERA voice in this register.** Paraphrased: **(a) TRANSFORMATION OF THE RAILWAY SYSTEM — five vectors:** "from analog to digital systems — physical vs. digital infrastructure; **generalization of ICT; increased connectivity**"; "from **proprietary HW/SW to COTS/open SW**; appearance of **cloud-based systems**"; "**national systems vs. European integration (CROSS-BORDER RISKS)**"; "**modal siloes vs. multimodality — intercommunication of threats**"; and "**application of ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE (AI) techniques**". **(b) AN OPERATIONAL-RESILIENCE MODEL** — a large causal map of unpunctuality (defined as actual minus planned arrival, graded short delays ~5 min / long delays hours / cancellations), in which **"cyber attacks" and "communication system not working" appear as two causes among many** — alongside interlocking problems, signalling-system problems, infrastructure defects, staff absence, strikes, construction, weather and animals. The **mitigations** are set out on the opposing side: "**infrastructure tolerance/spare capacity**", "**reserve trains**", "**reserve staff**", "**culture of excellence**", "**REDUNDANCY**", "**contingency (time/route)**". **(c) THE DELAY CASCADE AND SYSTEM STABILITY** — a "**delay cascade effect**" in which an initial disturbance generates **secondary disturbances**, and a stability graph (average delay per hour against time) distinguishing a **stable system**, a **tolerant system** and an **unstable system** — the last diverging after a disturbance rather than recovering. Framed as "**stable operation is needed**" and "rail operation is a complicated process", with roles mapped across **ECM, IM, RU and train manufacturer**, under the heading "**digitalisation and data sharing**". **(d) "ATTACKS DEMONSTRATE VULNERABILITY" — a dated incident list:** **2020** — "incidents in Germany and Czech Republic (**not causing operational disruptions**)"; **2021** — "**attack on Belgian train dispatching system, leading to disruptions in train schedules**"; **2021** — "**Swiss train manufacturer: theft of sensitive data**"; **2022** — "'**Digital Backpack 2**' attack on **3rd-party supplier**, resulting in a **COMPLETE STANDSTILL on Denmark's railways**"; **2023** — "'**Radiostop**' operational disruption in Poland"; **2024** — "attempts to interfere with rail networks **disclosed by [the] Czech Transport Minister**". Listed separately beneath: "**DE: cable cuts in October 2023**"; "**FR: attacks on rail cables in July 2024**"; "**DB: IT PROBLEMS BLOCK GSM-R — 07 SEPTEMBER 2024**"; and "(**positive example: France during 2024 Olympics**)". **(e) CYBERSECURITY @ERA** — *regulation considerations*: monitor relevant cybersecurity activities in the railway context; "cover safety requirements of the rail system, e.g. **the assessment of safety consequences originated by security threats**"; "**reflect the above in Technical Specifications for Interoperability and Common Safety Methods**". *Cooperation building*: close relationship with ENISA and the European Commission; "**cross-fertilisation with EASA and EMSA to develop a transport cybersecurity policy**"; dialogue with national cybersecurity agencies (ANSSI, BSI); and "**support sector-led Information Sharing initiatives**". **(f) ERA-ENISA COLLABORATION** — framework (**MoU**, conference, transversal activities); **mapping** cybersecurity to railway policy and certification; and "**review existing TSI/CSM → new requirements**". Two workstreams: **policy** (implementation of NIS2, impact of EU certification, common safety methods and TSIs) and **capacity and skills** (awareness raising, skills programmes, trainings, exercises, events). **(g) ERA'S OWN COMPLIANCE** — under **Regulation (EU, Euratom) 2023/2841**, which "redefines the cybersecurity landscape for institutions, bodies, offices and agencies of the European Union", **Article 6(8) requires appointment of a Local Cyber Security Officer (LCSO)**; ERA has appointed **Luca Trinca** as LCSO and **Kleon Kleanthous** as alternate/PoC, and has implemented **MFA for access to ERA resources**. No TLP marking observed | A (primary — the agency's Executive Director speaking for ERA at ERA's own conference; **THREE FLAGS**: **slide-deck**; **the incident list at (d) is a COMPILED CHRONOLOGY WITHOUT SOURCES**, mixing cyber attacks, physical sabotage and non-malicious IT failures in one frame — treat each entry as a **lead requiring its own primary**, per the caution applied at E-2026-08-15-45 and E-2026-07-29-03; **October 2024 vintage** — but note (e) is unchanged from ERA's 2020 formulation, see below) | **R3/R4 resilience & the DB GSM-R recurrence thread** (+ R1/R2 GSM-R; R8/PR13 supplier-caused standstill; R14/ADR-012; R9/R10 AI as a named transformation vector) | validate | **A — and it contains one line that matters more than anything else in the deck.** **⚠️ ① "DB: IT PROBLEMS BLOCK GSM-R — 07 SEPTEMBER 2024" IS A PRIOR DB GSM-R BLOCKING EVENT, NAMED BY THE ERA EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR, AND IT IS NOT ON FILE.** The engagement's central case study is the **23-24 June 2026** DB GSM-R nationwide outage (incident-annex.md), whose proximate cause was a planned network-component swap triggering a silent software fault so that automatic failover never engaged (E-2026-06-27-01/-03). The register's only prior-recurrence evidence is a **C-tier press line** that the outage was "known/recurring" (E-2026-06-24-02). **Here the ERA Executive Director dates a specific earlier DB GSM-R blocking event to 07.09.2024 — roughly 21 months before the June 2026 outage.** A check confirms **zero occurrences** of it in this register. **This materially strengthens the recurrence thesis behind R3 and R12**: if DB's GSM-R was blocked by "IT problems" in September 2024 and again catastrophically in June 2026, then the June event is a **repeat of a known failure mode**, not a first occurrence — which is precisely the awareness-gap argument R12 encodes and the single-point-of-failure argument R3 encodes. **CARRY TWO CAVEATS.** First, Doppelbauer places it under "attacks demonstrate vulnerability" but describes it as "**IT problems**", and the same slide mixes attacks (Belgium, Poland, Denmark), **physical sabotage** (DE cable cuts, FR cable attacks) and apparently **non-malicious failures** — so **this must not be read as a cyber attack on DB**; on its face it is an IT-caused availability failure, which is the same class as June 2026 and therefore *more* relevant to R3/R4, not less. Second, it is a single unsourced line. **FOLLOW-UP — now the highest-priority item on the board: obtain a primary account of the 07.09.2024 DB GSM-R event** (DB press/Störungsmeldung, EBA, or German press). If confirmed, it belongs in `incident-annex.md` as a precedent and changes how the June 2026 outage should be characterised to the client — from an unlucky first failure to the second known instance of the same class. **② (b)/(c) ARE THE ERA EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR FRAMING CYBERSECURITY AS AN OPERATIONAL-RESILIENCE PROBLEM — and the framing is exactly R3/R4's.** Cyber attacks appear not as a special category but as **one disturbance input among many** into a system whose response determines whether it is **stable, tolerant or unstable**, with **secondary disturbances** cascading from the first. And the countermeasures he names — **redundancy, spare capacity, reserve trains and staff, contingency time/route** — are R4's fail-soft properties in operational language. **This is a strong, quotable, agency-level endorsement of the engagement's central architectural position: what matters is not preventing every disturbance but ensuring the system absorbs one without diverging.** It also supplies a useful bridge for the client, because it places the **non-cyber** 23-June outage and a hypothetical cyber event in the **same analytical frame** — which is precisely why R3/R4 are argued architecturally rather than as security controls. **③ (d) NAMES THREE INCIDENTS THE REGISTER LACKS, ALL WORTH PURSUING.** "**Digital Backpack 2**" — a 2022 attack on a **third-party supplier** producing a "**complete standstill on Denmark's railways**" — is the DSB 2022 supply-chain event the register holds only as a passing reference in the ENISA Transport Threat Landscape (E-2026-07-20-01); **it is the clearest documented case on file of a supplier compromise halting a national railway**, and it is the strongest single argument for R8/PR13 and for ADR-012's procurement gate. The **2021 Belgian train-dispatching attack** and the **2024 Czech ministerial disclosure** are likewise unlogged. **④ (e) IS UNCHANGED FROM ERA'S 2020 POSITION — WORD FOR WORD IN SUBSTANCE.** Compare E-2026-08-15-02 (ERA, ~Nov 2020): monitor activities · cover safety requirements including "the assessment of safety consequences originated by security threats" · reflect in TSIs and CSMs · cooperate with ENISA/EC, EASA/EMSA, ANSSI/BSI, support ER-ISAC. **Four years later the Executive Director presents the same mandate in the same terms.** That is a fair and citable observation about the pace of regulatory change — and it sits pointedly beside the finding, from EUG in 2022 and from EPSF in 2025, that the TSIs still carry **no explicit cybersecurity requirement** (E-2026-08-15-19, -35). **The intent has been stable since 2019; the instrument has not followed.** **⑤ (a) NAMES AI AS ONE OF FIVE TRANSFORMATION VECTORS**, alongside COTS/cloud adoption and cross-border integration — the ERA Executive Director putting AI in the sector's risk picture in 2024. Minor but relevant to ADR-002/ADR-003, and consistent with the AI items surfacing later at the Swedish sector level (E-2026-08-15-38) and in Thron's human-factors deck (E-2026-08-15-44). **⑥ (g) IS PERIPHERAL BUT NEW: Regulation (EU, Euratom) 2023/2841** — the EU institutions' own cybersecurity regulation, requiring a **Local Cyber Security Officer** under Art 6(8) — is not otherwise in this register. It governs Union entities, not rail operators, so it is out of scope for ADR-012; logged only so the reference is traceable. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R3 stays "Strengthened post-incident — open", R4 Open — **the 07.09.2024 lead strengthens both but is unverified**; R1/R2 Accepted; R8 Recommended; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change — **pending verification of ①, which if confirmed should be reflected in incident-annex.md and in R3's evidence base.** **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **the 07.09.2024 DB GSM-R blocking event — primary source, cause, duration, scope**; (2) "**Digital Backpack 2**" / DSB 2022 — the supplier-compromise-to-national-standstill case; (3) the **2021 Belgian dispatching attack**; (4) the **2024 Czech ministerial disclosure**; (5) the **DE Oct-2023 and FR Jul-2024 cable attacks** as physical-sabotage data points, kin the Bremen/Spain items in E-2026-08-15-24. +| E-2026-08-15-47 | 2026-08-15 | **ENNO WIEBE, UNIFE DIRECTOR GENERAL, "speaking on behalf of CER, EIM and UNIFE"** — "**Managing Cyber Risk in the Railway Ecosystem: Towards a Joint Approach**"; **4th ENISA-ERA Conference, 02 October 2024, Lille**; 8 slides. Paraphrased: **(a) WHO** — three representative bodies: **CER** (Community of European Railway and Infrastructure Companies), **EIM** (European Rail Infrastructure Managers) and **UNIFE** (the European Rail Supply Industry) — "**representing the majority of the rail market**" — "**creating for the FIRST TIME an overarching joint working group on cyber security, in close co-operation with the security domain team of the EUROPE'S RAIL SYSTEM PILLAR**". **(b) NEED** — the railway is "digitalising, connected, complex and safety related"; there is a "**sharp increase in cyber threats**"; and there is "**new and challenging cyber legislation**". Two stated reasons for collaborating: "all these challenges can only be faced successfully by a **united rail sector** → **the bad guys collaborate, so the good guys have to collaborate as well**"; and "we need together to **apply the transversal legislation efficiently and successfully to the sector** and **provide input to sector specific legislation to ensure that it is FIT FOR PURPOSE**". **(c) SCOPE — focus stated as "ENSURE RAILWAY OPERATION (FOR MISSION CRITICAL SERVICES)"** under **NIS2/CRA**; collaboration between railway undertakings, infrastructure managers, manufacturers of rolling stock and manufacturers of railway equipment; with **targets** extending to ICT service providers and manufacturers of **ICS/OT** and **ICT** equipment; and alignment with a "detailed" proposal involving **EC, ERA, ENISA and national bodies**. Sourced in-slide to the "**ENISA report on railway cybersecurity**". **(d) MUTUAL CONCERNS — the substantive slide:** "**about 25 POINTS OF CONCERN were identified around NIS2 and CRA** (including **CYBER SECURITY VERSUS SAFETY** and **WORKFORCE issues**)"; and "to bootstrap our collaboration, we decided to start focusing on **two topics**: **① SYSTEM EXTENSIONS** and **② CLASSIFICATION OF SYSTEMS AND COMPONENTS IN RELATION TO CRA**". **(e) NEXT STEPS** — assess the approach and way of working so as to tackle other topics; "**assess/open up for other associations to join… specifically for URBAN RAIL**"; create a roadmap/programme of work with a **backlog**; and "**evolve into a RAIL SECTOR PLATFORM on cybersecurity**". **(f) CONCLUSION** — cybersecurity is a key area of concern; "we are creating the **first overarching sector wide group** on cyber security"; "we are willing to cooperate with EU institutions and other associations". It captures the moment the sector pivoted from seeking exemption to organising for compliance, and it names the two topics that CRSG later delivered.** **① THE ARC IS NOW DOCUMENTED END TO END, WITH THIS DECK AS THE MISSING MIDDLE.** **December 2022** (E-2026-08-15-20, tier **B**): **UNIFE alone**, arguing that "**the rail sector should be EXCLUDED from the scope of the CRA proposal**", on the premise that rail is "an already highly regulated industry". **October 2024** (this row, tier **A**): **UNIFE together with CER and EIM**, forming "the first overarching joint working group on cyber security", whose stated purpose is to "**apply the transversal legislation efficiently and successfully to the sector**". **December 2025** (E-2026-08-15-30, tier **A**): **CRSG** — the same three bodies **plus urban rail** — publishing sector-wide guidance on **how to apply the CRA in rail**. **The exemption demand is simply gone by October 2024, replaced by an implementation agenda.** That is a documented two-year pivot, and this deck is where it turns. **It closes the foundational question flagged at E-2026-08-15-20 beyond reasonable doubt: ADR-012's premise — that the CRA applies to FRMCS products with digital elements — is sound.** The remaining EUR-Lex check is now a formality for the record rather than a risk to the ADR. **② (d)'s TWO BOOTSTRAP TOPICS ARE EXACTLY WHAT CRSG DELIVERED — a clean, traceable throughline.** In 2024 the group chose to start on "**system extensions**" and "**classification of systems and components in relation to CRA**". In 2025 CRSG's guidance contains precisely those two: "**system extension (adding a new section to a line, adding a new coach to a train…) managed at the interface**", and the "**what is a product?**" model with its four-level granularity — **systems → sub-systems → sets of components → components**, credited to Fig 1 of IEC 63452 (E-2026-08-15-30). **The sector said what it would do and did it, on a 14-month cycle.** For the engagement that matters practically: **the classification question ADR-012 action 1 must answer (how to decompose the FRMCS estate for a CRA PDE inventory) is the very question the sector has been working since 2024, and its answer is in the CRSG guidance due 2026.** Do not solve it independently — track and adopt. **③ (e) FORECAST → OUTCOME, ALL THREE FULFILLED.** "Open up for other associations… **specifically for urban rail**" → CRSG includes **urban rail** (E-2026-08-15-30); "create a roadmap/programme of work… create a **backlog**" → CRSG reports "**more than 20 other topics identified**"; "**evolve into a rail sector platform on cybersecurity**" → CRSG is exactly that, a standing joint body. **This is the most cleanly verified plan-to-delivery sequence in the whole conference corpus**, and it is worth citing when advising the client on whether sector-level commitments are reliable: in this instance they were. **④ (d)'s "ABOUT 25 POINTS OF CONCERN… INCLUDING CYBER SECURITY VERSUS SAFETY AND WORKFORCE ISSUES" NAMES THE REGISTER'S TWO LARGEST THREADS.** "**Cyber security versus safety**" is the coupling ADR-012 and ADR-004 both turn on — the same seam ERA called "to be determined" in the same month (E-2026-08-15-43) and that IEC 63452/UITP/CRSG had framed by 2025. "**Workforce**" is the capacity constraint now attested by ENISA, EPSF, Traficom, EDA, the Estonian minister and a national CERT, and measured by ENISA's investment survey. **That the sector's own top-25 concern list leads with these two is useful corroboration that the engagement is tracking the right problems.** The full 25-point list is not disclosed here — **worth requesting**, since it is effectively the sector's own risk register for NIS-2 and CRA. **⑤ (a) LINKS THE ASSOCIATION EFFORT TO THE EUROPE'S RAIL SYSTEM PILLAR** — "in close co-operation with the security domain team" of the System Pillar. That connects this workstream to the standards line the register tracks from ESCG's 2023 handover (E-2026-08-15-19) through the System Pillar's cybersecurity specification work (E-2026-08-15-21, -22). **Note, though, that the System Pillar did not appear at the 5th conference**, so its current status remains a gap. **⑥ (c) DEFINES THE COLLABORATION'S FOCUS AS "ENSURE RAILWAY OPERATION (FOR MISSION CRITICAL SERVICES)"** — i.e. availability of mission-critical services as the organising objective, not confidentiality or compliance for its own sake. **That is the same framing ERA's Executive Director gave the same day** (cyber as one disturbance input to an operational-resilience problem, E-2026-08-15-46) **and it is R3/R4's framing.** The target list also reaches **ICS/OT and ICT equipment manufacturers and ICT service providers**, consistent with the NIS-2 "important entity" scope CER later set out (E-2026-08-15-18). **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open, ADR-012 Proposed — its CRA premise, already treated as sound at E-2026-08-15-30, is now historically corroborated; R8 Recommended; R11/ADR-004 Proposed; R13 Open. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **the 25-point concern list** around NIS-2 and CRA — the sector's own risk register, not disclosed here and directly useful to ADR-012; (2) the **CRSG guidance v1.0 (due 2026)**, already the standing watch item, whose system-extension and classification chapters originate here; (3) the **Europe's Rail System Pillar security domain team** — its current output remains the register's clearest open gap on the standards line. SPINELLI (Infrabel) — "EUROPEAN CISO FORUM FOR RAILS"**; **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, 02/10/2024, Lille**; 8 slides, marked **TLP:CLEAR** (though the slide template also carries an "Intern" header — an internal-template artefact inconsistent with the TLP marking; treat the **TLP:CLEAR** marking as governing but note the conflict). **This identifies the body flagged as unknown at E-2026-08-15-22.** Paraphrased: **(a) WHAT IT IS** — "a **community of railway sector operators**"; "**strategy** for cyber security issues"; "**common decisions & alignment**"; with the stated aim of "**increasing the cyber maturity of members**". **(b) WHAT IT IS NOT — an explicit demarcation against the ISAC:** "**CISO Forum : STRATEGIC body VS ISAC : OPERATIONAL body**", and "**no sharing of technical information about threats, vulnerabilities, incidents**". **(c) PRINCIPLES OF COOPERATION** — **trust** (confidentiality, integrity and reliability of shared information); **delegation** (each organisation delegates relevant experts, "esp. for [the] Project Roadmap"); **collaboration** ("active participation, involvement and commitment… the more we share, the better we can achieve our goal; the result is greater than the sum of its parts"); and **quality** ("valuable case studies, shared with all organisations, are meant to help other members improve their cyber security posture"). **(d) MEMBERSHIP — sixteen named active members**, described as "European railway organisations who **voluntarily joined**": **Trafikverket (SE), BaneNOR (NO), Network Rail (UK), NS (NL), CFL (LU), ÖBB (AT), Infrabel (BE), VR (FI), SNCF (FR), Abellio (UK), Infraestruturas de Portugal (PT), Bane (DK), Irish Rail (IE), ProRail (NL), SBB (CH) and DEUTSCHE BAHN (DE)**. Membership is "**open to all IM and RU in [the] EEA + Norway, UK, Switzerland**", public and private entities, subject to "**collective review of membership applications**"; contact **Info@CISO4Rail.eu**. Stakeholders: **industry** ("constant dialogue", "matching demand and supply", "**ad hoc guest members**") and **regulators** ("works in progress", "**open channel with ENISA**"). **(e) CADENCE AND OUTPUT** — meets **physically once per year and online twice per year**; delivers "documents, reports, presentations, notes" which "can contain **alignment of positions** and **actionable information**", and whose "**deliverables can be used directly by members**". **(f) PROJECT ROADMAP (marked Draft)** — goals: "**learn from each other**" and "**enhance the level of trust**"; participants: "contributors: **mandated expert groups of members**" plus "**external experts as guests**"; **foreseen topics: CyberSOC · OT · EU Legislation · Corporate Training Programs · Outsourcing**. **(g) "FICHE WG 1 CYBERSOC" (marked Under Discussion)** — *assessment*: "need to **supervise systems and networks** & guarantee an adequate response against incidents"; "**cyber incidents can CROSS NATIONAL BORDERS**"; "common interest for good practices on CyberSOC implementation". *Objective*: sharing good practices. *Deliverable*: a **Good Practices Report**. *Conditions and prerequisites*: "each contributor presents [their] organisation's approach, **regardless of its state of progress**", with "identification of successes, **difficulties**, opportunities, threats, risks". *Roles*: a project leader plus deputy as facilitator, and "**mandated contributors by organization: SOC Managers or CISOs**". READ: complete text (8 slides) | Sector-body conference presentation (primary — two officers of a member organisation presenting the Forum's own constitution and roadmap) | file "session 2-1 - ciso forum updates - cedric cecotti, sara spinelli.pdf", Download folder; md5 1fd645f2…9e97; 4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 02.10.2024. **TLP:CLEAR** | A (primary — a sector body describing itself, by named officers, publicly marked; **THREE FLAGS**: **slide-deck**; **October 2024 status** — the roadmap is marked "Draft" and WG 1 "Under Discussion", so nothing here is a delivered output; **the Forum did not appear on the 5th-conference agenda**, so its current status and WG-1 progress are **unknown** — a gap, not an obsolescence) | **R12 detection ownership** (+ R14/ADR-012 sector alignment; R8/PR13 outsourcing; R13 training) | validate | **A — and it closes an open identification while sharpening the register's §4a ownership map.** **① IT ANSWERS THE FOLLOW-UP FROM E-2026-08-15-22** ("identify the CISO Forum"): the **European CISO Forum for Rails** is a voluntary, strategic community of **sixteen named European rail operators and infrastructure managers**, meeting three times a year, producing member-usable deliverables, with an **open channel to ENISA** and industry participation by invitation. **② ITS SELF-DEFINITION SHARPENS THE §4a GAP RATHER THAN CLOSING IT — and the demarcation at (b) is the useful part.** The Forum states explicitly that it is a **strategic** body and the **ISAC** is the **operational** one, and that it does **not** share technical threat, vulnerability or incident information. Set against the register's standing §4a finding, the sector-level map is now: **ER-ISAC = operational threat-sharing** (E-2026-08-02-01) · **CISO Forum = strategic alignment and maturity** (this) · **CSSP = solutions/assurance cataloguing** (E-2026-08-15-17) · **CRSG = regulatory application** (E-2026-08-15-30). **Four sector bodies, and still none that owns operational hardening or monitoring of the rail-telecom/interconnect layer.** The gap is not an absence of institutions; it is that the institutions that exist are strategic, informational or regulatory, and the operational layer remains an individual-operator responsibility — which is exactly why Infrabel is at 10% coverage on its railway estate and blocked on suppliers (E-2026-08-15-39). **③ THE MEMBER LIST IS DIRECTLY ACTIONABLE FOR THIS ENGAGEMENT, BECAUSE DEUTSCHE BAHN IS A MEMBER.** The client's own organisation sits in a sixteen-member European forum whose **foreseen roadmap topics are CyberSOC, OT, EU legislation, corporate training programmes and outsourcing** — i.e. four of the register's live threads (detection ownership, OT security, CRA/NIS-2 conformance, and the capacity constraint) plus the build-versus-buy question. **That is an existing channel the engagement can recommend using rather than a body it needs to invent**, and it is worth establishing internally who represents DB there and what the Forum has produced since 2024. **④ WG 1 WOULD PRODUCE PRECISELY THE ARTEFACT R12 NEEDS.** A **CyberSOC Good Practices Report**, built from members presenting their own approaches "**regardless of state of progress**" and identifying "successes, **difficulties**, opportunities, threats, risks" — i.e. a candid, multi-operator account of rail SOC implementation, contributed by **SOC managers and CISOs**. Given that **Infrabel leads this presentation and is the operator with the most public CyberSOC experience** (three conferences running, and the only quantified coverage figures on file), such a report would be the single most useful document the register could obtain for R12 and for ADR-002's Risk Sentinel design. **Whether it was produced is unknown — WG 1 was "Under Discussion" in 10/2024 and the Forum did not appear on the 5th-conference agenda.** **⑤ (g)'s "CYBER INCIDENTS CAN CROSS NATIONAL BORDERS" IS THE SAME ARGUMENT THE REGISTER HAS MET FROM FOUR OTHER DIRECTIONS** — supervisory-competence divergence (FR/DE/FI/BE), technical under-specification driving per-operator solutions (E-2026-08-15-19), competence variance as "systemic risk" (E-2026-08-15-31) and RNE's national-focus fragmentation (E-2026-08-15-42). **Here it is the stated rationale for a voluntary CISO community: national bodies cannot address a cross-border problem alone.** **⑥ NOTE THE VOLUNTARY, TRUST-BASED CONSTITUTION (c).** Membership, contribution and delegation are all voluntary; the currency is trust and case studies. **That is the same model as the Finnish national cooperation network and ER-ISAC** — and it carries the same limitation the register has recorded elsewhere: voluntary sharing produces alignment, not obligation, and depends on the very capacity that four institutions have said is short. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R12 stays Proposed · ADR-002; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R8 Recommended; R13 Open. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **the WG 1 CyberSOC Good Practices Report** — if it exists, the highest-value R12 artefact identified anywhere in this corpus; (2) **DB's participation** — who represents the client, and what Forum deliverables it already holds; (3) the Forum's current status and roadmap progress since 10/2024, given its absence from the 5th-conference programme; (4) the **Outsourcing** topic, directly relevant to the managed-SOC build-versus-buy question raised by Infrabel's Thales contract (E-2026-08-15-39) against SNCF's in-housing (E-2026-08-15-15). +| E-2026-08-15-49 | 2026-08-15 | ⚠️ **TLP:GREEN.** **STÉPHAN ANDRÉ, NIS Team, CENTRE FOR CYBERSECURITY BELGIUM (CCB) — "under the authority of the Prime Minister"** — "**Transposition of the NIS2 Directive for the railway sector in Belgium**"; **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 02-03.10.2024** (PDF created 09.10.2024); 29 slides. **The most complete national NIS-2 implementation picture in this register.** Paraphrased: **(a) THE INSTRUMENTS** — Dir (EU) 2022/2555 transposed by the **Law of 26 April 2024** ("establishing a framework for the cybersecurity of networks and information systems of general interest for public security", the **NIS2 law**) and the **Royal Decree of 9 June 2024** implementing it; supported by the **CyberFundamentals Framework**, a CCB **NIS2 Quickstart Guide**, guidance on incident notification (then "to come"), CVD guidance and an FAQ. **NOTE — the Commission Implementing Act** laying down technical and methodological requirements for risk-management measures and specifying when an incident is significant applies to **DIGITAL sectors only** (DNS, TLD registries, cloud, data centres, CDN, managed service providers, managed security service providers, online marketplaces/search/social platforms, trust service providers) — **not to transport**. **(b) REGISTRATION** — mandatory, via **Safeonweb@work**; "**for the railway sector: deadline 18th March 2025**". **(c) COMPETENT AUTHORITIES — a two-body split.** **CCB** is designated **National cybersecurity authority**, and is simultaneously the **competent authority for supervision of essential and important entities**, the **National CSIRT**, the **single point of contact** for NIS2 implementation, and Belgium's representative in the **CSIRT network**, the **NIS Cooperation Group** and **EU-CyCLONe**. Separately, the **transport sectoral authority is the Federal Public Service (FPS) Mobility and Transport**, whose functions are listed as additional identification · registration of entities · **organisation of sectoral exercises** · analysis and management of the consequences of an incident for a sector · participation in NIS Cooperation Group work · raising awareness · cooperation at national level · **additional cybersecurity risk-management measures** · incident reporting · **supervision and inspection** · **administrative fines**. **(d) MEASURES — "Belgium specificities":** an **explicit obligation to run a risk analysis, adopt a formal information security policy (PSI/IBB) and a Coordinated Vulnerability Disclosure (CVD) policy**; and a **presumption of conformity if CyberFundamentals or ISO/IEC 27001 certification** is held "with the relevant scope". **(e) SCOPE — "NIS2 scope = WHOLE ENTITY"**, illustrated by a diagram spanning a smart coffee machine, management PCs, office PCs and company servers across three networks. **(f) MANAGEMENT ACCOUNTABILITY** — management bodies carry **liability for infringements by their entity**, must **approve** the cybersecurity risk-management measures, **oversee implementation**, and "**follow training & encourage their employees to follow similar training**". **(g) CHARACTERISTICS OF MEASURES** — technical, operational and organisational; **appropriate and proportionate**; taking account of **cost of implementation** and "state of the art and, where applicable, relevant European and international standards"; with proportionality determined by "the degree of the entity's exposure to risks, its size, the likelihood of occurrence of incidents and their severity, **including their societal and economic impact**" — "adapted to the concrete situation of the NIS2 entity". **(h) SUPPLY-CHAIN OBLIGATION — and its stated gap.** The NIS2 entity must **impose cybersecurity risk-management measures** on its **direct supplier / service provider** (which may itself be a "**potentially non-NIS2 entity**") and **control respect of [those] measures**. But: "**the NIS2 law only sets out supply chain security as a MINIMUM cybersecurity risk-management measure, but DOES NOT STATE HOW IT SHOULD BE ACHIEVED**" — with the CCB recommending use of the **CyberFundamentals (CyFun®) Framework**. **(i) INCIDENT NOTIFICATION — the full flow.** Essential and important entities notify the **National CSIRT (CCB) always**, plus **affected recipients if concerned**; the CCB then shares with **sectoral authorities**, **other Belgian NIS2 sectors** and the **CSIRT network** where there is cross-border impact. Clocks and content: **24 h** early warning by written online notification or phone, indicating "**if [the] incident [is] presumed to be caused by unlawful or malicious action and/or if [it] could have a cross-border impact**"; **72 h** information update with an initial assessment of severity and impact and, where available, **indicators of compromise**; **update upon request**; and a **1-month final report** giving "a detailed description of the incident, its severity and impact", "**the type of threat or ROOT CAUSE that likely triggered the incident**", and "applied and ongoing mitigation measures". The significance test is quoted from the Directive: an incident is "any event compromising the **availability, authenticity, integrity or confidentiality**" of data or related services, and is significant if it "**has caused or has the potential to cause severe substantial operational disruption or financial losses for the entity concerned**" or "**has affected or has the potential to affect other natural or legal persons by causing considerable material or non-material losses**". **(j) SUPERVISION — differentiated by entity class.** **Important entities: EX-POST supervision only** ("after an incident or if suspicion of potential infringements"); **essential entities: EX-POST AND EX-ANTE**. Supervisory measures include "**on-site and off-site inspections**", "**ad hoc audits**", "**SECURITY SCANS**" and requests for information and evidence. Regular conformity assessment runs either by **certification/verification under CyberFundamentals or ISO 27001 by an authorised Conformity Assessment Body (CAB)** — conferring **presumption of conformity** — or by **mandatory inspection by the CCB with fees paid by the entity**; **essential entities must do one or the other**, important entities may do so voluntarily; **additional sectoral requirements** may attach to either route. **(k) THE CyFun ECOSYSTEM** — framework mapping, a **selection tool (risk assessment)**, a self-assessment tool, BASIC policy templates, labels, and a conformity-assessment scheme for CABs; **publicly available at www.cyfun.eu**. The risk-assessment tool shown scores organisation size against **attack categories** (sabotage/disruption, information theft, crime/ransomware, hacktivism, disinformation) and **threat-actor types** (competitors, ideologues/hacktivists, terrorists, cyber criminals, **nation-state actors**) to yield a total score and a **CyFun Level** (the worked example resolving to **ESSENTIAL**). **(l) ENFORCEMENT AND FINES** — warnings and binding instructions; orders to cease conduct or ensure compliance; **orders to publish observed breaches and/or inform users**; designation of a **monitoring officer** [essential]; **temporary suspension of a certification or authorisation** [essential]; and **temporary prohibition on exercising managerial functions** [essential]. Fines: **€500–125 000** for non-compliance with the Art 12 identification/information obligations; **€500–200 000** against an entity that "**has sanctioned one of its employees or subcontractors for performing obligations of the NIS2 law in good faith**"; **€500–200 000** for non-compliance with supervision obligations; **doubled for repeated behaviour within three years**; and headline caps of **€500–7 000 000 or 1.4% of total worldwide annual turnover [important entities]** and **€500–10 000 000 or 2% of total worldwide annual turnover [essential entities]**, whichever is higher. **(m) TIMELINE** — all NIS2 obligations apply from **18 October 2024**; rail registration by **18 March 2025**; essential entities to obtain a **CyFun Basic or Important label (or equivalent inspection) during 2026** and a **CyFun Essential label by early 2027**; important entities' use of CyFun or ISO 27001 remains **voluntary**. READ: complete text (29 slides; the CyFun scoring table read via its cell values) | National-authority conference presentation (primary — the Belgian national cybersecurity authority's NIS team on its own transposition, citing the enacted instruments) | file "session 2-2 - be nis2 transposition for the railway sector - stéphan andré.pdf", Download folder; md5 f7e6071b…cce0; 4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 02-03.10.2024. **TLP:GREEN** | A (primary — a national cybersecurity authority under the Prime Minister, presenting enacted national law and its own supervisory scheme; **FOUR FLAGS**: **⚠️ TLP:GREEN**; **slide-deck**; **Belgian national law**, so directly informative rather than directly applicable to a German engagement; **October 2024 vintage** with a timeline running to 2027, so much of (m) was then forward-looking and its execution is unverified here) | **R14/ADR-012** (+ **R8/PR13 supply-chain flow-down**; R12 notification & security scans; R10 management accountability; R3/R4 the significance test) | validate | **⚠️ HANDLING: TLP:GREEN** — community-limited; logged because the repository is **PRIVATE**. **A — and the fullest worked NIS-2 implementation the register holds. Five points are directly usable.** **① A FIFTH NATIONAL COMPETENCE MODEL, AND THE RAIL NSA IS ABSENT FROM IT.** Belgium splits the job between the **CCB** — national cyber authority, national CSIRT, SPOC and supervisor of essential/important entities — and a **transport sectoral authority, the FPS Mobility and Transport**, which holds identification, sectoral exercises, additional risk-management measures, inspection and **administrative fines**. **The rail safety authority does not appear.** Set against the models already on file — **FR** (EPSF has no cyber competence; ANSSI has no rail mandate), **DE** (EBA reads its safety mandate as including IT security and approves security experts), **FI** (one agency holds NSA, NIS competent authority and CERT) — **Belgium places sectoral cyber supervision with the transport MINISTRY rather than the rail regulator.** That is a fourth distinct arrangement and it reinforces the register's finding that **national supervisory competence for rail cybersecurity diverges structurally across the Union** (E-2026-08-15-35, -11, -12) — a live cross-border assurance problem. **② "NIS2 SCOPE = WHOLE ENTITY" IS A SCOPING POINT ADR-012 SHOULD STATE EXPLICITLY.** The deck is emphatic and illustrates it with a smart coffee machine alongside company servers: the obligations attach to the **entire entity**, not to a carved-out set of critical systems. For an infrastructure manager that means corporate IT, office estate and OT alike. **ADR-012 currently frames NIS-2 as operator-side risk management and incident reporting; it does not say that the perimeter is the whole organisation.** Worth making explicit, because it materially changes the scale of the compliance surface — and it contrasts with the CRA, which attaches per product. **③ (h) IS THE SUPPLY-CHAIN FLOW-DOWN MECHANISM — AND A NATIONAL AUTHORITY CONCEDING ITS UNDER-SPECIFICATION.** NIS-2 obliges the entity to **impose** risk-management measures on direct suppliers and to **control respect** of them, reaching suppliers that are themselves **not NIS-2 entities** — but "**the NIS2 law… does not state HOW it should be achieved**". **That is exactly the gap ADR-012 action 2 fills with contractual terms**, and it is now supported by a national supervisor's own statement that the law leaves the method open. Belgium's answer is to recommend a framework (CyFun); **the engagement's answer should be contractual clauses** — and this row is the legal-basis evidence for insisting on them. It also connects to Infrabel's finding, from the same country, that supplier cooperation is the binding constraint on monitoring coverage (E-2026-08-15-39) and to ILT's appeal for manufacturers to define monitoring use cases (E-2026-08-15-40). **④ THE FINES ARE NEW TO THE REGISTER AND BELONG BESIDE THE CRA FIGURES.** ADR-012's legal basis records **CRA Art 64 at €15M / 2.5%**; the NIS-2 exposure is now documented at **€10M or 2% of worldwide turnover for essential entities** and **€7M or 1.4% for important entities**, doubled for repetition within three years — plus non-monetary powers that matter more in practice: **suspension of a certification or authorisation** and **temporary prohibition on exercising managerial functions**, both for essential entities. **For a client conversation about why ADR-012 is not optional, the ability to suspend an authorisation and to bar managers is a sharper argument than the fine ceiling.** Note also the **€200 000 penalty for sanctioning an employee or subcontractor who performed NIS-2 obligations in good faith** — a protection for those who report, which is the legal counterpart to the Organisational Just Culture that R12's evidence chain depends on (E-2026-08-15-43). **⑤ (j)'s DIFFERENTIATED SUPERVISION AND (d)'s PRESUMPTION OF CONFORMITY GIVE ADR-012 A COMPLIANCE ROUTE.** Essential entities face **ex-ante as well as ex-post** supervision and **must** undergo regular conformity assessment — via **CyFun or ISO 27001 certification by an accredited CAB**, which confers **presumption of conformity**, or else a **CCB inspection at the entity's own cost**. **That is the same "standards deliverables → presumption of conformity" architecture the register has now seen from three other directions** — EPSF's proposed chain of TS 50701/IEC 63452 → CRA → NIS-2 (E-2026-08-15-35), CENELEC WG 26's forthcoming IEC 63452 European Annex (E-2026-08-15-32) and CRSG's sector guidance (E-2026-08-15-30). **The consistent lesson for ADR-012: certification against a recognised framework is the practical route to demonstrating conformance, and the engagement should choose its framework deliberately rather than inherit one.** **⑥ (i)'s FINAL-REPORT REQUIREMENT IS POINTED FOR THIS ENGAGEMENT:** within one month, the entity must state "**the type of threat or ROOT CAUSE that likely triggered the incident**". The 23-June 2026 outage's root cause — a planned component swap triggering a silent software fault so failover never engaged — took days to establish publicly (E-2026-06-27-01/-03). **A one-month statutory root-cause deadline is a real operational requirement on the diagnostic capability R12 exists to provide**, and it is worth noting that the same regime requires the 24-hour early warning to state whether the cause is *presumed malicious* — a determination the Dutch and French authorities have both said is not a solved process (E-2026-08-15-10). **⑦ MINOR BUT WORTH RECORDING: the Commission Implementing Act specifying risk-management measures and significant-incident thresholds covers DIGITAL sectors only, not transport** (a). So rail has **no EU-level implementing specification** of its NIS-2 measures — the detail is left to national law and sectoral authorities, which is precisely the mechanism by which the divergence at ① arises. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R14 stays Open (ADR-012 Proposed); R8 Recommended; R12/R10 Proposed; R3/R4 unchanged. No matrix change, no ADR change — **though ADR-012 has three concrete additions available here** (the whole-entity scope, the NIS-2 penalty figures and non-monetary sanctions, and the supply-chain flow-down basis), offered not made. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **the German equivalent** — the NIS2UmsG and its BSIG recast, already an open residual at ADR-012 action 6 and now with a Belgian comparator to test it against: who is the German *transport sectoral authority*, and is it the EBA (cf. E-2026-08-15-36) or the ministry? (2) **CyFun** at www.cyfun.eu — a free, public framework with a risk-based assurance-level selection tool, worth examining as a candidate maturity instrument; (3) whether Belgium's rail entities in fact met the **18 March 2025** registration deadline and the 2026/2027 labelling milestones. Paraphrased: **(a) NIS2 CRITICALITY AND A POLICY PROPOSITION** — the eleven high-criticality sectors are listed (transport, energy, digital infrastructure, ICT service management, space; banking, financial market infrastructure, healthcare, drinking water, wastewater, public administration); "in member states it is going to be necessary to ensure that regulated services (high criticality) in transport and related fields are **fully interoperable**"; "the **railway system belongs to the critical — but most probably to the SUPER CRITICAL infrastructure**"; and, posed as a question, "**IS THERE A NEED FOR A NEW CYBER SECURITY TSI THEN?**". **(b) CZECH NIS2 TRANSPOSITION** — the 17 October 2024 deadline noted, with "**HR, IT, BE — already transposed**"; the Czech timeline running 17.09.2024 (first hearing, Chamber of Deputies) → mid-November 2024 (second hearing) → end Q1 2025 (third hearing, final approval) → end Q2 2025 (Senate) → **July 2025 presidential signature, transposition finished**. And the accompanying position: "**from the rail sector perspective, [a] REGULATION [is a] better option than [a] DIRECTIVE because of ensuring INTEROPERABILITY**". **(c) SPACE AND NIS2** — the **EU Space Programme** components named as **Galileo, EGNOS, USC, GOVSATCOM and Copernicus**; in the Czech Republic the security aspects of the components are managed by **NACIB** while the **Ministry of Transport manages the programme overall**; with a further slide (an unread image) titled "**impact of space technologies used in digitalization and automation of the rail system to cyber security**". **(d) SCOPE OF NIS2's GENERAL IMPACTS** — cybersecurity described as "one of the most important parts of all processes in the entire railway system", spanning "**production of rolling stock, signalling systems, and all infrastructure subsystems and components, monitoring and control systems, transport planning**" through to "**provisioning processes, the sale of travel documents to passengers, [and the] provision of consignment notes and other necessary documents for the transport of goods**" — mapped onto **TSIs**, **Regulation (EU) 2020/1056**, and **CIV (tickets), RID (dangerous-goods safety list) and CIM (consignment note)**. A further slide is titled "**reference to specific rail freight transport (MILITARY and RID)**". **(e) THE KEY SLIDE — "Key safety and operational indicators":** "from the point of view of the Member States, **rail transport is a key strategic sector which must ensure the operation of other key sectors AT ALL CIRCUMSTANCES**", those sectors being "**defence, national internal security, [the] integrated rescue system and strategic industries**". Then two requirements: first, "it is necessary to consider **how to ensure that the development of new smart information technologies, ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE, and automation of transport processes IS NOT THE MAIN RISK FACTOR OR A LIMITING FACTOR IN SAFETY of the rail system**"; and second, "**critical safety on-board and ground systems should be implemented in a way that ALLOWS FOR THEM TO BE CIRCUMVENTED IN THE CASE OF A CYBER THREAT** and, importantly, **REDUNDANCY needs to be ensured in 'CRISIS MODE' WITH MINIMAL IT SUPPORT**". No TLP marking observed | A (primary — the Member States' representation body within the Europe's Rail Joint Undertaking governance, presented by a national ministry official; **FOUR FLAGS**: **slide-deck with ≥6 unread image slides**, so several titled topics are captured only by their headings; **"SUPER CRITICAL" is rhetorical framing**, not a legal category; **October 2024** with a Czech transposition timeline that was then forward-looking; **the SRG is an advisory/representation body**, so its positions are policy views rather than decisions) | **R3/R4 crisis-mode redundancy and circumventability** (+ **R9/R10/R11 — AI must not become the risk factor**; **R7 a possible cybersecurity TSI**; R14/ADR-012; R5 space/GNSS) | validate | **A — and (e) is the most directly load-bearing paragraph the 4th conference produced for this engagement.** **① (e) STATES ADR-002's AND ADR-004's PREMISE AS A MEMBER STATES POLICY CONCERN.** "It is necessary to consider how to ensure that the development of new smart information technologies, **artificial intelligence**, and automation of transport processes **is not the main risk factor or a limiting factor in safety of the rail system**." **That is R9, R10 and R11 in one sentence, from the Member States.** ADR-002 proposes an agentic oversight layer; ADR-004 exists precisely to ensure the learning components cannot become a risk factor for the safety case. **Having the SRG articulate the same concern — in 2024, before the engagement's own framing — is useful external validation that the guardrail is the right one to build the architecture around**, and it is quotable to a client audience that includes national ministries. **② (e)'s SECOND REQUIREMENT IS R4, STATED MORE RADICALLY THAN THE REGISTER HAS IT — AND IT CARRIES A REAL TENSION WORTH NAMING.** "**Critical safety on-board and ground systems should be implemented in a way that allows for them to be CIRCUMVENTED in the case of a cyber threat**", and "**redundancy needs to be ensured in 'crisis mode' with MINIMAL IT SUPPORT**". The second half is straightforwardly R4 — fail-soft, degraded mode, and notably a **degraded mode whose defining property is minimal IT dependency**, which is a sharper formulation than "multi-bearer fallback" and closer to what the 23-June outage actually demanded (manual recovery, local working). **But the first half deserves careful handling: a safety system designed to be circumventable under cyber threat is, by construction, a system with a bypass path — and a bypass path is an attack surface.** The Member States' requirement (an intervention route that survives a cyber event) is in direct tension with the security requirement (no unauthorised bypass), and **resolving that tension is exactly what ADR-004's design does**: a unidirectional boundary with human-in-command interposition means the *human* can act when the automation cannot be trusted, without creating a machine-reachable bypass. **Recommend ADR-004 address this explicitly** — it is the strongest policy-side argument for the human-in-command gate, and it also connects to Thron's observation that operator privileges must be sufficient to act in a degraded mode (E-2026-08-15-44). **③ (a)'s "IS THERE A NEED FOR A NEW CYBER SECURITY TSI?" IS THE MEMBER-STATES-SIDE VERSION OF THE TSI GAP THE REGISTER HAS DOCUMENTED FROM THREE OTHER DIRECTIONS.** The ERTMS Users Group established that the CCS TSI subsets' security scope is confined to cross-border interoperability (RBC-Euroradio) and sits below commonly agreed standards (E-2026-08-15-19); EPSF stated in 2022 and again in 2025 that the TSIs and CSMs carry "no explicit requirement for cybersecurity" (E-2026-08-15-10, -35); ERA's own intent to reflect security in the TSIs has been unchanged since 2019 (E-2026-08-15-02, -46). **Here the Member States ask whether a dedicated cybersecurity TSI is needed at all.** That is a distinct policy option the register had not recorded — neither ERA nor the sector bodies have proposed a *separate* TSI — and it is worth tracking, because a cybersecurity TSI would change the instrument landscape ADR-012 assumes. **④ (b)'s "REGULATION BETTER THAN DIRECTIVE BECAUSE OF INTEROPERABILITY" DIAGNOSES THE FRAGMENTATION'S LEGAL CAUSE.** The register has now documented divergence in supervisory competence (FR/DE/FI/BE — E-2026-08-15-35, -11, -12, -49), in technical specification (per-operator security solutions, E-2026-08-15-19), in supplier practice (E-2026-08-15-34) and in capacity-management IT (E-2026-08-15-42). **A Member State representative here names the mechanism: a Directive is transposed twenty-seven ways, and for an interoperable system that is itself the problem.** Belgium's own transposition deck illustrates the point precisely — national law, a national framework (CyFun), a national sectoral authority and national fines (E-2026-08-15-49). **This is a clean, citable articulation of why rail cyber governance fragments, and it is worth carrying into any client discussion of cross-border assurance.** **⑤ (c) CONNECTS THE SPACE/GNSS THREAD TO RAIL AT POLICY LEVEL — and it is the earliest such connection on file.** Naming **Galileo, EGNOS and GOVSATCOM** alongside a slide titled "impact of space technologies used in digitalization and automation of the rail system to cyber security" places satellite dependency in the rail cyber picture in **October 2024**, more than a year before the Estonian minister's GNSS-jamming warning that opened this gap in the register (E-2026-08-15-24). **The gap flagged there — that GNSS appears in this register only as a surveying tool, never as a jammable dependency — is therefore not merely an oversight but a live sector topic the register had missed.** The relevant slide is an unread image; **worth re-rendering if the GNSS-denial follow-up is pursued.** **⑥ (e)'s STRATEGIC FRAMING IS THE THIRD MILITARY-MOBILITY INSTANCE AND THE EARLIEST.** "Rail transport is a key strategic sector which must ensure the operation of other key sectors at all circumstances… **defence**, national internal security, the integrated rescue system and strategic industries" — together with the "**military and RID**" freight slide. The register recorded this framing from Estonia and Finland at the 5th conference (E-2026-08-15-24, -27); **this shows it present at Member-States level a year earlier**, which strengthens the case that defence readiness is a durable third justification for R3/R4 alongside safety and economic continuity. **⑦ (d) IS A BREADTH ARGUMENT** — cybersecurity spanning rolling-stock production through signalling and control to ticketing and consignment notes, mapped onto TSIs, **Reg (EU) 2020/1056** and the CIV/RID/CIM instruments — consistent with the "whole entity" scoping the Belgian authority gave NIS-2 (E-2026-08-15-49) and a reminder that the regulated surface reaches well beyond the CCS estate. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R3 stays "Strengthened post-incident — open", R4 Open — **(e) restates them at policy level but proves nothing**; R9/R10/R11 Proposed; R7 Accepted — the cybersecurity-TSI question is recorded as a policy option, not a change; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed. No matrix change, no ADR change — **though ADR-004 has a specific item to address (the circumventability-versus-bypass tension at ②)**, offered not made. **FOLLOW-UPS:** (1) **re-render the six unread image slides**, particularly "impact of space technologies…" and "interoperability framework… (incl. telematics as non-safety related TSI)"; (2) whether the **cybersecurity-TSI question** has been taken up anywhere since — it did not appear at the 5th conference; (3) the **SRG's own outputs** on cybersecurity, if any are published. "included in the unified European transport corridor **with Ukraine**, as part of the Baltic Sea-Black Sea-Aegean Sea TEN-T corridor"; "**bridging a missing transport link by 2030**"; delivering EU, regional and national ambitions. **(b) SUPPLY-CHAIN SCALE** — "**Over 300 international and Baltic-based suppliers**", "**> 4.7bn EUR of suppliers' contracts signed**", drawn from Austria, Belgium, Bulgaria, Denmark, Finland, France, Germany, Hungary, Ireland, Italy, Poland, Slovakia, Spain, Sweden, Türkiye and the UK — with "partnerships and potential suppliers' interest from the **US, Japan, and other non-EU countries**". **(c) GREENFIELD OPPORTUNITY vs CHALLENGES** — on the opportunity side, "**GREENFIELD: adoption of latest technologies for CCS (LIKE FRMCS) and for cybersecurity; having interfaces between systems — **CCS/Rolling Stock/IT/ENE**") · **Technological Capabilities** ("market readiness, product availability"). **(e) "CHALLENGES, TOLERANCES, RISK APPETITE — looking for the RED LINES. Who manages the network? Who can see the data?**"; and **Artificial Intelligence** — "**Runs trains? NO! Fights against threats? ABSOLUTELY!**". Concluding: "all-in-all [the] **NIS2 directive is helpful in setting common policy and requirements for data centres, cloud services, AI, etc.**" **(f) KEY TAKEAWAYS** — "**cybersecurity is NOT A DOMAIN ON ITS OWN OR IN ISOLATION; it is PART OF ALL ASPECTS AND ACTIVITIES of the project.** Digital components and services are ubiquitous; therefore cybersecurity must be there. As railways become more digital, [a] new spectrum of threats arises"; "**cybersecurity considerations are IMPACTING [the] ARCHITECTURE of the system(s)**"; "**NIS2 is helpful although national legal acts are still fresh**"; and "**it is our responsibility to make a safe environment and provide feedback rather than PASSIVELY WAIT FOR NIS2 v2**".
