NO! FIGHTS AGAINST THREATS? ABSOLUTELY!" IS ADR-002's POSITION EXACTLY.** ADR-002 places learning agents **over** the bearer as autonomous **oversight, not control**; ADR-004 exists to keep them outside the vital perimeter; R9 makes autonomy a separate governable layer; R11 keeps the deterministic SIL-4 kernel free of them. **Rail Baltica states the same division as a RISK-APPETITE RED LINE for a project being built now** — AI is admitted for defence and threat detection, refused for train operation. **That is independent, in-domain corroboration that the engagement's guardrail is the position a serious greenfield project actually takes**, and it is the most compact articulation of it found anywhere in this corpus. It also sits directly alongside the Member States' concern, from the same conference, that AI and automation "must not be the main risk factor or a limiting factor in safety" (E-2026-08-15-50), and DB's own designation of CTMS as non-safety-critical with safety authority residing in the APS (E-2026-07-02-14). **Three independent sources, one boundary.** **② (c) NAMES FRMCS AS THE GREENFIELD CCS CHOICE**, alongside cybersecurity, with "joint initiatives with the existing 1520 mm network". **Rail Baltica is therefore a live European FRMCS-era greenfield deployment with a 2030 target** — and, unusually, one without a GSM-R legacy to migrate from. For **R2** (a decade-plus of coexistence) and **R13** (retrofit), it is the counterfactual: **the same technology choice without the brownfield constraint**, which makes it a useful comparator for isolating which costs and risks are inherent to FRMCS and which are inherent to *migration*. Worth watching as the engagement's cleanest external FRMCS reference point after Digirail (E-2026-08-15-27, which is pre-FRMCS on commercial radio). **③ (e)'s CONNECTIVITY QUESTIONS ARE THE FRMCS BEARER-GOVERNANCE QUESTIONS, ASKED PLAINLY: "Who owns the cable? Who manages the network? Who can see the data?"** The register has circled these from several directions — public-MNO transport under UIC FRMCS-T (E-2026-08-01-24), national roaming (E-2026-07-28-01), Digirail on commercial 5G (E-2026-08-15-27), and the §4a question of who owns operational security of the rail-telecom layer. **A greenfield project treating bearer ownership, network management and data visibility as explicit red-line decisions is the right framing, and the engagement should adopt those three questions verbatim** when assessing FRMCS bearer options. The adjacent cloud and data-centre-ownership questions ("Infra Manager owned / State owned / other") are the same problem one layer up, and connect to the Cloud4Rail and safety-critical-to-cloud threads (E-2026-07-02-27, E-2026-08-15-33). **④ (f)'s "CYBERSECURITY CONSIDERATIONS ARE IMPACTING THE ARCHITECTURE OF THE SYSTEM(S)" IS THE POINT ADR-012 AND ADR-004 BOTH DEPEND ON.** Not a control set bolted on, but a determinant of architecture — which is why security-by-design is cheap in greenfield and expensive in retrofit (cf. ILT's "expensive and complex to retrofit", E-2026-08-15-40). **And "it is our responsibility to make a safe environment and provide feedback rather than passively wait for NIS2 v2" is a notably mature regulatory posture** — of a piece with the sector's shift from resisting the CRA to applying it (E-2026-08-15-47, -30). **⑤ (c)'s "JOINT EFFORTS vs COUNTRY SEPARATION, e.g. for a CYBERSECURITY OPERATION CENTRE" IS THE CROSS-BORDER SOC QUESTION — and it is R12's problem at supranational scale.** Rail Baltica spans three Member States with three legislative bases, three ministries and three beneficiaries; whether it runs one SOC or three is a live question. **Set against Infrabel's single-operator SOC reaching 10% coverage on railway infrastructure (E-2026-08-15-39) and the CISO Forum's observation that "cyber incidents can cross national borders" (E-2026-08-15-48), a tri-national greenfield SOC would be the most instructive case study available for R12** — worth tracking which way it went. **⑥ (b) IS A SUPPLY-CHAIN SCALE DATUM FOR R8/PR13: over 300 suppliers and more than €4.7bn of signed contracts**, spanning 16 named countries plus US/Japan interest. Every one of those is a CRA manufacturer and, per CER's mapping, potentially a NIS-2 **important entity** (E-2026-08-15-18). **That is the concrete scale of the supply-chain conformance problem ADR-012 action 2 addresses**, on a project that also has the leverage to impose terms — which is why the Estonian minister could report cybersecurity written into Rail Baltica's design guidelines, architecture and tender conditions (E-2026-08-15-24). **⑦ (d) LISTS EULYNX ALONGSIDE TS 50701 AND IEC 62443** — EULYNX being the interface-standardisation initiative for signalling; it appears in this register only in passing (via the CER cooperation map and the ESCG inputs) and is worth noting as part of the greenfield standards baseline. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R9/R10/R11 stay Proposed — (e) is corroboration of the guardrail, not evidence about the engagement's own design; R5/R6 Accepted; R2/R13 unchanged; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R3/R4 unchanged. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **render and read the ~10 unread slides** — this row is materially incomplete and the unread pages may contain the design-guideline and tender-condition detail the Estonian minister referenced at E-2026-08-15-24, which is the ADR-012 action-2 precedent the register most wants; (2) **Rail Baltica's cybersecurity tender conditions and design guidelines** as documents; (3) whether the **tri-national Cybersecurity Operation Centre** was established jointly or per country; (4) Rail Baltica's **FRMCS** deployment plan, as a greenfield comparator to the German migration. Paraphrased: **(a) A EUROPEAN LEGISLATIVE TIMELINE 2022→2026 and beyond** — **2022 UNECE R155 & R156** ("new regulation for [the] automotive sector — cars and supply chain"); **2023 Cyber Skills Academy** ("EU-wide program for closing the cyber skills gap"); **2024 NIS 2** ("essential & important entities must reinforce cyber measures **and supply chain**"); **2025 DORA** (traditional and non-traditional financial institutes); and, for **2026 and beyond**, **CRA** ("all connected products must demonstrate cyber conformity"), **MPR** ("**machinery must account for AI and Cybersecurity for safety**") and **RED** ("**radio devices must reinforce cybersecurity**"). **(b) IMPACT ON THE VALUE CHAIN — four roles and the question each must answer:** *owners/operator* — "where are my risks? how can I prevent attacks? how can I prepare for attacks?"; *system integrator* — "am I aware of all the connected devices and systems? am I sure the entire system is designed securely?"; *manufacturers* — "am I in compliance with the requirements of the market? are the products I'm procuring secure?"; *supply chain* — "am I producing secure products? **how can I demonstrate my products are secure?**" **(c) THE REGULATORY HOOKS** — slides headed "**NIS2 Directive – supply chain requirements: should suppliers be NIS2 ready?**" pointing to **Articles 21 and 22**, and "**ISO/IEC 27001 supply chain requirements: should suppliers be ISO/IEC 27001 ready?**" pointing to **Domain A.15** (both slides are images; the article/domain text itself did not extract). **(d) THE SUPPLY-CHAIN PROCESS — three stages:** *qualification* ("high level checklist to be pre-filled by a supplier"; "qualification to be included in [an] **approved supplier list**"); *contracting* ("high level commitment to Cybersecurity"; "**agreement for third party audits once a year**"); *monitoring* ("review based (on site audits)"; "assessment based (detailed review of evidence)"). **(e) A FOUR-STEP METHOD WITH TIMELINES** — **Step 1** create supply-chain management procedures, covering **direct and indirect purchasing** (**2 months**); **Step 2** prepare the list of requirements for suppliers, "defining criteria to split suppliers into different categories" and questionnaires for low- and high-risk suppliers (**1 month**); **Step 3** supplier verification — reviews for low risks, assessments for high risks (**6-12 months**); **Step 4** monitoring — re-occurring review, follow-up on "reported supplier changes, incidents etc" (**re-occurring**). **(f) STEP 1 IN DETAIL — TWO PARALLEL TRACKS:** *supply chain management – **ISMS**, focus IT*, covering the **indirect purchasing** process (qualification, contracting, monitoring) under "**Information Security Requirements for Supplier Relationship Management**"; and *supply chain management – **SDLC**, focus components for products*, covering the **direct purchasing** process under "**Cyber Security Requirements for third party components**". Each with "required templates: **contract, supplier checklist, NDA** etc". **(g) STEPS 2-3** — *supplier review, low risk*: "quick check of suppliers based on [a] prepared checklist… based on the company requirements or best practice in cyber security (e.g. ISO 27001)"; *supplier assessment, high risk*: "detailed review of suppliers including risk assessments… requirements assessed **including evidence review from each supplier**". **(h) STEP 4** — "important to perform regular review of existing suppliers **at least once a year**"; "focus on potential changes of supplier details and specific risks"; "**reaction to incidents related to suppliers and integration into [the] general incident response process**"; "this is a **process** rather than [a] one-time activity"; and "using a **dedicated supply chain monitoring tool** will simplify the process". **(i) FIVE STATED BEST PRACTICES** — "the volume of supplies for a large organization can be in the **thousands**; **focus on critical and high risks first**"; "use requirements from **existing standards such as ISO27K and IEC 62443** — this will support **consistency in the eco-system**"; "**agree on the responsibilities for cyber security in [the] supply chain EARLY in the process**, whether it is purchasing, [the] IS team, [the] product security team etc."; "select the optimal contracting way to include cyber security — **negotiating will be a difficult process**"; and "pay attention to [the] specifics of direct and indirect purchasing — in certain cases even products used directly in production might come under **indirect** purchasing". This is the **third** independent source to raise it: **tmc** listed the EU Radio Equipment Directive among the instruments it complies with (E-2026-08-15-29), and **EPSF** referred to "general cybersecurity requirements + **specialised regulations (radio)**" with a warning about "risk of discrepancies in directive transpositions" (E-2026-08-15-35). **A check confirms the register still holds NO evidentiary row on the RED — the only two occurrences of the term are my own flags in those two notes.** For an engagement whose entire subject is a **radio** system — FRMCS cab radios, modems and on-board radio modules — a product directive requiring radio equipment to "reinforce cybersecurity" is a plausible **third binding instrument alongside the CRA and NIS-2**, and ADR-012's legal basis currently names only two. **This should now be closed: obtain the RED (Dir 2014/53/EU) and its cybersecurity delegated act, and determine whether FRMCS radio equipment falls in scope and how it interacts with the CRA.** **② AND (a) SURFACES A SECOND, ENTIRELY ABSENT INSTRUMENT: the MPR — "machinery must account for AI and CYBERSECURITY FOR SAFETY".** The Machinery Regulation has **zero occurrences** in this register. Its significance here is the phrasing: a product regulation that ties **AI and cybersecurity to SAFETY functions** is directly adjacent to ADR-004's territory (learning components inside or outside a safety perimeter) and to ADR-003's AI-Act classification work. **Worth a scoping check** — whether it reaches rail rolling stock or infrastructure machinery, and whether its AI-and-safety provisions bear on the agentic layer. Two new EU product instruments in one slide is a material finding for ADR-012's completeness. **③ (e)'s TIMELINES ARE THE MOST USEFUL SINGLE DATUM FOR ADR-012 ACTION 2: roughly 9-15 MONTHS to stand up supply-chain security management** (2 + 1 + 6-12 months, then perpetual). ADR-012 action 2 proposes CRA evidence as a bid condition but says nothing about how long building that capability takes. **Against a CRA full-application date of 11.12.2027 and an FRMCS procurement programme already running, a 9-15 month lead time is a scheduling constraint the engagement should state explicitly.** **④ (f)'s ISMS/SDLC SPLIT IS A DISTINCTION ADR-012 CURRENTLY BLURS.** Indirect purchasing (IT and services) is governed through the **ISMS** under supplier-relationship requirements; direct purchasing (components that go into the product) is governed through the **SDLC** under third-party-component requirements. **These are different obligations, different owners and different evidence** — and ADR-012 action 2 currently treats "manufacturer CRA evidence" as one category. The distinction matters for FRMCS: a cab-radio module is a direct-purchase product component; a managed SOC or a cloud service is indirect. **⑤ (i)'s BEST PRACTICES CORROBORATE THREE THINGS ALREADY ON FILE.** "Volume… in the thousands; **focus on critical and high risks first**" is the same prioritisation logic as CRSG's **Cyber Critical Assets** and EPSF's "concentrate on CCA" (E-2026-08-15-30, -35), and it is the practical answer to Rail Baltica's 300+ suppliers (E-2026-08-15-51). "**Agree on the responsibilities… early**" echoes the role-allocation problem IEC 63452 addresses with "flexibility on allocation of some requirements (e.g. who shall perform Risk Assessment)" (E-2026-08-15-32). And "**negotiating will be a difficult process**" is a consultancy's candid acknowledgement of what UNIFE's position implies — suppliers will resist, and the tailor-made exemptions permitting **chargeable patches** are exactly where they will resist (E-2026-08-15-28, -30). **⑥ (h)'s "REACTION TO INCIDENTS RELATED TO SUPPLIERS AND INTEGRATION INTO THE GENERAL INCIDENT RESPONSE PROCESS" IS A REQUIREMENT THE REGISTER HAS EVIDENCE FOR.** NCSC-EE recorded **service-provider attacks (Ridango, Collins Aerospace)** as a distinct incident class in Estonian transport (E-2026-08-15-25), and "Digital Backpack 2" halted Danish railways through a third-party supplier (E-2026-08-15-46). **Supplier incidents are not hypothetical, and an incident-response process that does not cover them has a documented gap.** **⑦ CAVEATS.** The method is asserted without case evidence; the firm sells the assessments it recommends; and the NIS-2 Article 21/22 and ISO 27001 A.15 detail is in unread images, so the regulatory grounding here is by reference only — the register's own primary-law rows govern. **DOES NOT MOVE A DECISION:** R8 stays Recommended; R14 Open, ADR-012 Proposed; R13 Open. No matrix change, no ADR change. **FOLLOW-UPS, ranked:** (1) **the Radio Equipment Directive (Dir 2014/53/EU) and its cybersecurity delegated act — does it reach FRMCS radio equipment, and how does it interact with the CRA?** Now flagged from three independent sources and still unevidenced; (2) the **Machinery Regulation (MPR)** — scope check, particularly its AI-and-cybersecurity-for-safety provisions against ADR-004/ADR-003; (3) whether ADR-012 action 2 should adopt the **ISMS/SDLC split** and state the **9-15 month** capability lead time. Benoliel across three affiliations); content here is programme fact, not vendor promotion, so the affiliation qualifies rather than downgrades it. **(a) WORKFLOW — 42 INPUT DOCUMENTS CONSOLIDATED INTO FOUR SPECS**, all dated **01/2025**: **Shared Cybersecurity Services Spec · Secure Component Spec · Secure Communication Spec · Security Program Requirements**. Named inputs: **UNISIG SUBSET-146 & 147**, **TSI CCS 2023**, **Baseline 4 R2 detailed security requirements**, **ESCG Security Measures**. Mirror groups: **UNIFE/UNISIG** and **EUG/EULYNX (RSEG)**. Output also feeds selected **Innovation Pillar** demonstrator projects. **(b) THE HEADLINE — THIS CLOSES THE LOOP ON E-2026-08-15-19.** ESCG's 2022 defect was that "current subsets focus on cross-border interoperability (RBC-Euroradio) ONLY", forcing operators into **"SPECIFIC SOLUTIONS PER OPERATOR"** — the fragmentation that drives **R7** and the lock-in that drives **R8**. Here **ESCG Security Measures appears as a named INPUT** to the System Pillar work, and the output is **common specs that the TSIs will reference**. The remedy ESCG asked for in 2022 is real, named, and in progress — **but at the date of this deck it was still forward-dated**, so this strengthens R7/R8 justification without moving either status. **(c) EIGHT SHARED CYBERSECURITY SERVICES DEFINED** (with interfaces): **STS** secure time synchronisation · **PKI** · **IAM** · **NAC** network access control · **LOG** security logging · **UAS** user authentication · **BKP** backup/restore · **DNS**. **⚠️ R12 CAVEAT — a shared LOG service is NOT the detection fix.** E-2026-08-15-03 (ENISA 2019-20 rail survey) measured EU rail operators as **HIGH-maturity at logging** and **LOW-maturity at DETECTION and at log correlation/analysis**. Mandating a common logging service builds the plumbing the ENISA gap already had; it does not supply correlation, analysis or security indicators. **R12's justification is unchanged by this deck and the gap it names remains open.** **(d) LINEAGE INTO IEC 63452 — CONFIRMED FROM THE REGISTER'S OWN PRIMARY MATERIAL.** Outlook slide: liaison with **IEC TC9 PT 63452** and "alignment of the specifications (**system pillar specification as a reference system for IEC PT 63452**)". The register can therefore trace a complete chain: **ESCG defect (2022, -19) → ERJU SP specs (01/2025, this row) → IEC 63452 §4 (CDV 2025, -17/-20/-15-32)**. **(e) RED — FOURTH INDEPENDENT POINTER, AND THE FIRST FROM THE BODY THAT WRITES THE SPECS.** The "European Security Compliance" slide sets **European legislation: NIS 2 · CSA · CRA · RED** against **standards: ISO 27001 · IEC 62443 (2-1, 2-4, 3-3, 4-1, 4-2) · CLC/TS 50701 · IEC 63452**, under "**Target: compliance and full tracing**". After **-29** (tmc), **-35** (EPSF) and **-52** (Secura), the **Radio Equipment Directive** is now named by ERJU System Pillar itself. **This is an institutional anchor, NOT a primary RED analysis — the register still holds no evidentiary row on the RED, and for an FRMCS programme (radio equipment) that remains the single most conspicuous scope gap.** → ADR-012. **(f) ALL-TSI CYBERSECURITY GAP ANALYSIS.** ERA request **Q1 2024**: gap analysis + Change Requests across **CCS, TELEM (former TAF/TAP), OPE, LOC&PAS, WAG, INF, ENE TSI, RINF, ERATV/EVR**. +| E-2026-08-15-54 | 2026-08-15 | "**Improving Cybersecurity on Legacy Trains through Retrofit**" — **A. Cowan (RazorSecure)** + **JT. de Feijter (NS / Nederlandse Spoorwegen)**; 10 slides; PDF created 2024-10-07, author metadata **Daniel Jaeggi** | Joint operator + vendor deployment case study | **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 02–03.10.2024**, session 5-2 | **B** | **R13** (primary) · **R8/PR13** · **R14/ADR-012** · **R3/R4** · **R12** · **ADR-009** · **ADR-007** · **ADR-011** | **validate** | **TIER B — AND THE TIERING IS THE POINT, BECAUSE THIS DECK IS TWO DOCUMENTS STAPLED TOGETHER.** It is a **product deployment case study co-presented by the vendor whose product is the subject**, so the row takes the lower tier. But the substance splits cleanly and the register should not lose that: **(i) VENDOR-GRADE, DO NOT CITE — RazorSecure's credentials slide (9 active operators · 5 train builders · 6 years production deployment; slide marked "Confidential") and especially the headline "391% increase in portscans + denial of service, 2022–2024" — measured on "threats recorded on trains protected by RazorSecure", i.e. THE VENDOR'S OWN INSTALL BASE, with no denominator, no methodology, no baseline definition. It is unfalsifiable as presented and must not be used as a threat-trend figure.** **(ii) OPERATOR-GRADE IN SUBSTANCE — NS's own account of its own retrofit programme, first-person, with concrete numbers AND its own failures. Were this sourced independently of the vendor it would be tier A; treat its factual content as strong, its product framing as B.** **(a) THE HEADLINE — THIS IS THE FIRST COMPLETED FLEET-SCALE CYBER RETROFIT IN THE REGISTER, WITH EXECUTION NUMBERS.** R13 has until now been carried entirely on **German funding/approval/supply** evidence (E-2026-06-24-11/-13/-17/-21 etc.) — i.e. on whether retrofit is *fundable and approvable*, never on whether it is *executable*. NS supplies the missing half: target **~1200 deployments**; **first fleet completed in 4 months, final August 2024**; **one train per day, 4 per week, 4 mechanics per day**; per-train work package of **7 new cables, OT and IT switch updates, WAP updates, 2 new devices, plus testing**; **pre-try-out + 4 try-outs** before series. **This is the empirical anchor ADR-009 lacked.** **(b) ⚠️ THE MOST VALUABLE LINE IS A FAILURE, AND IT IS THIS REGISTER'S RECURRING THEME VERBATIM: "3 week delay because we encountered issues in IP plan with 4 coach trains (NOT REVEALED DURING TESTING)."** A **fleet variant** (3-car vs 4-car) that the test regime did not surface, costing three weeks in series production. NS's own top tip names the fix — "**consider variations within a train fleet (3 vs 4 car)**" — alongside "**invest in a good test bench**" and "fully understand the onboard network: physical, logical and traffic flows". **This is the 23-June lesson in a new domain — a change to live legacy estate failing on a condition testing did not represent — and it is a direct, concrete requirement on ADR-007: the pre-change test surface must span fleet/configuration VARIANTS, not just the modal configuration.** → new ADR-009 action item. **(c) THE IPS/IDS QUESTION IS THIS ENGAGEMENT'S GUARDRAIL, REACHED INDEPENDENTLY BY AN OPERATOR.** Among the unresolved management dilemmas: "**Do we allow IPS IDS to intervene (block) in network traffic or not?**" On a moving train, an IPS that blocks is **autonomously actuating on a safety-carrying network** — precisely the *oversight, not control* boundary that ADR-002 draws and ADR-004 polices. **NS did not treat this as a technical default; it escalated it as a governance decision.** Strong independent corroboration that the guardrail is the real question, not an artefact of this engagement's framing. **(d) ANTI-LOCK-IN WAS A STATED PROCUREMENT REQUIREMENT AND IT IS VISIBLE IN THE ARCHITECTURE.** NS required the setup be "**interchangeable (no vendor lock-in, vendor agnostic stack)**". Slide 7 (read visually) realises it: the **Security Gateway** carries a **swappable firewall slot with three options — 1. open-source firewall · 2. open-source firewall + TRDP protocol filter · 3. next-gen firewall** — with **RazorSecure Delta**, a **Repository VM** and a **Network Services VM** as separate modules alongside. **Two of the three firewall options are open-source, and the vendor component is decoupled from the enforcement point.** This is a **directly copyable pattern for the FRMCS procurement gate (R8/PR13, ADR-012 action 2)**: specify the enforcement slot, keep it vendor-agnostic, let the vendor value sit in a replaceable module. (Caveat: this component view is on RazorSecure's own slide template, i.e. the vendor's rendering of its own decoupling.) **(e) OTHER STATED REQUIREMENTS** — in-train **network segmentation** with inspection between segments; **default-deny between segments** (only previously configured inter-segment traffic permitted); monitoring for traffic legitimacy via **IDS + deep packet inspection + anomaly detection**; scale to ~1200 units. Trigger for the programme: a **fleet-wide risk analysis** which "led to the insight that certain rolling stock is vulnerable to cyber risk **more than we initially anticipated**" — an operator discovering its own exposure was worse than assumed (**R12**). **(f) THE "HOT POTATO" GOVERNANCE PATTERN — CHEAP, COPYABLE, WORTH SURFACING TO THE CLIENT.** Unresolvable dilemmas were not left to drift: "some of these were classified as '**hot potato**' and transformed in a **steering committee decision memo**." **That is an ADR by another name, arrived at independently** — an operator inventing decision-records because unowned technical dilemmas were blocking a retrofit. Direct support for the arcKit method's core claim. **(g) OPEN QUESTIONS NS RECORDED AND DID NOT ANSWER** (useful because they map the real decision space): combined HW+SW or separated? allow OS **virtualization**? which host OS? **monitor MVB, CAN or other OT messaging buses**? one-size-fits-all across fleets? — noting that "**implementing FW as a Layer 3 device required a change to the ENTIRE network**" (a Rückwirkungsfreiheit-class scope explosion, **ADR-004**); and on the management side: **OT SOC or not** (**R12**)? field device replacement? security-incident management? "**How to prevent difficult homologation processes?**" (**the authorisation-vs-security-change tension ADR-011/ADR-012 name — independently confirmed by an operator**)? how to detect a **failing** device (**R3/R4** — the appliance is itself a new element that can fail silently)? manage 1200 devices? allow **cloud/SaaS-managed** solutions (cf. IEC 63452 Annex K)? **(h) MAINTAINABILITY ENGINEERED DELIBERATELY** — "much effort was focussed to transforming the solution into a solid and low effort '**field replaceable unit**' that fits NS RS mechanics processes (**auto detect and install**)". At 1200 units the sustainment model was designed, not assumed. **(i) READING CAVEATS, HONESTLY:** slide 5 presents **five network diagrams as thumbnails, illegible by design** — they convey "the dirty work" of asset analysis and hand-drawn segmentation zoning, not readable detail; I rendered it and confirm no extractable content. Slide 3's 391% chart was read but is vendor-sourced (see (i) above). **No verification of any NS claim was possible from independent sources.** **DOES NOT FLIP A STATUS:** R13 stays **Open — external dependency** (this is *executability* evidence from **NL rolling stock, a different operator, fleet and regulatory context**, and it does not touch the German funding/approval/chipset dependencies that actually gate R13); R8 **Open**; R14 **Open**; ADR-009/ADR-012 **Proposed**. Matrix: **R13 evidence-ref updated**. ADR-009: **empirical anchor + two action items added** (variant-spanning test coverage; retrofit execution-planning basis). Handling: **"Intern"** on every page + slide 2 **"Confidential"** — private repo only, not for client deliverables without consent. SECURITY item → also mirrored in `evidence-log-security.md`. |
+| E-2026-08-15-55 | 2026-08-15 | "**Rolling Stock — Cybersecurity / Cyber Onions**" — **Evan Jones**, Security Lead Architect & Business Developer, **Complete Cyber** (UK, Dudley; ~10 yrs; ex-Transport for London / London Underground systems engineer); 14 slides | Consultancy threat/risk taxonomy — offensive-testing perspective | **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille, 02–03.10.2024**, session 5-3 | **B** | **ADR-007** (primary) · **R3/R4** · **R11/ADR-004** · **R12** · **R14/ADR-012** · **R8/PR13** · R13 | **watch** | **TIER B — AND UNUSUALLY CLEARLY SO.** The deck opens on a company-overview slide listing the five services the firm sells (security architecture, **vulnerability & penetration testing**, audit & risk assessment, security monitoring, asset monitoring & management), carries a `whoami` slide, closes on contact details — and **key takeaway 4.I is literally "ensure services offering monitoring and response are added into bids and offered to Clients", i.e. advice to procure the presenter's own service category.** No client named, no engagement cited, no finding attributed to a specific test, train type or disclosure. Treat the taxonomy as a **checklist of what a rail pentester expects to find**, which is genuinely useful, and treat every specific claim as unevidenced. **(a) THE HEADLINE FOR THIS REGISTER IS A GAP IN OUR OWN ADR, NOT A FINDING IN THE DECK.** Reading this against **ADR-007** exposes that all six of its action items — incident-replay corpus, shadow-on-legacy, silent-fault/failover injection, canary-by-segment, MCX feature-parity, G3 criteria — are **reliability and functional-parity testing**. **ADR-007 currently has NO security test surface: no penetration testing, no red-team, no independent tester, no security regression.** ADR-012 makes security a first-class governed dimension and routes its change gate through ADR-007, so the two ADRs do not currently meet. **A tier-B consultancy deck cannot evidence that gap — it merely prompted the reading — but the gap is real and is now ADR-007 action item 7.** **(b) ⚠️ THE ALARMING CLAIM, AND WHY THIS REGISTER'S OWN PRECEDENT DEFUSES IT.** Under *Physical Security → Risks* the deck asserts "**Force Emergency Breaks [sic] On (when travelling at speeds of excess 125 kph)**", reached from panel access with a **generic key shape** plus cable removal (the "toilet example" — discreet panel access). Taken at face value this reads as a physical-access path to safety-critical actuation, i.e. an **ADR-004 freedom-from-interference** violation in the physical domain. **But apply the register's own established position, set at SIM-SEC-001: "malicious triggering of a safety function is an AVAILABILITY loss (fail-safe, not fail-soft)" and T0880 Loss of Safety is NOT applicable when the fail-safe operates as designed.** Forcing an emergency brake commands the **safe state**. So this is a **T0814/T0826 availability event bearing R4 (fail-soft) and R3**, of the nationwide-standstill class — **not a SIL-4 integrity breach and not an R11 finding**, and it should never be characterised as one. **Honest nuance: an emergency brake application at >125 kph carries real passenger-injury risk to unbraced/standing passengers, which is an operational-safety consequence the R-set does not currently frame — but that is distinct from loss of safety integrity.** **The claim is UNEVIDENCED and must not be cited; it is worth VERIFYING, which is why this row is `watch` and not `validate`.** **(c) THE "ONION" — A FIVE-LAYER ROLLING-STOCK THREAT TAXONOMY, MITRE ATT&CK CNI-mapped by the author.** Context set first: modern rolling stock uses **fault-tolerant Ethernet ring** networks, **serial buses for safety-critical systems (RS422/485, CAN, MVB** — the deck writes "MVP", read as MVB**)**, plus **wireless (passenger/staff WiFi, 4/5G remote connectivity)**. Layers: **1 Physical** (generic-key panel access, cable removal, discreet access, **lack of both manual AND automated physical inspection**) → **2 Network** (**flat network enabling lateral movement**, **default/weak credentials on switches**, **no ACLs for segregation**, poor network management; risk: "**lateral movement within network with no-detection in place**", routing-control change attacks) → **3 Web & API** (exposed services without authN/authZ, service enumeration, integrity/confidentiality absent from web/API development; risk: privilege abuse, **server-side web shell for backdoors**) → **4 System Asset Compromise** (limited hardening making initial access and escalation easy, **lack of monitoring/detection makes system modification possible**, service/data interception) → **5 Data Access & Manipulation** (core data integrity/confidentiality unprotected, a "**Trust-All**" posture enabling sniffing and extraction, **parameter modification**). **(d) THE PAIRING WITH E-2026-08-15-54 IS THE MOST VALUABLE THING HERE — THE SAME ESTATE FROM BOTH ENDS, AT THE SAME CONFERENCE, FROM UNCONNECTED PARTIES.** Jones (UK pentest consultancy, offensive) and NS/RazorSecure (NL operator + vendor, defensive) share no affiliation, yet the threat list and the appliance requirements map almost one-to-one: "**flat network for lateral access**" ↔ NS "must be possible to **segment** the network in the train"; "**lack of network ACLs for segregation**" ↔ NS **default-deny between segments** (only pre-configured inter-segment traffic); "**lateral movement with no-detection**" ↔ NS **IDS + deep packet inspection + anomaly detection**; "**default or weak credentials on switches**" ↔ NS's per-train **OT/IT switch updates**. **This is genuine independent corroboration that the on-train weakness set is real and generic across European fleets — the one substantive conclusion this row supports, and it holds despite the tier.** **(e) THE PRESCRIPTIONS ARE CONVENTIONAL AND MOSTLY ALREADY IN THE REGISTER'S ADRs** — secure-by-design through OEMs with a named **Product/Project Manager carrying cybersecurity responsibility**; **IEC 62443-3-3 for system security and 62443-4-1/-4-2 for product/supply-chain security** (deck typo "62243"), which matches **ADR-012**'s existing 62443 basis and reinforces the **R8/PR13 procurement-evidence gate**; controls across **technology, process AND people** with **budget explicitly factored for tooling, documentation and training** (the people/budget line is the one most often missing); NIS-D/2 + EU Cybersecurity Act cited as the drivers. **(f) TWO PRESCRIPTIONS ARE ADDITIVE TO ADR-007 AND ARE WHY ITEM 7 EXISTS:** "**enforce ad-hoc testing during the design process (Digital Twin testing)**" — testing the design before metal exists, which ADR-007 does not contemplate — and "**assignment of external party for continuous testing**" alongside internal, i.e. **independent adversarial testing as a standing function rather than a one-off acceptance activity**. Both are generic good practice, neither is evidenced here, and both fill a real hole. **(g) READING NOTES:** text extracted cleanly and in full, no images requiring render, no page unread; the deck carries **no confidentiality or distribution marking** — the first in this conference series without one. **DOES NOT FLIP A STATUS AND — DELIBERATELY — DOES NOT EARN A MATRIX EVIDENCE-REF.** R3/R4 stay **Open**, R11 **Proposed — load-bearing**, R12 **Proposed**, R14 **Open**, ADR-007/-004/-012 **Proposed**. **A tier-B, client-less, unattributed threat taxonomy from a firm selling the remedy is not matrix-grade evidence, and putting it there would inflate the spine** — the corroboration at (d) is carried by E-2026-08-15-54, which is where the executability claim already sits. Its one durable output is **ADR-007 action item 7** plus a **SIM-SEC-003 candidate** in the security log's simulation backlog. SECURITY item → also mirrored in `evidence-log-security.md`. |
+| E-2026-08-15-56 | 2026-08-15 | "**Digital Europe Programme and Horizon Europe on Cybersecurity**" — **Ivan Scannapiecoro, Team Leader, ECCC** (European Cybersecurity Competence Centre); **59 slides**, fully read, no unread pages, no confidentiality marking | EU funding-body call briefing | **4th ERA-ENISA Conference, Lille — 03.10.2024**, session 4-2 (agenda-confirmed vs E-2026-08-15-22, which lists it as "Digital Europe Programme on Cybersecurity") | **A** | **R12** · **R14/ADR-012** · **ADR-007** · R8 · R13 (funding kin) | **watch** | **TIER A ON TRUST — the ECCC is an EU body established by Regulation (EU) 2021/887, and this is its Team Leader presenting the ECCC's own calls, i.e. primary on the thing it describes. BUT THE RELEVANCE IS THE WEAKEST IN TODAY'S RUN AND THE ROW SHOULD SAY SO PLAINLY: across 59 slides there is NO rail-specific content whatsoever — not one mention of railway, rail, ERA, ERJU, TSI, ERTMS or FRMCS, no rail topic, no rail call, no rail beneficiary category. It is a generic EU-cyber-funding briefing delivered to a rail audience because of the venue. Tier A ≠ relevant; the two must not be conflated.** **⚠️ AND EVERY DEADLINE IN IT HAS EXPIRED: DEP call closed 21.01.2025, Horizon CL3-2024-CS-01 closed 20.11.2024, GA signature Oct 2025. As a funding opportunity this deck is DEAD. Its value is structural — what instruments exist, who they are for, and what the EU thinks the problem is.** **(a) THE ONE FINDING THAT MATTERS FOR THIS ENGAGEMENT — THE EU'S STRUCTURAL ANSWER TO THE DETECTION GAP, AND RAIL IS A CONSUMER OF IT, NOT A BUILDER.** Two DEP topics fund the **SOC layer**: **CYBER-07-SOC** (National SOCs, topic €5.8M + €15M joint procurement; grants €1–2M at 50%; 36 months; beneficiaries **public bodies acting as National SOCs**) and **CYBER-07-SOCPLAT** (Cross-Border SOC Platforms, €5M + €17M joint procurement; grants €2–3M). Their stated purpose is to "improve the **DETECTION** of cybersecurity threats", built on "**live network data**", with "**automation, analysis and CORRELATION tools**", CTI feeds from field data through SIEM to high-level CTI, AI/ML plus **supercomputing access** "to boost the **correlation and detection** features". **This lands precisely on E-2026-08-15-03, where ENISA measured EU rail operators as HIGH-maturity at logging and LOW-maturity at DETECTION and at log CORRELATION/ANALYSIS.** The EU's remedy is not to make each operator build detection — it is national and cross-border SOCs that "deliver **early warnings to targeted critical infrastructures on a need-to-know basis**". **That reframes the question NS left open at E-2026-08-15-54 — "Do we need an OT SoC or not?" — from build-vs-not into build-vs-consume-vs-both, and the register had no record of the consume option existing.** R12's justification gains a structural limb; **status unchanged (Proposed)**. **(b) LARGEOPER IS THE INSTRUMENT THAT WOULD HAVE FUNDED THE GAP I OPENED AT ADR-007 ITEM 7 YESTERDAY — AT 100%.** **CYBER-07-LARGEOPER**, "Preparedness Support and Mutual Assistance, Targeting Larger Industrial Operations and Installations", **€35M**, grants **€3–5M at a 100% funding rate**, 36 months, explicitly benefiting "entities in sectors indicated as **critical infrastructure sectors in NIS2**" — which includes rail transport — and explicitly providing "**penetration testing and threat assessments for providers of essential services and critical infrastructures**", **development of penetration-testing scenarios**, testing of operating CI for vulnerabilities, **continuous attack-surface monitoring**, and asset/vulnerability monitoring; **Financial Support to Third Parties is compulsory for this topic (max €60k, ≥50% of project budget)**. **ADR-007 item 7 asks for independent adversarial testing as a standing function; this is an EU instrument class purpose-built to pay for it. The 2024 round is closed — the actionable item is to check the CURRENT DEP work programme for a LARGEOPER successor**, which is why this row is `watch`. → noted on ADR-007 item 7. **(c) CYBERSEC-02 (€20M, grants €2–4M at 50%) FUNDS EXACTLY THE SUPERVISORY GAP THIS REGISTER LOGGED FROM THREE MEMBER STATES.** Its objectives include "**supporting market surveillance authorities / notifying authorities / national accreditation bodies to implement the CRA**", NIS-2 implementation alignment, sectorial CSIRTs, and cross-border automated incident notification. **The FR/DE/FI national supervisory-competence gap recorded at E-2026-08-15-10/-11 and after is not just an observation by this register — the Commission identified it and put €20M against it.** Independent institutional corroboration of a thread the register built from national-authority decks. Bears **ADR-012**, no ADR edit (the connection is to supervisory capacity, not to our conformance obligations). **(d) KEYTECH (€35M, grants €3–5M, 50%/75% SME)** — AI/big-data/quantum/HPC/SDN for "**real-time pattern recognition, log analysis, vulnerability scanning**", **anomaly detection**, incident investigation. Note the strategic-autonomy condition: deployments "must be protected against possible dependencies and vulnerabilities... to **pre-empt foreign influence and control**" — the same policy instinct that drives **R8**, expressed as a funding condition. **(e) HORIZON EUROPE — TWO TOPICS, AND ONE OF THEM CORROBORATES AN EXISTING REGISTER THREAD RATHER THAN OPENING ONE.** **CL3-2024-CS-01-01** (IA, **€37M**, 6 projects): security in hardware/software development and assessment — "testing of hardware and software in **virtual, closed and secure environments**", automated vulnerability discovery and **dynamic security assessment**, "**trustworthy certifiable hardware and software**", AI-based predictive security and intrusion detection. Feeds **ADR-007 item 7** (design-stage/digital-twin testing) and touches **ADR-004** (certifiability). **CL3-2024-CS-01-02** (RIA, **€23.4M**, 4 projects): **post-quantum cryptography transition** — algorithm maturity, hybrid pre-/post-quantum transition tools, phase-in to existing applications, demonstrators. **⚠️ I initially drafted this as a gap in the register and that was WRONG — I checked before writing: PQC is an ESTABLISHED thread here (harvest-now-decrypt-later on long-lived signalling traffic, crypto-agility, NIST FIPS 203/204/205, the AES-128-lifespan procurement flag). This deck therefore CORROBORATES that thread with an institutional anchor — the EU funded the PQC transition at research level in 2024 — and does not surface anything new. The narrow open question it does raise: did any of those 4 projects produce rail-relevant output?** **(f) INSTITUTIONAL FACTS WORTH HAVING ON FILE** — ECCC established by **Reg (EU) 2021/887** (20.05.2021); **financially autonomous since 24.09.2024**; seated at the Polytechnic University of Bucharest (operations from 09.05.2023, permanent premises inaugurated 30.10.2024); ~38 staff. Three-part structure: **ECCC + Network of National Coordination Centres (one NCC per Member State, may receive AND pass on financial support) + the Cybersecurity Competence Community**. Strategic Agenda 2023–2027. **ENISA–ERA trail item: ENISA is tasked to develop guidelines on the INTEROPERABILITY of Cross-Border Cyber Hubs, and the SOCSYS grantee is required to collaborate with ENISA on it** — a further ENISA mandate the register had not recorded. **(g) PORTFOLIO GEOGRAPHY — RECORDED WITH A CAVEAT.** Across DEP calls CYBER-02/03/03b/04/06: **686 beneficiaries, 136 coordinators**. Leaders **EL 105 · RO 76 · CY 56 · BE 52 · IT 44**, against **DE 35 · FR 31 · ES 27 · NL 15**; **HU 0 coordinators**. Types: **50% private for-profit, 22% research & technology, 12% higher/secondary education, 8% public bodies, 8% other; 39% SME**. For CYBER-06: 65% private for-profit, only **12.8% public body**. **Observation, offered as observation only: participation skews markedly toward smaller and newer Member States and away from the large rail nations, and public bodies are a small minority — relevant to any expectation that an infrastructure manager would win in these instruments. CAVEAT: beneficiary COUNTS are not funding VOLUME, and these are general cyber calls, not rail calls; do not read a budget share off this.** **DOES NOT FLIP A STATUS AND EARNS NO MATRIX EVIDENCE-REF — deliberately.** R12 **Proposed**, R14 **Open**, R8 **Open**, ADR-007/-012 **Proposed**. **A funding briefing with zero rail content and wholly expired deadlines is not matrix-grade for a rail architecture spine, however trustworthy its source** — the same discipline applied to E-2026-08-15-55. Its one durable output is a funding-route note on **ADR-007 item 7**. SECURITY item → also mirrored in `evidence-log-security.md`. |
